  THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE,
  WITH EXTENSIONS TO GRAPHS WITH FEW VERTICES OF ODD
                        DEGREE

                                                DAVE GOEL

                                      Version 2.30, October 23, 2026.


        Abstract. A nowhere-zero flow of a graph can be changed along a cycle: a constant is
        added to its values on the edges of the cycle, in the direction of the cycle. For a finite
        abelian group A, a graph is A-flow-connected if any two of its nowhere-zero A-flows are
        joined by a chain of such changes in which every member is a nowhere-zero A-flow. It is
        k-flow-connected if the same holds for its integer flows whose values have an absolute value
        from 1 to k − 1. The Cranston–Li–Su–Wang–Xu (2026) conjecture states that every graph
        in which all degrees are even is A-flow-connected for every finite abelian group A with at
        least four elements, and k-flow-connected for every k ≥ 4. This was known when |A| or
        k is even, and when |A| ≥ 5,251,339. We prove the conjecture; for the groups, the proof
        reduces to the graphs with all degrees at most 4 by a step of the paper of Cranston et al.
        For group flows, a chain with at most 16M 3 + 12M 2 + M changes exists, where M is the
        number of the edges. The number 4 is exact as a ratio: for the modular (p, q)-flows with
        p > 2q, the statement that every graph in which all degrees are even is flow-connected
        holds if and only if p/q ≥ 4. The conjectured property holds for more graphs. Let every
        connected component of a graph have at most t vertices of odd degree. For t = 2 the
        graph is A-flow-connected for every finite abelian group A with at least four elements, and
        k-flow-connected for every k ≥ 4. For t = 6 it is Zk -flow-connected and k-flow-connected
        for every k ≥ 5; already for t = 4 the number 5 cannot be replaced by 4 here. For t = 8 it
        is Zk -flow-connected for every k ≥ 6, and k-flow-connected for k = 6, 7, 8, 9, 12, 16, 18 and
        36; here 6 cannot be replaced by 5: we give a graph with twelve vertices, eight of them
        of odd degree, that is neither Z5 -flow-connected nor 5-flow-connected. For t = 2r with an
        integer r ≥ 4 it is Zk -flow-connected for every k ≥ 3r − 2. For t = 12 it is 16-flow-connected
        and 18-flow-connected, and for t = 24 it is 36-flow-connected. For t = 2N/2+1 − 4 it is
        Zn                                 n
          2 -flow-connected, where N = 2 ≥ 4 is the number of the elements of the group. Among
        the connected graphs with at least one edge and at most eight vertices of odd degree,
        the graphs with a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow that no change along a cycle turns into another
        nowhere-zero Z5 -flow are exactly the subdivisions of the graph with twelve vertices named
        above and of one more graph with twelve vertices. Some of these proofs use contraction
        results of Cranston, Li, Su, Wang and Xu, and proofs of the cases that we use are included.
        The statements on the integer flows for t ≥ 6 and k ≥ 6 use theorems of another paper of
        ours. The present paper is the full version of a shorter paper, which proves only the results
        on the graphs in which all degrees are even.

                                       1. Introduction
   Let G be a finite graph; loops and parallel edges are allowed. Let A be a finite abelian
group. A nowhere-zero A-flow of G gives every edge, in a fixed direction, a non-zero value
in A, such that at every vertex the sum of the values that enter is equal to the sum of
the values that leave. Esperet, Hendrey, Lagoutte, Marseloo, Norin and Steiner [3] began
the study of the reconfiguration of these flows. Two nowhere-zero A-flows are adjacent if
they differ only on the edges of one cycle, and G is A-flow-connected if the graph F(G, A)
of all nowhere-zero A-flows with these adjacencies is connected. In the same way G is
k-flow-connected if the graph F(G, k) of its integer flows with values of absolute value from
1 to k − 1 is connected. We count a graph of flows with no vertex or with one vertex as
connected. Thus a flow-connected graph need not have a nowhere-zero flow. The exact
definitions are in Section 2 and in Section 8.
   Cranston, Li, Su, Wang and Xu [2] studied which graphs are flow-connected for small
groups and for small k. They call a graph Eulerian if every vertex has even degree (the
graph need not be connected), and they made the following conjecture.
        The Cranston–Li–Su–Wang–Xu (2026) conjecture (their Conjec-
         ture 4.1). Let G be an Eulerian graph. The following two claims hold.
           • G is k-flow-connected whenever k ≥ 4.
           • G is A-flow-connected whenever A is an abelian group with |A| ≥ 4.
   In [2] the first claim is proved for even k (their Theorem 4.2), and the second claim is
proved for the groups of even order (their Theorem 4.3 and the sentences before it) and for
the groups with |A| ≥ 5,251,339 (their Corollary 4.5). The authors add that “the odd case
seems likely to be harder”. So [2] leaves the first claim open for every odd k ≥ 5, and the
second claim for the groups of odd order with 5 ≤ |A| < 5,251,339. Some of these cases
follow from results on all bridgeless graphs in two earlier papers: [4] gives the first claim, for
graphs with no loop, for some odd k, for example for every prime power k = pN with N ≥ 6
(so for k = 729); and [5] gives the second claim for every group with |A| ≥ 3024. We prove
both claims in full, in particular for every odd k ≥ 5 and every group of odd order at least
5. A shorter paper [7], which uses no other paper of ours, proves the conjecture, the bound
on the length of the chains and the exact ratio 4 for the modular (p, q)-flows. The present
paper is its full version: it adds the graphs with few vertices of odd degree.
Theorem 1.1. Let G be a graph in which every vertex has even degree.
  (a) G is A-flow-connected for every finite abelian group A with |A| ≥ 4.
  (b) G is k-flow-connected for every integer k ≥ 4.
   Part (a) is Theorem 2.3, and part (b) is Theorem 8.1. One step of the proof of part (a),
the replacement of a vertex of large degree by a triangle, is from [2]: see (U3) below. The
number 4 cannot be replaced by 3. This is known: by [2, Theorem 2.1], a graph that has a
nowhere-zero 3-flow and two vertices joined by more than four edge-disjoint paths is neither
Z3 -flow-connected nor 3-flow-connected. An example is the graph with two vertices and six
parallel edges; for it see also Theorem 9.2 with p = 3 and q = 1, and [3, Observation 2.5].
All results in one table. The conjecture is on graphs with no vertex of odd degree. The
other theorems of this paper are on graphs with few vertices of odd degree in each connected
component. Table 1 sums up the statements on flow-connectedness of Theorems 1.1 to 1.5
and of Theorem 1.8. Theorems 1.6 and 1.7 are stated separately below: the first shows that
two numbers of the table cannot be made better, and the second is on the isolated flows at
6                                                 DAVE GOEL


the modulus 5. The number of the vertices of odd degree of a component is even. A graph
with a bridge has no nowhere-zero flow, and for it each statement of the table is empty.

   The first row is the Cranston–Li–Su–Wang–Xu (2026) conjecture. What was known
before for each row, and what the proof of each row uses from other work, is said after the
theorem of that row; a list of all outside results that the proofs use is given below, and a
list of open questions after it. Some limits were known before, and they show that several
numbers of the table cannot be made better. In the first two rows 4 cannot be replaced
by 3 [2, Theorem 2.1]. The complete graph K4 has four vertices of odd degree and is not
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        7


Z4 -flow-connected [3, Section 2.4]; so in the second row “two” cannot be replaced by “four”
for k = 4, and in the third row 5 cannot be replaced by 4. For k = 5 some graph with no
bridge is not Z5 -flow-connected [3, Theorem 3.4]; the graph given there has twenty vertices
of odd degree. One more limit is proved in this paper: a graph with twelve vertices, eight of
them of odd degree, is neither Z5 -flow-connected nor 5-flow-connected (Theorem 1.6). So in
the fourth row “six” cannot be replaced by “eight” for k = 5, and in the fifth row 6 cannot be
replaced by 5. In the last row, for the group Z22 , the number 4 cannot be replaced by 6: the
complete bipartite graph K3,3 is not Z22 -flow-connected (this follows from [3, Theorem 4.3]).
Graphs with two vertices of odd degree. We go beyond the even graphs.
Theorem 1.2. Let G be a graph in which every connected component has at most two vertices
of odd degree, and let k ≥ 4 be an integer.
    (a) Any two nowhere-zero Zk -flows of G are joined by a chain of nowhere-zero Zk -flows
        in which each flow comes from the one before it by adding 1, −1, 2 or −2 along one
        cycle.
    (b) Any two vertices of F(G, k) are joined by a path in F(G, k).
    (c) G is A-flow-connected for every finite abelian group A with |A| ≥ 4.
   Parts (a) and (b) are Theorem 10.1, and part (c) is Theorem 16.1. For a cyclic group,
part (c) follows from part (a). In part (c) a step can have any value other than 0. The
number of the vertices of odd degree of a graph is even, so every component has none or two
of them; an even graph with one more edge is an example.
   What was known for these graphs: for every graph with no bridge and no loop, [4] gives
that F(G, Zk ) and F(G, k) are connected for k = 40, for k = 48, for every multiple k of 4
with k ≥ 52, and for further values of k; and by [3, Theorem 7.1] every 2-edge-connected
graph G has some k, which depends on G, such that F(G, k) is connected. For groups, every
2-edge-connected graph is A-flow-connected for A = Z82 and for every abelian group A that
is large enough [3, Theorems 6.15 and 6.23]; and [5] gives this for every graph with no bridge
and every abelian group A with |A| ≥ 3024, and for some smaller groups. For even graphs
the theorem is Theorem 1.1 and result (ii) below. Here also 4 cannot be replaced by 3, and
this is known as well: by [2, Theorem 2.1], a graph with a nowhere-zero 3-flow and a vertex
of odd degree is neither Z3 -flow-connected nor 3-flow-connected. The smallest example, two
vertices and three parallel edges, is worked out at the end of Section 15. And “two” cannot be
replaced by “four” for k = 4: the complete graph K4 is not Z4 -flow-connected [3, Section 2.4].
For k ≥ 5 it can: this is the next theorem.
Graphs with four, six or eight vertices of odd degree. For the cyclic groups and the
integer flows, and for k ≥ 5, we go further: to four vertices of odd degree, and to six, for
every k ≥ 5; and to eight for k = 6 and, for the Zk -flows only, for k = 7, 8, 9 and for every
k ≥ 13 (for k = 10, 11, 12 see Theorem 1.5; for the integer flows at seven more moduli see
Theorem 1.8). So eight vertices of odd degree are allowed for the Zk -flows for every k ≥ 6.
Theorem 1.3. Let G be a graph in which every connected component has at most four
vertices of odd degree, and let k ≥ 5 be an integer.
    (a) Any two nowhere-zero Zk -flows of G are joined by a chain of nowhere-zero Zk -flows
        in which each flow comes from the one before it by adding 1, −1, 2 or −2 along one
        cycle.
    (b) Any two vertices of F(G, k) are joined by a path in F(G, k).
8                                        DAVE GOEL


    (c) For every graph H in which every connected component has at most six vertices of
        odd degree, the graphs F(H, Zk ) and F(H, k) are connected.
    (d) For every graph H in which every connected component has at most eight vertices of
        odd degree, the graphs F(H, Z6 ) and F(H, 6) are connected.
    (e) If k ≥ 13, then for every graph H in which every connected component has at most
        eight vertices of odd degree, the graph F(H, Zk ) is connected. This part is for the
        Zk -flows only.
    (f) If k is 7, 8 or 9, then for every graph H in which every connected component has at
        most eight vertices of odd degree, the graph F(H, Zk ) is connected. This part is for
        the Zk -flows only.
   For k = 5 parts (a) and (b) are Theorem 22.1, and for k ≥ 6 they are Theorem 29.1; see
also Corollary 29.2. Part (c) is Theorem 36.1 for k = 5, it is Theorem 54.1 for k = 6, and it
is Theorems 45.1 and 45.2 for k ≥ 7. Part (d) is Theorem 61.1; it is for the modulus 6 only.
Part (e) is Theorem 71.1, and part (f) is Theorem 86.1; they are for the Zk -flows only (for
the integer flows see Theorem 1.8 below). Parts (d), (e) and (f), with Theorem 1.5 below
for k = 10, 11, 12, give together: every graph in which every connected component has at
most eight vertices of odd degree is Zk -flow-connected for every k ≥ 6 (Corollary 86.2). For
k = 5 this is false (Theorem 1.6 below). In Z5 every value other than 0 is one of 1, −1, 2, −2;
so for k = 5 part (a) says that G is Z5 -flow-connected, and no more. In parts (c), (d), (e)
and (f) a step can have every value other than 0; for the four values 1, −1, 2, −2 see (Q5)
below. In parts (a) and (b) a component has none, two or four vertices of odd degree; the
complete graph K4 is an example with four. In part (c) a component can have six; the
complete bipartite graph K3,3 and the triangular prism are examples. In parts (d), (e) and (f)
a component can have eight; the cube and the Wagner graph, which is a cycle with eight
vertices and its four diameters, are examples.
   The number 5 cannot be replaced by 4, and this is known: K4 has four vertices of
odd degree, the graph F(K4 , Z4 ) is not connected, and F(K4 , 4) has three components [3,
Section 2.4]. What was known for k ≥ 5: for k = 5 the two statements do not hold for
every graph with no bridge. By [3, Theorem 3.4] there is a graph G with a nowhere-zero
Z5 -flow that is an isolated vertex of F(G, Z5 ); so, as stated there, F(G, Z5 ) and F(G, 5)
are not connected. By part (c) of Theorem 1.3, for every k ≥ 5, every graph G for which
F(G, Zk ) or F(G, k) is not connected has a component with at least eight vertices of odd
degree; the graph of [3, Theorem 3.4] is such a graph for k = 5, with twenty vertices of odd
degree, and the graph of Theorem 1.6 below is one with eight. By part (d), for k = 6 such a
graph has a component with at least ten vertices of odd degree. By parts (d), (e) and (f)
and Theorem 1.5, for every k ≥ 6, every graph G for which F(G, Zk ) is not connected has a
component with at least ten vertices of odd degree. For large k part (e) also follows from
[5, Theorem 1.1]: every graph with no bridge is Zk -flow-connected for k ≥ 3024, and [5,
Theorem 1.2] gives this for some smaller k also. The results on all graphs with no bridge
that are named after Theorem 1.2 hold for the graphs of Theorem 1.3 also.
   Theorem 1.3 is for the cyclic groups and the integer flows. Results for groups that are not
cyclic are in part (c) of Theorem 1.2 and in Theorem 1.4 below, which is for the groups Zn2 .
Some lemmas on graphs with a bounded number of vertices hold for every finite abelian group
with at least the number of elements that the lemma names, cyclic or not (Lemmas 47.1,
72.2, 79.1, 84.1 and 87.3). For the graphs in which a component can have eight vertices of
odd degree, the two statements of part (c) are false for the modulus 5 (Theorem 1.6 below),
and the integer flows for a modulus k ≥ 7 are treated in Theorem 1.8 below, which is for
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                           9


k = 7, 8, 9, 12, 16, 18 and 36. For a graph in which a component has ten or more vertices of
odd degree the integer flows are treated in parts (b) and (c) of Theorem 1.8, and the Zk -flows
in Theorem 1.5 below; the results of the earlier papers on all graphs with no bridge, which
are named after Theorem 1.2, hold for these graphs also. In part (e) the number 13 is not
the smallest possible, and for k ≥ 13 eight is not the largest possible: by parts (d) and (f)
and by Theorem 1.5 below, the statement of part (e) holds for every k from 6 to 12 also, and
for k ≥ 13 a component can have ten vertices of odd degree. The place where the proof of
part (c) for k = 5 stops on the way from six to eight can be seen on the cube. The reference
flows of that proof are integer flows along an orientation in which the out-degree is one more
than the in-degree at half of the vertices of odd degree, and one less at the other half. Such
a choice of half of the vertices of odd degree is called possible for a graph if every connected
component has as many chosen vertices as other vertices of odd degree, and if, for every set
W of vertices that at least one edge leaves, the number of the chosen vertices in W and the
number of the other vertices of odd degree in W differ by less than the number of the edges
that leave W (Section 60). On the cube, where all eight vertices have odd degree, one of the
possible choices has no reference flow with the values 1 to 4 (Proposition 60.2). The cube
itself is not an example against the statement for eight vertices of odd degree and k = 5:
a count by computer gives that its 156 nowhere-zero Z5 -flows are in one component. The
graph of Theorem 1.6 is such an example. For k = 6 the reference flows can have the values
1 to 5, and then every possible choice has a reference flow (Lemma 66.2). The theorem is for
graphs with a bound on the number of the vertices of odd degree in each component.
   We now say what the proof of each part uses from other work. For k ≥ 7 the proof of
part (c) contracts induced cycles of length two, three and four. The statements that allow
this are due to Cranston, Li, Su, Wang and Xu [2, Theorem 5.9(2) and Corollaries 5.13
and 5.15(i)]; Appendix A gives proofs of the cases that we use, after their proofs. For k = 6
the proof uses no contraction result from outside: the one contraction (a triangle of the
prism) and the expansions of a vertex into a triangle are proved in the text; the expansion
is the method of [2, Observation 4.6]. For the integer flows, part (c) uses theorems of our
paper [6]: its Theorem 2.1 for k = 6, and its Theorems 2.2 and 2.3 for k ≥ 7. Theorems 1.1
and 1.2, parts (a) and (b) of Theorem 1.3, part (c) for k = 5, and part (c) for the Zk -flows
for every k ≥ 6 are proved in this paper.
   The proof of part (d) also uses no contraction result from outside. It uses one classical
theorem, which we cite and do not prove: the odd-vertex pairing theorem of Nash-Williams
[9]. From it Section 60 derives Proposition 60.1, and the proof of part (d) uses this proposition.
For the integer flows, part (d) uses [6, Theorem 2.1], as part (c) does for k = 6. So part (d)
for the Z6 -flows is proved in this paper from the theorem of Nash-Williams.
   The proof of part (e) contracts cycles with at most eight edges. That such a cycle can be
contracted for k ≥ 13 is due to Cranston, Li, Su, Wang and Xu [2, Theorems 5.9 and 5.11
and Corollaries 5.13 and 5.15]; Appendix A and Lemma 72.1 give proofs of what we use.
The proof has a base case, the graphs with at most twenty-two vertices (Lemma 72.2). The
proof of the base case is the method of [2, Corollary 5.4], and for the groups with at least
thirteen elements the base case also follows directly from [2, Theorem 5.6]. The proof of
part (e) uses Proposition 60.1 also, and so the theorem of Nash-Williams, as the proof of
part (d) does. The proof also gives two steps along cycles with any value other than 0 and
the bound of twenty-two vertices (Sections 73 and 74). So part (e) is proved in this paper
from the theorem of Nash-Williams.
10                                         DAVE GOEL


   The proof of part (f) contracts cycles with at most four edges for k = 7, and with at most
five edges for k = 8 and k = 9. That such a cycle can be contracted is due to Cranston, Li, Su,
Wang and Xu [2, Theorem 5.9 and Corollary 5.13]; Appendix A and Lemma 72.1 give proofs
of what we use. The proof has two base cases: the graphs with at most fourteen vertices,
for the groups with at least seven elements, and the graphs with at most nineteen vertices,
for the groups with at least eight elements; in both, every component has at most eight
vertices of odd degree (Lemma 87.3). Their method is that of [2, Lemma 5.5, Corollary 5.4
and Theorem 5.6]. The counts for the two base cases use the bound on the number of the
vertices of odd degree (Lemmas 87.1 and 87.2): for a graph with fourteen or with nineteen
vertices, none of degree 2, [2, Theorem 5.6] needs a group with at least twelve or at least
thirteen elements. Counts of this kind are classical. The proof of part (f) uses part (c) for
k ≥ 7, Proposition 60.1, and so the theorem of Nash-Williams, and lemmas and proofs of
the sections for part (e) and for Theorem 1.5; among them are the two steps along cycles
of Lemmas 73.1 and 73.2, the forest of the special edges (Lemmas 74.3 and 78.2), and the
reference flows of Lemma 75.1. Two methods are used again. For k = 7 the flows f , 2f and
4f are treated together; this is the method of the sections for part (c) and k = 5, where the
flows f , 2f , 3f and 4f are treated together (Section 41). For k = 8 a step along a cycle of
edges with odd values is used (Lemma 92.1); this is the method of the sections for k = 6
(Lemma 62.1) at another even modulus. The proof also gives: the extension of the argument
of the special edges to the vertices of degree 4 at which exactly two edges have the values 1
or −1; a count of the ends of the special edges that uses the components of the graph of the
edges with other values; for k = 7, a bound on the number of the vertices of three kinds,
which go into each other under the multiplications by 2 and by 4 (Lemma 89.1); for k = 8, a
forest of edges with the value 4 at the vertices just named; and for k = 9, one forest for two
kinds of such edges. So part (f) is proved in this paper from the theorem of Nash-Williams.
The groups Zn2 . For the groups in which every element other than 0 has order 2, a component
can have more vertices of odd degree.
Theorem 1.4. Let n ≥ 2 be an integer, let A = Zn2 , and let N = 2n be the number of the
elements of A. Every graph in which every connected component has at most 2N/2+1 − 4
vertices of odd degree is A-flow-connected.
   This is Theorem 68.1. The number 2N/2+1 − 4 is 4 for Z22 , it is 28 for Z32 , it is 508 for Z42 ,
and it is 131068 for Z52 . For n ≥ 6 the theorem also follows from [5, Theorem 1.2]: every
graph with no bridge is A-flow-connected for these groups, and a graph with a bridge has no
nowhere-zero flow. The other cases are the four groups with 4, 8, 16 and 32 elements. For Z22
the number 4 cannot be replaced by 6, and this is known: the graph K3,3 has six vertices of
odd degree and is not Z22 -flow-connected; this follows from [3, Theorem 4.3], and Section 68
gives a direct count. For n = 3, 4, 5 we do not know whether the number 2N/2+1 − 4 is the
best possible. The theorem is for group flows over the groups Zn2 only.
   The proof contracts an induced cycle with fewer than N edges. That such a cycle can be
contracted for the group Zn2 is due to Cranston, Li, Su, Wang and Xu [2, Theorem 5.9(2)
and Corollary 5.14]; Lemma 69.1 and Lemma A.2 of the appendix give proofs of what we
use. So Theorem 1.4 is proved in this paper.
More vertices of odd degree for larger moduli. For the Zk -flows the number of the
vertices of odd degree can grow with k.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                           11


Theorem 1.5. Let r ≥ 4 and k ≥ 3r − 2 be integers. For every graph G in which every
connected component has at most 2r vertices of odd degree, the graph F(G, Zk ) is connected.
In other words: for every integer k ≥ 10, every graph in which every connected component
has at most 2⌊(k + 2)/3⌋ vertices of odd degree is Zk -flow-connected. This theorem is for the
Zk -flows only.
   This is Theorem 82.1. Theorem 76.1 is the same statement for k ≥ 3r + 1; we keep it,
because the proof of Theorem 82.1 uses lemmas and proofs of its sections. The number
2⌊(k + 2)/3⌋ is 8 for k = 10, 11, 12, it is 10 for k = 13, 14, 15, and it is 12 for k = 16, 17, 18.
The case r = 4 and k ≥ 13 is part (e) of Theorem 1.3. We keep part (e) also: it has its own
proof, with a base case of twenty-two vertices, and the proofs of Theorems 76.1 and 82.1 use
lemmas of that proof. By the theorem, for every k ≥ 10, every graph G for which F(G, Zk )
is not connected has a component with at least 2⌊(k + 2)/3⌋ + 2 vertices of odd degree.
For large k the theorem also follows from [5, Theorem 1.1]: every graph with no bridge is
Zk -flow-connected for k ≥ 3024, and [5, Theorem 1.2] gives this for some smaller k also.
   In Theorem 1.5 a step can have every value other than 0. The theorem is for the Zk -flows;
for the integer flows see Theorem 1.8 below, at k = 12, 16, 18 and 36.
   We describe first the proof for k ≥ 3r + 1 (Theorem 76.1). It contracts cycles with at most
2r edges, as the proof of part (e) does for r = 4, and the credit to Cranston, Li, Su, Wang and
Xu for this is as there. The proof has a base case, the graphs with at most 8r − 10 + r−1      2
vertices (Lemma 79.1); this number is 25, 36, 48, 61 for r = 4, 5, 6, 7. The proof of the base
case is the count in the proof of [2, Lemma 5.5], followed by [2, Corollary 5.4], as in their
proof of [2, Theorem 5.6]. We give the argument for graphs with loops, parallel edges and
bridges. The proof uses Proposition 60.1 also, and so the theorem of Nash-Williams. The
proof also gives two counts for any number of vertices of odd degree: the count of the vertices
of degree 4 at which exactly two edges have the values 1 or −1 (Lemma 78.1), and the forest
of the special edges (Lemma 78.2).
   The proof for k ≥ 3r − 2 (Theorem 82.1) contracts cycles with at most 2r − 2 edges,
and the credit for this is the same. A cycle with 2r edges through all vertices of degree 3
is not contracted now. The proof treats this cycle with a count of the edges that meet it
(Proposition 83.1). The base case is now the graphs with at most 8r − 10 + ⌊(r − 1)2 /2⌋
vertices and at most 2r vertices of odd degree (Lemma 84.1); this number is 26, 38, 50, 64 for
r = 4, 5, 6, 7. Its method is that of [2, Lemma 5.5 and Corollary 5.4]. The count is another
one: the count of [2, Lemma 5.5] gives a cycle with at most eight edges in a graph with
twenty-six vertices, which is too long for k = 10; a count from a vertex of degree at least 4,
which uses the bound on the number of the vertices of odd degree, gives a cycle with at most
six edges. Counts of this kind are classical. So Theorem 1.5 is proved in this paper from the
theorem of Nash-Williams.
The modulus 5: eight vertices of odd degree are too many. For k = 5 the number
six of part (c) of Theorem 1.3 cannot be replaced by eight.
Theorem 1.6. There is a connected graph G0 with twelve vertices and twenty edges, with no
loop and no parallel edge, in which eight vertices have degree 3 and four have degree 4, such
that neither F(G0 , Z5 ) nor F(G0 , 5) is connected. With earlier results this gives:
    (a) for an integer k ≥ 4, the statement that every graph in which every connected
        component has at most eight vertices of odd degree is Zk -flow-connected holds if and
        only if k ≥ 6;
12                                               DAVE GOEL


     (b) in part (c) of Theorem 1.3, for k = 5, “six” cannot be replaced by “eight”, for the
         Z5 -flows and for the integer flows.
    This is Theorem 96.1 with Corollaries 96.2 and 96.3. The graph G0 has the vertices
pi , qi , ri , si for i = 0, 1, 2. Its edges, each directed from the first vertex to the second, are
                  p0 q0 , p0 q1 , p0 q2 , p1 q1 , p2 q2 , r0 s0 , r1 s0 , r2 s0 , r1 s1 , r2 s2
with the value 1, and
                  p0 r0 , p1 r0 , p2 r0 , p1 r1 , p2 r2 , q0 s0 , q0 s1 , q0 s2 , q1 s1 , q2 s2
with the value 2. These values are a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow f0 . The edges with the value 1
form two trees, and the edges with the value 2 are exactly the edges between the two trees,
all directed from the same one of the two trees to the other; the same holds with the two
values exchanged. So a cycle, passed in one direction, has an edge with the value 1 along
its direction and one against it, and the same for the value 2; and then each of the four
values of a step along the cycle gives the value 0 on one of these four edges. So no step leads
from f0 to another nowhere-zero Z5 -flow. The flow −f0 is another nowhere-zero Z5 -flow; so
F(G0 , Z5 ) is not connected, and it follows that F(G0 , 5) is not connected. In statement (a),
the case k ≥ 6 is Corollary 86.2, the case k = 5 is the graph G0 , and the case k = 4 is the
graph K4 .
   A graph that has a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow and is not Z5 -flow-connected was given by
Esperet, Hendrey, Lagoutte, Marseloo, Norin and Steiner [3, Theorem 3.4]: a graph with
twenty vertices, all of degree 3 (their Figure 3). They write that it was found with the help of
Y. Wigderson and P. Morawski and of the tool AlphaEvolve. Their Lemma 3.5 describes the
nowhere-zero Z5 -flows with the values 1 and 2 that are isolated vertices of the graph of the
flows. Our proof that f0 is isolated is the “if” direction of that lemma for one pair of maps,
and we write the short argument out. The step to the integer flows is the argument of [3,
Observation 2.5]; we give it with an explicit integer flow, and so without the lifting theorem
of Tutte. The graph G0 has eight vertices of odd degree in the place of twenty. The positive
parts of the statements (a) and (b) are part (c) for k = 5 and Corollary 86.2. By part (c),
a graph that is not Z5 -flow-connected has a component with at least eight vertices of odd
degree, and G0 shows that eight is possible. Remark 100.2 reports a count by computer; no
proof uses it.
   The flow f0 is an isolated vertex of F(G0 , Z5 ). We call a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow of a graph
G isolated if it is an isolated vertex of F(G, Z5 ), that is, if no step leads from it to another
nowhere-zero Z5 -flow. A subdivision of a graph with no loop comes from it when edges are
replaced by paths with new inner vertices; the graph is a subdivision of itself (the exact
definition is in Section 104).
Theorem 1.7. Let G be a connected graph with at least one edge and with at most eight
vertices of odd degree. Then G has an isolated nowhere-zero Z5 -flow if and only if G is a
subdivision of G0 or of the graph G′0 of Remark 100.1.
   This is Theorem 104.1. The graph G′0 comes from G0 when the edges q1 s1 and q2 s2 are
replaced by q1 s2 and q2 s1 ; the two graphs are not isomorphic. A graph with no edge has one
nowhere-zero Z5 -flow, the empty one, and this flow is isolated; so the theorem asks for an
edge. The proof has two steps. For the connected graphs with no loop in which exactly eight
vertices have degree 3 and all other vertices have degree 4, the statement is Theorem 101.1:
after a change of directions an isolated flow has the values 1 and 2 only; the edges of each
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                           13


value form a forest with exactly two trees, then each of four sets of vertices has three vertices,
and the graph is one of the two. For all degrees, a count shows that a graph with at least one
edge and with all degrees at least 3 that has an isolated nowhere-zero Z5 -flow has at least eight
vertices of degree 3; if it has at most eight vertices of odd degree, it has exactly eight vertices
of degree 3 and all its other vertices have degree 4 (Corollary 104.2(a), Proposition 105.2(d)).
A vertex of degree 2 with no loop can be taken away, when its two edges are made one edge:
the graph of the flows stays the same up to isomorphism (Lemma 106.1). The two forests
are from the proof of [3, Lemma 3.5]. The theorem is on the isolated flows; for the graphs
for which F(G, Z5 ) is not connected and has no isolated vertex, see (Q6).
Integer flows at more moduli. Part (d) of Theorem 1.3 gives the integer flows for eight
vertices of odd degree at the modulus 6 only. The next theorem gives them at seven more
moduli.
Theorem 1.8.       (a) Every graph in which every connected component has at most eight
      vertices of odd degree is k-flow-connected for k = 7, 8, 9, 12, 16, 18 and 36.
  (b) Every graph in which every connected component has at most twelve vertices of odd
      degree is 16-flow-connected and 18-flow-connected.
  (c) Every graph in which every connected component has at most twenty-four vertices of
      odd degree is 36-flow-connected.
   For k = 7 part (a) is Theorem 108.1. For k = 8, 9 and 12 it is part (a) of Corollary 112.1,
and parts (b) and (c) are the two other parts of that corollary; they contain part (a)
for k = 16, 18 and 36. With part (d) of Theorem 1.3 this gives: every graph in which
every connected component has at most eight vertices of odd degree is k-flow-connected for
k = 6, 7, 8, 9, 12, 16, 18 and 36. The theorem is for the seven moduli that it names.
   The modulus 7. The proof uses part (f) of Theorem 1.3 for k = 7 and does not replace
its proof. A graph with at most twenty vertices has no cycle with more than twenty edges,
and for k = 7 every step between two nowhere-zero Z7 -flows along a cycle with at most
twenty-one edges is the reduction of a step between two integer 7-flows, by Theorem 2.3
of [6]. That theorem does not say that such a step starts at a given integer flow with the
right reduction. But any two integer 7-flows with the same reduction are joined by a path
of integer flows with that reduction; so, along a path of Z7 -flows, the integer steps that are
chosen for its steps are joined by such paths, and for such a graph the statement follows
from part (f) (Lemma 109.2). For the graphs with more vertices the proof goes back to the
first counts of the proof of part (f), those before the flows f , 2f and 4f are treated together.
These counts need only steps with the values 1, −1, 2, −2, and such a step between two
nowhere-zero Z7 -flows is the reduction of a step between two integer 7-flows. With the paths
just named this gives: such a step does not lead out of the set of the reductions of the flows
of one component of F(G, 7) (Lemma 109.1). So the counts can be made for one component
of F(G, 7): if G is connected and has no loop, exactly eight vertices of degree 3 and all other
vertices of degree 4, and if no flow of some component of F(G, 7) has a balanced pair at a
vertex of degree 4, then G has at most twenty vertices (Proposition 110.1). The rest is as in
the proofs of parts (c) and (e): a balanced pair is split; the two graphs that come from two
such pairs have nowhere-zero flows, so they have no bridge, and with the same eight vertices
of odd degree they have a possible choice in common (Proposition 60.1); two reference flows
of one choice, with absolute values from 1 to 5, are joined by a path of integer flows, which
is the path in the proof of Lemma 75.1; a graph with at most six vertices of odd degree in
each component is 7-flow-connected by part (c); and a vertex of larger degree is replaced by
14                                         DAVE GOEL


a triangle. From [6] the proof takes the lift of a step with the values 1, −1, 2, −2 and the
theorem on the cycles with at most twenty-one edges.
   The six other moduli. Let k be one of 8, 9, 12, 16, 18, 36. For every graph G, reduction
modulo k induces a bijection between the connected components of F(G, k) and those of
F(G, Zk ) [6, Theorem 18.1]. So for these six k a graph is k-flow-connected if and only if it
is Zk -flow-connected, and the three parts follow from Corollary 86.2 and Theorem 1.5: the
number 2⌊(k + 2)/3⌋ is 8, 12, 12, 24 for k = 12, 16, 18, 36. The proof of the bijection is in [6],
and not here. It is a short deduction from a lemma on the steps that can be lifted, which
is from our earlier paper [4, Lemma 1.12], and [6] cites it from there. The six moduli were
found in the work on [4]. That deduction is not used for the modulus 7; the proof for k = 7
is another one.
More results. In this paper an even graph is a graph in which every vertex has even degree,
and a step is a change of a flow along one cycle.
    (i) An even graph in which all degrees are at most 4 is A-flow-connected for every finite
        abelian group A, also for the groups with fewer than four elements (Theorem 2.2).
   (ii) In every even graph, for the cyclic group Zm with m ≥ 4, steps with the values
        1, −1, 2, −2 are enough (Theorem 2.4). The proof of part (b) of Theorem 1.1 rests on
        this.
  (iii) The chains are short. In an even graph with M edges, any two nowhere-zero A-
        flows are joined by at most 16M 3 + 12M 2 + M steps if |A| ≥ 4, and by at most
        M 3 + 3M 2 + M steps if all degrees are at most 4 (Theorem 2.5).
  (iv) For integers q ≥ 1 and p > 2q, a modular (p, q)-flow is a Zp -flow whose values are
        residues of the integers q, q + 1, . . . , p − q; for q = 1 these are the nowhere-zero
        Zp -flows. The statement that every even graph is (p, q)-flow-connected holds if and
        only if p ≥ 4q (Theorems 9.1 and 9.2). So the number 4 of the conjecture is exact as
        a ratio p/q.
   (v) The Esperet–Hendrey–Lagoutte–Marseloo–Norin–Steiner (2025) lifting question [3,
        Problem 2.6] asks for a proof that F(G, k) is connected whenever F(G, Zk ) is con-
        nected. For even graphs this is true for every k ≥ 2 (Remark 8.8). For every graph
        it is true for k ≤ 6 and for k = 8, 9, 12, 16, 18, 36 [6, Theorems 2.1 and 18.1]; see
        Theorem 1.8.
What the proofs use from other work. We list the results of other papers that the
proofs of this paper use, and say for each one whether this paper proves it again. The details
and the exact credit are after the theorems above and in the sections.
  (U1) Integer flows and flows modulo k. Every nowhere-zero Zk -flow is the reduction
        modulo k of an integer k-flow (the lifting theorem of Tutte [11], in the form of [3,
        Theorem 2.3]), and any two integer k-flows with the same reduction are joined [3,
        Lemma 5.5]; the numbers are those of the fourth version of [3]. Both are known; we
        give the short proofs (Lemmas 8.3 and 8.4).
  (U2) The reference flows of the even graphs. The flows with one value along an Eulerian
        orientation are the reference flows in the proofs of [2, Theorems 4.2 and 4.3], and
        by [2, Lemma 2.3] any two Eulerian orientations are joined by reversals of directed
        cycles. We prove what we use (Lemma 3.3).
  (U3) A vertex of large degree is replaced by a triangle. This is the reduction in the proof of
        [2, Observation 4.6]: for |A| ≥ 5, every even graph is A-flow-connected if and only if
        every graph with all degrees 4 is. We use one direction only: if the graph with the
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          15


        triangle is A-flow-connected, then so is the graph in which the triangle is contracted.
        This is a case of [2, Theorem 5.9(1)]: a triangle is A-connected, in the sense of [8],
        when |A| ≥ 4. We prove it again, for |A| ≥ 4 and with the values and the number
        of the steps (Lemma 5.1); the later parts use the same reduction. So the proof of
        Theorem 1.1(a) rests on the statement for the graphs with all degrees at most 4
        (result (i)).
  (U4) Contraction of short induced cycles. For part (c) of Theorem 1.3 and k ≥ 7: induced
        cycles of length two, three and four [2, Theorem 5.9(2) and Corollaries 5.13 and 5.15(i)].
        For parts (e) and (f) and for Theorem 1.5: cycles with fewer than ⌈2k/3⌉ edges [2,
        Theorems 5.9 and 5.11 and Corollaries 5.13 and 5.15]. For Theorem 1.4: induced cycles
        with fewer than N edges for the group Zn2 [2, Theorem 5.9(2) and Corollary 5.14].
        In each case this paper gives proofs of what it uses (Appendix A, Lemma 72.1 and
        Lemma 69.1).
  (U5) Graphs with few vertices. The base cases of part (e) and of Theorem 1.5 (Lemmas 72.2
        and 79.1) use methods of [2]. The proof of the first is the method of [2, Corollary 5.4].
        The proof of the second is the count in the proof of [2, Lemma 5.5], followed by [2,
        Corollary 5.4], as in the proof of [2, Theorem 5.6]; we give it for graphs with loops,
        parallel edges and bridges. A third base case (Lemma 84.1) uses the same method
        with another count, which is of a classical kind. The two base cases of part (f)
        (Lemma 87.3) use the same method with two more counts of this kind (Lemmas 87.1
        and 87.2).
  (U6) The pairing theorem of Nash-Williams [9]; see also [1]. It is cited and not proved
        here. Proposition 60.1 is derived from it, and the proofs of parts (d), (e) and (f) of
        Theorem 1.3, of Theorem 1.5 and of Theorem 1.8 for k = 7 use this proposition.
  (U7) The integer flows for six and more vertices of odd degree. For the integer flows, part (c)
        of Theorem 1.3 for k ≥ 6 and part (d) use Theorems 2.1, 2.2 and 2.3 of our paper [6].
        Theorem 1.8 uses that paper also: for k = 7 its Theorem 2.3, its Corollary 4.2 on
        the steps with the values 1, −1, 2, −2, and the lifting facts of its Section 3; for the
        six other moduli its Theorem 18.1. These statements are cited and not proved here.
        Theorem 18.1 of [6] rests on a lemma of our earlier paper, [4, Lemma 1.12], which
        Section 17 of [6] cites.
  (U8) An isolated flow at the modulus 5. The proof of Theorem 1.6 is the “if” direction of
        [3, Lemma 3.5] for one pair of maps, and its step to the integer flows is the argument
        of [3, Observation 2.5]. We write both out (Section 98). The two statements of the
        theorem on all graphs use part (c) of Theorem 1.3 for k = 5, Corollary 86.2, and the
        graph K4 [3, Section 2.4]. The proof of Theorem 101.1 repeats, in the form that it
        needs, the argument of the “only if” direction of [3, Lemma 3.5]; its converse is the
        “if” direction. The proof of Theorem 1.7 uses Theorem 101.1 and gives the first part
        of that argument (the two forests) again, for all graphs with no loop (Lemma 105.1);
        its count and its step for the vertices of degree 2 use no other work.
The theorem of Menger is used in its usual form [10]. The papers [3], [4] and [5] are cited
also for the definitions, for what was known before, and for the examples that show limits.
Open questions. The following questions are open as far as we know.
 (Q1) Eight vertices of odd degree. Is every graph in which every connected component
      has at most eight vertices of odd degree k-flow-connected for every k ≥ 6? This
      is a case of (Q3). The answer is yes for k = 6 (part (d) of Theorem 1.3) and for
16                                           DAVE GOEL


          k = 7, 8, 9, 12, 16, 18, 36 (Theorem 1.8), and for k = 5 it is no (Theorem 1.6). At
          larger moduli the answer is yes for k = 40, for k = 48 and for every multiple k of 4
          with k ≥ 52, by [4, Theorem 9.2], and for the further values of k that are given in [4]
          (see the paragraph after Theorem 1.2; the graphs there have no loop, and a loop can
          be deleted, because a step on a loop changes the value of the loop only). This paper,
          [6] and [4] do not settle the question for k = 10, 11, 13, 14, 15, 17 and for every k
          from 19 to 39 other than 36; for k ≥ 41 we give no list of the moduli that are not
          settled. For the Zk -flows the answer is known for every k ≥ 4: it is yes if and only if
          k ≥ 6.
     (Q2) More vertices of odd degree. For a given k ≥ 4, what is the largest even number t
          such that every graph in which every connected component has at most t vertices of
          odd degree is Zk -flow-connected? For some k there is no largest t: for k ≥ 3024, and
          for the smaller k of [5, Theorem 1.2], every graph with no bridge is Zk -flow-connected.
          For k = 4 the answer is t = 2 (Theorem 1.2 and the graph K4 ). For k = 5 the answer
          is t = 6 (part (c) of Theorem 1.3 and Theorem 1.6). Theorem 1.3 gives t ≥ 8 for
          k = 6, 7, 8, 9, and Theorem 1.5 gives t ≥ 2⌊(k + 2)/3⌋ for k ≥ 10. For n = 3, 4, 5, is
          the number 2N/2+1 − 4 of Theorem 1.4 the largest possible?
     (Q3) Integer flows. Do parts (e) and (f) of Theorem 1.3 and Theorem 1.5 hold for the
          integer flows also? Theorem 1.8 gives this for part (f), for part (e) at k = 16, 18, 36,
          and for Theorem 1.5 at k = 12, at k = 16, 18 and at k = 36, where the largest
          numbers of vertices of odd degree that it allows are 8, 12 and 24. The other cases are
          not settled here; at the moduli of [4] that (Q1) names, the integer statements hold,
          because every graph with no bridge is k-flow-connected there. A positive answer to
          the lifting question of [3] would give all of them; [6] proves that statement for k ≤ 6
          and for k = 8, 9, 12, 16, 18, 36.
     (Q4) Groups that are not cyclic. Is every graph in which every connected component has
          at most four vertices of odd degree, or at most six, A-flow-connected for every finite
          abelian group A with at least five elements? Parts (a) and (c) of Theorem 1.3 give
          this for the cyclic groups.
     (Q5) The values of the steps. For k ≥ 6 and graphs in which every connected component
          has at most six, or at most eight, vertices of odd degree: are steps with the values
          1, −1, 2, −2 enough?
     (Q6) Fewer than twelve vertices at the modulus 5. Theorem 1.7 does not say which
          connected graphs with at most eight vertices of odd degree are Z5 -flow-connected: if
          a loop is added at a vertex of G0 , then the graph of the nowhere-zero Z5 -flows of the
          new graph is not connected and has no isolated vertex (a step on the loop changes
          the value of the loop only, so no flow is isolated; every other step is a step of G0 that
          keeps this value, so two flows that are not joined in F(G0 , Z5 ) are not joined in the
          new graph). Is there a connected graph with at most eight vertices of odd degree
          and with fewer than twelve vertices that is not Z5 -flow-connected? By Theorem 1.7
          no nowhere-zero Z5 -flow of such a graph is isolated.
The proofs in outline. Let f be a nowhere-zero A-flow of a graph with no loop. Two ends
of edges at a vertex v are a balanced pair of f if the values of f that leave v through these
two ends have the sum 0. The flows in which two given ends at v are a balanced pair are
the flows of a graph with one edge less: the two edges are replaced by one edge that does
not pass through v (Lemma 3.2). A step of the smaller graph is a step, or a chain of steps
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                       17


with the same value, of the given graph. In an even graph every pair of ends at a vertex is a
balanced pair of a flow that has one value a along all arcs of an Eulerian orientation, and
any two flows of this kind with the same a are joined (Lemma 3.3; see (U2) above). With
induction on the number of the edges this gives the reduction of Section 3: it is enough to
show that every flow is joined to a flow with a balanced pair at some vertex.
   For this last statement we fix a set of small values: 1 and −1 in a cyclic group, and in
general the generators of the cyclic summands of A and their negatives. If the edges whose
value is not small contain a cycle, steps along this cycle make one more edge small. If they
contain no cycle, they are fewer than the vertices. The reduction removes the loops and the
vertices of degree 2, and a graph with all degrees at least 4 has at least twice as many edges
as vertices; so the edges with small values contain cycles, and steps along one of these cycles
lead to a balanced pair. For a cyclic group Zm with m ≥ 4 this argument works for all degrees
at once (Section 6). For an arbitrary group it works when all degrees are 4 (Section 4); a
vertex of larger degree is then replaced by a triangle whose vertices have smaller degrees,
and the contraction of a triangle keeps a graph A-flow-connected if |A| ≥ 4 (Section 5). This
reduction to the graphs with all degrees at most 4 is that of [2, Observation 4.6]; see (U3)
above.
   For integer flows we use three facts: every nowhere-zero Zk -flow is the reduction modulo k
of an integer k-flow (Tutte); any two integer k-flows with the same reduction are joined; and
a step with the value 1 or 2 between two nowhere-zero Zk -flows is the reduction of a step
between two integer k-flows. So a chain with the values 1, −1, 2, −2 can be lifted step by
step (Section 8).
   A graph with two vertices u, v of odd degree has no Eulerian orientation. In its place we
take an orientation in which the out-degree is one more than the in-degree at one of u, v, one
less at the other, and equal to it at every other vertex, and the integer flows with the values
1 and 2 along such an orientation (the anchors of Section 12). For k ≥ 4 their reductions
modulo k are all joined, also those of the two choices at u and v. The count of the edges now
has one case of equality. In it the graph has, apart from vertices of degree 0, two vertices
and three parallel edges, and the flow is the reduction of an anchor (Proposition 13.2).
   For an arbitrary group A with |A| ≥ 4 and these graphs there are two cases. If A has
an element a of order at least 3, the reference flows are the products of a with the anchors
that have the values 1 and 2, and all of them are joined (Lemma 19.1); the two choices at
u and v are joined along three paths, with no use of the value 3a, which can be 0. For a
graph with all degrees at most 4 and at most two vertices of odd degree, any two flows are
joined; the proof is an induction on the number of the edges, and the graph with two vertices
and three parallel edges, and the graphs in which two or more parallel edges join the two
vertices of degree 3, are treated separately (Proposition 20.4). If every element of A other
than 0 has order 2, there are no such reference flows. Here we use a subgroup H of A: a
step with a value in H, along a cycle of edges whose values are not in H, makes no value 0
(Lemma 17.2). At a vertex of degree 4 the four ends can be divided into two pairs in three
ways. Steps of this kind lead to a flow with a balanced pair, and they join the flows that
are balanced in different ways (Proposition 18.3). In both cases a vertex of larger degree is
replaced by a triangle, as for the even graphs (Lemma 21.1).
   For a graph with four vertices of odd degree and a cyclic group Zk with k ≥ 5, the reference
flows are integer flows with the values 1, 2, 3 along an orientation in which the out-degree
is one more than the in-degree at two of the four vertices, and one less at the other two
(the 4-anchors of Section 27). The two vertices with one more can be chosen in six ways.
18                                        DAVE GOEL


Not every choice is possible for every graph with no bridge, but at least four of the six
are (Lemma 27.3). So two such graphs with the same four vertices of odd degree have a
common choice, and two reference flows of one choice are joined (Lemmas 27.5 and 34.1).
The count of the edges has a larger case of equality now. For k = 5 a vertex of degree 4
with no balanced pair has the values a, a, a, 2a on its four ends. In the case of equality the
edges with the values 2 and −2 form a spanning tree, then the edges with the values 1 and
−1 also, and at a vertex of degree 4 this gives a bridge, which a graph with a flow does not
have (Proposition 25.1). For k ≥ 6 the proof uses a lemma on a cycle in which exactly one
edge has a value other than 1 and −1 (Lemma 31.1). With it the case of equality leaves only
graphs with six vertices, and in them steps along a cycle with four edges lead to a balanced
pair (Proposition 32.1). The two connected graphs with four vertices, all of degree 3, and no
loop are treated directly (Lemmas 26.2 and 33.2).
   For a graph with six vertices of odd degree and k = 5, the out-degree of the orientation of
a reference flow is one more than the in-degree at three of the six vertices, and one less at
the other three. The three vertices can be chosen in twenty ways, and two graphs with no
bridge and the same six vertices of odd degree have at least two possible choices in common
(Lemma 38.4). That two such graphs have a possible choice in common follows, for any
number of vertices of odd degree, from the odd-vertex pairing theorem of Nash-Williams [9]
(Proposition 60.1); Lemma 27.3 and Lemma 38.4 are elementary proofs of the cases that we
need for four and for six vertices of odd degree. The count of the edges alone is not enough
now. The idea is to count the edges with the values 1 and −1 for all flows that are joined
to f , 2f , 3f or 4f , where f is the given flow, and to take a flow with the largest count.
Multiplication by 2 maps flows to flows and steps to steps, and it exchanges the values 1, −1
with the values 2, −2; it is not assumed that f and 2f are joined. If none of these flows
has a balanced pair, then for a flow with the largest count this shows that the edges with
the values 1, −1 form a forest, and those with the values 2, −2 also, unless two vertices of
degree 3 are joined by two parallel edges (Proposition 41.1); that case is reduced to four
vertices of odd degree (Lemma 37.5). Two such forests leave only graphs with seven or eight
vertices, and in them every case leads to a balanced pair (Lemma 40.2). The graphs with
six vertices, all of degree 3, are treated directly; the simple ones are K3,3 and the triangular
prism (Lemmas 42.1 and 42.2).
   For a graph with six vertices of odd degree and k ≥ 7, the reference flows are those of the
case k = 5, read in Zk (Lemma 50.1). If a connected graph with no loop, with all degrees 3
or 4 and with six vertices of degree 3 has a flow from which restricted steps lead to no flow
with a balanced pair, then the graph has at most fourteen vertices (Proposition 49.4); the
proof uses cycles in which one or two edges have a value other than 1 and −1 (Lemmas 48.1
and 48.2). The graphs with no bridge, with at most fourteen vertices and with at most six
vertices of odd degree in each component are treated with no list of cases (Lemma 47.1).
After the loops and the vertices of degree 0 are deleted and each vertex of degree 2 is replaced
by one edge, as long as this is possible, every component that remains has a cycle with at
most four edges. On each further edge between two vertices of this cycle one new vertex
of degree 2 is put; then the cycle is induced, and it is contracted by Corollary A.3 of the
appendix, a statement of [2]. In the contracted graph each new vertex is replaced by one
edge again, which is a loop, and these loops are deleted. The induction is on the number of
the vertices: it is applied to this graph, which has fewer vertices than the graph in which
the cycle was found. For the integer flows, a step along a cycle with at most nine edges
(twenty-one for k = 7) is lifted to the integer flows, and in the graphs that remain every
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        19


longer cycle has a chord (Theorem 46.8, which is from [6]); for k ≥ 8 this needs the bound
thirteen in place of fourteen (Proposition 51.1).
   For a graph with six vertices of odd degree and k = 6, the step to a balanced pair is
short: in a graph with no loop, at most two steps lead from every nowhere-zero Z6 -flow to
a flow with a balanced pair at a given vertex of degree 4 (Lemma 56.2). The proof reads
the flow modulo 2 and modulo 3: the edges with an odd value form a subgraph with all
degrees even, and the edges with a value other than 3 carry a nowhere-zero Z3 -flow. The
reference flows are again those of the case k = 5, read in Z6 (Lemma 58.1). The graphs with
six vertices, all of degree 3, are treated directly (Proposition 57.4); for K3,3 the proof is by
hand (Lemma 57.1). Some of these steps have the value 3, and we do not lift them to the
integer flows one by one; the statement on the integer flows follows from [6, Theorem 2.1].
   For a graph with eight vertices of odd degree and k = 6, the step to a balanced pair is the
same Lemma 56.2: it has no condition on the number of the vertices of odd degree. The
reference flows are integer flows with the values 1 to 5 along an orientation in which the
out-degree is one more than the in-degree at four of the eight vertices, and one less at the
other four. Every possible choice of the four vertices has such a flow (Lemma 66.2), and two
reference flows of one choice are joined (Lemma 66.3). In the proof of the second statement a
directed cycle is reversed by an integer step whose value is from 2 to 6; a step with the value
6 changes nothing modulo 6. Two graphs with no bridge and the same eight vertices of odd
degree have a possible choice in common by Proposition 60.1. The graphs with eight vertices,
all of degree 3, are treated directly (Proposition 65.3). Those with no parallel edges and no
triangle are the cube and the Wagner graph, and for both the proof is by hand (Lemmas 63.1
and 64.2); it reads the flow modulo 2 and modulo 3 again (Lemma 62.1). A graph with two
parallel edges or a triangle is reduced to a graph with six vertices (Lemmas 65.1 and 65.2).
The statement on the integer flows follows from [6, Theorem 2.1], as for six vertices of odd
degree.
   For a graph with eight vertices of odd degree and the Zk -flows with k ≥ 13, take first
a graph with no loop in which every degree is 3 or 4 and eight vertices have degree 3. If
it has a cycle with an even number of edges on which every vertex has degree 3, then this
cycle has at most eight edges. Such a cycle can be contracted for k ≥ 13 (Lemma 72.1, from
Lemmas A.1 and A.2), and the graph after the contraction has at most six vertices of odd
degree; so part (c) gives the statement. A graph with at most twenty-two vertices is treated
by contractions of cycles with at most seven edges (Lemma 72.2). Now let the graph have no
such cycle and more than twenty-two vertices, and suppose that steps reach no balanced
pair from some flow. Among the flows that steps reach from it, take one with the largest
number of edges with the values 1 and −1. Then the edges with these values form a forest,
and the other edges form a forest (Lemma 74.1). Two steps, each along one cycle and with a
value that can be any value other than 0 (Lemmas 73.1 and 73.2), and a count give that the
graph has at most twenty-two vertices (Proposition 74.4), which is not so. So steps reach a
balanced pair from every flow, and the rest is as for k = 6: reference flows with the values
1 to 5 (Lemma 75.1) and Proposition 60.1. A graph with a vertex of degree more than 4
is reduced to these graphs by the expansion of a vertex into a triangle, as in the proofs of
part (c) for k ≥ 7 and of part (d). The steps of this proof are along cycles of any length and
have any value other than 0. We do not lift them to integer flows; part (e) is for the Zk -flows
only.
   For Theorem 1.5 the proof for eight vertices of odd degree is made general. We describe
first the proof for k ≥ 3r + 1 (Theorem 76.1). Fix k. The proof is an induction on r with
20                                       DAVE GOEL


k ≥ 3r + 1: it starts with six vertices of odd degree, which is part (c) of Theorem 1.3, and
it uses the statement for r − 1 for graphs with any degrees. Take a graph with no loop in
which every degree is 3 or 4 and 2r vertices have degree 3. A cycle with an even number of
edges on which every vertex has degree 3 has at most 2r edges. It can be contracted because
k ≥ 3r + 1 (Lemma 72.1), and the graph after the contraction has at most 2r − 2 vertices of
odd degree. Now let the graph have no such cycle, and suppose that steps reach no balanced
pair from some flow. As for eight, among the flows that steps reach from it take one with
the largest number of edges with the values 1 and −1; these edges form a forest, and it has
at most r − 1 trees. The number of the vertices of degree 4 with exactly two such edges is at
most the number of the pairs of trees (Lemma 78.1), and the number of those with three
                          the number of the trees minus 2 (Lemma 78.2). So the graph has
such edges is at most twice
at most 8r − 10 + r−1  2   vertices (Proposition 78.3). A graph with at most this number of
vertices is treated by contractions of cycles with at most 2r edges; the count that gives such
a cycle is that of [2, Lemma 5.5] (Lemma 79.1). The reference flows have the values 1 to
r + 1 (Lemma 80.1). As in part (e), the steps are not lifted to integer flows.
   For k ≥ 3r − 2 (Theorem 82.1) the plan is the same, with two changes. A cycle with an
even number of edges on which every vertex has degree 3 is contracted only if it has fewer
than 2r edges; then it has at most 2r − 2 edges, which is less than ⌈2k/3⌉. So one such cycle
with exactly 2r edges can remain; it passes through all 2r vertices of degree 3. In a flow
with the largest number of edges with the values 1 and −1, these edges now form a forest,
or this one cycle and at most r − 1 trees. An edge that meets the cycle and has another
value has the value 2 or −2, and with this the two counts are made again: the graph has at
most 8r − 10 + ⌊(r − 1)2 /2⌋ vertices (Proposition 83.1). The second change is the base case.
A graph with at most this number of vertices and at most 2r vertices of odd degree has,
after the usual reductions, a cycle with at most 2r − 2 edges: the count starts at a vertex of
degree at least 4, and a graph with all degrees 3 has at most 2r vertices (Lemma 84.1). The
reference flows are those of Lemma 80.1; its proof works for every k > r + 2.
   For a graph with eight vertices of odd degree and the Zk -flows with k = 7, 8, 9 (part (f)),
the plan is again that of part (e). The reference flows are the same, with the values 1 to
5; Lemma 75.1 joins them for every k ≥ 7 (the proof of Lemma 80.1 gives the same). The
cycles that can be contracted are shorter now: at most four edges for k = 7, and at most
five edges for k = 8 and k = 9. So the base cases need two more counts: a graph with at
least one and at most fourteen vertices, all of degree at least 3, and at most eight vertices
of odd degree has a cycle with at most four edges, and such a graph with at most nineteen
vertices has a cycle with at most five edges (Lemmas 87.1 and 87.2). Now take a graph with
no loop in which every degree is 3 or 4 and eight vertices have degree 3, suppose that steps
reach no balanced pair from some flow, and take a flow with the largest number of edges
with the values 1 and −1 among the flows that steps reach from it. For k = 8 and k = 9 a
count of the vertices of degree 4 gives that the graph has at most eighteen vertices. For k = 8
the count uses a step along a cycle of edges with odd values, which keeps these values odd
(Lemma 92.1; the method is that of Lemma 62.1 for k = 6), and a forest made of edges with
the value 4. For k = 9 it uses one forest made of edges of two kinds, with the values 3, −3
and 4, −4. For k = 7 the base case has only fourteen vertices, and this count alone is not
enough. Here the flows f , 2f and 4f are treated together, as for six vertices of odd degree
and k = 5: multiplication by 2 maps flows to flows and steps to steps, and it is not assumed
that f and 2f are joined. Three kinds of vertices of degree 4 go into each other under these
multiplications, and a flow from which steps reach no balanced pair has at most two vertices
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                         21


of each kind (Lemma 89.1). With this, a graph with more than fourteen vertices has a cycle
with at most four edges whose contraction leaves at most six vertices of odd degree, or steps
reach a balanced pair from every flow. As in part (e), the steps are not lifted to integer flows.
   For the groups Zn2 and more vertices of odd degree, the step to a balanced pair at a vertex
of degree 4 is that of Proposition 18.3: it does not depend on the number of the vertices of
odd degree (Lemma 69.2). The other case is that of the graphs in which all degrees are 3.
Let N = 2n . Such a graph with no parallel edges and at most 2N/2+1 − 4 vertices has a cycle
with fewer than N edges, and a shortest cycle has no chord; the proof counts the vertices
near one edge (Lemma 70.1). Two parallel edges are a cycle with two edges. For the group
Zn2 an induced cycle with fewer than N edges can be contracted (Lemmas 69.1 and A.2), and
the graph after the contraction has fewer vertices of odd degree. So the proof is an induction
on the number of the vertices of odd degree, and it starts with part (c) of Theorem 1.2.
   For Theorem 1.8, on the integer flows at the modulus 7 and at six more moduli, the outline
of the proof is in the two paragraphs after that theorem.
The parts and their notation. After this introduction the paper has nine parts and an
appendix. Each of the first seven parts proves statements of one or two rows of Table 1, the
eighth part gives the graph of Theorem 1.6 and proves Theorem 1.7, and the last part proves
Theorem 1.8. The parts were written one after the other, and each group of sections gives
its own words; so some things have two names. Table 2 says which sections prove which
statement and how they write the graphs of flows: Mk (G) is F(G, Zk ), Ik (G) is F(G, k), and
FA (G) is F(G, A). A reference flow is called an anchor, and a step with one of the values
1, −1, 2, −2 is called restricted. The paragraph on the sections, after the table, gives more
details.

by themselves: they give their own words, they repeat the arguments that they take from

the one with Theorem 16.1 on prove part (c) of Theorem 1.2. They also can be read by
themselves: they give their own words and repeat the arguments that they take from the
sections before them. In them F(G, A) is written FA (G). The seven sections from the one
with Theorem 22.1 on prove parts (a) and (b) of Theorem 1.3 for k = 5, and the seven
sections from the one with Theorem 29.1 on prove them for k ≥ 6. The nine sections from
