24                                           DAVE GOEL


Part 1. Even graphs: the Cranston–Li–Su–Wang–Xu (2026) conjecture
  This part proves Theorem 1.1 and results (i) to (v) of the introduction. Its first six sections
are on group flows, Section 8 is on integer flows, and Section 9 is on modular (p, q)-flows.

                                    2. Words and results
Graphs. All graphs are finite multigraphs; loops and parallel edges are allowed. A graph is
even if every vertex has even degree, where a loop counts twice. (An even graph need not be
connected. An even graph is what [2] call an Eulerian graph: every vertex has even degree.)
A cycle is a connected subgraph in which every vertex has degree 2; a loop is a cycle with
one edge, and two parallel edges form a cycle with two edges. A directed cycle is a cycle with
one of its two directions of traversal.
Flows. Let A be a finite abelian group with at least two elements, and let G be a graph.
Fix a reference direction of every edge. An A-flow of G is a map f : E(G) → A such that at
every vertex the sum of f over the edges that leave the vertex is equal to the sum over the
edges that enter it. The value of f on an edge e is f (e) in the reference direction of e and
−f (e) in the other direction. In this text a flow is a nowhere-zero A-flow: f (e) ̸= 0 for every
edge e.
   For a directed cycle Q let χQ (e) be 1 if Q passes e in its reference direction, −1 if Q passes
e in the other direction, and 0 if e is not on Q. A step is a pair of flows f and f ′ = f + tχQ
with a directed cycle Q and an element t ̸= 0 of A; t is the value of the step. The graph
F(G, A) has the flows of G as its vertices, and two flows are adjacent if they differ by a step.
This is the reconfiguration graph of [3, 2]: two flows that agree outside a cycle differ by a
flow on that cycle, which is constant along the cycle. G is A-flow-connected if F(G, A) is
connected (a graph with at most one vertex is connected). For the group with one element
we use the same words: then a graph with an edge has no flow, a graph with no edge has
one flow, and every graph is flow-connected.
   Let T be a set of non-zero elements of A with T = −T . A T -step is a step whose value is
in T , and two flows are T -joined if a chain of T -steps leads from one to the other (the chain
may have no step: every flow is T -joined to itself).
   A multi-step with the value t ̸= 0 is a pair of flows f and f ′ = f + t(χQ1 + · · · + χQr ),
where r ≥ 1 and Q1 , . . . , Qr are directed cycles no two of which have a common edge. Every
partial sum f + t(χQ1 + · · · + χQj ) is then a flow, because on every edge its value is the value
of f or the value of f ′ . So a multi-step with the value t is a chain of r steps with the value t.
Ends. Every edge has two ends; both ends of a loop at v are ends at v. The degree of v is
the number of ends at v. For a flow f and an end i at v on the edge e, the outward value
yi (f ) ∈ A \ {0} is the value of f on e in the direction that leaves v through i. The flow
condition at v says
                                        X
(1)                                            yi (f ) = 0.
                                       i an end at v
If a directed cycle Q enters v through the end i and leaves v through the end j, then
(2)                 yi (f + tχQ ) = yi (f ) − t,       yj (f + tχQ ) = yj (f ) + t,
and the other ends at v keep their outward values.
Definition 2.1. Let G have no loop. A balanced pair of a flow f at a vertex v is a pair of
two different ends i, j at v with yi (f ) + yj (f ) = 0.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          25


Results.
Theorem 2.2. Let A be a finite abelian group. Every even graph in which all degrees are at
most 4 is A-flow-connected.
Theorem 2.3. Let A be a finite abelian group with |A| ≥ 4. Every even graph is A-flow-
connected.
Theorem 2.4. Let m ≥ 4. In every even graph, any two nowhere-zero Zm -flows are joined
by a chain of steps whose values are in {1, −1, 2, −2}.
Theorem 2.5. Let G be an even graph with M edges. In the situation of Theorem 2.2, any
two flows of G are joined by at most M 3 + 3M 2 + M steps. In the situation of Theorem 2.3,
any two flows of G are joined by at most 16M 3 + 12M 2 + M steps.
   Theorem 2.3 is the statement on groups in the Cranston–Li–Su–Wang–Xu (2026) conjecture
(the abelian groups of [2] are finite, as here). For groups of even order it is known: it is
[2, Theorem 4.3] and the sentences before it (their Theorem 4.2 is the statement on integer
2k-flows). For groups of odd order it was known for |A| ≥ 5,251,339 [2, Corollary 4.5]. For
A = Z3 , Theorem 2.2 agrees with [2, Theorem 2.1]: an even graph with all degrees at most 4
has no two vertices joined by more than four edge-disjoint paths. Theorem 2.4 allows only
steps with the values 1, −1, 2, −2; it is stated in this form for its use with integer flows in
Section 8.

                  3. The reduction: splitting off a balanced pair
   In this section A is a finite abelian group with at least two elements and T = −T is a set
of non-zero elements of A. We use two conditions on T :
  (T1) any two non-zero elements of A are the ends of a chain of non-zero elements of A in
        which the difference of any two neighbours is in T ;
  (T2) there is a non-zero a ∈ A with 2a = 0 or 2a ∈ T .
Both hold for T = A \ {0}. Both hold for A = Zm with m ≥ 3 and T = {1, −1, 2, −2}: the
chain 1, 2, . . . , m − 1 gives (T1), and a = 1 gives (T2).
Lemma 3.1 (loops). Let T satisfy (T1), and let l be a loop of G. If any two flows of G − l
are T -joined, then any two flows of G are T -joined.
Proof. A loop adds the same term to both sides of the flow condition at its vertex. So a flow
of G is a flow of G − l together with any non-zero value on l. A step of G − l is a step of G
that does not change the value on l, and a change of the value on l by an element of T is a
T -step along the cycle l. Given two flows of G: join their restrictions to G − l by T -steps of
G − l, and then change the value on l along a chain of (T1).                                  □
Lemma 3.2 (splitting off). Let G have no loop, let v be a vertex, and let e = vu and e′ = vw
be two different edges at v (u = w is allowed). Let G′ be the graph G − e − e′ + g with a
new edge g = uw (a loop if u = w): the first end of g is at u, in the place of the end of e at
u, and its second end is at w, in the place of the end of e′ at w. The direction of g from u
to w is the direction from its first end to its second end; if u = w, this fixes one of the two
directions of the loop g. For a flow f ′ of G′ let Lf ′ be the map on E(G) that agrees with f ′
on the common edges and has on e in the direction from u to v, and on e′ in the direction
from v to w, the value x of f ′ on g in the direction from u to w. Let B(v; e, e′ ) be the set of
the flows f of G in which the ends of e and of e′ at v are a balanced pair.
   26                                          DAVE GOEL


        (a) L is a bijection from the flows of G′ onto B(v; e, e′ ).
        (b) If f2′ comes from f1′ by a multi-step of G′ with the value t, then Lf2′ comes from Lf1′
            by a multi-step of G with the value t. In particular, if two flows of G′ are T -joined,
            their images under L are T -joined.
   Proof. (a) In Lf ′ the ends of e and e′ at v have the outward values −x and x, so the flow
   condition holds at v; at u the edge e leaves with the value x as g did, and at w the edge e′
   enters with the value x as g did. So Lf ′ is a flow in B(v; e, e′ ). Conversely, for f ∈ B(v; e, e′ )
   the value of f on e from u to v is equal to its value on e′ from v to w; giving g this value
   from u to w, and keeping the other values, defines a flow f ′ of G′ with Lf ′ = f .
      (b) Let Q′ be a directed cycle of G′ . If g is not on Q′ , then Q′ is a directed cycle of G. If g
   is on Q′ , replace g by the two edges e, e′ , passed through v in the direction of Q′ (first e from
   u to v and then e′ from v to w if Q′ passes g from u to w, and the reverse otherwise); this
   gives a closed walk W of G that uses every edge at most once. If u = w, then Q′ = g and
   W is the cycle formed by e and e′ . If u ̸= w and Q′ does not pass through v, then W is a
   directed cycle. If u ̸= w and Q′ passes through v, then W visits v twice and no other vertex
   twice; cut at v, it is the union of two directed cycles with no common edge. In every case let
   χW be the sum of the χ of these one or two directed cycles; then L(f ′ + tχQ′ ) = Lf ′ + tχW .
   If Q′1 , . . . , Q′r are directed cycles of G′ no two of which have a common edge, the directed
   cycles of G obtained from them have, pairwise, no common edge: they use the edges of the
   Q′j , with e, e′ in the place of g. So a multi-step of G′ is mapped to a multi-step of G with
   the same value.                                                                                   □
   Lemma 3.3 (constant flows). Let G be even and let a ̸= 0 be an element of A. For an
   Eulerian orientation D of G (in-degree equal to out-degree at every vertex; it exists because
   G is even) let a1D be the flow with the value a along every arc of D.
      (a) For two Eulerian orientations D, D2 of G, either a1D2 = a1D , or a1D2 comes from
          a1D by one multi-step with the value −2a.
      (b) In the situation of Lemma 3.2, B(v; e, e′ ) contains a flow a1D .
   Proof. (a) The arcs of D that are reversed in D2 form a directed graph with in-degree equal
   to out-degree at every vertex, so they are the union of directed cycles Q1 , . . . , Qr of D no
   two of which have a common arc. The reversal of an arc changes its value along D from a to
   −a. So a1D2 = a1D − 2a(χQ1 + · · · + χQr ). If 2a = 0 the two flows are equal; otherwise this
   is a multi-step with the value −2a.
      (b) G′ is even. Take an Eulerian orientation D′ of G′ , and let D agree with D′ on the
   common edges and direct e and e′ through v in the direction of g in D′ (from u to v to w if
   D′ directs g from u to w, and the reverse otherwise). Then D is an Eulerian orientation of
   G and a1D = L(a1D′ ).                                                                         □
  Proposition 3.4 (the reduction). Let G be a class of even graphs such that G − l ∈ G for
  every G ∈ G and every loop l of G, and G′ ∈ G for every G ∈ G with no loop and every graph
  G′ obtained from G as in Lemma 3.2. Let T satisfy (T1) and (T2). Suppose that
(LOCAL) for every G ∈ G with at least one edge and no loop in which every vertex has degree 0
         or at least 4, every flow of G is T -joined to a flow that has a balanced pair at some
         vertex.
  Then in every G ∈ G any two flows are T -joined.
   Proof. Induction on the number of edges of G. A graph with no edge has exactly one flow. If
   G has a loop l, use Lemma 3.1 and the induction hypothesis for G − l. Let G have no loop.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                           27


  If some vertex v has degree 2, its two ends belong to two different edges e, e′ , and by (1)
they are a balanced pair in every flow. So every flow of G is in B(v; e, e′ ), and by Lemma 3.2
and the induction hypothesis for G′ any two flows of G are T -joined.
  Otherwise G has an edge and every vertex has degree 0 or at least 4. Fix a as in (T2).
For a vertex v and two different edges e, e′ at v: by the induction hypothesis for G′ and
Lemma 3.2, any two flows in B(v; e, e′ ) are T -joined; by Lemma 3.3 (b) one of them is a flow
a1D ; and by Lemma 3.3 (a) and (T2) all flows a1D are equal or T -joined (if 2a ̸= 0, the
value −2a is in T , because 2a ∈ T and T = −T ). A flow with a balanced pair is in some
B(v; e, e′ ), because G has no loop. So all flows with a balanced pair are T -joined to one
another, and (LOCAL) joins every flow to one of them.                                        □

            4. The local step for every group, on 4-regular graphs
   Let A be a finite abelian group with at least two elements. Write A as a direct sum of
cyclic groups,
                                  A = ⟨s1 ⟩ ⊕ · · · ⊕ ⟨sr ⟩,  sk ̸= 0,
so that every element of A is in exactly one way a sum of elements of the groups ⟨sk ⟩ (its
parts). Let S = {s1 , −s1 , . . . , sr , −sr }. An element of S is small, and its colour is the one
index k for which it lies in ⟨sk ⟩. We have S = −S and 0 ∈     / S, and S generates A. For a flow
f an edge is small if its value is small (this does not depend on the direction); the colour
of a small edge is the colour of its value, which is the same in both directions. ρ(f ) is the
number of the edges that are not small.
Lemma 4.1. Let f be a flow of a graph G and Q a cycle of G none of whose edges is small.
Then there is a step f ′ = f + tχQ with ρ(f ′ ) ≤ ρ(f ) − 1.
Proof. Fix a direction of Q, and let X be the set of the values of the edges of Q in this
direction. Then 0 ∈   / X and X ∩ S = ∅. We claim that there are x ∈ X and s ∈ S with
x−s ∈  / X. If not, then X − S ⊆ X; as S = −S and A is finite, X + ⟨S⟩ ⊆ X, and as S
generates A and X is not empty, X = A, which contains 0. Let t = s − x. Then t ̸= 0
because x ∈/ S. A new value x′ + t with x′ ∈ X is 0 only if x′ = x − s, which is not in X. So
f ′ = f + tχQ is a flow. An edge of Q with the value x has the new value s and is small in f ′ ;
the edges outside Q did not change; and no edge of Q was small in f .                      □
Corollary 4.2. Every flow f of a graph G is joined by at most ρ(f ) steps to a flow whose
edges that are not small contain no cycle.
Lemma 4.3. Let G be a graph with no loop in which every vertex has degree 0 or 4. Let f
be a flow of G with no balanced pair, and Q a cycle of G all of whose edges are small. Then
some step f + tχQ is a flow with a balanced pair.
Proof. Let v be a vertex of Q, let 1, 2 be the ends of Q at v and 3, 4 the other two ends at
v, and write yi = yi (f ). Direct Q so that it enters v through the end 1 and leaves through
the end 2. For k ∈ {3, 4} let tk = y1 + yk ; it is not 0 because f has no balanced pair. Let
{k, k ′ } = {3, 4}. By (2) and (1), in f + tk χQ the end 1 has the outward value y1 − tk = −yk
and the end 2 has the outward value y2 + tk = −yk′ . Both are not 0, and the ends 1 and k
are a balanced pair. The edges of Q that are not at v have small values in the direction of Q.
Hence:
    (∗) if −tk is not the value, in the direction of Q, of an edge of Q that is not at v, then
          f + tk χQ is a flow with a balanced pair.
28                                         DAVE GOEL


In particular this holds if tk is not small, and it holds if Q has only two edges (both are at
v). Note that t3 + t4 = 2y1 + y3 + y4 = y1 − y2 .
   Case 1: two neighbouring edges of Q have different colours. Let v be their common vertex.
Then y1 = a is small of a colour i and y2 = b is small of a colour j ̸= i. Suppose that t3 and
t4 are both small. Their sum a − b has exactly two non-zero parts, a in ⟨si ⟩ and −b in ⟨sj ⟩.
Two small elements of the same colour have a sum with at most one non-zero part; so t3 and
t4 have different colours, they are the parts of their sum, and {t3 , t4 } = {a, −b}. But t3 = a
means y3 = 0 and t4 = a means y4 = 0. This is impossible. So t3 or t4 is not small, and (∗)
gives the step.
   Case 2: all edges of Q have one colour i. Let v be any vertex of Q. Then y1 , y2 ∈ {si , −si }
and y1 + y2 ̸= 0, so y1 = y2 = a and 2a ̸= 0. (If 2si = 0 this case does not occur: the ends
1, 2 would be a balanced pair.) The values of the edges of Q in the direction of Q are a or −a.
Now −t3 = a means y3 = −2a and then y4 = −2a − y3 = 0; and −t3 = −a means y3 = 0.
Both are impossible, and (∗) gives the step t3 .                                              □
Proposition 4.4. Let G be a graph with at least one edge and no loop in which every vertex
has degree 0 or 4. Every flow f of G is joined by at most ρ(f ) + 1 steps to a flow with a
balanced pair.
Proof. By Corollary 4.2, f is joined by at most ρ(f ) steps to a flow f ∗ whose edges that are
not small contain no cycle. Let n ≥ 1 be the number of the vertices of degree 4. G has 2n
edges, all on these n vertices; at most n − 1 of them are not small in f ∗ (a forest on n vertices
has at most n − 1 edges), so at least n + 1 are small, and the small edges contain a cycle Q. If
f ∗ has no balanced pair, Lemma 4.3 gives one more step to a flow with a balanced pair. □

                           5. Proofs of Theorems 2.2 and 2.3
Proof of Theorem 2.2. If |A| = 1, a graph with an edge has no flow and a graph with no
edge has one flow. Let |A| ≥ 2, let G be the class of the even graphs with all degrees at
most 4, and T = A \ {0}. The deletion of a loop lowers one degree by 2. The splitting off of
Lemma 3.2 lowers the degree of v by 2 and keeps the other degrees (a new loop at u = w
counts twice). So G is closed under both operations. A graph of G with no loop and no vertex
of degree 2 has only vertices of degree 0 or 4, so (LOCAL) is Proposition 4.4. Proposition 3.4
gives the theorem.                                                                          □
Lemma 5.1 (contracting a triangle). Let |A| ≥ 4. Let G+ be a graph and ∆ a triangle of
G+ with the vertices x1 , x2 , x3 , such that the three edges of ∆ are the only edges of G+ with
both ends in {x1 , x2 , x3 }. Let G = G+ /∆ be the graph obtained by deleting the three edges of
∆ and identifying x1 , x2 , x3 to one vertex x. For a flow f of G+ let pf be its restriction to
E(G) = E(G+ ) \ E(∆).
    (a) pf is a flow of G, and every flow of G is pf for some flow f of G+ .
    (b) If f2 comes from f1 by a multi-step with the value t, then pf2 = pf1 or pf2 comes
        from pf1 by a multi-step with the value t.
        +
So if G is A-flow-connected, then G is A-flow-connected.
Proof. (a) The flow condition of pf at x is the sum of the flow conditions of f at x1 , x2 , x3 ,
in which the edges of ∆ cancel. Let h be a flow of G. For i = 1, 2, 3 let βi be the sum of the
outward values of h over the ends at x that are ends at xi in G+ ; then β1 + β2 + β3 = 0.
Give the edges x1 x2 , x2 x3 , x3 x1 of ∆, in these directions, the values z, z − β2 , z + β1 .
The flow conditions at x1 , x2 , x3 are β1 + z − (z + β1 ) = 0, β2 + (z − β2 ) − z = 0 and
                  THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                               29


β3 +(z+β1 )−(z−β2 ) = 0; they hold for every z. The three values are not 0 if z ∈
                                                                                / {0, β2 , −β1 },
and |A| ≥ 4 leaves such a z.
  (b) Let f2 = f1 + t(χQ1 + · · · + χQr ) with directed cycles of G+ no two of which have a
common edge. Let H be the directed graph on V (G) whose arcs are the edges of Q1 , . . . , Qr
outside ∆, each with the direction of its cycle. At a vertex other than x the in-degree of H
equals its out-degree, as in the cycles. At x the same holds: over the three vertices x1 , x2 , x3
together, the arcs of the cycles have equal total in-degree and out-degree, and every arc of
the cycles inside ∆ counts once in each. So the arcs of H are the union of directed cycles of
G no two of which have a common arc, and pf2 − pf1 is t times the sum of their χ. If H has
no arc, pf2 = pf1 ; otherwise this is a multi-step of G with the value t.
   The last statement follows: a multi-step is a chain of steps, and p is onto.                 □
Proof of Theorem 2.3. By Lemma 3.1P    (with T = A \ {0}) we may delete the loops one by
one; let G have no loop. Let Φ(G) = v max(deg v − 4, 0). We use induction on Φ(G). If
Φ(G) = 0, this is Theorem 2.2. Otherwise let v be a vertex of degree d ≥ 6. Let G+ be the
graph in which v is replaced by a triangle ∆ with the vertices x1 , x2 , x3 , where two of the
ends at v become ends at x1 , two become ends at x2 , and the other d − 4 become ends at x3 .
The new degrees are 4, 4 and d − 2, so G+ is even and Φ(G+ ) = Φ(G) − 2. G+ has no loop,
and the edges of ∆ are the only edges with both ends in {x1 , x2 , x3 }, because G has no loop
at v. Also G = G+ /∆. By the induction hypothesis G+ is A-flow-connected, and Lemma 5.1
gives the same for G.                                                                        □

             6. Cyclic groups: all degrees at once, with small steps
   Let A = Zm with m ≥ 4, S = {1, −1} and T = {1, −1, 2, −2}. As in Section 4 an edge is
small for a flow f if its value is 1 or −1, and ρ(f ) is the number of the other edges. The
value of such an edge is, in each direction, the residue of an integer in {2, . . . , m − 2}.
Lemma 6.1. Let f be a flow of a graph G and Q a cycle none of whose edges is small. Then
f is T -joined, by steps along Q with the value −1, to a flow f ′ with ρ(f ′ ) ≤ ρ(f ) − 1.
Proof. Fix a direction of Q. For an edge e of Q let xe ∈ {2, . . . , m − 2} be the integer whose
residue is the value of e in this direction, and let x be the least of the xe . For j = 1, . . . , x − 1
the map f − jχQ has on e the value xe − j, an integer with 1 ≤ xe − j ≤ m − 3; so it is a
flow. In f ′ = f − (x − 1)χQ an edge with xe = x has the value 1.                                     □
Lemma 6.2. Let G have no loop, let f be a flow with no balanced pair, Q a cycle all of
whose edges are small, and v a vertex of Q.
   (a) The two ends of Q at v have the same outward value a ∈ {1, −1}.
   (b) If another end at v has an outward value c ̸= −2a, then f is T -joined, by steps along
       Q, to a flow with a balanced pair at v.
Proof. (a) Both values are in {1, −1} and their sum is not 0.
   (b) Direct Q so that it enters v through the end 1 and leaves through the end 2, and let
t = a + c. Then t ̸= 0 (f has no balanced pair), t ̸= a (c ̸= 0), and t ̸= −a (c ̸= −2a). So t is
the residue of an integer τ with 2 ≤ τ ≤ m − 2. The values of the edges of Q in the direction
of Q are 1 or −1. For j = 2, 3, . . . , τ the map f + jχQ has on the edges of Q the values 1 + j
or −1 + j, integers between 1 and m − 1; so it is a flow. The chain f , f + 2χQ , f + 3χQ , . . . ,
f + τ χQ consists of one step with the value 2 and τ − 2 steps with the value 1. In f + tχQ
the end 1 has the outward value a − t = −c by (2), and the end with the outward value c
did not change.                                                                                □
30                                         DAVE GOEL


Proposition 6.3. Let m ≥ 4, and let G be an even graph with at least one edge and no loop
in which every vertex has degree 0 or at least 4. Every Zm -flow of G is T -joined to a flow
with a balanced pair.
Proof. Let n ≥ 1 be the number of the vertices of degree at least 4; all edges are on these
vertices, and |E(G)| ≥ 2n. By Lemma 6.1, used as long as the edges that are not small
contain a cycle, a flow is T -joined to a flow f ∗ in which they contain no cycle; so at most
n − 1 edges are not small (they form a forest on the n vertices). Let f ∗ have no balanced
pair, and suppose that Lemma 6.2 (b) can be used for no cycle Q of small edges and no
vertex v of Q. Then at every vertex v of such a cycle all ends outside the cycle have the
outward value −2a with a ∈ {1, −1}. As m ≥ 4, −2a is not 1 or −1: −2a = a would mean
3 ≡ 0 and −2a = −a would mean 1 ≡ 0 modulo m. So these ends are not small, and every
vertex of a cycle of small edges has exactly two small ends. Hence every cycle of small
edges is a whole component of the graph formed by the small edges on the n vertices; every
component of this graph is a tree or a cycle; and there are at most n small edges. Then
|E(G)| ≤ n + (n − 1) < 2n, a contradiction.                                                □
Proof of Theorem 2.4. Proposition 3.4 for the class G of all even graphs and T =
{1, −1, 2, −2}; (T1) and (T2) hold as noted in Section 3, and (LOCAL) is Proposi-
tion 6.3.                                                                      □
  Theorem 2.4 proves Theorem 2.3 for cyclic groups again, without Lemma 5.1. We have no
count of this kind for all groups at vertices of degree 6 or more. The count in the proof of
Proposition 6.3 needs that the value −2a of Lemma 6.2 (b) is not small. This holds in Zm
with m ≥ 4. It fails, for example, in Z3 × Z3 with S as in Section 4, where −2a = a; there a
vertex can have six ends with the same small outward value a. This is why Section 5 goes
through the graphs of degree at most 4.

                                  7. The number of steps
Proof of Theorem 2.5. If A has only one element, a graph has at most one flow and nothing
is to prove. Let |A| ≥ 2 and fix a ̸= 0 in A. For an even graph G with all degrees at most 4
let c(G) be the least number N such that every flow of G is joined to some flow a1D by a
chain of at most N multi-steps. We show c(G) ≤ M (M + 3)/2 for a graph with M edges, by
induction on M along the proof of Proposition 3.4. For M = 0 the only flow is a flow a1D . If
G has a loop l: at most c(G−l) multi-steps of G−l, and at most one step along l to give it the
value a. If G has no loop and a vertex of degree 2: c(G) ≤ c(G′ ) by Lemma 3.2 (b), because
L(a1D′ ) is a flow a1D (proof of Lemma 3.3 (b)). Otherwise a flow f is joined by at most
ρ(f ) + 1 ≤ M + 1 steps to a flow in some B(v; e, e′ ) (Proposition 4.4), and this flow by at most
c(G′ ) multi-steps to a flow a1D , again by Lemma 3.2 (b). In all cases c(G) ≤ c(G− ) + M + 1
for a graph G− of the class with M − 1 edges, so c(G) ≤ M
                                                              P
                                                                 j=1 (j + 1) = M (M + 3)/2.
   By Lemma 3.3 (a) two flows a1D are at most one multi-step apart. So any two flows of G
are joined by at most M 2 + 3M + 1 multi-steps. The cycles of a multi-step have no common
edge, so a multi-step is a chain of at most M steps. This gives M 3 + 3M 2 + M steps.
   Now let |A| ≥ 4 and let G be any even graph with M edges. Let G0 be G without its λ
loops. The proof of Theorem 2.3 replaces G0 , by at most Φ(G0 )/2 triangles, by an even graph
G∗ with all degrees at most 4; each triangle adds three edges, and Φ(G0 ) ≤ 2(M −λ) (the sum
of the degrees of G0 ), so G∗ has at most 4(M − λ) edges. By the first part and Lemma 5.1,
used for the triangles in the reverse order of their making, any two flows of G0 are joined by
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                       31


at most 16(M − λ)2 + 12(M − λ) + 1 multi-steps of G0 , each a chain of at most M − λ steps.
The loops cost at most λ more steps. The total is at most 16M 3 + 12M 2 + M .           □
Remark 7.1. The proofs above are constructive: each of them describes how to find a chain
of steps between two given flows. For Theorems 2.2 and 2.3, Theorem 2.5 bounds the number
of the steps by a polynomial in the number of the edges that does not depend on A. We give
no bound for the number of the steps in Theorem 2.4, and no bound for a running time.
Remark 7.2. Theorem 2.3 for A = Z4 says that the complete graph K5 is Z4 -flow-connected;
this case is also in [2] (their Theorem 4.2 and the sentence after its proof). Before their
Corollary 5.15 the authors of [2] write that their Theorem 3.3 shows that “no graph containing
a triangle can be Z4 -flow-connected”. Their Theorem 3.3 is a statement on 3-edge-colourable
cubic graphs that contain a triangle; the quoted sentence does not hold for all graphs, as K5
shows. So the “only if” half of their Corollary 5.15 (iii) for A = Z4 , read as a statement on
each single graph with a spanning triangularly connected subgraph, does not hold for K5 .
We do not use these statements.

                                     8. Integer flows
Words. An integer flow of a graph G is a map F : E(G) → Z that satisfies the flow condition
at every vertex. For a map x from E(G) to Z or to the real numbers, the boundary (∂x)(v)
at a vertex v is the sum of x over the edges that enter v minus the sum over the edges that
leave v (a loop at v is in both sums). So x is a flow if and only if ∂x = 0, and ∂χQ = 0 for
every directed cycle Q.
   Let k ≥ 2. A k-flow of G is an integer flow F with 1 ≤ |F (e)| ≤ k − 1 for every edge e.
The graph F(G, k) has the k-flows of G as its vertices, and two k-flows are adjacent if they
differ by λχQ with a directed cycle Q and an integer λ ̸= 0. G is k-flow-connected if F(G, k)
is connected. This is the graph F(G, k) of [3, 2]. In this section a Zk -flow is a nowhere-zero
Zk -flow. An integer lift of a Zk -flow f is a k-flow F with F (e) ≡ f (e) modulo k for every
edge e. The residues modulo k of a k-flow are a Zk -flow, because no value of a k-flow is a
multiple of k.
Theorem 8.1. Let k ≥ 4. Every even graph is k-flow-connected.
  This is the statement on integer flows in the Cranston–Li–Su–Wang–Xu (2026) conjecture;
an even graph is what [2] call an Eulerian graph (every degree is even; the graph need not
be connected). For even k it is [2, Theorem 4.2]; for odd k ≥ 5 [2] leaves it open. The proof
takes a chain of Theorem 2.4 and lifts it step by step. Lemma 8.3 and Lemma 8.4 below are
known: the first is Tutte’s lifting theorem in the form of [3, Theorem 2.3], the second is [3,
Lemma 5.5] (the numbers are those of the fourth version of [3]). We give the short proofs,
because the proof of Lemma 8.6 uses the same rounding.
Lemma 8.2 (rounding). Let G be a graph and x a map from E(G) to the real interval [0, 1]
such that (∂x)(v) is an integer for every vertex v. Then there is a map z : E(G) → {0, 1}
with ∂z = ∂x and with z(e) = x(e) for every edge e with x(e) ∈ {0, 1}.
Proof. Call an edge e fractional if 0 < x(e) < 1. If no edge is fractional, take z = x.
Otherwise the fractional edges contain a cycle Q: a fractional loop is such a cycle; and if no
loop is fractional, no vertex v meets exactly one fractional edge, because the other edges at
v have integer values and (∂x)(v) is an integer; and a graph with no loop, at least one edge
and no vertex of degree 1 contains a cycle. Give Q a direction, and let δ > 0 be the least
32                                         DAVE GOEL


number such that x + δχQ has the value 0 or 1 on some edge of Q. The map x + δχQ has its
values in [0, 1], the boundary of x, the values of x on the edges that are not fractional, and
fewer fractional edges. Repeat.                                                              □
  For a Zk -flow f and an edge e let rf (e) ∈ {1, . . . , k − 1} be the integer whose residue is
the value of f on e in the reference direction. Then ∂rf ≡ 0 modulo k at every vertex, and
an integer lift of f has on every edge e the value rf (e) or rf (e) − k.
Lemma 8.3 (integer lifts). Let k ≥ 2. Every Zk -flow f of a graph G has an integer lift.
Proof. The map x = rf /k has its values in (0, 1) and an integer boundary. Let z be as in
Lemma 8.2, and F = rf − kz. Then ∂F = ∂rf − k ∂x = 0, F ≡ f modulo k, and F (e) is
rf (e) ∈ [1, k − 1] or rf (e) − k ∈ [−(k − 1), −1].                                    □
Lemma 8.4 (the fibres are connected). Let k ≥ 2 and let f be a Zk -flow of a graph G. Any
two integer lifts of f are joined by a path of F(G, k).
Proof. Let F, F ′ be integer lifts of f . On every edge both have the value rf (e) or rf (e) − k,
so h = (F ′ − F )/k is an integer flow with values in {−1, 0, 1}. Direct every edge e with
h(e) ̸= 0 so that h is 1 in this direction. By the flow condition these arcs form a directed
graph with in-degree equal to out-degree at every vertex, so they are the union of directed
cycles Q1 , . . . , Qj no two of which have a common arc, and h = χQ1 + · · · + χQj . Let
Fi = F + k(χQ1 + · · · + χQi ). On every edge Fi has the value of F or the value of F ′ , so
Fi is an integer lift of f ; and Fi − Fi−1 = kχQi . So F = F0 , F1 , . . . , Fj = F ′ is a path of
F(G, k).                                                                                        □
Lemma 8.5 (a step with the value 1). Let k ≥ 2, let Q be a directed cycle of a graph G,
and let f and f + χQ be Zk -flows of G. Then for every integer lift F of f , the map F + χQ
is an integer lift of f + χQ .
Proof. Choose the reference directions so that Q passes all its edges in the reference direction.
(The statement does not depend on this choice: a change of the reference direction of an
edge changes the signs of F (e), of f (e) and of χQ (e).) F + χQ is an integer flow with the
residues of f + χQ . Let e be an edge of Q. Then rf (e) ̸= k − 1, because f + χQ is not 0 on e.
The value F (e) is rf (e) or rf (e) − k, so F (e) + 1 is in [2, k − 1] or in [2 − k, −1].      □
Lemma 8.6 (a step with the value 2). Let k ≥ 3, let Q be a directed cycle of a graph G, and
let f and f + 2χQ be Zk -flows of G. Then there is an integer lift F of f such that F + 2χQ
is an integer lift of f + 2χQ .
     The map f + χQ can have zeros, so Lemma 8.5 cannot be used twice.
Proof. As in the last proof, choose the reference directions so that Q passes all its edges in
the reference direction, and write r = rf . No edge of Q has r(e) = k − 2, because f + 2χQ is
not 0 there. Let E1 be the set of the edges e of Q with r(e) = k − 1, and
                                        x = (r + χQ )/k.
All values of x are in (0, 1], and x(e) = 1 exactly for e ∈ E1 . The boundary ∂x = (∂r)/k is
an integer at every vertex. By Lemma 8.2 there is z : E(G) → {0, 1} with ∂z = ∂x and with
z(e) = 1 for every e ∈ E1 . Let F = r − kz. As in the proof of Lemma 8.3, F is an integer
lift of f .
    F + 2χQ is an integer flow with the residues of f + 2χQ , and outside Q it is equal to F .
For e ∈ E1 : F (e) = −1, and the new value is 1. For an edge e of Q outside E1 : r(e) ≤ k − 3;
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          33


if F (e) = r(e), then 3 ≤ F (e) + 2 ≤ k − 1; if F (e) = r(e) − k, then 3 − k ≤ F (e) + 2 ≤ −1.
So F + 2χQ is a k-flow.                                                                     □
Proposition 8.7 (the transfer). Let k ≥ 3 and let G be a graph. If any two Zk -flows of G
are joined by a chain of steps whose values are in {1, −1, 2, −2}, then G is k-flow-connected.
Proof. Let F and F ′ be k-flows of G, and let f and f ′ be the Zk -flows of their residues. If
f = f ′ , then F and F ′ are two integer lifts of f , and Lemma 8.4 joins them. Otherwise
let f = f0 , f1 , . . . , fs = f ′ with s ≥ 1 be a chain of Zk -flows with fi = fi−1 + ti χQi and
ti ∈ {1, −1, 2, −2}. If ti is not 1 or 2, replace Qi by the same cycle with the other direction
and ti by −ti ; so let ti ∈ {1, 2}. By Lemma 8.3 and Lemma 8.5 (if ti = 1), or by Lemma 8.6
                                                                       +
(if ti = 2), there are, for every i from 1 to s, an integer lift Fi−1      of fi−1 and an integer
       −      +
lift Fi = Fi−1 + ti χQi of fi ; they are adjacent in F(G, k). By Lemma 8.4, F is joined to
F0+ , Fi− is joined to Fi+ for 0 < i < s, and Fs− is joined to F ′ . So F and F ′ are joined in
F(G, k).                                                                                        □
Proof of Theorem 8.1. Theorem 2.4 with m = k, and Proposition 8.7.                             □
Remark 8.8. The lifting question of Esperet et al. [3, Problem 2.6], which is Conjecture A
of [2], asks for a proof that F(G, k) is connected whenever F(G, Zk ) is connected. For even
graphs the answer is yes for every k ≥ 2. For k ≥ 4 both graphs are connected (Theorems 2.3
and 8.1). For k = 3 every step of F(G, Z3 ) has the value 1 or −1; so, if F(G, Z3 ) is connected,
Proposition 8.7 applies (for every graph G). For k = 2 a graph has at most one Z2 -flow,
every 2-flow is an integer lift of it, and Lemma 8.4 joins any two integer lifts; so F(G, 2) is
connected for every graph G.

                    9. Modular (p, q)-flows: the bound 4 is exact
Words. Let p, q be integers with q ≥ 1 and p > 2q, and let I = I(p, q) be the set of the
residues modulo p of the integers q, q + 1, . . . , p − q. Then I = −I, 0 ∈   / I, and q ̸= −q in
Zp . A (p, q)-flow of a graph G is a Zp -flow of G all of whose values are in I (the value of an
edge in one direction is in I if and only if its value in the other direction is). These are the
modular (p, q)-flows of [2, Section 2] (which allows p ≥ 2q; we take p > 2q); for q = 1 they
are the nowhere-zero Zp -flows. In this section a step is a pair of (p, q)-flows f and f + tχQ
with a directed cycle Q and t ̸= 0 in Zp ; the graph F(G; p, q) has the (p, q)-flows of G as its
vertices and the steps as its edges, and G is (p, q)-flow-connected if this graph is connected.
This graph is the graph Fmod (G; p, q) of [2]: the difference of two flows is supported on a
cycle if and only if it is tχQ with a directed cycle Q and t ̸= 0. The integer form (the circular
(p, q)-flows of [2] and their graph) is not treated in this section. T -steps, T -joined flows and
multi-steps are defined as in Section 2, with (p, q)-flows in the place of flows; a multi-step
is again a chain of steps with the same value, because on every edge a partial sum has the
value of one of the two (p, q)-flows. Outward values and balanced pairs are as in Section 2,
and (1) and (2) hold.
Theorem 9.1. Let p ≥ 4q ≥ 4. In every even graph, any two (p, q)-flows are joined by
a chain of steps whose values are in {1, −1, 2q, −2q}. In particular every even graph is
(p, q)-flow-connected.
Theorem 9.2. Let 2q < p < 4q. The graph with two vertices and 2p parallel edges is even
and is not (p, q)-flow-connected.
34                                          DAVE GOEL


   So, for p > 2q: every even graph is (p, q)-flow-connected if and only if p ≥ 4q. For
q = 1, Theorem 9.1 is Theorem 2.4. For p = 2k + 1 and q = k the even graphs that are
(p, q)-flow-connected are known [2, Theorem 2.9]: that theorem is stated for the graphs that
admit a circular (2k + 1, k)-flow, and every even graph admits one (the value k on every arc
of an Eulerian orientation).
The reduction.
Lemma 9.3. Let p > 2q and T = {1, −1, 2q, −2q} ⊆ Zp . Suppose that for every even graph
G with at least one edge and no loop in which every vertex has degree 0 or at least 4, every
(p, q)-flow of G is T -joined to a (p, q)-flow that has a balanced pair at some vertex. Then in
every even graph any two (p, q)-flows are T -joined.
Proof. This is Proposition 3.4 for the class of all even graphs, with this rule of replacement:
(p, q)-flows take the place of flows; the values of the flows, and the element a of Lemma 3.3
and of (T2), are taken from I in the place of the set of the non-zero elements; the set T
and the values t of the steps stay non-zero elements of Zp . The set T has no zero, because
p ≥ 3 and 0 < 2q < p, and T = −T . Condition (T2) holds with a = q, which is in I, because
2q ∈ T . The class of all even graphs is closed under the deletion of a loop and under splitting
off, because both operations keep all degrees even (a loop counts twice). We go through
Section 3 and say what changes.
   Lemma 3.1. A (p, q)-flow of G is a (p, q)-flow of G − l together with any value of I on the
loop l. The chain q, q + 1, . . . , p − q joins any two elements of I inside I with differences 1 or
−1 of neighbours; this takes the place of (T1). The rest of the proof is the same.
   Lemma 3.2. The map L gives e and e′ the value of g and keeps the other values, and its
inverse does the opposite; so L is a bijection from the (p, q)-flows of G′ onto the (p, q)-flows
of G in which the ends of e and e′ at v are a balanced pair. Part (b) is an identity between
maps, L(f ′ + tχQ′ ) = Lf ′ + tχW , and it sends a multi-step of (p, q)-flows to a multi-step of
(p, q)-flows.
   Lemma 3.3 with a = q. The flow q1D is a (p, q)-flow. In (a), the two flows are (p, q)-flows
and 2q ̸= 0, so q1D2 is equal to q1D (if D2 = D) or comes from q1D by one multi-step with
the value −2q ∈ T . In (b), q1D = L(q1D′ ).
   Proposition 3.4. Its proof uses these three lemmas, (1), condition (T2) with a = q, the
fact that the class is closed under the two operations, the fact that a graph with no edge has
exactly one (p, q)-flow, and the hypothesis (LOCAL), which is the hypothesis of the present
lemma.                                                                                            □
The local step. For a (p, q)-flow f call an edge small if its value is q or −q, and let ρ(f )
be the number of the edges that are not small. The value of an edge that is not small is, in
each direction, the residue of an integer in {q + 1, . . . , p − q − 1}. Let T = {1, −1, 2q, −2q}.
Lemma 9.4. Let p > 2q, let f be a (p, q)-flow of a graph G and Q a cycle none of whose
edges is small. Then f is T -joined, by steps along Q with the value −1, to a (p, q)-flow f ′
with ρ(f ′ ) ≤ ρ(f ) − 1.
Proof. Fix a direction of Q. For an edge e of Q let xe ∈ {q + 1, . . . , p − q − 1} be the
integer whose residue is the value of e in this direction, and let x be the least of the xe . For
j = 1, . . . , x − q the map f − jχQ has on e the residue of xe − j, and q ≤ xe − j ≤ p − q − 2;
so it is a (p, q)-flow. In f ′ = f − (x − q)χQ an edge with xe = x has the value q; the edges
outside Q did not change, and no edge of Q was small in f .                                    □
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                         35


Lemma 9.5. Let p ≥ 4q, let G have no loop, let f be a (p, q)-flow with no balanced pair, Q
a cycle all of whose edges are small, and v a vertex of Q. Let J be the set of the residues
modulo p of the integers 2q, 2q + 1, . . . , p − 2q.
    (a) The two ends of Q at v have the same outward value a ∈ {q, −q}.
    (b) If another end at v has an outward value c with a + c ∈ J, then f is T -joined, by
        steps along Q, to a (p, q)-flow with a balanced pair at v.
    (c) If another end at v has an outward value c with a + c ∈/ J, then this end is not small.
Proof. (a) Both values are in {q, −q} and their sum is not 0.
   (b) Call the two ends of Q at v the end 1 and the end 2, direct Q so that it enters v
through the end 1 and leaves through the end 2, and let τ be the integer with 2q ≤ τ ≤ p − 2q
whose residue is a + c. In the direction of Q every edge of Q has the value q or −q. For an
integer j with 2q ≤ j ≤ p − 2q the map f + jχQ has on an edge of Q the residue of q + j or
of p − q + j; here 3q ≤ q + j ≤ p − q, and p + q ≤ p − q + j ≤ 2p − 3q, which has the residue
of an integer between q and p − 3q. So f + jχQ is a (p, q)-flow. (The hypothesis p ≥ 4q is
used here: it says that 2q ≤ p − 2q, so that J is not empty and the integers j exist.) The
chain f , f + 2qχQ , f + (2q + 1)χQ , . . . , f + τ χQ consists of one step with the value 2q and
τ − 2q steps with the value 1. In f + τ χQ the end 1 has the outward value a − (a + c) = −c
by (2), and the end with the outward value c did not change: a balanced pair.
   (c) First let a = q. Let γ ∈ {q, . . . , p − q} be the integer whose residue is c. Then
q + γ is an integer between 2q and p whose residue is a + c. If q + γ ≤ p − 2q, then
a + c ∈ J. So γ ≥ p − 3q + 1. Also γ ̸= p − q, because c ̸= −a (f has no balanced pair). So
p − 3q + 1 ≤ γ ≤ p − q − 1. As p ≥ 4q, we have q < p − 3q + 1; so γ is not q and not p − q,
and the end is not small. Now let a = −q. The map −f is a (p, q)-flow with no balanced
pair and with the same small edges, its outward values are the negatives of those of f , and
J = −J. So the case a = q, used for −f , gives the statement.                                   □
Proposition 9.6. Let p ≥ 4q, and let G be an even graph with at least one edge and no
loop in which every vertex has degree 0 or at least 4. Every (p, q)-flow of G is T -joined to a
(p, q)-flow with a balanced pair.
Proof. Let n ≥ 1 be the number of the vertices of degree at least 4; all edges are on these
vertices, and |E(G)| ≥ 2n. By Lemma 9.4, used as long as the edges that are not small
contain a cycle, a (p, q)-flow is T -joined to a (p, q)-flow f ∗ in which they contain no cycle;
so at most n − 1 edges are not small. Let f ∗ have no balanced pair, and suppose that
Lemma 9.5 (b) can be used for no cycle Q of small edges, no vertex v of Q and no end at v
outside Q. Then by Lemma 9.5 (c) every vertex of a cycle of small edges has exactly two
small ends (the two ends of the cycle). Hence every cycle of small edges is a whole component
of the graph formed by the small edges on the n vertices; every component of this graph is a
tree or a cycle; and there are at most n small edges. Then |E(G)| ≤ n + (n − 1) < 2n, a
contradiction.                                                                               □
Proof of Theorem 9.1. Lemma 9.3 and Proposition 9.6.                                          □
Proof of Theorem 9.2. Let u, w be the two vertices. Every vertex has degree 2p. Direct all
edges from u to w and let f0 have the value q on every edge; the flow condition holds because
2pq ≡ 0 modulo p, and f0 is a (p, q)-flow. A cycle of this graph consists of two of the parallel
edges; a directed cycle Q passes one of them from u to w, where f0 has the value q in the
direction of Q, and the other from w to u, where f0 has the value −q in the direction of Q.
Let t be an integer with 1 ≤ t ≤ p − 1. The residue of q + t is in I only if q + t ≤ p − q,
36                                           DAVE GOEL


because for p − q < q + t ≤ p + q − 1 the residue is one of p − q + 1, . . . , p − 1, 0, . . . , q − 1.
The residue of −q + t is in I only if −q + t ≥ q, because for 1 − q ≤ −q + t < q the residue is
one of p − q + 1, . . . , p − 1, 0, . . . , q − 1. So a step from f0 needs 2q ≤ t ≤ p − 2q, and there is
no such t because p < 4q. So f0 is adjacent to no other (p, q)-flow. But −f0 is a (p, q)-flow
and −f0 ̸= f0 , because q ̸= −q.                                                                      □
Remark 9.7. Below the ratio 4 the answer depends on the graph (for the pairs (2k + 1, k)
see [2, Theorem 2.9]): for every pair with 2q < p < 4q the graph of Theorem 9.2 is not
(p, q)-flow-connected, and a graph that is one cycle is (p, q)-flow-connected (any two different
(p, q)-flows of it differ by a non-zero constant along the cycle, which is one step). Fewer than
2p parallel edges can fail. By a count of all (p, q)-flows and all steps (a program; not a proof
in this text), the graph with two vertices and six parallel edges has three components for
(p, q) = (11, 3), where 2p = 22, and three components for (p, q) = (7, 2), where 2p = 14; the
graph with eight parallel edges has three components for (p, q) = (7, 2); the complete graph
K5 has one component for (p, q) = (7, 2).
