Part 2. Two vertices of odd degree
   This part proves Theorem 1.2. Its first six sections prove parts (a) and (b), and its last
six sections prove part (c). Each of these two groups of sections can be read by itself. In
the first group F(G, Zk ) and F(G, k) are written Mk (G) and Ik (G); in the second group
F(G, A) is written FA (G).

                            10. Statement and conventions
   All graphs are finite multigraphs, with loops and parallel edges allowed. A loop contributes
two to the degree. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph, including a loop or a pair of
parallel edges. Fix a reference direction for each edge. Divergence is the outgoing sum minus
the incoming sum; a loop contributes zero. A circulation has zero divergence. For a cycle C
with a direction of traversal, χC has value 1 on forward edges, −1 on backward edges, and 0
elsewhere. For a directed path P , define χP in the same way.
   An integer k-flow is an integer circulation F with 1 ≤ |F (e)| ≤ k − 1 on every edge. A
nowhere-zero Zk -flow is a circulation with nonzero values in Zk . Their reconfiguration graphs
Ik (G) and Mk (G) have these flows as vertices, with a step F 7→ F + tχC , or f 7→ f + tχC ,
whenever the coefficient is nonzero and both endpoints are flows of the indicated kind. This
agrees with adjacency by cycle support in [3, 2]: a circulation supported on a cycle is constant
along its traversal. We call a modular step restricted if its coefficient is in {1, −1, 2, −2}
modulo k. A conformal decomposition of an integer circulation is a sum of signed unit cycle
vectors whose nonzero signs agree with that circulation on every edge; in particular there is
no cancellation on an edge.
   A graph with no vertices, or with one vertex, is regarded as connected. In particular,
an empty reconfiguration graph counts as connected. If G has a bridge, every circulation
vanishes there, by conservation on one side of its cut. Thus both nowhere-zero flow spaces
are empty.
Theorem 10.1. Let G be a finite multigraph with at most two odd-degree vertices. For every
integer k ≥ 4:
    (1) any two nowhere-zero Zk -flows of G are joined by restricted steps;
    (2) the integer flow reconfiguration graph Ik (G) is connected.
The same conclusions hold if every connected component of G has at most two odd-degree
vertices. The uniform threshold 4 cannot be lowered to 3.
   An even graph with one added edge is a special case. The number of odd-degree vertices
is even, so at most two means zero or two. The theorem is about cyclic groups and integer
flows. For every finite abelian group with at least four elements see Theorem 16.1.
   Cranston et al. [2, Conjecture 4.1] proposed connectivity for even graphs at all integer
moduli at least four, and for all finite abelian groups of order at least four. Their Theorem
4.2 proves the cases of even integer modulus. Earlier work [4] gives ordinary connectivity
for every bridgeless loopless graph at moduli 40, 48, every multiple of four from 52 on, and
further families; [3, Theorem 7.1] gives a modulus depending on the graph. The present
assertion covers every k ≥ 4 for the stated class and restricts the modular steps.
   We reuse the balanced-pair splitting of Section 3 and the two local cyclic moves of Section 6,
repeating their arguments below. The proof here includes the even case for cyclic groups
and integers and does not use Theorem 2.3 for general abelian groups. For two odd vertices
we use a common family of reference flows and the equality case of the local edge count. The
38                                         DAVE GOEL


bounded lifting, connected-fibre, unit-step and double-step lifting lemmas are earlier results
for loopless graphs [4, Section 1]; Section 15 supplies their proofs here, including loops.
   By [2, Theorem 2.1], every graph with an odd-degree vertex that admits a 3-flow has
disconnected integer and cyclic reconfiguration graphs at modulus three. Thus the obstruction
to lowering the threshold is known; we give the smallest example and both its classes explicitly.
Likewise the old example K4 has disconnected Z4 -flow reconfiguration [3, Section 2.4], so
four odd-degree vertices cannot replace two at modulus four.

                              11. Splitting a balanced pair
   For an incident end of a nonloop edge at v, its outward value is the value read away from
v. Two different edges incident with the same vertex v form a balanced pair at v if their
outward values sum to zero.
Lemma 11.1. Let G have no loop and let uv, vw be distinct edges, where u = w is allowed.
Replace them by a new edge g = uw, retaining v as a vertex, to obtain G′ . Flows of G′
correspond bijectively to flows of G in which the chosen pair at v is balanced. A cycle step in
G′ lifts to a sequence of cycle steps in G with the same coefficient. The operation preserves
every vertex degree parity and reduces the number of edges by one.
Proof. Orient g from u to w, specifying its two ends if it is a loop. Replace its value x by
the same value along u → v → w. The flow equations at all vertices are preserved, and the
inverse is forced by balance at v. This proves the bijection, over integers as well as over Zk .
   A directed cycle downstairs that uses g becomes a closed trail upstairs after this replacement.
Its signed vector has entries 0, 1, −1 and decomposes into edge-disjoint directed cycle vectors
after orienting its support according to the signs. A cycle avoiding g needs no change. Apply
the same coefficient on the resulting cycles in turn. Each edge takes its starting or final
value, so every intermediate flow is valid. The argument includes a loop g, which lifts to the
two-edge cycle. Only the two endpoints of the lifted sequence must have the specified pair
balanced; an intermediate flow need not keep it balanced. Only the degree at v changes, by
two.                                                                                           □
   For integer flows the inverse splitting also preserves the divergence vector of their positive
orientations: at v it inserts one entering and one leaving edge, and elsewhere it replaces an
edge end by one with the same direction.

                      12. A common family of reference flows
   The positive orientation of an integer nowhere-zero flow directs each edge in the direction
of its positive value. For an orientation D, let 1D be its unit edge map in the fixed reference
directions. Its divergence is outdegree minus indegree.
   For a graph with at most two odd-degree vertices in total, call D near-Eulerian if this
divergence is zero at every even-degree vertex and is +1 at one odd-degree vertex and −1 at
the other, if there are two. If there are none, D is Eulerian. An anchor is an integer 3-flow
whose positive orientation is near-Eulerian. Its edge magnitudes are one or two. The unit
divergence vector of an anchor is div 1D , where D is its positive orientation; it is not the
divergence of the flow, which is zero.
Lemma 12.1. Every bridgeless graph with at most two odd-degree vertices has an anchor.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        39


Proof. Work componentwise. An even component has an Eulerian orientation and its unit
circulation is an anchor. For a component with odd vertices u, v, add an auxiliary uv edge,
orient the resulting even graph Eulerian, and delete that edge. Name its ends so that the
remaining orientation D has unit divergence +1 at u and −1 at v.
   For every nontrivial cut the difference between the outgoing and incoming arc counts has
absolute value at most one. Since the component is bridgeless, the cut has at least two edges
and cannot be one-way. Thus D is strongly connected. Choose a directed path P from v to
u. The circulation 1D + χP has positive values one or two. It is an anchor. Isolated vertices
and loops cause no exception.                                                              □
Lemma 12.2. For k ≥ 4, the reductions modulo k of anchors with the same unit divergence
vector are joined by restricted steps.
Proof. Let the positive orientations be D and E, so div 1D = div 1E at every vertex of the
whole graph. The edges on which they differ form a balanced directed subgraph in D: at
each vertex the number of reversed outgoing edges equals the number of reversed incoming
edges. Decompose that subgraph into edge-disjoint D-directed cycles.
   Starting with the first anchor, reverse these cycles one at a time. The current positive
values on such a cycle C are one or two. Subtract b = maxC F + 1, where b = 2 or 3. The
resulting values are negative with magnitude one or two. This gives another integer 3-flow,
reversing exactly C. Later cycles are still directed because they have disjoint edge sets.
   For b = 2 the reduction is a restricted step. For b = 3, replace its modular effect by k − 3
successive steps of size +1 along C. After the jth step, 1 ≤ j ≤ k − 3, every along-cycle
value is F (e) + j ∈ [2, k − 1], so it is nonzero. The final residue is F (e) − 3. At k = 4 this
replacement consists of one step.
   The final positive orientation is E. Two positive integer 3-flows in this orientation are
joined by conformal unit-cycle decomposition of their difference: each intermediate coordinate
lies between its positive endpoints one and two. These steps are restricted after reduction
modulo k.                                                                                     □
Lemma 12.3. Let G be bridgeless with exactly two odd-degree vertices. For k ≥ 4, the
reductions of anchors corresponding to the two opposite choices of unit divergence at these
vertices belong to the same restricted-step component.
Proof. Work in the connected component containing the odd vertices u, v, and fix a near-
Eulerian orientation D with divergences +1 at u and −1 at v, as in the construction of
Lemma 12.1. On every other component choose an Eulerian orientation, extend D by it,
and give each of F0 , F1 , F2 below the constant value one in that orientation. For a vertex
set containing u but not v, the cut size d is odd by degree parity and is at least three by
bridgelessness. Its outgoing and incoming counts satisfy o − i = 1 and o + i = d, so o ≥ 2.
The directed edge version of Menger’s theorem [10, Section 4.1, Corollary 4.1b] supplies two
edge-disjoint directed u–v paths P1 , P2 .
   Delete their arcs. The residual unit map has divergence −1 at u, +1 at v, and zero
elsewhere. It contains a directed v–u path P3 : otherwise the set reachable from v would
have no outgoing arc and total divergence +1, a contradiction. The three paths are pairwise
edge-disjoint; internal vertex disjointness is not needed.
   Put
          F0 = 1D + χP3 ,      F1 = 1D + χP2 + 2χP3 ,        F2 = 1D − 2χP1 + χP2 .
40                                        DAVE GOEL


Each is a circulation. The first is an anchor. The circulation χP2 + χP3 decomposes into
edge-disjoint directed cycles. Add one along these cycles to pass from F0 to F1 . Values on
P2 change from one to two, and on P3 from two to three.
   Next decompose χP1 + χP3 into edge-disjoint directed cycles and subtract two on each.
This changes values on P1 from one to minus one and those on P3 from three to one, reaching
F2 . In both sequences each affected edge changes once between its valid endpoints, and
all magnitudes are at most three. Thus their reductions are legal restricted steps for every
k ≥ 4.
   The positive orientation of F2 reverses only P1 from D. Its unit divergences are −1 at u
and +1 at v, and F2 has magnitudes one or two. It is an opposite anchor. Apply Lemma 12.2
within each of the two families.                                                          □
Corollary 12.4. For k ≥ 4, all anchor reductions of any fixed bridgeless graph with at most
two odd-degree vertices lie in one restricted-step component.
Proof. Use Lemmas 12.2 and 12.3. If there are no odd-degree vertices, Lemma 12.2 alone
applies, since every anchor has zero unit divergence vector.                        □

                                    13. The local step
  Fix k ≥ 4. Call an edge small if its modular value is 1 or −1. The following two local
operations are the cyclic operations of Section 6; neither uses the parity of the vertex degrees.
Lemma 13.1. Let f be a nowhere-zero Zk -flow.
  (1) If its non-small edges contain a cycle, restricted steps can increase the number of
      small edges without changing any previously small edge.
  (2) Suppose G is loopless, f has no balanced pair, and Q is a cycle of small edges. At a
      vertex v of Q its two outward cycle values are equal, say a ∈ {1, −1}. If an end at v
      not on Q has value c ̸= −2a, restricted steps along Q reach a flow with a balanced
      pair at v.
Proof. For (1), orient the non-small cycle and represent its values by integers xe ∈ [2, k − 2].
Subtract one repeatedly until the minimum becomes one. The values remain nonzero, and
no previously small edge is touched.
   For (2), opposite outward cycle values would already be a balanced pair. Set t = a + c.
Then t ∈/ {0, a, −a}: respectively these exclusions use the absence of a balanced pair, c ̸= 0,
and c ̸= −2a. Choose its representative τ ∈ [2, k − 2]. Direct Q to enter v through one chosen
cycle end. Perform a step of size two followed by τ − 2 steps of size one. At cumulative
increments j = 2, . . . , τ , all along-cycle values are 1 + j or −1 + j, in [1, k − 1]. At the
entering end at v the new outward value is a − t = −c, balancing the unchanged end of value
c. That end belongs to an edge not on Q, since G is loopless.                                □
Proposition 13.2. Let G be loopless, with at most two odd-degree vertices, at least one edge,
and no vertex of degree one or two. Every nowhere-zero Zk -flow is restricted-step connected
to a flow with a balanced pair or to an anchor reduction.
Proof. Use Lemma 13.1(1) until the non-small edges form a forest. This terminates because
each use increases the number of small edges. A balanced pair or an application of part (2)
finishes the first alternative, so assume neither is present.
   At every vertex on a small cycle all additional incident ends have outward value −2a, with
a ∈ {1, −1}. For k ≥ 4, this is neither 1 nor −1. Hence every such vertex has exactly two
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                         41


small ends, and every component of the small-edge graph is a tree or a whole cycle. Take the
small-edge graph on the n ≥ 2 vertices nonisolated in G. It therefore has at most n edges.
The non-small forest has at most n − 1.
  Every even-degree nonisolated vertex has degree at least four, and at most two vertices
have odd degree, each at least three. Therefore
                         2n − 1 ≤ |E(G)| ≤ n + (n − 1) = 2n − 1.
If there are no odd vertices, the lower bound is 2n, a contradiction. Otherwise equality forces
exactly two vertices of degree three, every other nonisolated vertex of degree four, exactly n
small edges, and a non-small spanning tree. The small-edge graph must be a spanning union
of cycles: a tree component, including a vertex isolated in the small-edge graph, would make
its edge count smaller than n.
   A degree-four vertex on a small cycle has outward values a, a, −2a, −2a, with sum −2a ̸= 0
in Zk , contradicting conservation. Thus n = 2, and, apart from isolated vertices, the loopless
graph is the three-edge dipole. In a common direction exactly two of its edge values are
small. They cannot be opposite, which would force the third value to be zero. They are both
a ∈ {1, −1} and the third is −2a. This is the reduction of an anchor, with integer values
a, a, −2a.                                                                                   □

                          14. Proof of modular connectivity
Proof of Theorem 10.1(1). Induct on the number of edges, allowing disconnected graphs
throughout. The bridge case is empty; this includes a vertex of degree one, whose incident
edge is a bridge. An edgeless graph has one flow. A loop is an independent coordinate; unit
steps through 1, 2, . . . , k − 1 connect all its nonzero values. Delete loops and use induction.
At a degree-two vertex its two edges are balanced in every flow, so Lemma 11.1 and induction
give connectivity.
   We may therefore assume G is loopless and its nonisolated vertices have degree at least
three. Fix a vertex v and a pair of its incident edges, and consider a nonempty family of flows
in which that pair is balanced. Its split graph G′ has fewer edges and at most two odd-degree
vertices. It has a nowhere-zero modular flow, so has no bridge; hence it has an anchor by
Lemma 12.1. By induction every flow of G′ reaches the reduction of that anchor by restricted
steps. Lemma 11.1 lifts this path. The lifted integer anchor retains its magnitudes and, by
the positive-orientation observation immediately after Lemma 11.1, its positive orientation is
near-Eulerian on G. Thus every balanced-pair flow of G reaches an anchor reduction.
   All anchor reductions lie in one component by Corollary 12.4. Proposition 13.2 joins every
remaining flow to a balanced-pair flow or an anchor reduction. This proves the induction. □

                         15. Integer transfer and sharpness
  For clarity we record the earlier lifting facts used here.
Lemma 15.1. For every finite multigraph and every k ≥ 2, every nowhere-zero Zk -flow has
an integer k-lift, and its integer lifts induce a connected subgraph of Ik (G). For k ≥ 3, every
legal modular step of size 1, −1, 2, −2 has adjacent integer lifts at its endpoints.
Proof. We first recall elementary rounding. If x ∈ [0, 1]E(G) has integral divergence, its
fractional edges contain a cycle whenever any remain. A fractional loop is itself a cycle.
If there is none, a vertex with exactly one fractional nonloop incident end would have
nonintegral divergence, so the fractional-edge subgraph contains a cycle. Vary x along such
42                                        DAVE GOEL


a cycle until another coordinate reaches zero or one. This preserves divergence and fixes all
previously integral coordinates. Iteration gives z ∈ {0, 1}E(G) with the same divergence.
   For modular representatives r(e) ∈ {1, . . . , k − 1}, round r/k. Then F = r − kz is an
integer k-lift. For two lifts F, H, the circulation (H − F )/k has entries 0, 1, −1. Decompose
it conformally into edge-disjoint signed cycle vectors. Adding k on them changes every
coordinate once, directly between its valid endpoint values, giving connected fibres.
   For a positive unit step on C, orient reference edges along C. Reversing a reference edge
and negating its coordinate is an isomorphism of the flow spaces and their reconfiguration
graphs, so this is harmless. Its legality excludes residue k − 1 there, so either allowable
integer representative remains nonzero and within bounds after adding one. For a step of
size two, legality excludes residue k − 2 on C. Round
                                       x = (r + χC )/k
in the unit box. Its divergence is integral because χC is a circulation. The rounded z is one
wherever the cycle residue is k − 1. The lift F = r − kz is then minus one on those edges and
becomes one after adding two. All other cycle residues are at most k − 3, and either integer
representative accepts the addition of two. This gives the adjacent lifts. Reverse steps for
negative coefficients.                                                                      □
Completion of Theorem 10.1. For integer k-flows F, H, part (1) joins their reductions by
restricted modular steps. Lemma 15.1 supplies adjacent integer lifts at each step; its
connected-fibre assertion joins successive choices and the prescribed endpoints F, H. This
proves part (2).
   For the per-component extension, each of the integer, modular, and restricted-step modular
reconfiguration graphs is the Cartesian product of the corresponding graphs of the connected
components. Every cycle belongs to one component. If a factor is empty the product is
empty; otherwise a finite product of connected factors is connected.
   Finally let G consist of three parallel edges between two vertices, all referenced in the
same direction. Its only nowhere-zero Z3 -flows are (1, 1, 1) and (2, 2, 2). A cycle uses
two edges, so neither state has a legal move to the other. These are its two modular
components. The integer 3-flows form two classes: the three permutations of (1, 1, −2) and
the three permutations of (−1, −1, 2). Each class is a triangle under exchanging two unequal
coordinates by a cycle step of size three. No path joins the classes, since their reductions
lie in different modular components. Thus the integer graph is the disjoint union of two
triangles and the threshold four is sharp in both assertions.                              □

                    16. Statement, conventions and provenance
   All graphs are finite multigraphs; our convention allows loops. A loop contributes two to
the degree. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph, so loops and pairs of parallel edges
are cycles. Fix reference directions on the edges. A flow with values in an abelian group A,
written additively, has outgoing-minus-incoming sum zero at every vertex. A loop contributes
zero to this sum. A nowhere-zero flow has no zero edge value. Reversing a reference direction
negates that value.
   For a directed traversal of a cycle or path C, let χC be its signed edge vector, with
entries 1, −1, 0. The graph FA (G) has nowhere-zero A-flows as its vertices; a legal step is
f 7→ f + tχC , where C is a cycle, t ∈ A \ {0}, and the resulting values are all nonzero. We
call an increment safe if it creates no zero edge value; a safe nonzero cycle increment is a
legal step, whereas a zero increment is stationary. Integer coefficients in this notation act on
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          43


group elements by repeated addition. This agrees with adjacency by cycle support in [3, 2].
We regard a reconfiguration graph with zero or one vertex as connected; in particular, an
empty flow space counts as connected. An edgeless graph has the unique empty flow.
Theorem 16.1. Let A be a finite abelian group with |A| ≥ 4. If every connected component
of a finite multigraph G has at most two odd-degree vertices, then FA (G) is connected. The
uniform bound |A| ≥ 4 cannot be replaced by |A| ≥ 3.
   The assertion uses arbitrary nonzero group coefficients. The integer flows are treated in
Theorem 10.1.
   Cranston, Li, Su, Wang and Xu [2, Conjecture 4.1] proposed the group assertion for
even graphs. The even-order case is known from their Theorem 4.3; their Corollary 4.5
also covers groups of order at least 5,251,339 on even graphs. Theorem 2.3 proves the full
finite-abelian assertion. The cyclic and integer extension to two odd vertices is proved in
Theorem 10.1. Here we extend the group assertion to the same graph class. We repeat the
balanced-pair splitting, forest normalization and triangle projection from Sections 3 to 5, and
the near-Eulerian orientations and directed paths from Section 12. The proof below supplies
all of these ingredients and does not assume either of these two connectivity theorems. The
additional steps are the quotient-support arguments, the gluing of pairing families in exponent
two, and the common references and local repair for the other groups. The obstruction at
order three is already covered by [2, Theorem 2.1]; we give a small instance at the end.
   For general bridgeless graphs, earlier results already give connectivity over Z82 and over all
sufficiently large finite abelian groups [3]. The earlier group manuscript [5] gives the bound
|A| ≥ 3024 for every finite bridgeless multigraph, including loops, and also covers various
smaller groups. The present theorem removes the remaining group restrictions at orders at
least four for graphs with at most two odd-degree vertices per component.

                           17. Operations used in both cases
  The outward value of an incident end at v is the edge value read away from v. A balanced
pair consists of two distinct nonloop edges at v whose outward values sum to zero.
Lemma 17.1 (Balanced splitting). In a loopless graph replace distinct edges uv, vw by one
edge uw, retaining v as a vertex, and allowing u = w and hence a new loop. Flows of the
new graph correspond bijectively to flows of the old graph balanced on the chosen pair. Every
cycle move in the new graph lifts to a sequence of cycle moves with the same coefficient in the
old graph. The operation reduces the number of edges by one, preserves every degree parity,
and does not increase the maximum degree.
Proof. Orient the new edge from u to w, distinguishing its ends if it is a loop. Give its value
to both edges along u → v → w. Conservation is preserved, and balance forces the inverse
map. A cycle using the new edge lifts to a closed trail with a signed vector having entries
0, 1, −1. Orient its support according to those signs. The resulting balanced directed graph
decomposes into edge-disjoint directed cycles. Apply the same coefficient on those cycles
in turn: each edge has its old or final value at every intermediate state, so no zero appears.
A cycle avoiding the new edge lifts unchanged. The degree at v falls by two and all other
degrees are unchanged. Intermediate flows need not keep the chosen pair balanced.            □
   The same correspondence works for integer flows. The lift from the split graph back to
the original graph preserves edge magnitudes and the divergence of the positive orientation:
it inserts one incoming and one outgoing end at v and changes no other end direction.
44                                          DAVE GOEL


Lemma 17.2 (Quotient support). Let H ≤ A and let f be an A-flow. Delete all edges whose
values lie in H. The remaining support of f modulo H is bridgeless. On any cycle in that
support, adding any element of H is safe.
Proof. The surviving values give a nowhere-zero A/H-flow. A bridge in its support would
have value zero, by summing conservation on one side of the bridge. Thus every surviving
edge lies on a cycle. Values outside H remain outside H after addition or subtraction of an
element of H.                                                                             □
   A loop is an independent nonzero coordinate of a flow. Any two choices are joined by
one move on that loop, unless already equal. Flow graphs of components form a Cartesian
product, since every cycle belongs to one component. Empty factors give an empty product
flow space; otherwise connected factors give a connected flow space.
Lemma 17.3 (The dipole). For |A| ≥ 4, the flow graph of two vertices joined by three
parallel edges is connected.
Proof. Orient two edges forward and one backward. A state has values (x, y, z) with x+y = z
and all three nonzero. It is determined by the ordered pair (x, z) of distinct elements of
A \ {0}. Changing x with z fixed is a move on the first two edges; changing z with x fixed is
a move on the last two edges. To reach (x′ , z ′ ), if x′ ̸= z set the first entry to x′ and then the
second to z ′ . If x′ = z, first set the second entry to an element w ∈ A \ {0, x, x′ }, then set
the first to x′ and the second to z ′ . Such w exists because |A| ≥ 4. Omit stationary changes.
Every pair along these paths has distinct nonzero entries.                                         □

                     18. Part A: elementary abelian two-groups
  Throughout this section A has exponent two and |A| ≥ 4. Signs of values are immaterial.
Balance of a pair means equality of its two values.
Lemma 18.1. At a fixed degree-four vertex of a loopless graph, every nowhere-zero A-flow
reaches a flow having a balanced pair at that vertex in at most one step.
Proof. Write the four values as a, b, c, d, with a + b + c + d = 0. If any two coincide, there
is nothing to prove. Otherwise K = ⟨a + b, a + c⟩ is a subgroup of order four and the four
values constitute its coset a + K. Since they are all nonzero, this coset is not K. All four
incident edges therefore survive in the quotient support modulo K. By Lemma 17.2 choose
a cycle through the vertex in that support. Rename the ends so that a, b are on the cycle
and c, d are off it; the difference subgroup K does not change under this renaming. Adding
t = a + c = b + d ̸= 0 along the cycle is safe, since t ∈ K. The changed values become c, d,
respectively, matching the two unchanged values.                                            □
Lemma 18.2. In a loopless graph, label the four ends at a degree-four vertex by 1, 2, 3, 4.
For each of their three partitions π into two pairs, let Bπ be the family of flows equal on
the pairs. A nonempty family either contains a state common to all three families or has a
one-step link to another nonempty family.
Proof. Take a state in the family. If all four values are equal, it belongs to every family.
Otherwise the values are a, a, b, b with a ̸= b. Put t = a + b and H = {0, t}. Neither a nor b
is in H: either possibility would make a or b zero. The four incident edges survive in the
quotient support. Choose a cycle through the vertex there and add t on it, which is safe
by Lemma 17.2. This swaps a and b on precisely its two incident ends. If these had equal
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                            45


values, all four new values are equal; if they had different values, the equal pairs give a
different partition. In the latter case the resulting flow explicitly witnesses that other family’s
nonemptiness.                                                                                    □
Proposition 18.3. If G has maximum degree at most four and at most two odd-degree
vertices in total, then FA (G) is connected.
Proof. Induct on the number of edges, allowing disconnected graphs throughout. An edgeless
graph has the unique empty flow. A bridge makes the flow space empty, and a degree-one
vertex has a bridge. Delete a loop and use induction with its independent coordinate. Hence
assume the graph is loopless. At a degree-two vertex the two edges are balanced in every
flow, so splitting and induction settle the assertion. Remove isolated vertices, which change
no flows. The remaining degrees are three and four, with at most two of degree three. If
there is no degree-four vertex, the nonempty graph has exactly two vertices: its number of
odd-degree vertices is positive, even, and at most two. Both have degree three and there are
no loops, so it is the three-edge dipole, handled by Lemma 17.3.
   Otherwise fix a degree-four vertex v. For each nonempty Bπ , split one of its prescribed
pairs. Conservation forces the other pair to be equal as well. The split graph still has
maximum degree at most four and at most two odd vertices, and has fewer edges. Induction
and Lemma 17.1 join any two states in Bπ in the ambient flow graph; the intermediate states
need not stay in that family.
   Make a graph on the nonempty families Bπ , joining two when they intersect or have a legal
one-step link. There are at most three vertices. If a monochromatic state exists, all three
families intersect. Otherwise Lemma 18.2 shows that every family vertex has a neighbour.
Thus there are two or three family vertices, and a graph on two or three vertices without
isolated vertices is connected. The one-family case would already be connected. Thus the
union of the families lies in one component of FA (G). Every flow reaches that union by
Lemma 18.1. If there are no flows at all the assertion was already empty. This proves the
induction.                                                                                 □

 19. Part B: common references when an element has order at least three
   Fix a ∈ A with ord(a) ≥ 3, and continue to assume |A| ≥ 4. An integer 3-flow is an
integer circulation with edge magnitudes one or two. Its positive orientation directs each
edge in the direction of its positive value. Call an orientation D near-Eulerian when the unit
edge map 1D has divergence zero at each even vertex and +1, −1 at the two odd vertices, if
present. With no odd vertices this means Eulerian. An anchor is an integer 3-flow with such
a positive orientation. Its unit divergence refers to div 1D , not to the divergence of the flow,
which is zero. A reference is the group flow aF obtained by multiplying an anchor F by a;
its values ±a, ±2a are nonzero.
Lemma 19.1. Every bridgeless graph with at most two odd vertices in total has an anchor.
All its references for the fixed element a lie in one component of FA (G).
Proof. An even component has an Eulerian orientation and its unit circulation is an anchor.
In the component with odd vertices u, v, add an auxiliary uv edge, orient Eulerian, and
delete that edge. Name the ends so that the remaining orientation D has unit divergence
+1 at u and −1 at v. Across every cut the difference of outgoing and incoming arc counts
has absolute value at most one. Since the graph is bridgeless, every nontrivial cut has at
least two edges, hence has arcs in both directions. Thus D is strongly connected. A directed
46                                       DAVE GOEL


return path P from v to u gives the anchor 1D + χP . Isolated vertices and loops cause no
exception.
   First join anchors with equal unit divergence vectors. If their positive orientations are
D, E, the edges on which they differ form a balanced directed subgraph in D: its signed edge
vector is half of 1D − 1E , an integer circulation. Decompose it into edge-disjoint directed
cycles. Reverse them one at a time: on a current directed cycle the positive integer values are
one or two, so subtracting their maximum plus one (either two or three) makes them negative
with magnitudes one or two. This remains an integer 3-flow. Later cycles are still directed
because the cycles are edge-disjoint. The final orientation is E. In that orientation the
difference from the desired anchor decomposes conformally into unit cycle vectors, meaning
that all signs agree with the difference on each edge. Applying them one at a time keeps
each coordinate between its positive endpoints. This joins the integer anchors through
integer 3-flows. Multiplying the entire path by a gives legal group moves; any coefficient that
becomes zero in A gives equality and is omitted. In particular no assumption 3a ̸= 0 is used.
   It remains to join the opposite unit divergence choices at u, v. Fix D as above. For a
set containing u and not v, its cut size d is odd and, by bridgelessness, at least three. The
outgoing and incoming counts satisfy o − i = 1 and o + i = d, so o ≥ 2. The directed edge
form of Menger’s theorem [10, Corollary 4.1b] supplies two edge-disjoint directed paths P1 , P2
from u to v. After deleting their arcs, the residual unit map has divergence +1 at v, −1 at
u, and zero elsewhere. It contains a directed path P3 from v to u: otherwise the vertices
reachable from v have no outgoing arc but total divergence +1, a contradiction. The three
paths are edge-disjoint; they may share internal vertices. Since u ̸= v, each path has at least
one edge. Extend D by fixed Eulerian orientations on every other component, so that the
following edge maps are defined on the whole graph.
   Give every edge outside these paths value a in orientation D, and give the three paths
values x, y, z, respectively. The resulting map is
                       a1D + (x − a)χP1 + (y − a)χP2 + (z − a)χP3 .
It is a nowhere-zero flow exactly when x + y = z and x, y, z are nonzero. Changing x while
fixing z changes it by t(χP1 − χP2 ); changing z while fixing x changes it by t(χP2 + χP3 ).
Each parenthesized vector is an integer circulation with entries 0, 1, −1, and decomposes
into edge-disjoint signed cycle vectors. Applying t on these cycles in turn is safe, because
every coordinate has its old or final value at every prefix. Consequently the dipole paths of
Lemma 17.3 join any two members of this family in the ambient flow graph. Intermediate
states after only some of the cycle moves need not belong to the family.
   In particular they join (a, a, 2a) to (−a, 2a, a). These are the references obtained from
the anchors 1D + χP3 and 1D − 2χP1 + χP2 . The latter reverses precisely P1 in its positive
orientation, so its unit divergences at u, v are opposite to those of the former. On other
components both references use the fixed Eulerian unit anchors. This route avoids multiplying
the cyclic proof’s intermediate integer flow of magnitude three by a, which could produce zero
values when 3a = 0 (Section 12). The equal-divergence argument then joins all references. If
there are no odd vertices, that argument alone suffices.                                    □

                 20. Part B continued: reaching a balanced pair
  Fix a decomposition of A as a direct sum of nontrivial cyclic groups, choose a generator of
each summand, and let S consist of these generators and their negatives. An edge is small if
                  THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                           47


its value is in S; its colour is the unique summand containing that signed generator. This
notion is unchanged by reversing its reference direction.
Lemma 20.1 (Forest normalization). Every nowhere-zero A-flow reaches one whose non-
small edges form a forest, without changing any edge that was already small.
Proof. If a cycle has only non-small edges, orient its traversal and let X be the set of its
along-cycle values. There exist x ∈ X, s ∈ S with x − s ∈/ X. Otherwise X − s ⊆ X for every
s ∈ S; finiteness gives equality, and translation invariance under the generating set S gives
X = A, contradicting 0 ∈ / X. Add s − x on the cycle. This coefficient is nonzero since x ∈
                                                                                          / S.
A zero new value would mean x − s ∈ X, which was excluded. At least one edge becomes
small and no old small edge changes. Repeat; the number of small edges strictly increases at
each application.                                                                           □
Lemma 20.2 (Local repair). Suppose a flow of a loopless graph has no balanced pair anywhere
and a small-edge cycle contains a degree-four vertex v. One legal cycle move reaches a flow
with a balanced pair. The move may use a different cycle.
Proof. Only the absence of a balanced pair at v is used in this proof. Let y1 , y2 be the two
outward cycle values and y3 , y4 the other outward values at v. Their sum is zero. Direct the
small cycle entering through end 1. Set t3 = y1 + y3 and t4 = y1 + y4 . They are nonzero,
since no pair is balanced. If tj ∈ / S, adding tj on this cycle is safe: every along-cycle value
belongs to the symmetric set S. The new outward value at end 1 is −yj , giving a balanced
pair.
   If y1 = a0 , y2 = b0 have different colours, then t3 + t4 = a0 − b0 . If both t3 , t4 belonged to
S, the direct-sum coordinates would force their unordered pair to be {a0 , −b0 }. Indeed the
two distinct nonzero coordinates of their sum require one term in each of those summands.
But tj = a0 would make yj = 0. Thus at least one candidate lies outside S and the preceding
move works.
   Otherwise y1 , y2 have the same colour. They are not opposite, so y1 = y2 = a0 and 2a0 ̸= 0.
Writing t = t3 gives t4 = −t and
                           (y1 , y2 , y3 , y4 ) = (a0 , a0 , −a0 + t, −a0 − t).
If t ∈
     / S we are done. If t is a signed generator of the same summand as a0 , then t = ±a0 ,
making y3 or y4 zero. Thus the remaining case has t in a different cyclic summand. Put
H = ⟨t⟩. All four values at v lie outside H, by their nonzero a0 -coordinate. Choose a cycle
through v in the quotient support modulo H. Every H-increment on it is safe.
   If this cycle uses ends i < j, direct it entering through i and leaving through j. Their
values change by (−h, +h). The following choices produce the displayed balanced pairs; the
two unlisted values stay fixed.
                        {i, j} h new values at i, j             balanced pairs
                        {1, 2} t   a0 − t, a0 + t                (1, 3), (2, 4)
                        {3, 4} t     −a0 , −a0                   (3, 1), (4, 2)
                        {1, 3} −t   a0 + t, −a0                  (1, 4), (3, 2)
                        {1, 4} t    a0 − t, −a0                  (1, 3), (4, 2)
                        {2, 3} −t   a0 + t, −a0                  (2, 4), (3, 1)
                        {2, 4} t    a0 − t, −a0                  (2, 3), (4, 1)
Each coefficient is nonzero. Lemma 17.2 proves safety on the entire new cycle, regardless of
the values elsewhere on the original small cycle. This also covers t of order two.        □
48                                         DAVE GOEL


Lemma 20.3 (A parallel pair at the odd vertices). Let |A| ≥ 4, and let a connected loopless
graph have all degrees three or four. Suppose it has exactly two degree-three vertices u, v, and
these vertices have at least two parallel edges between them. Then the graph is the dipole or
its flow connectivity follows from that of a smaller even graph of maximum degree at most
four.
Proof. Three parallel edges make the entire connected graph the dipole. Otherwise there are
exactly two. Let the other edges be pu, vq, where p, q ∈ / {u, v} and p = q is allowed. Delete
u, v and all four edges, replacing them by e = pq, a loop if p = q. The resulting graph G′ is
even, retains all other degrees, and has three fewer edges.
   Orient the configuration p → u, two edges u → v, and v → q. Conservation gives nonzero
outside values both equal to f and internal values x, y with x + y = f . Projection sends this
to value f on e. Every downstairs flow extends, by choosing x ∈   / {0, f } and y = f − x. For
fixed downstairs flow the extensions form a clique under the internal two-edge cycle moves.
   For a downstairs move using e, let its old and new values be f, f ′ . Choose x ∈ / {0, f, f ′ },
possible for |A| ≥ 4, and first adjust within the initial fibre to that x. Keep this internal
edge fixed, and replace e in the downstairs cycle by the path p − u − v − q using the other
internal edge. This is a cycle upstairs; if p = q, the downstairs loop becomes the triangle
p − u − v − p. The changed internal value goes from f − x to f ′ − x, both nonzero; the outside
values go from f to f ′ . All other coordinates behave as downstairs. A move not using e lifts
unchanged. Final adjustments within the terminal fibre prove that connectedness downstairs
implies connectedness upstairs.                                                                 □
Proposition 20.4. Suppose A contains an element of order at least three and |A| ≥ 4. If
G has maximum degree at most four and at most two odd vertices in total, then FA (G) is
connected.
Proof. Induct on the number of edges. The edgeless graph has the unique empty flow.
Bridges give empty flow spaces; in particular degree one is settled. Delete loops and use
their independent coordinates, so the graph is now loopless. At a degree-two vertex split its
necessarily balanced pair and use induction. Isolated vertices can be discarded, and if more
than one component has edges, each has fewer edges, so induction applies to them and their
product. Thus it suffices to consider a connected loopless graph with degrees three and four.
There are at most two degree-three vertices. Handle the dipole and a parallel pair between
these vertices by Lemmas 17.3 and 20.3, using induction on the smaller graph.
   In every remaining case start with any flow and apply Lemma 20.1. On n vertices at most
two degrees are three and all others are four, so the degree sum is at least 4n − 2. Thus the
graph has at least 2n − 1 edges and the non-small forest has at most n − 1 edges. There
are therefore at least n small edges, so the small-edge graph has a cycle. A cycle avoiding
all degree-four vertices would lie on the at most two degree-three vertices and be a parallel
pair already handled. Thus some degree-four vertex lies on it. If a balanced pair is already
present, keep it; otherwise Lemma 20.2 creates one.
   For a fixed pair of incident edges, its balanced-pair family consists of all flows balanced on
that pair. For any nonempty such family, split the pair. The split graph has fewer edges and
remains in the induction class. It has a flow, so it is bridgeless; by Lemma 19.1 it has an
anchor. Induction joins every downstairs flow to a reference aF of the split graph. Lifting
this path joins each member of the original family to a reference of G: the lift of aF is a
times the lift of F , and the latter is an anchor, since lifting preserves integer magnitudes and
the unit divergence of the positive orientation. The starting flow also shows that G itself is
                  THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                               49


bridgeless. All references for the fixed a lie in one component by Lemma 19.1. Hence every
nonempty balanced-pair family reaches that same component. The preceding local argument
brings every flow into one such family, completing the induction.                       □

              21. Arbitrary degrees and completion of the theorem
Lemma 21.1 (Triangle projection). Suppose a loopless graph G is obtained from a graph
Gb by contracting a triangle whose edges are the only edges among its three vertices. If
|A| ≥ 4, every nowhere-zero A-flow of G extends to one of b
                                                          G. Cycle moves upstairs project
to sequences of cycle moves downstairs, possibly empty. Thus connectivity upstairs implies
connectivity downstairs.
Proof. Orient the triangle cyclically, with values r1 , r2 , r3 on v1 v2 , v2 v3 , v3 v1 . Let βi be the
outward divergence of the prescribed external edges at vi , so β1 + β2 + β3 = 0. The equations
are solved by
                           r1 = z,     r 2 = z − β2 ,     r3 = z + β1 .
At most three choices of z ∈ A are forbidden, giving an extension. Projection simply retains
the values off the triangle and preserves conservation after contraction.
   The signed vector of a cycle upstairs, restricted to retained edges, is an integer circulation
downstairs with entries 0, 1, −1. If nonzero, its signed support decomposes into edge-disjoint
cycles. Apply the original coefficient on them in turn. Each retained coordinate has its old
or final nonzero value at every prefix. If the vector is zero, the projected step is stationary.
This gives the claim by lifting any two downstairs endpoints and projecting a path between
the lifts. The proof uses no connectedness of the extension fibres.                                   □
Proof of Theorem 16.1. First assume at most two odd vertices in total. If no element of A
has order at least three, then A is an elementary abelian two-group; otherwise it is covered by
Part B. Consequently Propositions 18.3 and 20.4 give the assertion when maximum degree is
at most four.
   Delete loops first and restore their independent coordinates at the end. For a loopless
graph induct on                          X
                              Φ(G) =         max{deg(v) − 4, 0}.
                                         v∈V (G)
The case Φ = 0 was just proved. For an even-degree vertex of degree d ≥ 6, expand it
to a triangle, distributing its original incident ends in groups of sizes 2, 2, d − 4. The new
degrees are 4, 4, d − 2, all even, and Φ drops by two. For an odd-degree vertex of degree
d ≥ 5, use group sizes 1, 2, d − 3. The new degrees are 3, 4, d − 1, so exactly one remains odd
and Φ drops by one. The number of odd vertices is preserved in each case. As the original
graph was loopless, the only edges among the three new vertices are the added triangle.
The expanded graph satisfies induction, and Lemma 21.1 transfers connectivity back to G.
If flows downstairs exist, the extension lemma ensures flows upstairs exist as well, so the
empty-space convention causes no difficulty.
   Finally take Cartesian products over connected components to obtain the stated per-
component assertion, including isolated vertices and empty factors. To see sharpness, orient
the three edges of the dipole in the same direction and take A = Z3 . Its only nowhere-zero
flows are (1, 1, 1) and (2, 2, 2). A cycle uses only two edges, so neither state can reach the
other. The threshold cannot be lowered to three.                                              □
