Part 3. Four vertices of odd degree
   This part proves parts (a) and (b) of Theorem 1.3: its first seven sections for k = 5, and
its last seven sections for k ≥ 6.

 22. Four odd-degree vertices at modulus five: statement and conventions
   All graphs are finite multigraphs, with loops and parallel edges allowed. A loop contributes
two to the degree. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph, including a loop or a pair of
parallel edges. Fix a reference direction for each edge. Divergence is the outgoing sum minus
the incoming sum; a loop contributes zero. A circulation has zero divergence. For a cycle C
with a direction of traversal, χC has value 1 on forward edges, −1 on backward edges, and 0
elsewhere. For a directed path P , define χP in the same way.
   An integer k-flow is an integer circulation F with 1 ≤ |F (e)| ≤ k − 1 on every edge.
A nowhere-zero Zk -flow is a circulation with nonzero values in Zk ; we say flow for short.
The reconfiguration graphs Ik (G) and Mk (G) have these flows as vertices, with a step
F 7→ F + tχC , or f 7→ f + tχC , whenever the coefficient t is nonzero and both endpoints are
flows of the indicated kind. On loopless graphs this is the adjacency of [3, 2]; counting a
loop as a cycle is the usual extension to graphs with loops. Two flows are joined if a finite,
possibly empty, sequence of steps leads from one to the other; so every flow is joined to itself.
A reconfiguration graph with no vertices, or with one vertex, is regarded as connected. If G
has a bridge, every circulation vanishes there, so G has no flow.
   For a vertex set W , let δ(W ) be the set of edges with exactly one end in W . We use
two standard facts: |δ(W )| has the parity of the number of odd-degree vertices in W , and
|δ(A ∩ B)| + |δ(A ∪ B)| ≤ |δ(A)| + |δ(B)|.
Theorem 22.1. Let G be a finite multigraph in which every connected component has at
most four odd-degree vertices. Then
   (1) any two nowhere-zero Z5 -flows of G are joined by cycle steps, that is, M5 (G) is
       connected;
   (2) the integer flow reconfiguration graph I5 (G) is connected.
Scope. The statement is for the modulus five only; every modulus k ≥ 6 is treated in
Theorem 29.1.
   The modulus cannot be lowered for this class, in either part of the theorem: K4 has four
odd-degree vertices, its Z4 -flow reconfiguration graph is disconnected, and its integer 4-flow
reconfiguration graph has three components [3, Section 2.4]. On the other side, [3, Theorem
3.4] gives a graph with a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow that is an isolated vertex of its reconfiguration
graph; as stated there, M5 and I5 of that graph are disconnected. By Theorem 22.1, every
graph G for which M5 (G) or I5 (G) is disconnected has a connected component with at least
six odd-degree vertices.
   The class with at most two odd-degree vertices per component is treated, for every modulus
k ≥ 4, in Theorem 10.1; the present proof uses that result at k = 5 and three lemmas of
the sections that prove it, which we restate in Section 23 without repeating their proofs.
Everything else is proved here, apart from standard facts: a connected graph with all degrees
even has an Eulerian orientation; a set of arcs with as many arcs leaving as entering at every
vertex is a union of edge-disjoint directed cycles; an integer circulation is a sum of signed
unit cycle vectors whose signs agree with it on every edge; a connected graph with n vertices
and m edges has exactly 5m−n+1 circulations over Z5 ; and a Cartesian product of connected
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          51


graphs is connected. The short proofs for the two base graphs of Section 26 are included to
keep the argument complete. The uncrossing argument in Lemma 27.3 is standard.
   At modulus five every nonzero coefficient is 1, −1, 2 or −2. Thus every step between
Z5 -flows is a restricted step in the sense of the section with Theorem 10.1.

            23. Tools from the sections on two odd-degree vertices
   For an incident end of a nonloop edge at v, its outward value is the value read away from
v. Two different edges incident with the same vertex v form a balanced pair at v if their
outward values sum to zero. The positive orientation of an integer nowhere-zero flow directs
each edge in the direction of its positive value. For an orientation D, 1D is its unit edge map;
div 1D is outdegree minus indegree.
   The following three lemmas are restated from the sections that prove Theorem 10.1. The
first is Lemma 11.1 together with its proof and the paragraph after it: the explicit map of
part (1), over Zk and over the integers, is in that proof; part (4) is that paragraph, and the
magnitudes are kept because the map copies the value of g to the two edges and changes no
other value. The other two are Lemma 13.1 and Lemma 15.1. Their statements and proofs
there do not restrict the number of odd-degree vertices.
Lemma 23.1 (Splitting). Let G have no loop and let uv, vw be distinct edges, where u = w
is allowed. Replace them by a new edge g = uw, retaining v as a vertex, to obtain G′ .
    (1) Put the value of g, read from u to w, on both edges along u → v → w and keep all
        other values. This is a bijection from the flows of G′ to the flows of G in which the
        chosen pair at v is balanced, over Zk and over the integers.
    (2) A cycle step in G′ lifts to a sequence of cycle steps in G with the same coefficient.
    (3) The operation preserves every vertex degree parity and reduces the number of edges by
        one.
    (4) For integer flows the bijection keeps all magnitudes and, at every vertex, the divergence
        of the unit map of the positive orientation.
Lemma 23.2 (Two local moves). Fix k ≥ 4; a step between Zk -flows is restricted if its
coefficient is 1, −1, 2 or −2 modulo k. Call an edge small if its value is 1 or −1, and let f
be a nowhere-zero Zk -flow.
    (1) If its non-small edges contain a cycle, restricted steps can increase the number of
         small edges without changing any previously small edge.
    (2) Suppose G is loopless, f has no balanced pair, and Q is a cycle of small edges. At a
         vertex v of Q its two outward cycle values are equal, say a ∈ {1, −1}. If an end at v
         not on Q has value c ̸= −2a, restricted steps along Q reach a flow with a balanced
         pair at v.
Lemma 23.3 (Lifting). For every finite multigraph and every k ≥ 2, every nowhere-zero
Zk -flow has an integer k-lift, and its integer lifts induce a connected subgraph of Ik (G). For
k ≥ 3, every legal modular step of size 1, −1, 2, −2 has adjacent integer lifts at its endpoints.
  We also use Theorem 10.1 at k = 5: if every component of G has at most two odd-degree
vertices, then M5 (G) is connected.
52                                         DAVE GOEL


                            24. Three facts at modulus five
  From here on all flows are nowhere-zero Z5 -flows. An edge is small if its value is 1 or −1
and large if its value is 2 or −2; every edge is one or the other. For a ̸= 0 the values a and
−a are in one of the two classes and 2a, −2a are in the other.
Lemma 24.1. Let G be loopless, let f be a flow and let v be a vertex.
  (1) If v has degree three, its outward values are (a, a, −2a) for some a ̸= 0, and there is
      no balanced pair at v.
  (2) If v has degree four and no balanced pair, its outward values are (a, a, a, 2a) for some
      a ̸= 0.
  (3) In the situation of (2), let C be a cycle through v that uses two of the three ends of
      value a, with a direction of traversal. If no edge of C has the value −2a along C, the
      step +2a on C is legal and its result has a balanced pair at v. If no edge of C has the
      value 2a along C, the same holds for the step −2a.
Proof. If no two outward values at v are opposite, they all lie in a set {s, t} with s ∈ {1, −1}
and t ∈ {2, −2}; so t = 2s or t = −2s.
   (1) Two opposite values among three would force the third to be zero. So there is no
balanced pair, two of the three values are equal, say to a, and the third is −2a by conservation.
   (2) Let i ∈ {0, . . . , 4} ends have the value s and 4 − i the value t. If t = 2s the sum is
(8 − i)s, which vanishes modulo five only for i = 3: the values are (s, s, s, 2s). If t = −2s the
sum is (3i − 8)s, which vanishes only for i = 1: the values are (s, t, t, t), and s = 2t because
2t = −4s = s.
   (3) Let C enter v by the end 1 and leave by the end 2, both of value a, and let the end 3
be the third end of value a; the three ends belong to three different edges. After the step
t on C the outward values at the ends 1, 2, 3 are a − t, a + t, a. For t = 2a the first is −a;
for t = −2a the second is −a; in both cases it is balanced with the end 3. A step t is legal
exactly when no edge of C has the value −t along C.                                            □
Corollary 24.2. Let G be loopless and let f be a flow such that no flow joined to f has a
balanced pair. Let g be joined to f , and let v have degree four, with outward values (a, a, a, 2a)
in g. Then every cycle through v that uses two of the three ends of value a contains at least
two edges of the size class to which a does not belong.
Proof. Fix a direction of the cycle. The result of a legal step on it is joined to f , so it has no
balanced pair. By Lemma 24.1(3) neither of the two steps is legal: one edge of the cycle has
the value −2a along it and another edge has the value 2a. Both values lie in the other size
class.                                                                                           □

                                25. The local proposition
Proposition 25.1. Let G be connected and loopless, with all degrees at least three and exactly
four odd-degree vertices. Suppose that G is not a cubic graph on four vertices. Then every
flow of G is joined to a flow with a balanced pair.
Proof. Suppose that f is a flow such that no flow joined to f has a balanced pair. Among
the finitely many flows joined to f choose g with the largest number of small edges. Let n
be the number of vertices, and let S and N be the spanning subgraphs of G formed by the
small and by the large edges of g. Since G has a flow it has no bridge.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        53


   Step 1: structure. By Lemma 23.2(1) and the choice of g, the graph N is a forest. Let Q
be a cycle of S and v a vertex of Q, with outward cycle values a, a, where a ∈ {1, −1}. By
Lemma 23.2(2) every other end at v has the value −2a, which is large. So every vertex on a
cycle of S has exactly two small ends. Hence a component of S that contains a cycle is that
cycle, and every component of S is a tree (possibly a single vertex) or a cycle. Moreover, a
vertex of degree d on a cycle of S has outward values a, a, −2a, . . . , −2a with sum 2a(3 − d),
so
(3)                 d≡3     (mod 5)       for every vertex on a cycle of S.
  Step 2: the count. Let s be the number of tree components of S and c ≥ 1 the number of
components of N . Then |E(S)| = n − s and |E(N )| = n − c. The four odd-degree vertices
have degree at least three and all others have degree at least four, so
(4)                         4n − 4 ≤ 2|E(G)| = 4n − 2s − 2c.
Hence s + c ≤ 2, and so s ≤ 1.
   Let s = 0. Then every vertex lies on a cycle of S, and by (3) every even degree is at
least eight and every odd degree is three or at least thirteen. By (4) the degree sum is
at most 4n − 2. If n ≥ 5 there is a vertex of even degree and the degree sum is at least
12 + 8(n − 4) ≥ 4n. If n = 4 and some degree is not three, the degree sum is at least
9 + 13 > 14. Both are impossible. So n = 4 and all degrees are three, which is excluded by
hypothesis.
   So s = c = 1 and (4) holds with equality. Therefore: the four odd-degree vertices have
degree exactly three, every other vertex has degree exactly four, N is a spanning tree, and S
has exactly one tree component T , its other components being cycles. Since G is not cubic
on four vertices, n ≥ 5 and there is a vertex of degree four. By Lemma 24.1, in g every vertex
of degree three has the values (a, a, −2a) and every vertex of degree four has the values
(a, a, a, 2a).
   Step 3: no vertex of degree four has three large ends. Suppose v has the values (a, a, a, 2a)
with a ∈ {2, −2}. Then v has degree three in the tree N , and N − v has exactly three
components A1 , A2 , A3 , which partition V (G) \ {v}; the part Ai is joined to v in N by the
ith end of value a. Suppose a small edge xy has x ∈ Ai , y ∈ Aj , i ̸= j. The paths of N from
x to v and from y to v meet only in v, and with xy they form a cycle through the ith and
jth ends of value a whose only small edge is xy. This contradicts Corollary 24.2. So no small
edge joins two different parts, and no large edge does, because all large edges lie in N . The
fourth edge at v ends in one part, say Al . For j ̸= l the only edge of G between Aj and the
rest is the edge of N from Aj to v: a bridge, a contradiction.
   Step 4: S is a spanning tree. By Step 3 and Lemma 24.1(2), every vertex of degree four
has three small ends and one large end. A vertex of degree three with values (a, a, −2a) has
one large end if a ∈ {1, −1} and two if a ∈ {2, −2}. Let q be the number of vertices of degree
three of the second kind. Counting the ends of the edges of N gives
                         2(n − 1) = (n − 4) + (4 − q) + 2q = n + q,
so q = n − 2. Since q ≤ 4 we get n ≤ 6, and the number of vertices of degree three with
two small ends is 4 − q = 6 − n ≤ 1. A cycle component of S has at least two vertices,
because G is loopless, and each of them has exactly two small ends (Step 1). Vertices of
degree four have three small ends, so there are not enough such vertices. Hence S has no
cycle component, and S = T is a spanning tree.
54                                         DAVE GOEL


   Step 5: contradiction. Let v have degree four. Its values are (a, a, a, 2a) with a ∈ {1, −1},
so v has degree three in the tree T , and T − v has exactly three components B1 , B2 , B3 ,
which partition V (G) \ {v}. A large edge between two different parts would close, with two
paths of T , a cycle through two ends of value a at v whose only large edge it is, contradicting
Corollary 24.2. No small edge joins two different parts, because all small edges lie in T . The
large edge at v ends in one part Bl . For j ̸= l the only edge of G between Bj and the rest is
the edge of T from Bj to v: a bridge. This contradiction proves the proposition.              □

                                26. The two base graphs
Lemma 26.1. A connected loopless cubic graph on four vertices is K4 or the graph D
obtained from a cycle of length four by doubling two opposite edges.
Proof. If the graph is simple it is K4 . A triple edge would be a component. So let xy be a
double edge; the third edges at x and at y go to vertices z and w outside {x, y}. If z = w,
the fourth vertex could only be adjacent to z, by three parallel edges, but z has one free end.
So z ̸= w, and the two remaining ends at each of z, w form a double edge zw.                 □
Lemma 26.2. For B = K4 and for B = D the graph M5 (B) is connected.
Proof. In each case we name four directed cycles of B, called faces, such that every edge lies
on exactly two faces and is traversed by them in opposite directions. Call two faces adjacent
if they share an edge.
    For K4 on {1, 2, 3, 4} the faces are the directed triangles 1 → 2 → 3 → 1, 1 → 4 → 2 → 1,
1 → 3 → 4 → 1 and 2 → 4 → 3 → 2. Any two of them are adjacent.
    For D let the vertices be x, z, w, y, with single edges p = xz and r = wy, parallel edges
e1 , e2 between x and y, and parallel edges g1 , g2 between z and w. The faces are
                      p    g1    1r    e                            e
                                                                    1    e
                                                                         2
                I:x−
                   → z −→ w −
                            → y −→ x,                       ∆1 : x −→ y −→ x,
                      e2    r     g2   p                           g2     g1
                O : x −→ y−
                          → w −→ z −
                                   → x,                     ∆2 : z −→ w −→ z.
The adjacent pairs are IO, I∆1 , ∆1 O, I∆2 , O∆2 ; the faces ∆1 and ∆P    2 are not adjacent.
   In both cases, for a map c from the four faces to Z5 put ∂c =              C c(C)χC . It is a
circulation, and its value on an edge is c(C) − c(C ′ ), where C is the face that traverses the
edge along its reference direction and C ′ the other one. If ∂c = 0 then c is constant, because
the adjacency relation is connected. So ∂ takes 53 values. A connected graph with four
vertices and six edges has exactly 56−4+1 = 53 circulations over Z5 : the values on the three
edges outside a spanning tree are free, and they fix the values on the tree. Hence every
circulation is ∂c for some c, and ∂c is a flow exactly when c is proper : adjacent faces get
different values. If c and c′ are proper and differ on one face C only, then ∂c′ = ∂c + tχC
with t ̸= 0: one cycle step. It therefore suffices to show that any two proper maps c, c′ are
joined by changes of one face at a time, through proper maps. Below, a change that would
change nothing is left out.
   K4 : the proper maps are the injective maps, and at each moment exactly one value is free,
that is, not used. Any face may move to the free value. Take the faces in some order. To
bring a face to its value under c′ : if that value is used by another face, first move that face
to the free value. The face moved away is not one that has already been brought to its value
under c′ , because c′ is injective.
   D: a map c is proper exactly when c(I) ̸= c(O) and c(∆1 ), c(∆2 ) lie outside {c(I), c(O)}.
We first note: if β ̸= c(O), then I can be brought to the value β. Indeed, choose γ outside
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        55


{c(I), c(O), β}, move ∆1 to γ, move ∆2 to γ, and then move I to β; every intermediate map
is proper. The same holds with I and O exchanged. Now, if c(O) = c′ (I), first bring O to a
value outside {c(I), c′ (I)}. Then bring I to c′ (I), then O to c′ (O), which is different from
c′ (I), and finally move ∆1 to c′ (∆1 ) and ∆2 to c′ (∆2 ).                                   □

                      27. Reference flows and their patterns
  Throughout this section H is a bridgeless multigraph with exactly four odd-degree vertices;
X denotes the set of these four vertices. Loops and several components are allowed. Every
component of H contains an even number of vertices of X.
Definition 27.1. A pattern is a two-element subset P of X. Its two vertices are the plus
vertices, the other two vertices of X are the minus vertices, and πP is the function on V (H)
that is +1 at plus vertices, −1 at minus vertices and 0 elsewhere. An orientation D of
H has the pattern P if div 1D = πP . A 4-anchor of pattern P is an integer flow F with
1 ≤ |F (e)| ≤ 3 on every edge whose positive orientation has the pattern P .
   The pattern P is feasible for H if
    (1) every component of H contains as many plus as minus vertices, and
    (2) there is no vertex set W with |δ(W )| = 2 that contains both minus vertices and no
         plus vertex.
Let Φ(H) be the set of patterns that are feasible for H.
  Condition (2) is unchanged if plus and minus are exchanged, since δ(W ) = δ(V (H) \ W ).
The anchors of Section 12 are integer 3-flows; a 4-anchor is an integer 4-flow.
Lemma 27.2. Every feasible pattern of H has a 4-anchor.
Proof. Let P be feasible. We first find an orientation D of H with the pattern P , one
component K at a time. If K has no vertex of X, take an Eulerian orientation. If K has
two vertices of X, they are a plus vertex p and a minus vertex m by condition (1): add an
auxiliary edge mp, take an Eulerian orientation in which this edge is directed from m to p
(reverse all arcs if necessary), and delete the auxiliary edge.
   Let K contain all four vertices: plus vertices p1 , p2 and minus vertices m1 , m2 . Add
auxiliary edges a1 = m1 p1 and a2 = m2 p2 . The result is connected with all degrees even;
take an Eulerian orientation of it. The right direction of ai is from mi to pi . Reversing all
arcs if necessary, a1 has its right direction. Suppose that a2 does not, so that it is directed
from y = p2 to z = m2 . Let W be the set of vertices that can be reached from z by directed
paths not using a1 . If y ∈ W , take such a path from z to y with no repeated vertex; it does
not use a2 , which ends in z. This path and the arc a2 form a directed cycle that avoids a1 ;
reversing it keeps the orientation Eulerian and puts a2 right. If y ∈ / W , the only arc that
can leave W is a1 , and a2 enters W . In an Eulerian orientation as many arcs enter W as
leave it, so a1 and a2 are the only edges between W and its complement. Then no edge of
K joins W , which contains z, to its complement in V (K), which contains y, although K is
connected. So both auxiliary edges can be given their right directions. Delete them.
   In each case the resulting orientation D of K has div 1D = πP on V (K): at pi an entering
auxiliary arc was deleted, at mi a leaving one.
   Next, D is strongly connected on every component K. Otherwise there is a nonempty
proper subset W of V (K) that no arc of D leaves. Summing div 1D over W gives
            #{plus vertices in W } − #{minus vertices in W } = −|δ(W )| ≤ −2,
56                                        DAVE GOEL


because K is connected and bridgeless. The left side is at least −2, with equality only if W
contains both minus vertices and no plus vertex. Then |δ(W )| = 2, against condition (2).
   Finally define F on each component: F = 1D if K has no vertex of X; F = 1D + χR for
a directed path R of D from m to p if it has two; F = 1D + χR1 + χR2 for directed paths Ri
of D from mi to pi if it has four. The divergence of χRi is +1 at mi and −1 at pi , so F is
a circulation. Its values in the directions of D are 1, 2 or 3. So F is a 4-anchor of pattern
P.                                                                                         □
Lemma 27.3. The set Φ(H) is either the set of all six patterns, or it consists of the four
patterns that take one vertex from each pair of one partition of X into two pairs. In particular
|Φ(H)| ≥ 4.
Proof. Case 1: no component contains all of X. Then two components K1 , K2 contain
two vertices of X each, say {x1 , x2 } and {x3 , x4 }. Condition (1) holds exactly for the four
patterns that take one of x1 , x2 and one of x3 , x4 . For such a pattern condition (2) holds as
well: a set W as in (2) would contain exactly one odd-degree vertex of K1 and exactly one
of K2 , so δ(W ) would have an odd number of edges in K1 and an odd number in K2 , that is,
exactly one in each. That edge would be a bridge.
   Case 2: one component contains all of X. Then condition (1) holds for every pattern. Call
a partition of X into two pairs bad if some W with |δ(W )| = 2 meets X in exactly one of the
two pairs. A pattern P fails condition (2) exactly when {P, X \ P } is bad. We show that at
most one partition is bad; this gives the claim. Suppose two different partitions are bad. After
renaming and passing to complements there are sets W1 , W2 with |δ(W1 )| = |δ(W2 )| = 2,
W1 ∩ X = {x1 , x2 } and W2 ∩ X = {x1 , x3 }. Then W1 ∩ W2 contains one vertex of X and
W1 ∪ W2 contains three. So |δ(W1 ∩ W2 )| and |δ(W1 ∪ W2 )| are odd, and they are not 1
because H is bridgeless. Their sum is at least 6, but it is at most |δ(W1 )| + |δ(W2 )| = 4. A
contradiction.                                                                                □
Corollary 27.4. If H1 and H2 are bridgeless multigraphs with the same set X of four
odd-degree vertices, then Φ(H1 ) ∩ Φ(H2 ) ̸= ∅.
Proof. Each of the two sets lacks at most two of the six patterns.                            □
Lemma 27.5. Let G be a finite multigraph and let F, F ′ be integer flows of G with all
magnitudes in {1, 2, 3} whose positive orientations D, D′ satisfy div 1D = div 1D′ . Then the
reductions of F and F ′ modulo five are joined by cycle steps.
Proof. This is the argument of Lemma 12.2, written out for magnitudes up to three. Let R
be the set of arcs of D that are reversed in D′ . Then 1D − 1D′ is twice the unit map of R,
so at every vertex as many arcs of R leave as enter. Hence R is the union of edge-disjoint
directed cycles C1 , . . . , Cr of D.
   Reverse these cycles one at a time, starting from F . Let C be the next cycle; the current
values along C are in {1, 2, 3}. Subtract bχC , where b is one plus the largest current value on
C, so b ∈ {2, 3, 4}. The new values along C are in [1 − b, −1], hence in {−3, −2, −1}. This is
an integer flow with all magnitudes in {1, 2, 3} whose positive orientation differs from the
previous one exactly on C. Modulo five it is one cycle step with the nonzero coefficient −b
between two flows. The later cycles are untouched.
   After the last reversal we have an integer flow F ′′ with magnitudes in {1, 2, 3} and positive
orientation D′ . The integer circulation F ′ − F ′′ is a sum of signed unit cycle vectors whose
signs agree with it on every edge. Add them one at a time. Every coordinate moves
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          57


monotonically from its value in F ′′ to its value in F ′ , so in the directions of D′ it stays in
{1, 2, 3}. Each addition is a cycle step with coefficient 1 modulo five.                       □

                                28. Proof of the theorem
Proof of Theorem 22.1(1). Let C be the class of all finite multigraphs in which every con-
nected component has at most four odd-degree vertices. We prove that M5 (G) is connected
for every G ∈ C by induction on the number of edges.
   Reductions. A graph without edges has exactly one flow. If G has a bridge, in particular a
vertex of degree one, it has no flow. If G has a loop ℓ, then G − ℓ ∈ C; the value on ℓ is an
independent coordinate, steps on the cycle ℓ with coefficient 1 join all its nonzero values,
and the claim follows from the induction hypothesis. So let G be loopless. If v has degree
two, its two edges are balanced in every flow; by Lemma 23.1 the flows of G correspond
to the flows of the split graph G′ , which has one edge fewer, and steps of G′ lift. Splitting
keeps all degree parities and every component of G′ lies inside a component of G; so G′ ∈ C,
and the claim follows by induction. Isolated vertices can be deleted. If G has at least two
components with edges, each of them is in C and has fewer edges; M5 (G) is the Cartesian
product of the graphs M5 of the components, since every cycle lies in one component; a
product of connected graphs is connected, and it is empty if a factor is empty. If G has at
most two odd-degree vertices, the claim is Theorem 10.1.
   The main case. So let G be connected and loopless, with all degrees at least three, exactly
four odd-degree vertices, forming the set X, and at least one flow; then G is bridgeless. If G
is cubic on four vertices, the claim is Lemmas 26.1 and 26.2. Suppose it is not.
   Call family every nonempty set B that consists of all flows of G in which one given pair
of edges at one given vertex is balanced. Let G′ be the corresponding split graph. By
Lemma 23.1, B corresponds to the set of all flows of G′ ; the graph G′ has one edge fewer,
the same four odd-degree vertices, and every component of it lies inside G, so G′ ∈ C. By
induction any two flows of G′ are joined, and the steps lift. So
   (i) any two flows of one family are joined in G.
Since G′ has a flow, it is bridgeless, and Section 27 applies to H = G′ . Let P ∈ Φ(G′ ). By
Lemma 27.2, G′ has a 4-anchor of pattern P . By Lemma 23.1(1),(4) it corresponds to an
integer flow of G with the same magnitudes whose positive orientation has the same unit
divergence at every vertex: a 4-anchor of G of pattern P . The correspondence commutes
with reduction modulo five. So
   (ii) for every P ∈ Φ(G′ ) the family B contains the reduction of a 4-anchor of G of pattern
P.
Let B1 , B2 be two families, with split graphs G′1 , G′2 . By Corollary 27.4 there is a pattern
P ∈ Φ(G′1 ) ∩ Φ(G′2 ). By (ii) each Bi contains the reduction of a 4-anchor of G of pattern
P ; the two anchors have the same unit divergence πP , so their reductions are joined by
Lemma 27.5. With (i), all flows of B1 ∪ B2 are joined. Hence all flows of G that have a
balanced pair are joined. By Proposition 25.1 every flow of G is joined to a flow with a
balanced pair. So M5 (G) is connected.                                                       □
Proof of Theorem 22.1(2). Let F, F ′ be integer 5-flows of G. By part (1) their reductions
are joined by a sequence of cycle steps, and every coefficient is 1, −1, 2 or −2 modulo five.
By Lemma 23.3 with k = 5 each of these steps has adjacent integer lifts at its endpoints,
and any two integer lifts of one flow are joined in I5 (G). Going along the sequence joins F
to F ′ .                                                                                   □
58                                        DAVE GOEL


29. Four odd-degree vertices at every modulus at least six: statement and
                                 conventions
   All graphs are finite multigraphs, with loops and parallel edges allowed. A loop contributes
two to the degree. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph, including a loop or a pair of
parallel edges. Fix a reference direction for each edge. Divergence is the outgoing sum minus
the incoming sum; a loop contributes zero. A circulation has zero divergence. For a cycle C
with a direction of traversal, χC has value 1 on forward edges, −1 on backward edges, and 0
elsewhere. For a directed path P , define χP in the same way.
   An integer k-flow is an integer circulation F with 1 ≤ |F (e)| ≤ k − 1 on every edge;
an integer flow is a nowhere-zero integer circulation, that is, an integer k-flow for some
k. A nowhere-zero Zk -flow is a circulation with nonzero values in Zk ; we say flow for
short. The reconfiguration graphs Ik (G) and Mk (G) have these flows as vertices, with a step
F 7→ F + tχC , or f 7→ f + tχC , whenever the coefficient t is nonzero and both endpoints
are flows of the indicated kind. On loopless graphs this is the adjacency of [3, 2]; counting
a loop as a cycle is the usual extension to graphs with loops. A step between Zk -flows is
restricted if its coefficient is 1, −1, 2 or −2 modulo k, as in the section with Theorem 10.1.
   In this section and in the six sections after it two Zk -flows are called joined if a finite,
possibly empty, sequence of restricted steps leads from one to the other. So every flow is
joined to itself. This is stronger than being in one component of Mk (G). A reconfiguration
graph with no vertices, or with one vertex, is regarded as connected. If G has a bridge, every
circulation vanishes there, so G has no flow.
Theorem 29.1. Let G be a finite multigraph in which every connected component has at
most four odd-degree vertices, and let k ≥ 6 be an integer. Then
   (1) any two nowhere-zero Zk -flows of G are joined by restricted steps; in particular Mk (G)
       is connected;
   (2) the integer flow reconfiguration graph Ik (G) is connected.
Corollary 29.2. Let G be as in Theorem 29.1. For every integer k ≥ 5 the graphs Mk (G)
and Ik (G) are connected.
Proof. For k = 5 this is Theorem 22.1; for k ≥ 6 it is Theorem 29.1.                         □
Scope. The theorem is for cyclic groups and integer flows, for every modulus k ≥ 6.
   In Corollary 29.2 the bound five cannot be lowered, for either of the two graphs: K4 has
four odd-degree vertices, its Z4 -flow reconfiguration graph is disconnected, and its integer
4-flow reconfiguration graph has three components [3, Section 2.4]. The class with at most
two odd-degree vertices per component is treated, for every modulus k ≥ 4, in Theorem 10.1;
the modulus five for the present class is treated in Theorem 22.1.
   The proof follows the plan of the proof of Theorem 22.1. We use lemmas of the sections
that prove Theorem 10.1 and of those that prove Theorem 22.1, restated in Section 30
without their proofs. The three lemmas taken from the former carry the bounds k ≥ 4, k ≥ 2
and k ≥ 3, which all hold for k ≥ 6; the statements taken from the latter involve no modulus.
Everything else is proved in this text, apart from standard facts: a set of arcs with as many
arcs leaving as entering at every vertex is a union of edge-disjoint directed cycles; an integer
circulation is a sum of signed unit cycle vectors whose signs agree with it on every edge;
the values of a circulation on the edges outside a spanning tree fix its values on the tree;
and exchanges of neighbours lead from any cyclic order of finitely many labels to any other.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          59


Section 33 gives a complete proof for its two base graphs. The lemma on tokens there is an
elementary tool.

                            30. Tools from earlier sections
   For an incident end of a nonloop edge at v, its outward value is the value read away from
v. Two different edges incident with the same vertex v form a balanced pair at v if their
outward values sum to zero. The positive orientation of an integer nowhere-zero flow directs
each edge in the direction of its positive value. For an orientation D, 1D is its unit edge
map; div 1D is outdegree minus indegree. For a vertex set W , δ(W ) is the set of edges with
exactly one end in W .
From the sections on two odd-degree vertices. The following three lemmas are restated
from the sections that prove Theorem 10.1. The first is Lemma 11.1 together with its proof
and the paragraph after it: the explicit map of part (1), over Zk and over the integers, is in
that proof; part (4) is that paragraph, and the magnitudes are kept because the map copies
the value of g to the two edges and changes no other value. The other two are Lemma 13.1
and Lemma 15.1. Their statements and proofs there do not restrict the number of odd-degree
vertices.
Lemma 30.1 (Splitting). Let G have no loop and let uv, vw be distinct edges, where u = w
is allowed. Replace them by a new edge g = uw, retaining v as a vertex, to obtain G′ .
    (1) Put the value of g, read from u to w, on both edges along u → v → w and keep all
        other values. This is a bijection from the flows of G′ to the flows of G in which the
        chosen pair at v is balanced, over Zk and over the integers.
    (2) A cycle step in G′ lifts to a sequence of cycle steps in G with the same coefficient.
    (3) The operation preserves every vertex degree parity and reduces the number of edges by
        one.
    (4) For integer flows the bijection keeps all magnitudes and, at every vertex, the divergence
        of the unit map of the positive orientation.
Lemma 30.2 (Two local moves). Fix k ≥ 4, call an edge small if its value is 1 or −1, and
let f be a nowhere-zero Zk -flow.
     (1) If its non-small edges contain a cycle, restricted steps can increase the number of
         small edges without changing any previously small edge.
     (2) Suppose G is loopless, f has no balanced pair, and Q is a cycle of small edges. At a
         vertex v of Q its two outward cycle values are equal, say a ∈ {1, −1}. If an end at v
         not on Q has value c ̸= −2a, restricted steps along Q reach a flow with a balanced
         pair at v.
Lemma 30.3 (Lifting). For every finite multigraph and every k ≥ 2, every nowhere-zero
Zk -flow has an integer k-lift, and its integer lifts induce a connected subgraph of Ik (G). For
k ≥ 3, every legal modular step of size 1, −1, 2, −2 has adjacent integer lifts at its endpoints.
  We also use Theorem 10.1, its item (1) together with the sentence after its list: if k ≥ 4
and every component of G has at most two odd-degree vertices, then any two nowhere-zero
Zk -flows of G are joined by restricted steps.
60                                        DAVE GOEL


From the sections on the modulus five. The following statements are Lemma 26.1, Defi-
nition 27.1, Lemma 27.2 and Corollary 27.4; the proof of Corollary 27.4 rests on Lemma 27.3.
They are statements on graphs, on orientations and on integer flows; no modulus occurs in
them or in their proofs.
Lemma 30.4. A connected loopless cubic graph on four vertices is K4 or the graph D
obtained from a cycle of length four by doubling two opposite edges.
Definition 30.5. Let H be a bridgeless multigraph with exactly four odd-degree vertices,
forming the set X. A pattern is a two-element subset P of X. Its two vertices are the plus
vertices, the other two vertices of X are the minus vertices, and πP is the function on V (H)
that is +1 at plus vertices, −1 at minus vertices and 0 elsewhere. A 4-anchor of pattern P
is an integer flow F of H with 1 ≤ |F (e)| ≤ 3 on every edge whose positive orientation D
satisfies div 1D = πP . The pattern P is feasible for H if
    (1) every component of H contains as many plus as minus vertices, and
    (2) there is no vertex set W with |δ(W )| = 2 that contains both minus vertices and no
         plus vertex.
Let Φ(H) be the set of patterns that are feasible for H.
Lemma 30.6. Let H be as in Definition 30.5.
  (1) Every feasible pattern of H has a 4-anchor.
  (2) If H1 and H2 are bridgeless multigraphs with the same set X of four odd-degree
      vertices, then Φ(H1 ) ∩ Φ(H2 ) ̸= ∅.

                      31. A cycle with exactly one large edge
  Fix k ≥ 4. In a nowhere-zero Zk -flow an edge is small if its value is 1 or −1, and large
otherwise. Lemma 30.2(2) is about a cycle with no large edge. The next lemma is about a
cycle with exactly one.
Lemma 31.1. Let k ≥ 4, let G be loopless and let f be a nowhere-zero Zk -flow with no
balanced pair. Let C be a cycle in which exactly one edge h is large. Let v be an end of h,
and let v have degree four. Let e be the other edge of C at v, with outward value a ∈ {1, −1}
at v, and let c1 , c2 be the outward values at v of the two edges at v that are not on C.
    (1) If c1 and c2 are not both equal to −2a, then restricted steps along C lead from f to a
        flow with a balanced pair at v.
    (2) If c1 = c2 = −2a, the outward value of h at v is 3a: the outward values at v are
        (a, 3a, −2a, −2a).
Proof. Since G is loopless, the four ends at v belong to four different edges, and e ̸= h. Let
y be the outward value of h at v. Conservation at v reads a + y + c1 + c2 = 0; this gives (2).
  (1) The map f 7→ −f takes restricted steps to restricted steps and balanced pairs to
balanced pairs; so let a = 1. Let c be one of c1 , c2 with c ̸= −2, let c′ be the other one, and
put t = −1 − c. Then
      • t ̸= 0, because the ends of values 1 and c are not balanced;
      • t ̸= 1, because c ̸= −2;
      • t ̸= −1, because c ̸= 0;
      • t ̸= y, because y − t = 1 + y + c = −c′ ̸= 0.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          61


Also y ̸= 0, 1, −1, because h is large. So t and y have representatives T and Y in the interval
[2, k − 2], and T ̸= Y .
   Direct C so that it leaves v by e and comes back to v by h. Along C the value of f is 1 on
e, it is 1 or −1 on every other small edge, and it is −y on h. Hence f + jχC is nowhere-zero
whenever j is not congruent to 1, to −1 or to Y modulo k.
   If Y > T : do one step with coefficient 2 on C and then T − 2 ≥ 0 steps with coefficient 1.
The total increments reached are 2, 3, . . . , T . They lie in [2, k − 2] and are smaller than Y .
   If Y < T : do one step with coefficient −2 on C and then k − 2 − T ≥ 0 steps with
coefficient −1. The total increments reached are congruent to k − 2, k − 3, . . . , T . They lie
in [2, k − 2] and are larger than Y .
   In both cases all flows on the way are nowhere-zero, all steps are restricted, and the last
flow is f + tχC . In it the outward value of e at v is 1 + t = −c, and the edge at v with
outward value c is not on C, so its value is unchanged. This is a balanced pair at v.           □
  For k = 4 the lemma is empty. In part (1) the interval [2, k − 2] has one element, so the
case c1 , c2 not both −2a does not occur; in part (2) the value 3a = −a of h would be small.

                                32. The local proposition
  From here on k ≥ 6 is fixed and all flows are nowhere-zero Zk -flows. For a ∈ {1, −1} we
use: the values 2a and 3a are large, 2 ̸= −2 and 4a ̸= 0, which hold already for k ≥ 5; and
5a ̸= 0, which is the only place where k ≥ 6 is needed.
Proposition 32.1. Let k ≥ 6. Let G be connected and loopless, with all degrees at least three
and exactly four odd-degree vertices. Suppose that G is not a cubic graph on four vertices.
Then every flow of G is joined to a flow with a balanced pair.
Proof. Suppose that f is a flow such that no flow joined to f has a balanced pair. Among
the finitely many flows joined to f choose g with the largest number of small edges. Every
flow joined to g is joined to f , so it has no balanced pair. Let n be the number of vertices,
and let S and N be the spanning subgraphs of G formed by the small and by the large edges
of g. All values below are values of g.
   Step 1: structure. By Lemma 30.2(1) and the choice of g, the graph N is a forest. Let
Q be a cycle of S and v a vertex of Q. By Lemma 30.2(2) the two outward cycle values at
v are equal; call this value ε(v) ∈ {1, −1}, the sign of v; and every other end at v has the
value −2ε(v), which is large. So every vertex on a cycle of S has exactly two small ends.
Hence a component of S that contains a cycle is that cycle, and every component of S is a
tree (possibly a single vertex) or a cycle. A vertex v of degree d on a cycle of S has outward
values ε(v), ε(v), −2ε(v), . . . , −2ε(v), with sum 2ε(v)(3 − d), so
(5)       2(d − 3) ≡ 0    (mod k)       for every vertex of degree d on a cycle of S.
As k ≥ 6, such a vertex has d = 3 or d ≥ 6. Finally, a small edge uv of Q reads ε(u) out of u
and ε(v) out of v, so ε(v) = −ε(u): the signs alternate along Q. Therefore
(6)       every cycle of S has even length, and half of its vertices have each sign.
  Step 2: the count. Let s be the number of tree components of S and c ≥ 1 the number of
components of N . Then |E(S)| = n − s and |E(N )| = n − c. The four odd-degree vertices
have degree at least three and all others have degree at least four, so
(7)                          4n − 4 ≤ 2|E(G)| = 4n − 2s − 2c.
62                                        DAVE GOEL


Hence s + c ≤ 2, and so s ≤ 1.
   Let s = 0. Then every vertex lies on a cycle of S, so by (5) every even degree is at least
six and every odd degree is three or at least seven. By (7) the degree sum is at most 4n − 2,
and it is at least 12 + 6(n − 4) = 6n − 12; so n ≤ 5. If n = 5, the cycles of S are disjoint and
cover all five vertices, against (6). If n = 4 and some degree is not three, the degree sum is
at least 9 + 7 > 14. So n = 4 and all degrees are three, which is excluded by hypothesis.
   So s = c = 1 and (7) holds with equality. Therefore: the four odd-degree vertices have
degree exactly three, every other vertex has degree exactly four, N is a spanning tree, and S
has exactly one tree component T , its other components being cycles. Since G is not cubic
on four vertices, n ≥ 5 and there is a vertex of degree four.
   Let Z be the set of the vertices on cycles of S; every vertex is in T or in Z. By (5) every
z ∈ Z has degree three, with values (ε(z), ε(z), −2ε(z)). So |Z| ≤ 4, and by (6) |Z| is even
and exactly |Z|/2 vertices of Z have each sign. The third edge at z is the only large edge at
z; so z is a leaf of the tree N . Its neighbour in N is not a leaf of N , because N is a tree on
n ≥ 5 vertices; so this neighbour is in T . We call this edge the Z-edge of z. At its end in T
its outward value is 2ε(z). Consequently
(8)    for each of the two values 2 and −2, exactly |Z|/2 ≤ 2 of the Z-edges have
       that outward value at their end in T .
A large edge that is not a Z-edge has both ends in T .
   Step 3: the vertices of degree four. Let v have degree four. Then v ∈
                                                                       / Z, so v ∈ T . We
claim:
(9) v is on at least two Z-edges, and all Z-edges at v have the same outward value at v.
First let h = vx be a large edge at v that is not a Z-edge. Then x ∈ T , x ̸= v, and h together
with the path of T from x to v is a cycle with exactly one large edge. No flow joined to g
has a balanced pair, so by Lemma 31.1 the outward values at v are
(10) (a, 3a, −2a, −2a),       with a ∈ {1, −1} on the edge of that path at v and 3a on h.
In particular, in this case v has exactly one small end. Now we go through the number of
small ends at v. Two small ends at v with different values would be balanced; so all small
ends at v have one value a ∈ {1, −1}.
   (i) Two or more small ends. Then every large edge at v is a Z-edge. Four small ends
would give 4a = 0, which is false. With three small ends the fourth value is −3a, and for a
Z-edge it is 2 or −2, that is, 2a or −2a; but −3a = 2a means 5a = 0 and −3a = −2a means
a = 0, and both are false. So v has exactly two small ends and is on two Z-edges.
   (ii) One small end. Suppose two of the three large edges at v, say h and h′ , are not Z-edges.
The path of T in (10) starts at v with the only small edge at v, both for h and for h′ . So (10)
for h gives the value 3a on h, and (10) for h′ gives the value −2a on h. Then 5a = 0, which
is false. So at least two of the three large edges at v are Z-edges.
   (iii) No small end. Then T is the single vertex v, and a large edge at v that is not a Z-edge
would be a loop. So v is on four Z-edges.
Two Z-edges at v with the outward values 2 and −2 would be a balanced pair. This proves (9).
   Step 4: the only case left. Let v have degree four. By (9) and (8) it is on exactly two
Z-edges, and they have one outward value at v; then |Z|/2 ≥ 2, so |Z| = 4. So Z is the
set of the four vertices of degree three, and T consists of all vertices of degree four. Two
different vertices of degree four use Z-edges of different values, by (8); so there are at most
two vertices of degree four.
                  THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                            63


    If there is one, T is a single vertex, and by Step 3(iii) it is on four Z-edges, against “exactly
two”. So there are two, v1 and v2 : n = 6, and T is one small edge v1 v2 . Each vi has exactly
one small end and is on exactly two Z-edges; its fourth edge is large and not a Z-edge, so
it goes to the other vertex of T . Thus there is exactly one large edge h between v1 and v2 ,
and by (10) the outward values at vi are (ai , 3ai , −2ai , −2ai ), where ai is the outward value
of the small edge v1 v2 at vi . So a2 = −a1 ; put a = a1 . The two Z-edges at v1 have the
outward value −2a there; this value is 2ε(z) for the other end z, and ε(z) = a would give
4a = 0, which is false for k ≥ 6; so the two neighbours of v1 in Z have the sign −a. In the
same way the two neighbours of v2 in Z have the sign a. Every vertex of Z is a neighbour of
exactly one of v1 , v2 by its Z-edge.
    Step 5: a balanced pair after all. Let z ′ be a neighbour of v1 in Z, and let z be a neighbour
of z ′ on its cycle of S. By (6), ε(z) = −ε(z ′ ) = a, so z is a neighbour of v2 . The four vertices
v1 , v2 , z, z ′ are different, and the edge h, the Z-edge of z, the small edge zz ′ and the Z-edge
of z ′ form a cycle C, which we direct as v1 → v2 → z → z ′ → v1 . Along C the values are:
3a on h; 2ε(z) = 2a on the Z-edge of z, read out of v2 ; ε(z) = a on zz ′ , read out of z; and
−2ε(z ′ ) = 2a on the Z-edge of z ′ , read out of z ′ . Do k − 4 steps with coefficient a on C.
After j of them, 1 ≤ j ≤ k − 4, the values along C are
                          (3 + j)a,    (2 + j)a,   (1 + j)a,    (2 + j)a,
and the integers 3 + j, 2 + j, 1 + j lie in [2, k − 1]; so all these flows are nowhere-zero, and the
steps are restricted. After the last step the Z-edge of z ′ has the value (k − 2)a = −2a along
C, that is, into v1 ; its outward value at v1 is 2a. The other Z-edge at v1 is not on C and still
has the outward value −2a. This is a balanced pair in a flow joined to f : a contradiction,
which proves the proposition.                                                                     □

                   33. The two base graphs with restricted steps
Lemma 33.1 (Tokens). Let m ≥ 2 and k ≥ m + 1. Put m labelled tokens on m different
elements of Zk , called sites. A move adds 1, −1, 2 or −2 to the site of one token, if the new
site is free. Then any two placements are joined by moves.
Proof. A move by 1 or −1 is a slide; slides do not change the cyclic order of the tokens.
   (A) Fix a placement and one of its tokens, X1 . Name the tokens X1 , . . . , Xm in their cyclic
order, and let p1 < p2 < · · · < pm < p1 + k be integers that represent their sites. Slide Xm
forward to p1 + k − 1, then Xm−1 forward to p1 + k − 2, and so on, down to X2 . Only free
sites are passed, and no token moves backward, because pj ≤ p1 + k − 1 − (m − j). Now the
tokens fill m sites in a row, in the order X2 , . . . , Xm , X1 . Since k ≥ m + 1, the site after X1
is free. Sliding X1 , then Xm , then Xm−1 , and so on, one site forward each, moves the whole
row forward by one. So the placement is joined to the row at q, for any site q: the placement
with X1 at q and Xj at q − 1 − (m − j) for 2 ≤ j ≤ m. The row at q depends only on q, on
the token X1 and on the cyclic order. Hence two placements with the same cyclic order are
joined.
   (B) In the row at q the site q + 1 is free. Let Xm , which is at q − 1, move to q + 1. In the
new placement the cyclic order is the old one with Xm and X1 exchanged. Here X1 was any
token and Xm is the token before it in the cyclic order. So, with (A) before and after such a
move, any two tokens that are neighbours in the cyclic order can be exchanged. Exchanges
of neighbours lead from any cyclic order to any other. With (A) again, any two placements
are joined. All moves can be reversed. For m = 2 there is only one cyclic order, and (A)
alone is enough.                                                                                  □
64                                          DAVE GOEL


Lemma 33.2. Let k ≥ 5 and let B be K4 or D. Any two nowhere-zero Zk -flows of B are
joined by restricted steps.
Proof. As in Lemma 26.2 we name four directed cycles of B, called faces, such that every
edge lies on exactly two faces and is traversed by them in opposite directions. Two faces are
adjacent if they share an edge.
    For K4 on {1, 2, 3, 4} the faces are the directed triangles 1 → 2 → 3 → 1, 1 → 4 → 2 → 1,
1 → 3 → 4 → 1 and 2 → 4 → 3 → 2. Any two of them are adjacent.
    For D let the vertices be x, z, w, y, with single edges p = xz and r = wy, parallel edges
e1 , e2 between x and y, and parallel edges g1 , g2 between z and w. The faces are
                      p    g1    1r    e                            e
                                                                    1    2e
                I:x−
                   → z −→ w −
                            → y −→ x,                       ∆1 : x −→ y −→ x,
                      e2    r     g2    p                           g2     g1
                O : x −→ y−
                          → w −→ z −
                                   → x,                     ∆2 : z −→ w −→ z.
The adjacent pairs are IO, I∆1 , ∆1 O, I∆2 , O∆2 ; the faces ∆P   1 and ∆2 are not adjacent.
   In both cases, for a map c from the four faces to Zk put ∂c = C c(C)χC . It is a circulation
over Zk , the map ∂ is additive, and the value of ∂c on an edge is c(C) − c(C ′ ), where C is
the face that traverses the edge along its reference direction and C ′ the other one. If ∂c = 0
then c is constant, because the adjacency relation is connected. So ∂ takes k 4 /k = k 3 values.
On the other side, fix a spanning tree of B; a circulation over Zk is determined by its values
on the three edges outside the tree, so there are at most k 3 circulations. (No division in Zk is
used; k need not be prime.) Hence every circulation is ∂c for some c, and ∂c is a flow exactly
when c is proper : adjacent faces get different values. If c and c′ are proper and differ on one
face C only, by t = c′ (C) − c(C), then ∂c′ = ∂c + tχC : one cycle step, and it is restricted if
t ∈ {1, −1, 2, −2}. So it suffices to show that any two proper maps are joined by changes of
one face at a time by 1, −1, 2 or −2, through proper maps. Regard a map c as a placement
of four tokens, the faces, on sites of Zk .
   K4 : the proper maps are the placements on four different sites, and the allowed changes
are the moves of Lemma 33.1 with m = 4. Since k ≥ 5, that lemma applies.
   D: a map c is proper exactly when c(I) ̸= c(O) and c(∆1 ), c(∆2 ) lie outside {c(I), c(O)};
the two tokens ∆1 , ∆2 may share a site. We first show that every proper map is joined to a
proper map with c(∆1 ) = c(∆2 ), by changes of ∆1 alone. The only sites closed to ∆1 are
c(I) and c(O). Suppose c(∆1 ) ̸= c(∆2 ). If c(I) and c(O) are neighbours in the cyclic order
of Zk , that is, differ by 1 or −1 modulo k, they lie together on one of the two arcs of the
cyclic order of Zk between c(∆1 ) and c(∆2 ), and ∆1 slides to c(∆2 ) along the other arc. If
they are not neighbours, let ∆1 go forward: it slides while the next site is not closed; when
the next site is closed, the site after that one is not closed, and ∆1 moves there by 2. The
closed sites are different from c(∆2 ), so ∆1 arrives at c(∆2 ).
   Now consider the proper maps with c(∆1 ) = c(∆2 ). Treat ∆1 , ∆2 as one token ∆; then
the proper maps of this kind are the placements of the three tokens I, O, ∆ on three different
sites. A move of I or of O in the sense of Lemma 33.1 is one allowed change. A move of ∆
to a free site is two allowed changes, of ∆1 and then of ∆2 , and the map between them is
proper. By Lemma 33.1 with m = 3 any two such maps are joined. Together with the first
part, any two proper maps are joined.                                                          □
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                         65


                              34. Anchors of one pattern
Lemma 34.1. Let k ≥ 5, let G be a finite multigraph and let F, F ′ be integer flows of G
with all magnitudes in {1, 2, 3} whose positive orientations D, D′ satisfy div 1D = div 1D′ .
Then the reductions of F and F ′ modulo k are joined by restricted steps.
Proof. We follow Lemma 27.5 and replace its one step of coefficient −b by unit steps. Let R
be the set of arcs of D that are reversed in D′ . Then 1D − 1D′ is twice the unit map of R,
so at every vertex as many arcs of R leave as enter. Hence R is the union of edge-disjoint
directed cycles C1 , . . . , Cr of D.
   Reverse these cycles one at a time, starting from F . Let C be the next cycle and F0 the
current integer flow; its values along C are in {1, 2, 3}. Let b be one plus the largest of them,
so b ∈ {2, 3, 4}, and put F1 = F0 − bχC . The values of F1 along C are in [1 − b, −1], hence
in {−3, −2, −1}. So F1 is an integer flow with all magnitudes in {1, 2, 3} whose positive
orientation differs from that of F0 exactly on C. Modulo k we go from F0 to F1 by k − b ≥ 1
steps with coefficient 1 on C: after j of them, 1 ≤ j ≤ k − b, the values along C are congruent
to F0 (e) + j, and
                              2 ≤ F0 (e) + j ≤ (b − 1) + (k − b) = k − 1,
so they are nonzero modulo k; all other values are those of F0 . After the last step we have
F0 + (k − b)χC ≡ F1 . The later cycles are untouched.
   After the last reversal we have an integer flow F ′′ with magnitudes in {1, 2, 3} and positive
orientation D′ . The integer circulation F ′ − F ′′ is a sum of signed unit cycle vectors whose
signs agree with it on every edge. Add them one at a time. Every coordinate moves
monotonically from its value in F ′′ to its value in F ′ , so in the directions of D′ it stays in
{1, 2, 3}, and it is nonzero modulo k. Each addition is a cycle step with coefficient 1 or
−1.                                                                                            □

                                35. Proof of the theorem
Proof of Theorem 29.1(1). Fix k ≥ 6. Let C be the class of all finite multigraphs in which
every connected component has at most four odd-degree vertices. We prove by induction on
the number of edges that any two flows of every G ∈ C are joined. Recall that joined means
joined by restricted steps.
   Reductions. A graph without edges has exactly one flow. If G has a bridge, in particular a
vertex of degree one, it has no flow. If G has a loop ℓ, then G − ℓ ∈ C; the value on ℓ is an
independent coordinate, steps on the cycle ℓ with coefficient 1 join all its nonzero values,
every step of G − ℓ is a step of G with the same coefficient, and the claim follows from the
induction hypothesis. So let G be loopless. If v has degree two, its two edges are balanced in
every flow; by Lemma 30.1 the flows of G correspond to the flows of the split graph G′ , which
has one edge fewer, and steps of G′ lift to steps of G with the same coefficients. Splitting
keeps all degree parities and every component of G′ lies inside a component of G; so G′ ∈ C,
and the claim follows by induction. Isolated vertices can be deleted. If G has at least two
components with edges, each of them is in C and has fewer edges; a flow of G is a choice
of a flow on each component, every cycle lies in one component, and the claim follows by
changing one component at a time (if a component has no flow, G has none). If G has at
most two odd-degree vertices, the claim is Theorem 10.1(1).
   The main case. So let G be connected and loopless, with all degrees at least three, exactly
four odd-degree vertices, forming the set X, and at least one flow; then G is bridgeless. If G
is cubic on four vertices, the claim is Lemmas 30.4 and 33.2. Suppose it is not.
66                                        DAVE GOEL


    Call family every nonempty set B that consists of all flows of G in which one given pair
of edges at one given vertex is balanced. Let G′ be the corresponding split graph. By
Lemma 30.1, B corresponds to the set of all flows of G′ ; the graph G′ has one edge fewer,
the same four odd-degree vertices, and every component of it lies inside G, so G′ ∈ C. By
induction any two flows of G′ are joined by restricted steps of G′ , and by Lemma 30.1(2)
these lift to restricted steps of G. So
   (i) any two flows of one family are joined in G.
Since G′ has a flow, it is bridgeless, and Definition 30.5 applies to H = G′ . Let P ∈ Φ(G′ ).
By Lemma 30.6(1), G′ has a 4-anchor of pattern P . By Lemma 30.1(1),(4) it corresponds to
an integer flow of G with the same magnitudes whose positive orientation has the same unit
divergence at every vertex: an integer flow of G with magnitudes in {1, 2, 3} whose positive
orientation has the unit divergence πP . We call it a 4-anchor of G of pattern P . Its reduction
modulo k is a flow, because 3 < k, and the correspondence commutes with reduction modulo
k. So
   (ii) for every P ∈ Φ(G′ ) the family B contains the reduction of a 4-anchor of G of pattern
P.
Let B1 , B2 be two families, with split graphs G′1 , G′2 . Both are bridgeless with the same set
X of four odd-degree vertices. By Lemma 30.6(2) there is a pattern P ∈ Φ(G′1 ) ∩ Φ(G′2 ). By
(ii) each Bi contains the reduction of a 4-anchor of G of pattern P ; the two anchors have
the same unit divergence πP , so their reductions are joined by Lemma 34.1. With (i), all
flows of B1 ∪ B2 are joined. Hence all flows of G that have a balanced pair are joined. By
Proposition 32.1 every flow of G is joined to a flow with a balanced pair. So any two flows of
G are joined.                                                                                  □
Proof of Theorem 29.1(2). Let F, F ′ be integer k-flows of G. By part (1) their reductions
are joined by a sequence of restricted steps. By Lemma 30.3 each of these steps has adjacent
integer lifts at its endpoints, and any two integer lifts of one flow are joined in Ik (G). Going
along the sequence joins F to F ′ .                                                            □

Part 4. Six vertices of odd degree
   This part proves part (c) of Theorem 1.3: nine sections for k = 5, then nine sections for
k ≥ 7, then six sections for k = 6. The sections for k ≥ 7 use Appendix A. For the integer
flows and k ≥ 6 this part uses theorems of [6].

  36. Six odd-degree vertices at modulus five: statement and conventions
   All graphs are finite multigraphs; our convention allows loops and parallel edges. A loop
contributes two to the degree. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph, including a loop or
two parallel edges. Fix reference directions on the edges. Divergence is the outgoing sum
minus the incoming sum, with loops contributing zero. A circulation has divergence zero.
For a directed traversal of a cycle or path C, its signed edge vector χC has entries 1, −1, 0.
   A nowhere-zero Z5 -flow is a circulation with nonzero values in Z5 . Its reconfiguration
graph M5 (G) has these flows as vertices, with a move f 7→ f + tχC when t ∈ Z5 \ {0}
and both endpoints are nowhere-zero. An integer 5-flow is an integer circulation F with
1 ≤ |F (e)| ≤ 4 on every edge. Its reconfiguration graph I5 (G) uses the same cycle moves
with nonzero integer coefficients, provided both endpoints obey these bounds. On loopless
graphs this is the adjacency of [3, 2]; counting a loop as a cycle is the usual extension to
graphs with loops. A reconfiguration graph with zero or one vertex counts as connected. An
edgeless graph has the unique empty flow. A bridge carries value zero in every circulation,
so a graph with a bridge has no nowhere-zero flow.
   Two group flows are joined when they lie in the same component of M5 (G). At modulus
five all nonzero coefficients are 1, −1, 2, −2. Multiplication by a nonzero scalar λ ∈ Z5 is an
automorphism of the flow reconfiguration graph: it changes an increment t to λt. It also
preserves balanced pairs, defined below. We do not assume a flow is joined to its scalar
multiples.
Theorem 36.1. Let G be a finite multigraph in which each connected component has at most
six odd-degree vertices. Then M5 (G) and I5 (G) are connected.
   The assertion is only at modulus five; the modulus six is treated in Theorem 54.1, and
every modulus k ≥ 7 in Theorems 45.1 and 45.2. The four-odd-vertex case is Theorem 22.1.
The present proof uses that result, along with three explicitly stated lemmas from the sections
that prove Theorem 10.1. The remaining ingredients are proved here, apart from standard
facts: Hall’s theorem; decomposition of a balanced set of arcs into edge-disjoint directed
cycles; decomposition of an integer circulation into unit cycle vectors with consistent signs;
the count 5m−n+1 of Z5 -circulations on a connected graph with n vertices and m edges;
and connectivity of a product of connected graphs. Section 42 gives complete proofs of the
small cubic base results, including K3,3 and the triangular prism at modulus five. The cut
inequalities and prescribed-outdegree matching argument are standard tools.
   For unrestricted bridgeless graphs, connectivity at five fails by [3, Theorem 3.4], which
constructs a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow that is an isolated vertex of the reconfiguration graph.
Thus our conclusion is a genuine restriction on the graph class. By the theorem and the
evenness of the number of odd vertices, any graph for which either reconfiguration graph at
five is disconnected has a component with at least eight odd-degree vertices.
68                                         DAVE GOEL


                    37. Earlier tools and elementary reductions
   For a nonloop edge incident with v, its outward value at v is the value read away from
v. Two distinct incident edges are a balanced pair if their outward values sum to zero. The
positive orientation of an integer nowhere-zero flow directs every edge in the direction of
its positive value. Write 1D for the unit edge map of an orientation D; its divergence is
outdegree minus indegree.
   We use the following statements from the sections that prove Theorem 10.1, without
repeating their proofs. None of the three lemmas restricts the number of odd-degree vertices.
Lemma 37.1 (Balanced splitting; Lemma 11.1, its proof and the paragraph after it). In a
loopless graph replace distinct edges uv, vw by an edge uw, retaining v as a vertex; u = w is
allowed and gives a loop. Flows of the split graph correspond bijectively to flows of the original
graph balanced on the chosen pair: copy the new edge’s value along u → v → w. A cycle
move downstairs lifts to a sequence of cycle moves upstairs with the same coefficient. Splitting
decreases the edge count by one, preserves every degree parity, and decreases the degree at v by
two while leaving all other degrees unchanged. For integer flows, lifting preserves magnitudes
and the unit divergence of the positive orientation.
Lemma 37.2 (Two local moves; Lemma 13.1). For a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow, call values 1, −1
small. If the non-small edges contain a cycle, cycle moves can increase the number of small
edges without changing any previously small edge. If the graph is loopless, the flow has no
balanced pair, and Q is a small-edge cycle, its two outward values at any vertex v of Q are
equal, say a ∈ {1, −1}. If an incident end off Q has outward value c ̸= −2a at v, cycle moves
on Q reach a flow balanced on a pair at v.
Lemma 37.3 (Integer lifting; Lemma 15.1). Every nowhere-zero Z5 -flow of a finite multigraph
has an integer 5-lift, and its integer lifts induce a connected subgraph of I5 (G). Every legal
modular cycle move with coefficient 1, −1, 2, −2 has adjacent integer lifts at its endpoints.
   We also use Theorem 22.1: M5 (G) is connected when each component has at most four
odd-degree vertices. Its integer conclusion is not needed as an input here. Loops are
independent nonzero coordinates and any two choices are joined by a loop move. Flow graphs
of components form a Cartesian product, since each cycle lies in one component; an empty
factor makes the whole flow space empty.
Lemma 37.4. At a degree-three vertex of a loopless nowhere-zero Z5 -flow, the outward values
are (a, a, −2a) for some a ̸= 0. At a loopless degree-four vertex with no balanced pair they are
(a, a, a, 2a) for some a ̸= 0. If a cycle through that degree-four vertex uses two of the three
ends of value a, an increment 2a or −2a on it, if it is legal, creates a balanced pair there.
Proof. Without an opposite pair, values lie in a set {s, t} with one representative from each
of {1, −1}, {2, −2}. In degree three an opposite pair would force a zero third value; two
values are equal and conservation gives the third as −2a. In degree four put i copies of s and
4 − i copies of t. If t = 2s, the sum (8 − i)s vanishes only at i = 3 in 0 ≤ i ≤ 4. If t = −2s,
the sum (3i − 8)s vanishes only at i = 1; here s = 2t. Both cases give the asserted multiset.
On a cycle entering one a-end and leaving another, the two values become a − t, a + t. For
t = 2a or −2a one is −a, balancing the third untouched a-end.                                □
Lemma 37.5 (Parallel cubic pair). Let G be connected and loopless, with exactly six odd-
degree vertices. If two degree-three vertices are joined by two parallel edges, then M5 (G) is
connected.
                  THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                             69


Proof. Three parallel edges would form a two-vertex component, impossible here. There are
exactly two internal edges uv, with outside edges pu, vq, where p, q ∈     / {u, v}; p = q is allowed.
Delete u, v and the four edges and replace them by pq, a loop if p = q. The smaller graph
is connected, and the degrees of p, q are unchanged, including when p = q. It has four odd
vertices in total, so Theorem 22.1 gives its flow connectivity; that theorem allows loops and
bridges.
   Orient p → u, both internal edges u → v, and v → q. Conservation gives a common
nonzero outside value f and internal values x, f − x. Every flow of G therefore projects to a
flow of the smaller graph, with value f on pq. Conversely, every flow of the smaller graph
extends by choosing x ∈ / {0, f }; its fibre is a clique under internal two-cycle moves. Before a
projected move changing f to f ′ , choose x ∈    / {0, f, f ′ }. Keep that edge fixed and replace pq
in the projected cycle by the path through the other internal edge and both outside legs.
This is a cycle of G, since the new internal vertices u, v are absent from the projected cycle.
The changing internal values f − x, f ′ − x are nonzero. If p = q, the loop lifts to the triangle
p − u − v − p. Moves avoiding pq lift unchanged. Connecting fibres between these lifts proves
the assertion.                                                                                      □

                       38. Feasible patterns on six odd vertices
   Throughout this section H is a bridgeless multigraph with exactly six odd-degree vertices,
whose set is X. Loops and multiple components are allowed. For W ⊆ V (H) write
d(W ) = |δH (W )|. Cut parity gives d(W ) ≡ |W ∩ X| (mod 2). Every odd cut therefore has
size at least three. We use submodularity and its difference form:
       d(A ∩ B) + d(A ∪ B) ≤ d(A) + d(B),            d(A \ B) + d(B \ A) ≤ d(A) + d(B).
Both follow by counting edges between the four regions of the two sets.
Definition 38.1. A pattern is a three-element subset P of X. Set βP = 1 at vertices of P ,
βP = −1 on X \ P , and zero elsewhere. The pattern is feasible if βP sums to zero on each
component and |βP (W )| < d(W ) for every W with d(W ) > 0. Write Φ(H) for the set of
feasible patterns. Complementing P preserves feasibility. A two-element subset R of X is a
weak pair if some U satisfies U ∩ X = R and d(U ) ≤ 2. Let P(H) be the set of weak pairs,
and let B(P) be the three-element subsets of X choosing exactly one element from each pair
in P.
Lemma 38.2. The weak pairs form a matching on X. Feasible patterns are precisely the
elements of B(P(H)) except those equal to one side of a partition of X into triples realized
by a three-edge cut.
Proof. If distinct weak pairs overlap in one terminal, their witnesses have intersection with
one terminal and union with three. The two cuts are odd and each at least three, contradicting
submodularity and the bound of four on the sum of the witness cuts. Thus the pairs are
disjoint.
   A zero- or two-edge cut contains an even number of terminals. If it contains two, those
terminals form a weak pair; if four, its complement has the weak remaining pair. Choosing
one member of that pair gives exactly βP (W ) = 0. With zero or six terminals the sum is
already zero. This also accounts for component balance, using zero-edge cuts. Cuts of size
at least four impose no additional condition, since |βP (W )| ≤ 3. A cut of size three fails
strictness exactly when its terminal set is P or X \ P .                                    □
70                                        DAVE GOEL


Lemma 38.3. At most one complementary pair of patterns is removed from B(P(H)) by
three-edge cuts. If P(H) contains at least two pairs, none is removed.
Proof. Suppose two different partitions into triples are realized by three-edge cuts and have
their triple sides in B(P(H)). Choose or complement witness sides W1 , W2 so that their
terminal triples overlap in one terminal. Their differences each contain two terminals. The
difference cut inequality bounds the sum of their cut sizes by six. Both sizes are even, so
one is at most two. Its two terminals form a weak pair, but the first triple contains both or
neither of them, contradicting membership in B(P(H)).
   Now take two weak pairs. They admit disjoint vertex-set witnesses: starting with U1 , U2 ,
the differences retain their respective terminal pairs, and the sum of the difference-cut sizes
is at most four. At least one difference has cut size at most two. Replace that witness by its
difference, leaving the other unchanged. The resulting witnesses are disjoint.
   If a set W separates the two terminals in one such witness U , the sets A = U ∩ W and
B = U \ W each contain one odd terminal, so d(A), d(B) ≥ 3. The identity
                             d(A) + d(B) = d(U ) + 2|EH (A, B)|
shows |EH (A, B)| ≥ 2. Every such edge belongs to δH (W ). A triple pattern choosing one
terminal from each of two disjoint witnesses would therefore require at least four distinct edges
in its cut, and cannot be realized by a three-edge cut. This proves the second assertion. □
  It follows that, if r = |P(H)|, the possible forms of Φ(H) are as follows. The permitted
removal in the row r = 1 is an upper bound only.
                      r |B(P(H))|       permitted removal
                      0    20     none or one complementary pair
                      1    12     none or one complementary pair
                      2     8                  none
                      3     8                  none
When r = 2, the two unmatched terminals must also have opposite signs, since a pattern has
three plus vertices. Thus its eight patterns are exactly the signings choosing one vertex from
every edge of the perfect matching completed by that last pair.
Lemma 38.4 (Pattern intersection). If H1 , H2 are bridgeless multigraphs with the same set
X of six odd vertices, then |Φ(H1 ) ∩ Φ(H2 )| ≥ 2.
Proof. If one weak-pair matching is empty, its feasible set omits at most two of all twenty
patterns, whereas the other contains at least eight. If both matchings have size at least two,
complete them to perfect matchings. Their union consists of even alternating cycles, including
doubled common edges. Alternating signs on each cycle choose one terminal per edge of
either matching and exactly three plus vertices overall. This pattern and its complement are
common feasible patterns.
   If one matching has size at least two and the other size one, start with the eight signings of
the completed perfect matching. Requiring opposite signs on the single other pair preserves
all eight if that pair is a matching edge, and four if its ends belong to different matching
edges. Removing at most two patterns leaves at least two.
   Finally suppose both matchings have size one. The two pair constraints have twelve
common solutions if the pairs coincide, eight if disjoint, and six if they overlap in one
terminal. For the last count, the two nonshared terminals must have the sign opposite to the
shared one; for either sign of the shared terminal there are three choices on the other three
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        71


terminals giving three plus vertices in total. Each graph removes at most two patterns. At
least 6 − 4 = 2 survive in every case.                                                  □

                 39. Anchors and connections within one pattern
  An anchor of pattern P is an integer 5-flow whose positive orientation has unit divergence
βP . Its magnitudes are one through four. This definition does not require P to be feasible in
the graph of the anchor.
Lemma 39.1. Every feasible pattern of a bridgeless graph with six odd vertices has an
anchor.
Proof. Set b(v) = (deg(v) + βP (v))/2. These are nonnegative integers, their sum is |E(H)|,
and for every vertex set W ,
                    X                       d(W ) + βP (W )
                         b(v) = |EH (W )| +                 ≥ |EH (W )|.
                                                   2
                    v∈W
For empty cuts the pattern sum is zero by component balance. To obtain the required
orientation, make a bipartite graph with a node for each edge and b(v) slots at each vertex
v. An edge node is adjacent to all slots of its endpoints. For any set F of edge nodes
                                                                                    P with
endpoint set W , its neighbourhood consists of these slots and |F | ≤ |EH (W )| ≤ W b(v).
Hall’s theorem gives a matching saturating the edge nodes; since total slot count equals edge
count, every slot is used. Orient each edge out of its assigned endpoint. A loop uses one slot
at its vertex and contributes one incoming end as well. The resulting orientation D has unit
divergence βP .
   In each nontrivial component both directions of every proper cut have positive arc counts,
namely (d(W ) + βP (W ))/2 and (d(W ) − βP (W ))/2. Thus D is strongly connected on each
component. Pair the minus and plus terminals within each component and take simple
directed paths from each minus to its paired plus. There are three paths in total. Adding
their signed vectors to 1D cancels its divergence; all positive magnitudes lie in {1, 2, 3, 4},
even where paths overlap. This is an anchor. Components without terminals use their
Eulerian unit circulation, and loops can retain value one.                                   □
Lemma 39.2. Anchors of the same pattern have joined reductions modulo five.
Proof. Their positive orientations have the same integer unit divergence. The disagreement
arcs, oriented as in the first orientation, form a balanced directed subgraph: its signed vector
is half the difference of the two unit maps. Decompose it into edge-disjoint directed cycles.
On the next such cycle subtract b, where b is one plus the largest current positive value there.
Then b ∈ {2, 3, 4, 5} and the new values are negative with magnitudes one through four. This
reverses exactly that cycle’s positive orientation and keeps an integer 5-flow. Modulo five the
move is legal for b < 5. If b = 5, the two group states are equal; omit the stationary step
while retaining the new integer representative for subsequent operations. Later cycles are
edge-disjoint and remain directed. Once orientations agree, decompose the difference of the
two integer flows conformally into signed unit cycles. Applying them one at a time keeps
each coordinate between its same-sign endpoints, so all magnitudes remain one through four
and all group states nonzero.                                                                 □
72                                          DAVE GOEL


                          40. The two-forest local reduction
   For a Z5 -flow write S for the spanning subgraph of values 1, −1 and N for that of values
2, −2. These are its two size classes.
Lemma 40.1. A cycle with exactly one edge in one size class and all other edges in the
other class has a legal increment of one of the two values in the class of the single edge. If
it uses two equal majority ends at a degree-four vertex with no balanced pair, such a legal
increment creates a balanced pair there.
Proof. For one small edge and otherwise large values, both increments 1, −1 are safe on
the large edges, and at most one is forbidden by the single small edge. The argument with
increments 2, −2 applies with the classes exchanged. Here the majority ends at a degree-four
vertex are the three ends with repeated outward value a in (a, a, a, 2a). At that vertex the
two equal majority values used by the cycle are a, a in a multiset (a, a, a, 2a). The proposed
increments are exactly 2a, −2a up to order, so Lemma 37.4 applies.                          □
Lemma 40.2 (Two forests). Let G be connected, bridgeless and loopless, with minimum
