degree three, maximum degree four and exactly six odd vertices. Suppose G has a degree-four
vertex. If both size classes of a flow are forests, that flow is joined to a flow with a balanced
pair.
Proof. Suppose not. All reachable flows have no balanced pair. There are six degree-three
vertices and all others have degree four, so m = 2n−3. If the two forests have s, c components,
then s + c = 3; one is a spanning tree and the other has two components. Multiplication by
two exchanges the classes and preserves the assumption that balance is unreachable. Thus
we may suppose N is a spanning tree. This uses a graph automorphism; the proof does not
use that the original flow reaches twice itself.
   No degree-four vertex has three large ends. Otherwise N − v has three branches. A small
edge between two branches closes with their paths to v a cycle through equal large ends
with exactly one small edge. Lemma 40.1 would give a balanced pair. No large edge crosses
branches of a tree, and the unique small edge at v can reach only one branch. Another
branch would then attach to the whole graph by a single tree edge, a bridge. Consequently
every degree-four vertex has three small ends and one large end.
   At a cubic vertex the values (a, a, −2a) occupy both size classes, so its N -degree is one
or two. All N -degrees are therefore at most two. Hence N is a spanning path, and every
degree-four vertex is an endpoint of it. There is at least one such vertex by hypothesis and
at most two because a path has two endpoints. Thus n = 7 or 8. We record two further
exclusions. First, a small and a large parallel pair cannot be incident with a degree-four
vertex: its outward values are s, s, s, 2s with s ∈ {1, −1}; on the two-cycle directed out along
the small edge and in along the large, increment −2s changes both outward values to −s,
balancing the untouched small ends. Second, an N -edge cannot join two branches at a
degree-four vertex within one tree component of S: that edge and the small tree path would
contradict Lemma 40.1.
   For n = 8 write the N -path as v, x1 , x2 , x3 , x4 , x5 , x6 , w, with degree-four endpoints v, w.
Let v have small outward value s. Its large value is 2s. At each internal cubic vertex the two
large outward values are equal, so along the path the large values alternate 2s, −2s. The
small outward value at xi is (−1)i s, and at w it is −s. The small degrees are three at v, w
and one at each xi . If the two centres v, w lie in different components of S, these components
are three-leaf stars. Signs force v to join x1 , x3 , x5 , giving the prohibited small/large parallel
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          73


pair vx1 . If the centres lie in the same component they are adjacent, since no other vertex
has small degree at least two. That component has four leaves, and the other component is
an edge between the two unused leaves. The vertex x1 cannot attach to v by the parallel-pair
exclusion or to w by its equal small sign. The vertex x6 cannot attach to w by the parallel-pair
exclusion or to v by its equal small sign. Thus the separate edge is x1 x6 , and the remaining
small edges are vw, vx3 , vx5 , wx2 , wx4 . The large edge x2 x3 then crosses two small-tree
branches at v, a contradiction.
   For n = 7 the path is v, x1 , x2 , x3 , x4 , x5 , w, with v degree four and w cubic. The same
calculation gives small values (−1)i s at xi and two outward small values −s at w. The small
degrees are three at v, two at w, and one elsewhere. If v, w lie in different small components,
v is a three-leaf star and w is the middle of a two-edge path; signs again force the forbidden
pair vx1 . If they lie in the same component, they are adjacent, since all other small degrees
are one. The other two neighbours of v are two of x1 , x3 , x5 , and the other neighbour of w is
x2 or x4 . The two unused leaves form a separate edge. Excluding vx1 forces v to join x3 , x5 .
If the separate edge is x1 x2 , then w joins x4 and the large edge x3 x4 crosses small branches
at v. If it is x1 x4 , then w joins x2 and x2 x3 does so. Both contradict the second exclusion.
All cases are exhausted.                                                                       □

                               41. Scalar-orbit maximality
Proposition 41.1. Let G be connected and loopless, of minimum degree three and maximum
degree four, with exactly six odd vertices and a degree-four vertex. If no two cubic vertices
are joined by parallel edges, then every Z5 -flow is joined to a flow with a balanced pair.
Proof. Suppose f is not. Let U be the union of the flow components containing λf , over all
nonzero λ ∈ Z5 . This finite set is closed under legal moves and nonzero scalar multiplication.
It contains no balanced-pair flow: a path from λf to such a flow would scale back to a path
from f to balance. This uses scaling as an automorphism, not as a path within a component.
   Choose g ∈ U maximizing its number of small edges, and write S, N for its spanning
size-class subgraphs. By the first assertion of Lemma 37.2, N is a forest: otherwise a legal
sequence would increase the small-edge count while remaining in U, contradicting maximality.
Since 2g ∈ U and multiplication by two exchanges the classes,
(11)                                   |E(S)| ≥ |E(N )|.
At any vertex of a small cycle, the second assertion of Lemma 37.2 forces equal outward cycle
values a, a and all other ends −2a: otherwise a legal sequence in U would reach a balanced
pair, which no flow in U has. A degree-four vertex would have sum −2a ̸= 0, impossible.
Thus every vertex of a small cycle is cubic and has exactly two small ends. Each component
of S is therefore a tree or a whole cycle. Signs alternate along such a cycle, so its length is
even and its along-cycle values consist of equal numbers of 1 and −1.
   Let s be the number of tree components of S, including isolated vertices, and c the number
of components of the forest N . The degree sum gives m = 2n − 3, hence |E(S)| = n − s,
|E(N )| = n − c, and s + c = 3. Equation (11) gives s ≤ c, and c ≥ 1. Thus (s, c) = (0, 3) or
(1, 2). In the first case every vertex lies on a small cycle and is cubic, contrary to hypothesis.
Consequently
                               |E(S)| = n − 1,      |E(N )| = n − 2.
If S has a cycle Q of length ℓ, then ℓ ̸= 2: its vertices are cubic, and such a pair of parallel
edges was excluded. Therefore ℓ ≥ 4. The legal move h = g + 2χQ changes each along-cycle
74                                          DAVE GOEL


1 to 3 (large) and each −1 to 1 (small). Thus
                                  |E(N (h))| = n − 2 + ℓ/2 ≥ n.
But 2h ∈ U and its number of small edges equals this large count, strictly exceeding n − 1, a
contradiction. Hence S is a forest as well. The graph is bridgeless because it has a flow, so
Lemma 40.2 now joins g to balance, contradicting the defining property of U.               □

                              42. The cubic six-vertex bases
Lemma 42.1. The nowhere-zero Z5 -flows of K3,3 are connected by cycle moves.
Proof. Orient all edges from its three row vertices to its three column vertices. A flow is a
3 × 3 matrix of nonzero residues  with all row and column sums zero. On two chosen rows
                            1 −1
and columns, adding t −1       1   is a four-cycle move whenever its final entries are nonzero.
   For a ̸= 0 let A(a; i, j) have value a outside row i and column j, value 3a on that row
or column away from their intersection, and value 4a at (i, j). Let B(b; π), for b ̸= 0 and a
permutation π of three indices, have value 3b at (r, π(r)) and value b elsewhere. Both forms
have zero row and column sums.
   These forms exhaust all flows. By Lemma 37.4 every row is a permutation of (a, a, 3a). For
classification only, scale and permute columns to make the first row (1, 1, 3). If the second
row’s exception is also in column three, write it (b, b, 3b). Nonzero entries in the forced third
row exclude precisely b = 4. The remaining row multiplier triples are (1, 1, 3), (1, 2, 2), (1, 3, 1),
giving form A. If its exception is in column one, the second row is (3b, b, b). The inequalities
1 + 3b ̸= 0, 1 + b ̸= 0, 3 + b ̸= 0 exclude b = 3, 4, 2, leaving b = 1. The third row is (1, 3, 1),
giving form B; column two is symmetric. Scaling and column permutations preserve the two
forms. So there are thirty-six A matrices and twenty-four B matrices. The scaling serves the
classification only; it is not used as a cycle path.
   For fixed a, consider the nine matrices A(a; i, j) and six matrices B(3a; π). In A(a; i, j)
the 2 × 2 block outside row i and column j is all a. Adding 2a or −2a on that block gives,
respectively,                                              
                                      3a 4a            4a 3a
                                               ,                .
                                      4a 3a            3a 4a
The whole resulting matrices are exactly the two B(3a; π) with π(i) = j. These fifteen states
are connected: view the A states as nine cells and the B states as permutations incident
with their three cells. Two cells in different rows and columns share a permutation. Two in
the same row have an intermediate cell in another row and the column unused by either;
the same-column case is symmetric. Thus every two cell states, and then every permutation
state, are joined.
   Finally choose r ̸= i, c ̸= j in A(a; i, j). On rows (r, i) and columns (c, j) the move of
coefficient 2a changes                                     
                                      a 3a               3a a
                                                 to             .
                                     3a 4a               a a
The result is B(a; π): if t is the remaining row and l the remaining column, then π(r) =
c, π(t) = j, π(i) = l. It belongs to the fifteen-state family with parameter 2a, since 3(2a) = a
modulo five. Thus the family with parameter a is joined to the one with parameter 2a.
Multiplication by two cycles through all four nonzero parameters, and the families cover
every flow. All entries in the displayed moves are nonzero, so these are actual cycle paths. □
Lemma 42.2. The nowhere-zero Z5 -flows of the triangular prism are connected.
                  THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                               75


Proof. Let its top vertices be x1 , x2 , x3 , bottom vertices y1 , y2 , y3 , with matching edges xi yi .
With indices cyclic modulo three, take the five directed cycles
                    U = x1 x2 x3 x1 ,   V = y1 y3 y2 y1 ,   Ri = xi yi yi+1 xi+1 xi .
Every edge occurs in exactly two cycles with opposite traversal directions. The Ri are
pairwise adjacent through shared edges; U,P     V are each adjacent to all Ri and not to each
other. For colours c(C) ∈ Z5 , the map ∂c = C c(C)χC is a circulation with kernel precisely
the constant colourings, because this cycle-adjacency graph is connected. Its image has 54
elements. A spanning tree gives four free coordinates for the circulation group of the prism,
which has nine edges and six vertices, so this image is the whole group. The circulation is
nowhere-zero exactly when adjacent cycles have different colours. A nontrivial one-cycle
recolouring is one cycle move of the original graph.
   In a proper colouring the three Ri colours are distinct and U, V both avoid them. First
recolour U to V ’s colour, omitting a stationary change. Regard the equal-coloured pair as
one object together with the three Ri . These four labelled objects occupy different colours
among five, leaving one free. Set them to any desired four distinct colours one at a time: if a
target colour is occupied, first move its holder to the free colour. That holder cannot already
be fixed, since the target colours are distinct. A move of the paired object is implemented
by recolouring U and then V , remaining proper because they are nonadjacent. Reversing
the target’s initial coalescence joins arbitrary proper colourings, and hence all nowhere-zero
flows.                                                                                       □
Corollary 42.3. Every connected bridgeless cubic multigraph on six vertices has connected
nowhere-zero Z5 -flow reconfiguration.
Proof. A loop at a cubic vertex leaves a single nonloop edge, which is a bridge. A nonsimple
loopless graph has a parallel pair, handled by Lemma 37.5. For a simple cubic graph on six
vertices, the complement is simple 2-regular, hence a six-cycle or two disjoint triangles. Their
complements are the triangular prism and K3,3 , respectively. The two preceding lemmas
finish the proof.                                                                             □

                       43. Connectivity in maximum degree four
Proposition 43.1. If G has maximum degree at most four and at most six odd vertices per
component, then M5 (G) is connected.
Proof. Induct on edge count within this class, allowing disconnected graphs. An edgeless
graph has one flow. A bridge, including a degree-one edge, makes the flow space empty.
Delete a loop and use its independent coordinate. Then assume looplessness. Degree-two
vertices have a balanced pair in every flow; splitting and induction apply, with lifted paths
from Lemma 37.1. Splitting preserves parity, decreases edge count and cannot merge original
components, so the class is preserved. Isolated vertices do not change flow spaces. If there are
multiple components with edges, each has fewer edges and their product gives the result. A
remaining connected graph with at most four odd vertices uses the earlier four-odd theorem.
   Thus consider a connected loopless graph of minimum degree three with exactly six odd
vertices, and suppose it has a flow. It is bridgeless. A parallel pair at cubic vertices is handled
by Lemma 37.5. If all vertices are cubic there are exactly six, and Corollary 42.3 applies.
Otherwise Proposition 41.1 brings every flow to a balanced pair.
   For a fixed incident pair, call its nonempty set of balanced flows a family. Its split graph
G′ has fewer edges, retains the same six odd vertices and is bridgeless because the family is
76                                           DAVE GOEL


nonempty. Induction and lifted paths join all members of the family in the ambient flow
graph. For every P ∈ Φ(G′ ), Lemma 39.1 gives an integer 5-anchor of G′ . Its lift is an anchor
of G with the same pattern, magnitudes and positive-orientation unit divergence. Reduction
modulo five commutes with the lift, so the family contains this reference reduction.
    For any two families, their split graphs share a feasible pattern by Lemma 38.4. Their
lifted anchors for that pattern have joined reductions in G by Lemma 39.2, which does not
require the pattern to be feasible in G. The whole two families are therefore joined. Pairwise
intersection is sufficient here; a common pattern for every split graph is not needed. All
balanced-pair flows lie in one component, and the local proposition brings every flow to that
component. This completes the induction.                                                     □

                       44. Arbitrary degrees and integer flows
   Expansion of a vertex into a triangle is the method used by Cranston et al. in [2, Observation
4.6].
Lemma 44.1 (Triangle projection). Expand one vertex of a loopless graph G into a triangle,
distributing its old incident ends among the three new vertices, to obtain G.
                                                                           b Every nowhere-
zero Z5 -flow of G extends to one of G. Every cycle move upstairs projects to a sequence of
                                      b
cycle moves downstairs, possibly stationary. Thus connectivity upstairs implies connectivity
downstairs.
Proof. Label the new triangle v1 v2 v3 v1 and orient it in that order. Let βi be the outward
divergence of the prescribed external edges at its three vertices. Their sum is zero. The
triangle values can be chosen as z, z − β2 , z + β1 on v1 v2 , v2 v3 , v3 v1 , respectively, so at most
three choices of z ∈ Z5 are forbidden. No other edge joins two new vertices, since G was
loopless. Retaining the values off the triangle therefore projects a flow to G. The restriction
of an upstairs cycle vector after contraction is an integer circulation with entries 0, 1, −1.
Orient its support by those signs and decompose into edge-disjoint directed cycles. Apply the
same coefficient on these cycles in turn; each retained edge has its old or final nonzero value
at every prefix. A zero projected vector gives no move. Lift any two downstairs endpoints
and project a path between their lifts to obtain the connectivity conclusion. This direction
uses no connectedness of extension fibres.                                                           □
Proof of Theorem 36.1. Delete loopsP and later restore their independent coordinates. On
loopless graphs use induction on v max{deg(v) − 4, 0}. The zero case is Proposition 43.1.
At an even-degree vertex of degree d ≥ 6, expand to a triangle with old end groups of
sizes 2, 2, d − 4. The new degrees are 4, 4, d − 2 and the induction parameter falls by two.
At an odd-degree vertex of degree d ≥ 5, use group sizes 1, 2, d − 3. The new degrees are
3, 4, d − 1, exactly one remains odd, and the parameter falls by one. In either case each
original component remains one component and its number of odd vertices is unchanged.
Induction and Lemma 44.1 give modular connectivity for the original graph. If downstairs
flows exist, the extension clause ensures upstairs flows exist, so the empty-space convention
creates no exception.
    Finally take two integer 5-flows. Their reductions are joined by the modular assertion.
Every nonzero coefficient modulo five is 1, −1, 2, −2. Lemma 37.3 supplies adjacent integer
lifts at each step, and its connected-fibre conclusion joins the successive choices of lifts and
the two prescribed endpoints. This proves integer connectivity.                                □
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                         77


45. Six odd-degree vertices at every modulus at least seven: statements and
                                  conventions
   All graphs are finite multigraphs. Loops and parallel edges are allowed; a loop contributes
two to degree. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph, including a loop or two parallel
edges. Fix reference directions on the edges. The divergence of an edge map is its outgoing
sum minus its incoming sum at each vertex; loops contribute zero. A circulation has zero
divergence. A directed traversal of a cycle C gives its signed edge vector χC , with entries
0, 1, −1.
   A nowhere-zero Zk -flow is a circulation with nonzero values in Zk . Its reconfiguration
graph Mk (G) joins f to f + tχC for every nonzero t ∈ Zk for which both endpoints are
nowhere-zero. An integer k-flow is an integer circulation F with 1 ≤ |F (e)| ≤ k − 1 on
every edge. Its reconfiguration graph Ik (G) uses nonzero integer cycle increments, again
with valid endpoints. On loopless graphs these are the adjacencies of [3, 2]; counting loops
as cycles extends them to graphs with loops. A graph with zero or one flow is regarded as
flow-connected. In particular a bridge, which has value zero in every circulation, gives an
empty nowhere-zero flow space. An edgeless graph has the empty flow.
   An integer k-lift of a modular flow is an integer k-flow with that reduction modulo k. A
modular step is restricted when its coefficient lies in {1, −1, 2, −2}. Unqualified connectivity
below allows all nonzero coefficients. The restricted relation is used only where stated.
Theorem 45.1 (Modular conclusion). Let G be a finite multigraph with at most six odd-degree
vertices in each connected component. For every integer k ≥ 7, Mk (G) is connected.
Theorem 45.2 (Integer conclusion). Under the same hypotheses, Ik (G) is connected for
every integer k ≥ 7.
   Both theorems use the contraction of induced cycles of length two, three and four. The
statements are due to Cranston, Li, Su, Wang and Xu [2, Theorem 5.9(2) and Corollaries
5.13 and 5.15(i)]; the cases that we use are proved in Appendix A (Corollary A.3).
   Part A, which is Sections 47 to 50, proves Theorem 45.1 without using Theorem 45.2.
Part B, which is Sections 51 to 53, uses Part A and additional lifting arguments to prove the
integer conclusion. The modulus-five theorem, Theorem 36.1, treats k = 5, and Theorem 54.1
treats k = 6.

    46. Explicit inputs from earlier sections and from a companion text
   We state all tools imported from earlier sections and from the companion text [6] below;
the contraction input, which is proved in Appendix A, is stated at the start of Section 47.
The proofs of these tools are in the cited places. The other deductions after this section are
proved here; they do not use a computer enumeration. We also use standard elementary facts
about graph products, degree parity, and decompositions of circulations into cycle vectors.
   For a nonloop edge incident with v, its outward value is the value read away from v. A
balanced pair consists of two distinct incident edges whose outward values sum to zero. The
coefficient group or the integers is specified when this distinction matters.
Lemma 46.1 (Balanced splitting, Lemma 11.1, its proof and following paragraph). In a
loopless graph replace distinct edges uv, vw by uw, retaining v; u = w is allowed and gives a
loop. Copying the new edge value along u → v → w identifies flows of the split graph with
flows balanced on that pair in the original graph, both modularly and integrally. A cycle
step downstairs lifts to a sequence of cycle steps upstairs with the same coefficient. Splitting
78                                        DAVE GOEL


reduces the edge count by one, reduces deg(v) by two, and preserves all other degrees and
all degree parities. For integer flows it preserves magnitudes and the unit divergence of the
positive orientation.
   Here the positive orientation directs an edge in the direction of its positive integer value.
Its unit map 1D puts value one on each edge in that orientation, and div 1D is outdegree
minus indegree.
Lemma 46.2 (Local normalization, Lemma 13.1). Let k ≥ 4, fix a nowhere-zero Zk -flow,
and call the values 1, −1 small and all other nonzero values large.
   (1) If the large edges contain a cycle, restricted steps can increase the number of small
        edges without changing any previously small edge.
   (2) Suppose the graph is loopless, a flow has no balanced pair, and Q is a small-edge cycle.
        Its two outward cycle values at a vertex v are equal, say a ∈ {1, −1}. If an end off Q
        at v has outward value c ̸= −2a, restricted steps on Q reach a balanced pair at v.
Lemma 46.3 (One large edge, Lemma 31.1). Let k ≥ 4. In a loopless flow without a
balanced pair, suppose a cycle has exactly one large edge h. Let v be an endpoint of h of
degree four, and let the other cycle edge have outward value a ∈ {1, −1} at v. Unless both
off-cycle outward values are −2a, restricted steps on the cycle reach a balanced pair at v. In
the exceptional case the outward pattern is (a, 3a, −2a, −2a), with 3a on h.
Theorem 46.4 (Four odd vertices, Theorem 29.1). If each component of a finite multigraph
has at most four odd-degree vertices and k ≥ 6, its modular flows are connected by restricted
steps and its integer k-flow graph is connected.
   The next input concerns exactly six labelled odd vertices, possibly in different components.
It is independent of the modulus.
Definition 46.5. Let H be bridgeless with odd-vertex set X, where |X| = 6. A pattern is
a three-element subset P ⊂ X. Put  P βP = 1 on P , βP = −1 on X \ P , and zero elsewhere.
For W ⊆ V (H), write βP (W ) = v∈W βP (v) and let δH (W ) be the edges with exactly one
endpoint in W . The pattern is feasible for H if βP sums to zero on each component and
|βP (W )| < |δH (W )| for every vertex set W with nonempty edge cut. An anchor of pattern
P is an integer circulation with all magnitudes in {1, 2, 3, 4} whose positive orientation has
unit divergence βP . The anchor definition itself does not require feasibility in its graph.
Lemma 46.6 (Patterns and anchors, Definition 38.1, Lemmas 38.4 and 39.1). Two bridgeless
multigraphs with the same six labelled odd vertices have a common feasible pattern. Every
feasible pattern has an anchor. Loops and disconnected graphs are permitted in both statements.
Lemma 46.7 (Integer lifting, Lemma 15.1). Every nowhere-zero Zk -flow has an integer k-lift
for k ≥ 2. Its lifts induce a connected subgraph of Ik (G). For k ≥ 3 every legal restricted
modular cycle step has adjacent integer lifts at its endpoints. These statements impose no
odd-vertex restriction.
Theorem 46.8 (Short cycles and chords, [6, Theorems 2.2 and 2.3]). For every k ≥ 3, a
legal modular step on a cycle of length at most nine has adjacent integer endpoint lifts. At
k = 7 the length bound can be twenty-one. Moreover, if every cycle of a finite multigraph
longer than nine has an edge outside it joining two distinct nonneighboring cycle vertices,
reduction induces a bijection between the components of Ik (G) and Mk (G) for every k ≥ 2.
For k = 7 this also holds with nine replaced by twenty-one.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                       79


   The direct-lift statement is Theorem 2.3 of the cited text; the chord statement is Theorem
2.2. An extra edge parallel to a cycle edge is not a nonneighbor chord. Part B will separate
parallel edges before using a simple-graph chord count.

        47. A hand reduction for graphs on at most fourteen vertices
   For a finite abelian group A, its flow reconfiguration graph is defined as above with
nonzero values and increments in A in place of Zk . We say that G is A-flow-connected
when this graph is connected; on loopless graphs this is the notion of [2]. We use the
following contraction statement. It is due to Cranston, Li, Su, Wang and Xu [2, Definition
5.8, Theorem 5.9(2) and Corollaries 5.13 and 5.15(i)], and Appendix A proves it in the form
that we use (Corollary A.3): if |A| ≥ 7, the graph G is loopless, C is an induced cycle of
G of length two, three or four, and G/C is A-flow-connected, then G is A-flow-connected.
Thus induced cycles of lengths two, three and four may be contracted for every finite abelian
group of order at least seven. The respective sufficient thresholds are four, five and seven
(Lemma A.1). Only this lifting direction is used. It concerns group flows with all increments,
not integer flows or restricted modular steps. Loops are independent coordinates and can be
deleted before applying the loopless result.
Lemma 47.1. Let A be a finite abelian group with |A| ≥ 7. Every bridgeless multigraph on
at most fourteen vertices with at most six odd vertices per component is A-flow-connected.
Proof. Induct on the number of vertices, treating components separately. After each step
delete the independent loop coordinates; this changes no vertex. Isolated vertices do not
matter. A vertex of degree two can be suppressed: orient its two edges along their path,
whose common value becomes the new edge value. This identifies flow spaces and their cycle
moves, including the case that the new edge is a loop. Suppression preserves odd counts and
reduces the number of vertices. A nontrivial bridgeless component has no degree-one vertex.
We may therefore assume minimum degree three.
   A parallel pair supplies a cycle of length two. Otherwise suppose, for a contradiction, that
the remaining simple graph has girth at least five. The vertices at distance at most two
from a vertex of degree at least five are distinct and number at least 1 + 5 + 10 = 16. All
degrees are consequently three or four. We are considering a connected component. Write p
for the number of cubic vertices and q for the number of degree-four vertices, so p ≤ 6 and
p + q = n ≤ 14. If q = 0, the radius-two count 1 + 3 + 6 = 10 contradicts n = p ≤ 6.
   At a degree-four vertex with t cubic neighbors, the same count gives
                             n ≥ 1 + 4 + 2t + 3(4 − t) = 17 − t.
In particular n ≥ 13. At n = 13 every degree-four vertex has four cubic neighbors, giving
4q ≤ 3p, contrary to q ≥ 7 and p ≤ 6. At n = 14 every degree-four vertex has at least three
cubic neighbors, giving 3q ≤ 3p, contrary to q ≥ 8 and p ≤ 6.
  Thus a cycle C of length two, three or four exists. The contraction statement is for an
induced cycle; so subdivide once each edge outside C with both endpoints on C, obtaining
G∗ . This includes extra edges parallel to an edge of C. Subdivision is the inverse of the
degree-two suppression above, so it preserves the flow reconfiguration graph. Now C is
induced in G∗ , and we may apply Corollary A.3 to (G∗ , C). Although G∗ may have more
than fourteen vertices, the corollary has no bound on the order of its ambient graph.
   In G∗ /C, every new subdivision vertex has degree two and lies on a digon with the
contracted vertex. Suppress each such vertex, giving an independent loop, and delete those
80                                         DAVE GOEL


loops. The resulting graph G′ is exactly the graph obtained from G by identifying V (C) and
deleting loops. It has |V (G)| − |V (C)| + 1 < |V (G)| vertices. It remains bridgeless: every
retained nonloop edge lies on a cycle after contraction. The contracted vertex is odd precisely
when the number of odd vertices identified into it was odd, so the odd count cannot increase.
Induction applies to G′ , not to G∗ . Restore its independent loops and undo the degree-two
suppressions to obtain connectivity of G∗ /C; Corollary A.3 then gives connectivity of G∗ ,
and undoing the initial subdivisions gives connectivity of G. Contraction may create large
degrees; the induction class has no maximum-degree bound.                                    □
  Suppression is part of this argument: a long degree-two cycle need not contain any short
cycle before suppression. No integer contraction statement is inferred from Lemma 47.1.

         48. Restricted steps on cycles with at most two large edges
   Throughout the rest of Part A let k ≥ 7. Small and large always refer to the value
classes {1, −1} and their nonzero complement. For a fixed direction around a cycle, an
edge’s traversal value is its flow value read in that direction. A legal cumulative increment
is a residue t for which f + tχC is nowhere-zero, equivalently one that is not the negative
of a traversal value. It is reachable from zero by restricted steps if there is a sequence of
legal cumulative increments from zero to t, consecutive ones differing by an element of
{1, −1, 2, −2}.
Lemma 48.1. On a cycle with exactly one large edge, every legal cumulative increment is
reachable from zero by restricted steps on that cycle. The same holds for a cycle with exactly
two large edges of traversal values u, w, except possibly when both small signs occur and
{u, w} = {2, −2}. In that exception zero is isolated among the legal cumulative increments.
Proof. Legal increments are the residues that do not negate a traversal value. Join residues
of Zk differing by 1 or 2 up to sign. Deleting at most three residues leaves this graph
connected: a gap of one deleted residue can be crossed by a step of two, and separation
would require two distinct gaps each containing at least two deleted residues. This proves
the assertion unless two large values and both small signs occur. In that case the forbidden
set is {1, −1, −u, −w}.
   After deleting 1, −1, the remaining vertices are zero and the interval [2, k −2]. Its endpoints
2, k − 2 attach to zero. Delete the at most two additional forbidden residues. An isolated
interior deletion can be jumped; two consecutive interior deletions separate pieces attached
to the two different endpoints. If one endpoint is deleted, every surviving site still connects
to the other, unless both endpoints are deleted. Only deletion of both 2 and k − 2 isolates
zero. This is exactly {u, w} = {2, −2}.                                                         □
Lemma 48.2. Let k ≥ 7, and let f be a nowhere-zero Zk -flow of a loopless graph. Suppose
a degree-four vertex has outward values (a, a, b, c) with a ∈ {1, −1} and b, c large. Suppose a
cycle with exactly two large edges uses an a-end and the b-end at this vertex. Direct it out
along the a-end, and let d be its other large traversal value. Restricted steps on the cycle
reach a balanced pair at this vertex unless both small signs occur on the cycle and b = d = 2a.
Proof. The two totals −2a and a + b balance, respectively, the changed small end and the
changed b-end with the untouched a-end. They are distinct, since equality would give
b = −3a and hence c = a by conservation, whereas c is large. They are safe on all small
edges: −2a ∈/ {1, −1} for k ≥ 7, and a + b ∈
                                           / {1, −1} because b ̸= 0 and b ̸= −2a (the latter
would force c = 0). They are safe on the incident large edge as well: b = −2a is excluded
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                         81


and a ̸= 0. The other large edge can forbid at most one total. Lemma 48.1 therefore gives a
path unless the large traversal values −b, d are 2, −2 and both small signs occur. In that
case b = d ∈ {2, −2}, and b ̸= −2a forces b = d = 2a.                                    □

           49. The bounded obstruction to reaching a balanced pair
   Let G be connected and loopless, with all degrees three or four and exactly six cubic
vertices. Suppose a flow f cannot reach a balanced pair by restricted steps. Choose in its
restricted component a flow g maximizing the number of small edges. Every flow of this
component has no balanced pair. Let S, N be the spanning subgraphs of small and large
edges, respectively, including isolated vertices.
Lemma 49.1. The graph N is a forest. Every component of S is a tree or a whole even
cycle whose vertices are cubic. If s counts the tree components of S and c the components of
N , then s + c = 3 and c ≥ 1. If s = 0, then |V (G)| = 6. If s = 1, then |V (G)| ≤ 9.
Proof. Maximality and Lemma 46.2(1) make N a forest. At any small-cycle vertex the two
small outward values agree, say a. Every off-cycle end must have value −2a by Lemma 46.2(2).
A degree-four vertex on the cycle would then have total −2a ̸= 0, which is impossible. A
cubic vertex on it has the pattern (a, a, −2a) and has no further small end. Thus the cycle is
a whole component of S. Along its traversal the values alternate 1, −1, so the cycle is even.
   Writing n = |V (G)|, the degree sum gives |E(G)| = 2n − 3. Since |E(S)| = n − s and
|E(N )| = n − c, we have s + c = 3. The forest has at least one component. If s = 0, every
vertex is on a small cycle and hence cubic, so n = 6.
   Suppose s = 1, and let T be the small tree and Z the union of the small-cycle vertex sets.
Every degree-four vertex v lies in T . A large edge from v to another vertex of T closes a one-
large-edge cycle with the small-tree path. Lemma 46.3 forces the pattern (a, 3a, −2a, −2a)
there, with exactly one small end. If v has at least two small ends, all its large ends therefore
go to Z. Four small ends would sum to 4a ̸= 0; three small ends would require its remaining
value to be −3a, which cannot be the ±2 value of an edge to Z. Thus it has two large ends
to Z. If v has one small end, at most one large edge goes back to T : two such edges would
force 3a = −2a, impossible for k ≥ 7. Again at least two large ends go to Z. If it has no
small end, then T = {v} and all four ends go to Z. Each vertex of Z is cubic and supplies
exactly one large end, and |Z| ≤ 6. There are therefore at most three degree-four vertices,
giving n ≤ 9.                                                                                  □
  It remains to consider s = 2, c = 1. Write T1 , T2 for the two small trees and Z for the
small-cycle vertices. Each vertex of Z is a leaf of the large spanning tree N . Two such leaves
cannot be adjacent in N , since N is connected and n ≥ 6. Deleting all these leaves leaves a
connected tree on V (T1 ) ∪ V (T2 ). We call a large edge between T1 and T2 a crossing edge.
Lemma 49.2. In this case no degree-four vertex has exactly two small ends.
Proof. Suppose v ∈ T1 has outward values (a, a, b, c) with b, c large. A large edge from v
back into T1 would close a one-large-edge cycle; an untouched small end has value a ̸= −2a,
so Lemma 46.3 would reach balance. Hence both large neighbors are in T2 or Z. Both cannot
be in Z, since that would give a three-vertex component of N . Choose a crossing edge e at v.
   There is a crossing edge e′ not incident with v. Indeed, T1 − v is nonempty, as v has two
small ends. If every crossing edge met v, this set would have no connection to its complement
in the pruned large tree: no large edge from v goes back into T1 . This contradicts that tree’s
connectedness. The edges e, e′ and the unique paths in the two small trees form a cycle with
82                                         DAVE GOEL


exactly two large edges. At v it uses a small end and e. Lemma 48.2 forces the outward
value of e to be 2a. If the other large edge at v is crossing, the same argument forces its
value to be 2a. If it goes to Z, it has value ±2, and absence of a balanced pair with the
2a-end again forces 2a. Conservation would give 6a = 0, impossible.                      □
Lemma 49.3. At most two degree-four vertices have three small ends. If such a vertex
v ∈ T1 has small outward value a, its sole large edge e crosses to T2 , and every other crossing
edge has value 2a when read from T1 to T2 . All degree-four vertices with three small ends are
endpoints of this same edge e.
Proof. The sole large outward value at v is −3a. Its edge cannot go to Z, where it would
have value ±2, or back to T1 , where Lemma 46.3 would reach balance. Hence it crosses. For
any other crossing edge e′ of value d read from T1 to T2 , the small-tree paths with e, e′ form a
two-large-edge cycle. It leaves v by a small edge, crosses along e′ , and returns along e, so its
large traversal values are d, 3a. Total −2a balances the changed small end with an untouched
small end at v. It is safe on all small edges and the 3a edge; the only possible obstruction is
d = 2a. Otherwise Lemma 48.1 makes it reachable, since 3a ∈     / {2, −2} excludes that lemma’s
exceptional case. Thus every such d is 2a. Every other degree-four vertex with three small
ends likewise has its sole large edge crossing. If different from e, its value read from T1 to T2
would be ±3, which cannot equal 2a. Consequently all such vertices are endpoints of e. □
Proposition 49.4 (Local entry above fourteen vertices). If a connected loopless graph of
degrees three and four has exactly six cubic vertices and more than fourteen vertices, every
nowhere-zero Zk -flow reaches a balanced pair by restricted steps for k ≥ 7.
Proof. Normalize a contrary flow as above. Lemma 49.1 handles s = 0, 1, so take s = 2, c = 1.
Let n1 , n3 , n0 count degree-four vertices having small degrees 1, 3, 0, respectively. Small
degree two is excluded by Lemma 49.2; four would force 4a = 0. Let pi count cubic vertices
of small degree i, for i = 0, 1, 2. ThreePwould force 3a = 0, and p0 + p1 + p2 = 6. The two
tree components of S contribute −4 to v (degS (v) − 2) and its cycles contribute zero. Thus
     −n1 + n3 − 2n0 − 2p0 − p1 = −4,         n1 + n3 + n0 = 2n3 + 4 − n0 − 2p0 − p1 ≤ 8,
using n3 ≤ 2 from Lemma 49.3. Hence n ≤ 6 + 8 = 14.                                            □

               50. Joining balanced families and completing Part A
Lemma 50.1. Two anchors of the same pattern are joined by integer cycle moves through
circulations of magnitudes one through four. Their reductions are consequently joined in
Mk (G) for every k ≥ 7.
Proof. As in the proof of Lemma 39.2, the two positive orientations have equal unit divergence.
Orient the disagreement edges as in the first orientation. Their signed map is half the difference
of the two unit maps, so is a circulation; decompose it into edge-disjoint directed cycles. On
each such cycle subtract b = 1 + maxe F (e), reading its current values positively along the
cycle. Here 2 ≤ b ≤ 5. Every new value is negative with magnitude between one and four.
This reverses that cycle’s positive orientation and preserves an integer circulation. Later
disagreement cycles remain directed because their edge sets are disjoint. After all orientations
agree, decompose the difference to the second anchor conformally into signed unit cycles.
Successive unit moves keep each coordinate between its same-sign endpoints. All magnitudes
remain between one and four. Reduction modulo k ≥ 7 makes every indicated move legal.
                  THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                            83


This is a bounded-circulation argument, not an invocation of the integer connectivity theorem
being proved in Part B.                                                                     □
Proposition 50.2. The modular conclusion holds for graphs of maximum degree four.
Proof. Induct on the edge count within this class, allowing up to six odd vertices per
component. Components give products; isolated vertices and independent loops may be
removed. Bridges give empty spaces, so we may assume that G has no bridge. Degree-two
suppression identifies flows and cycle moves and decreases the edge count. We may assume
connectedness, no loops, minimum degree three, and hence degrees three or four. If there are
at most four odd vertices, use Theorem 46.4. Otherwise there are exactly six cubic vertices.
At order at most fourteen apply Lemma 47.1 with A = Zk .
   At larger order, Proposition 49.4 sends every flow into some balanced-pair family. A
balanced pair cannot be at a cubic vertex, as its remaining value would be zero. Each
nonempty family therefore corresponds by Lemma 46.1 to a smaller split graph of maximum
degree four, with the same six labelled odd vertices. It is bridgeless, since it has a nowhere-
zero flow. Induction connects its flows; lifting their paths connects the corresponding family
in the ambient graph.
   For two nonempty families, the two split graphs have a common feasible pattern by
Lemma 46.6. Choose an anchor of that pattern in each split graph, and lift both to G by
balanced splitting. Their magnitudes and unit divergences are preserved. Their reductions
belong to the two respective families, and Lemma 50.1 joins them. Thus all nonempty
families lie in one component, and every flow reaches this component.                        □
Lemma 50.3 (Triangle extension and projection). Replace a vertex of a loopless graph by
a triangle and distribute its incident ends among the three new vertices. Every prescribed
modular flow extends across the triangle for k ≥ 4. Contracting this triangle projects every
upstairs modular cycle step to a downstairs modular path, possibly stationary. The same
projection assertion holds for integer cycle steps whenever an upstairs integer flow is given.
Proof. Orient the P triangle v1 v2 v3 v1 . If βi is the outward divergence of the prescribed external
values at vi , then i βi = 0. Triangle values z, z − β2 , z + β1 give conservation, and at most
three residues z are forbidden. This proves modular extension.
   The restriction of an upstairs cycle vector to the retained edges, after contracting the
triangle, is an integer circulation with values 0, 1, −1. Orient its support by these signs and
decompose it into edge-disjoint directed cycles. Apply the original coefficient on these cycles
successively. At each prefix every retained edge has its old or final value. This proves validity
in both settings, with the same integer coefficient in the integer setting. A zero restriction
gives no step.                                                                                     □
                                                                       P
Proof of Theorem 45.1. Remove loops and induct on Ψ(G) = v max{deg(v) − 4, 0}. The
zero case is Proposition 50.2. At an even vertex of degree d ≥ 6, replace it by a triangle with
external end groups of sizes 2, 2, d − 4. The new degrees are 4, 4, d − 2, and Ψ decreases by
two. At an odd vertex of degree d ≥ 5, use groups 1, 2, d − 3, giving degrees 3, 4, d − 1 and
decreasing Ψ by one. Each component remains connected and its odd count is unchanged.
The even expansion is also used in [2, Observation 4.6]; the projection argument was given
in Section 44. Extend any two downstairs modular flows by Lemma 50.3, join them upstairs
by induction, and project the path. Restore the independent loop coordinates. This proves
the modular theorem.                                                                               □
84                                       DAVE GOEL


  The inductions above are distinct: vertex count for Lemma 47.1, edge count in maximum
degree four, and then degree excess for arbitrary degrees. In particular the small-base
contraction argument never assumes the theorem for graphs of arbitrarily large order.

             51. The sharper local bound for moduli at least eight
Proposition 51.1. For k ≥ 8, the bound fourteen in Proposition 49.4 can be replaced by
thirteen.
Proof. It suffices to exclude n = 14 in the normalized no-balance situation of Section 49.
The earlier count forces s = 2, c = 1, n3 = 2, n1 = 6, n0 = p0 = p1 = 0, and p2 = 6. Call
a degree-four vertex with i small ends a vertex of type i. The two vertices of type 3 are
endpoints of one crossing edge e. Fix the small outward sign a at its endpoint in T1 . Every
other crossing edge has value 2a from T1 to T2 , by Lemma 49.3.
   Let v have type 1, with unique small outward value σ ∈ {1, −1}. It is not an endpoint of
e, whose endpoints both have type 3, so every crossing edge at v has outward value ±2a.
If none of its three large ends goes into its own small tree, they all go to the other tree or
to Z. Their values are then all ±2. The sum of σ and three such values is an odd, nonzero
integer of absolute value at most seven; it cannot vanish modulo k ≥ 8. Thus an internal
large edge exists. Lemma 46.3 forces the pattern (σ, 3σ, −2σ, −2σ), with exactly one 3σ-end.
The type-3 pattern is (σ, σ, σ, −3σ), and every cubic vertex has pattern (σ, σ, −2σ), each
with its own sign σ. These describe every vertex.
   Let J consist of the edges valued ±1, ±3. Its degrees are four at type-3 vertices and two at
all other vertices, so J is even, and every edge of an even graph lies on a cycle of it. Choose
a cycle in J containing the unique edge of value 3 or −3 at a type-3 vertex v (this is e).
Its other edge at v is small, of outward value σ. Direct the cycle out along this edge and
add −2σ. Every traversal value on it is ±1 or ±3, so its new value is among ±1, ±3, ±5, all
nonzero modulo k ≥ 8. At v the changed small end has value −σ, balancing an untouched
small end. This is one legal restricted step, a contradiction.                               □
   Two local thresholds are worth identifying. Lemma 49.2 uses 6a ̸= 0, which fails at k = 6;
for the modulus six another local argument is given in Lemma 56.2. The proof just given
needs k ≥ 8 to exclude an odd sum of magnitude at most seven: at k = 7, 1 + 2 + 2 + 2 can
vanish. The contraction input in Part A separately requires group order at least seven for a
four-cycle.

                 52. Parallel edges and the finite integer bases
Lemma 52.1. If a loopless degree-four vertex is incident with two parallel edges, every
modular flow reaches a balanced pair there by restricted steps for k ≥ 7.
Proof. Let x, y be their outward values and a, b the other two outward values. On the digon,
the total t = −a − x changes them to x + t = −a and y − t = −b, by conservation. Both are
nonzero. There are at most two forbidden totals on this digon, so the cyclic step-one/step-two
graph of legal totals is connected, as in Lemma 48.1. A restricted path reaches this target. If
t = 0, the pair is already balanced. The four incident edges are distinct, even when additional
parallel edges occur.                                                                        □
Lemma 52.2. Let G be connected and loopless with exactly six odd vertices. If two cubic
vertices are joined by parallel edges, its modular flows are connected by restricted steps and
its integer flows are connected for every k ≥ 7.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                           85


Proof. A triple parallel edge would be a two-vertex component, impossible under the odd
count. Write the two outside legs of the parallel pair uv as pu, vq, allowing p = q. Replace
these four edges and u, v by a single edge pq, a loop when p = q. The reduced connected
graph has four odd vertices and unchanged degrees at all remaining vertices. Theorem 46.4
gives restricted modular connectivity there.
   Orient the legs from p to u and from v to q, and both internal edges from u to v. Their
outside values agree at some nonzero value f , and the internal values are x, f − x. Each
flow of G therefore projects to a reduced flow with value f on pq, and each reduced flow
extends by choosing x ∈ / {0, f }. The allowed fibre parameters omit 0, f , hence are connected
by restricted digon steps. Before a reduced step changes f to f ′ , choose x ∈/ {0, f, f ′ } within
the fibre. Hold that internal edge fixed, and lift the reduced cycle through the other internal
edge and the two legs. Its coefficient is unchanged and its endpoint values are nonzero. If
p = q, the loop becomes a triangle. Moves avoiding pq lift unchanged. Connecting the fibres
between such lifts proves restricted connectivity upstairs. If the flow spaces are empty there
is nothing to prove. Lemma 46.7 now transfers this restricted modular connectivity to integer
connectivity, by joining consecutive lift choices inside their fibres.                           □
Lemma 52.3. Let G be a simple bridgeless graph of degrees three and four with exactly six
cubic vertices. Its integer k-flow graph is connected if k = 7 and |V (G)| ≤ 14, or if k ≥ 8
and |V (G)| ≤ 13.
Proof. In both cases modular connectivity follows from Lemma 47.1. At k = 7 every cycle
has at most fourteen edges, hence at most twenty-one. The direct-lift clause of Theorem 46.8,
together with the connected fibres of Lemma 46.7, transfers modular paths to integer paths.
   For k ≥ 8, put n = |V (G)| ≤ 13. Suppose a cycle of length L ≥ 10 has no edge joining
two nonneighboring cycle vertices. Since G is simple, every incident edge other than the two
cycle edges goes outside the cycle. At most six cycle vertices are cubic; the remaining cycle
vertices have degree four. Thus at least 2L − 6 edges go outside. The total outside degree is
at most 4(n − L), giving
                          2L − 6 ≤ 4(n − L),        6L ≤ 4n + 6 ≤ 58,
contrary to L ≥ 10. Every cycle longer than nine consequently has a nonneighbor chord.
The component-bijection clause of Theorem 46.8 proves the result.                   □

               53. Integer balanced families and arbitrary degrees
Proposition 53.1. The integer conclusion holds for graphs of maximum degree four.
Proof. Induct on edge count. Remove isolated vertices and independent loops, and use
products for separate components. Bridges give empty spaces, so we may assume that G has
no bridge; degree-two suppression preserves integer bounds and cycle moves. Theorem 46.4
handles at most four odd vertices per component. The remaining graph is connected, loopless,
of degrees three and four, with exactly six cubic vertices.
   A parallel pair between cubic vertices is handled outright by Lemma 52.2. A parallel pair
incident with a degree-four vertex gives restricted local entry by Lemma 52.1. If no parallel
pair exists, the graph is simple. Apply Lemma 52.3 for order at most fourteen at k = 7, or
at most thirteen at k ≥ 8. At larger orders, Propositions 49.4 and 51.1 give restricted local
entry.
   It remains to justify precisely how local entry reaches an integer balanced family. Given an
integer k-flow F , follow a restricted modular path from its reduction to a flow g balanced on
86                                        DAVE GOEL


a chosen pair. Lemma 46.7 supplies adjacent integer lifts at each step and connected fibres
between choices, so F reaches some integer lift of g. Split that modular balanced pair. The
resulting modular flow has an integer k-lift on the split graph. Lift it back by Lemma 46.1;
this gives an integer -balanced lift of g in G. The connected fibre over g joins the previous
lift to this one. Modular balance alone was not treated as integer balance.
    Each nonempty integer balanced family comes from a smaller split graph. It is bridgeless
because it supports a flow, retains the same six labelled odd vertices, and stays within
maximum degree four. Induction and balanced splitting join all members of this family in
the ambient integer flow graph. Any two such split graphs have a common feasible pattern,
with anchors of magnitudes one through four. Splitting lifts these anchors preserving their
positive unit divergences. Lemma 50.1 joins the lifted anchors by integer moves within those
same magnitude bounds, which are valid for every k ≥ 7. All families therefore join, and
every integer flow reaches one of them.                                                    □
Proof of Theorem 45.2. Remove loops and use induction on the same degree excess Ψ(G) as
in Part A, starting with Proposition 53.1. Use the same even and odd triangle expansions,
which lower Ψ and preserve the odd count of each component.
   We do not presume that a prescribed integer flow extends with its outside values fixed.
Instead, given a downstairs integer k-flow F , reduce it to f , extend f modularly over the
triangle by Lemma 50.3, and choose an integer k-lift upstairs by Lemma 46.7. Its projection
F ′ is a downstairs integer k-flow with reduction f . The connected downstairs fibre joins F
to F ′ . Perform this construction at each of two desired endpoints.
   Induction joins the chosen upstairs integer lifts. Project their path by the integer clause of
Lemma 50.3: the edge-disjoint cycle decomposition uses the same integer coefficient, and every
intermediate retained value is old or final, hence within the required bounds. Concatenate
the two downstairs fibre paths with this projected path. Restore the independent loop
coordinates. This proves the integer conclusion without an integer contraction theorem or
an integer extension-surjectivity assumption.                                                  □

     54. Six odd-degree vertices at modulus six: statement and conventions
   Graphs are finite multigraphs, with loops and parallel edges allowed. A loop contributes
two to degree. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph, including a loop or two parallel
edges. Fix a reference direction on every edge. Divergence is outgoing sum minus incoming
sum at a vertex; a loop contributes zero. A circulation has divergence zero. For a directed
traversal of a cycle C, its signed vector χC has entries 0, 1, −1.
   A nowhere-zero Z6 -flow is a circulation taking nonzero values in Z6 . Its reconfiguration
graph M6 (G) has these flows as vertices, with an edge for each legal step f 7→ f + tχC ,
where t ∈ Z6 \ {0} and both endpoints are nowhere-zero. An integer 6-flow is an integer
circulation F with 1 ≤ |F (e)| ≤ 5 on every edge. Its graph I6 (G) uses nonzero integer cycle
increments with valid endpoints. These are the usual flow reconfiguration graphs of [3] on
loopless graphs, extended by treating loops as cycles. A graph with zero or one flow counts
as flow-connected. Bridges give empty nowhere-zero flow spaces; an edgeless graph has the
unique empty flow. Two modular flows are joined if they belong to the same component of
M6 (G), with every nonzero modular increment permitted.
Theorem 54.1. Let G be a finite multigraph with at most six odd-degree vertices in each
connected component. Then M6 (G) and I6 (G) are connected.
                THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        87


   Theorem 36.1 and Theorems 45.1 and 45.2 treat the same graph class at modulus five and
at every modulus at least seven, respectively. Theorem 54.1 supplies modulus six; with those
theorems, under the inputs named there, it gives both modular and integer flow connectivity
for six odd vertices per component at every k ≥ 5.
Scope. The theorem is for the cyclic group Z6 and the integer 6-flows, with steps of any
nonzero value. Eight odd vertices per component are the subject of Theorem 61.1 at modulus
six, and of Theorem 71.1 for the Zk -flows at every modulus from thirteen on; more odd
vertices at larger moduli are the subject of Theorem 76.1. The proof uses no result of
Cranston et al. as an input. The expansion of a vertex into a triangle in Section 59 is their
method [2, Observation 4.6], and we give the argument in full. In particular the proof does
not use the bounded-order reduction or the contraction input of the sections that prove
Theorems 45.1 and 45.2.

          55. Inputs from earlier sections and from a companion text
   For a nonloop edge incident with v, its outward value is its value read away from v. A
balanced pair consists of two distinct incident nonloop edges with outward sum zero.
Lemma 55.1 (Balanced splitting; Lemma 11.1, its proof and the paragraph after it). In a
loopless graph replace distinct edges uv, vw by uw, retaining v; u = w is allowed and gives a
loop. Copying the new edge’s value along u → v → w identifies flows of the split graph with
flows balanced on the specified pair in the original graph. This holds over Z6 and over the
integers. A cycle step downstairs lifts to a sequence of cycle steps upstairs with the same
coefficient. The edge count decreases by one, the degree at v decreases by two, and all other
degrees and all degree parities are unchanged. For integer flows the correspondence preserves
magnitudes and the unit divergence of the positive orientation.
  The positive orientation directs an edge in the direction of its positive integer value. Its
unit map 1D puts value one along each edge, so div 1D is outdegree minus indegree.
Theorem 55.2 (Four odd vertices; Theorem 29.1). If each component of a finite multigraph
has at most four odd-degree vertices, its nowhere-zero Z6 -flows are connected by steps with
increments 1, −1, 2, −2, and its integer 6-flows are connected.
Definition 55.3. Let H be bridgeless with exactly six labelled odd vertices, forming a set X.
A pattern is a three-element subset P ⊂ X. P Put βP = 1 on P , βP = −1 on X \ P , and zero
elsewhere. For W ⊆ V (H) put βP (W ) = v∈W βP (v), and let δH (W ) consist of edges with
exactly one endpoint in W . A pattern is feasible if βP sums to zero on each component and
|βP (W )| < |δH (W )| whenever that cut is nonempty. An anchor of pattern P is an integer
circulation of magnitudes one through four whose positive orientation has unit divergence
βP . Feasibility is not part of the anchor definition itself.
Lemma 55.4 (Pattern intersection and anchor existence; Definition 38.1, Lemmas 38.4
and 39.1). Two bridgeless multigraphs with the same six labelled odd vertices share a feasible
pattern. Every feasible pattern has an anchor. Loops and disconnected graphs are allowed.
Theorem 55.5 (Component correspondence at six, [6, Theorem 2.1]). For every finite
multigraph, reduction modulo six induces a bijection between the connected components of its
integer 6-flow graph and its nowhere-zero Z6 -flow graph.
88                                        DAVE GOEL


   This last input allows paths between lifting fibres for an increment of three; the proof
uses these paths, not adjacent integer endpoint lifts of each such step. The four imported
statements (Lemmas 55.1 and 55.4, Theorems 55.2 and 55.5) are proved in the cited places.
We use standard cycle decompositions of even graphs and integer circulations, the product
description of the flow graph of a disconnected graph, and connectivity of products of
connected graphs. All further arguments are given below.

                              56. A local parity argument
   All arithmetic in this section is modulo six unless another modulus is specified. A small
value means 1 or −1.
Lemma 56.1. A zero-sum quadruple of nonzero residues with no opposite pair is, up to
permutation, one of
            (a, 3a, −2a, −2a),     (a, a, 2a, 2a),    (a, a, a, 3a),   a ∈ {1, −1}.
Call these types 1, 2, 3, respectively, according to the number of small entries.
Proof. There is at most one entry equal to three, since two would be opposite. All small
entries have the same sign, and all entries from {2, 4} have the same value, for the same
reason. With no small entry, reduction modulo two forces an even number of threes, hence
zero; four equal values from {2, 4} do not sum to zero. Four small entries would sum to
4a ̸= 0. With one small entry, parity forces exactly one three, and conservation forces the
two even entries to be −2a. With two small entries, parity excludes a three and conservation
forces both even entries to be 2a. With three small entries, the last is −3a = 3a. These
exhaust the cases.                                                                         □
Lemma 56.2 (Two moves to balance). Let G be loopless, let f be a nowhere-zero Z6 -flow,
and let v have degree four. In at most two cycle steps, f reaches a flow with a balanced pair
at v. There is no restriction on other vertex degrees or on the number of odd-degree vertices.
Proof. If v is already balanced there is nothing to do. Otherwise use Lemma 56.1. Let O be
the spanning subgraph of odd-valued edges, those with values 1, 3, 5. Reduction modulo two
shows that O is even. Every edge of O therefore lies on a cycle of O. An increment of 2 or
−2 on such a cycle is always legal: all its values remain odd and hence nonzero.
   Let H be obtained by deleting the edges of value three. The reduction of f modulo three
is a nowhere-zero Z3 -flow on H.
   Suppose first that v has type 2, with outward values (a, a, 2a, 2a). Since G is loopless,
the four ends belong to four distinct edges, with their other endpoints different from v. All
four edges lie in H. There is a cycle in H using one a-end and one 2a-end at v. Indeed, if a
component of H − v is incident with both kinds, a path inside it gives such a cycle (if the
two edges have the same other end, they form a digon). Otherwise take the union U of all
components incident with an a-end. Both a-edges go into U by its definition, and no 2a-edge
does by the supposition that no component meets both kinds. No edge of H − v leaves this
union of components. Thus its cut in H consists exactly of the two a-edges. Their sum read
away from v is 2a ̸= 0 modulo three, contrary to flow conservation across that cut. Direct
the resulting cycle out along an a-end and back along a 2a-end, and add three. This is legal
on every edge of H, because none has value three and 3 = −3. The changed outward values
are a + 3 = −2a and 2a − 3 = −a. The latter balances the untouched a-end.
   For type 3, choose an O-cycle through the unique value-three edge at v. Its other edge
there has outward value a. Direct the cycle out along this small edge and add −2a. The
                  THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                            89


two changed outward values are a − 2a = −a and 3a + 2a = 5a = −a. Each balances an
untouched a-end. This is one legal step.
   For type 1, choose an O-cycle through its value-three edge. Since v has degree two in O,
the other cycle edge is its unique a-edge. Direct the cycle out along that edge and add −2a.
The two changed outward values are −a and 3a + 2a = 5a = −a, and the untouched values
are −2a, −2a. This is type 2 with small sign −a. Apply the type-2 argument to the new
flow and its new subgraph H. Thus at most two moves suffice in every case.                □

                              57. The six-vertex cubic bases
Lemma 57.1. The graph M6 (K3,3 ) is connected.
Proof. Orient edges from the three row vertices to the three column vertices. A flow is a 3 by
3 matrix of nonzero residues whose row and column sums vanish modulo six. Its odd-valued
subgraph O has degree zero or two everywhere. Since K3,3 is simple and bipartite, O is
empty, a four-cycle or a six-cycle.
   If O is empty, each value is two or four. At a cubic vertex, three such outward values sum
to zero only if they are equal. Connectedness then forces the whole matrix to be constant
two or constant four.
   If O is a four-cycle, it is a rectangle of the matrix. All five entries outside it equal 2a
for some a ∈ {1, −1}: the row and column vertices outside the cycle each have three even
entries, which are equal, and their common edge identifies the two values. The rectangle
consequently has the form
                                                 
                                     x     4a − x
                                                    ,    x odd.
                                  4a − x      x
An even increment 0, 2 or 4 around the rectangle makes x = −a; all four entries stay odd
throughout this one step, or no step when the increment is zero. All four then equal −a.
Adding three on the rectangle makes them all 2a, so the full matrix is constant.
    If O is a six-cycle, the three remaining edges form a perfect matching joining opposite
vertices of that cycle, each with value two or four. Direct O cyclically, write its vertices as
v0 , . . . , v5 , and let qi ∈ Z3 be the traversal value on vi vi+1 reduced modulo three; indices are
modulo six, and qi may be zero. If mi is the matching edge’s outward value at vi , conservation
gives qi − qi−1 = −mi modulo three. Since mi is nonzero modulo three, consecutive qi are
distinct. Some three consecutive qi use only two residues. Otherwise every triple uses all three
and the sequence has period three. But the matching edge joins vi to vi+3 , so conservation
at its two ends gives
                                        qi+3 − qi+2 = −(qi − qi−1 ).
Period three would make the left side equal to qi − qi−1 , a nonzero residue; twice a nonzero
residue cannot vanish in Z3 .
    Choose the three-edge path with only two residues, and add an even increment 0, 2 or 4
around O (these are 0, 2, 1 modulo three) that sends the missing third residue to zero modulo
three. The six cycle edges stay odd and nonzero, and the chosen path now avoids value three.
Its endpoints are joined by a matching edge, giving a four-cycle all of whose values avoid
three. Add three on this four-cycle. The step is legal and toggles the edge parities there.
The odd support is now the other three-edge half of O together with that matching edge, a
four-cycle. The preceding case therefore reaches a constant matrix.
90                                         DAVE GOEL


     Finally the two constant matrices are joined by the explicit path
                                                                    
                   2 2 2          3 2 1             1 4 1            4 4 4
                 2 2 2 −→ 1 3 2 −→ 1 1 4 −→ 4 4 4 .
                   2 2 2          2 1 3             4 1 1            4 4 4
The successive increments are 1, 2, 3 on cycle vectors C1 , C2 , C1 , where
                                                                      
                           1     0 −1                    −1 1          0
                   C1 = −1 1          0 ,      C2 =  0 −1 1  .
                           0 −1 1                          1      0 −1
Both are directed six-cycle vectors. All displayed entries are nonzero and all row and
column sums vanish modulo six. This is a hand proof; no enumeration of the flow space is
required.                                                                             □
Lemma 57.2. The triangular prism has connected modular 6-flow graph.
Proof. Contract one triangular face to obtain K4 , whose connectivity follows from Theo-
rem 55.2. We give the lifting argument directly. Orient the replaced triangle v1 v2 v3 v1 . If βi
is the prescribed external outward divergence at vi , then β1 + β2 + β3 = 0, and the triangle
values can be z, z − β2 , z + β1 . At most three choices of z are forbidden, so every downstairs
flow extends. Any two extensions differ on this triangle and are equal or joined by a single
triangle move.
   A downstairs cycle avoiding the contracted vertex lifts unchanged. One through that
vertex uses two outside edges. Their ports upstairs are different, since each triangle vertex
has just one outside edge. Join those ports by the direct triangle edge. To lift the prescribed
step, require the three initial triangle values and the final value on that one triangle edge
to be nonzero. At most four parameters are forbidden, fewer than six. Choose such an
extension within the fibre, then lift the step. Joining fibres between these lifted steps proves
connectivity upstairs. No outside contraction theorem is used.                                 □
Lemma 57.3. Let G be connected and loopless with exactly six odd-degree vertices. If two
cubic vertices are joined by parallel edges, then M6 (G) is connected.
Proof. A triple parallel edge would form a two-vertex component, impossible here. Write the
two outside legs of the double edge uv as pu, vq, allowing p = q; both p, q are different from
u, v, since the graph is loopless and there is no triple edge. Delete u, v and those four edges,
replacing them by pq, a loop when p = q. The reduced graph is connected with four odd
vertices, and all remaining degrees are unchanged. Its flows are connected by Theorem 55.2.
   Orient the legs p → u and v → q, and both internal edges u → v. Their outside values
agree at some nonzero f and the internal values are x, f − x. Each flow projects to the
reduced graph, and each reduced flow extends by choosing x ∈    / {0, f }. The fibres are cliques
under digon moves. Before a reduced step changes f to f ′ , choose x ∈      / {0, f, f ′ }; such a
choice exists modulo six. Hold that internal edge fixed and lift the step along the other
internal edge and both outside legs. All values at both endpoints are nonzero. If p = q, the
loop lifts to a triangle. Moves avoiding pq lift unchanged. Joining fibres between lifts gives
the assertion. This is the parallel-pair reduction of Lemma 37.5, with the modulus changed
to six and the same parameter argument given explicitly.                                        □
Proposition 57.4. Every connected loopless cubic graph with six vertices has connected
modular 6-flow graph.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          91


Proof. Bridges give empty spaces. A loopless multigraph is either simple or has a parallel
pair. Three parallel edges at cubic vertices would form a two-vertex component, excluded
by connectedness and order six. Parallel pairs are handled by Lemma 57.3. Otherwise the
graph is simple and its simple complement is 2-regular on six vertices, hence a six-cycle or
two disjoint triangles. The original graph is respectively the triangular prism or K3,3 . Apply
Lemmas 57.2 and 57.1.                                                                        □

                           58. Connecting balanced families
  At a loopless degree-four vertex, a pairing partitions its four distinct incident edges into
two unordered pairs. There are three pairings. Its balanced family consists of the flows for
which each of these two pairs is balanced. If one pair is balanced, conservation forces the
other to be balanced too. Splitting either pair therefore identifies the split graph’s flow space
with that family.
Lemma 58.1. Let H be a bridgeless multigraph with six labelled odd vertices. Two anchors
of the same pattern in H have joined reductions modulo six.
Proof. We give the bounded-circulation argument of the proof of Lemma 39.2 at the present
modulus. The positive orientations have equal unit divergence. Their disagreement arcs,
oriented as in the first orientation, form a balanced directed subgraph: its signed vector is
half the difference of the two unit maps. Decompose it into edge-disjoint directed cycles.
On each such cycle subtract b, where b is one plus its largest current positive value. Then
2 ≤ b ≤ 5, and the new values are negative with magnitudes one through four. This reverses
the cycle’s positive orientation and preserves an integer circulation. Later disagreement
cycles remain directed since their edge sets are disjoint.
   Once orientations agree, decompose the difference of the integer flows conformally into
signed unit cycles. Each successive unit step keeps every coordinate between its same-sign
endpoints. All magnitudes remain one through four. Reducing the entire path modulo six
gives legal moves: the coefficients −b and the unit coefficients are nonzero modulo six, and
no endpoint value is zero.                                                                 □
Proposition 58.2. If a finite multigraph has maximum degree at most four and at most six
odd vertices per component, then its modular 6-flow graph is connected.
Proof. Induct on edge count within the stated class. Remove loops first, using their indepen-
dent nonzero coordinates, and discard isolated vertices. If several components with edges
remain, use their product; each has fewer edges. Bridges give empty spaces, so assume there
is no bridge. A degree-two vertex now has two distinct edges. Suppress it by replacing those
edges with one edge: conservation gives equal values along the path, so this identifies flows
and cycle moves, also when the replacement edge is a loop. The edge count falls and degree
parities are unchanged; if a loop is created, it is removed next by the same loop reduction.
   We may assume that G is connected, loopless, of degrees three and four. With at most
four odd vertices use Theorem 55.2. Otherwise there are exactly six cubic vertices. If every
vertex is cubic, apply Proposition 57.4. Otherwise fix any degree-four vertex. Lemma 56.2
sends each flow into a balanced-pair family at that vertex.
   Every nonempty family corresponds by Lemma 55.1 to a smaller split graph, still of
maximum degree at most four with the same six labelled odd vertices. It is bridgeless because
it supports a nowhere-zero flow. It may be disconnected, but every component has at most
92                                         DAVE GOEL


six odd vertices. Induction joins the flows of the split graph, and lifting joins the family by
legal steps in the ambient graph, by Lemma 55.1.
   The split graphs are bridgeless but may be disconnected. The pattern intersection and
anchor-existence lemmas (Lemmas 38.4 and 39.1), restated in Lemma 55.4, apply in precisely
this setting. Any two split graphs have a common feasible pattern. Choose an anchor in each
and lift them by balanced splitting, preserving magnitudes and positive unit divergences.
Each lifted anchor lies in its balanced family because it comes from that family’s split graph.
Their reductions are joined by Lemma 58.1. Thus all nonempty families join, and every flow
reaches one of them.                                                                         □

                       59. Arbitrary degrees and integer flows
                                                                  P
Proof of Theorem 54.1. Remove loops and induct on Ψ(G) = v max{deg(v) − 4, 0}. Propo-
sition 58.2 is the zero case. At an even vertex of degree d ≥ 6, expand into a triangle with
external end groups of sizes 2, 2, d − 4. Its degrees are 4, 4, d − 2, lowering Ψ by two. At an
odd vertex of degree d ≥ 5, use groups 1, 2, d − 3, giving degrees 3, 4, d − 1 and lowering Ψ
by one. Each component remains connected and its odd count is unchanged.
   Every prescribed modular flow extends across the triangle: with external outward diver-
gences βi of sum zero, choose the triangle values z, z − β2 , z + β1 as in the prism argument.
There are at most three forbidden residues, fewer than six. After contraction, the restriction
of an upstairs cycle vector to the retained edges is an integer circulation with entries 0, 1, −1.
Orient its support by its signs and decompose it into edge-disjoint directed cycles. Applying
the same modular coefficient to these cycles in turn keeps each retained edge at its old or
final nonzero value at every prefix. Thus any upstairs cycle step projects to a downstairs
path, possibly stationary. Extend two desired endpoints, join them upstairs by induction,
and project. Only the implication from connectivity of the expanded graph to connectivity
of the original graph is used. Restore the independent loop coordinates. This proves modular
connectivity. The expansion is the method of Cranston et al. [2, Observation 4.6]. This
extension/projection is also the direct argument of Section 44; no outside contraction theorem
is assumed.
   Finally Theorem 55.5 transfers modular connectivity to integer connectivity on the same
graph. In particular, the increments of three used above are covered by that theorem’s paths
between lifting fibres; we do not assume direct adjacent lifts for them. This proves both
assertions.                                                                                     □
A computer check. As supplementary evidence only, a program checks the parity reduction
on all 200 modular flows of K3,3 , the displayed three-step bridge, and the degree-four
arithmetic types; none of the arguments above depends on that enumeration.
