Part 5. Eight vertices of odd degree at the modulus six
  Section 60 derives Proposition 60.1 from the pairing theorem of Nash-Williams [9]. The
seven sections after it prove part (d) of Theorem 1.3; for the integer flows they use [6,
Theorem 2.1].

60. Patterns for any number of odd-degree vertices: classical pairing and
                                 the cube
   All graphs are finite multigraphs, with loops and parallel edges allowed. A loop contributes
two to degree and nothing to a cut. Write dH (W ) = |δH (W )| for the number of edges with
exactly one end in W . Suppose the odd-degree vertex set is X, where |X| = 2r. A pattern is
a subset P ⊆ X ofPsize r. Its sign function βP equals 1 on P , −1 on X \ P , and 0 elsewhere.
Write βP (W ) = v∈W βP (v). The pattern is feasible in H if βP sums to zero on every
connected component and
              |βP (W )| < dH (W )     for every vertex set W with dH (W ) > 0.
Denote the family of feasible patterns by Φ(H).
   Fix a reference direction for every edge. An integer circulation assigns integer edge values
with outgoing sum equal to incoming sum at every vertex; loops contribute zero to divergence.
For an integer k ≥ 2, an integer k-flow is such a circulation F with 1 ≤ |F (e)| ≤ k − 1 for
every edge e. An integer k-anchor of pattern P is an integer k-flow whose positive orientation
D has unit divergence βP : at every vertex, outdegree minus indegree in D equals βP . The
positive orientation directs each edge in the direction of its positive flow value; a loop has
zero divergence. Feasibility of P is not part of the definition of an anchor.
Proposition 60.1 (A consequence of Nash-Williams’ theorem). Let H1 , H2 be bridgeless
multigraphs with the same odd-degree vertex set X. Their even-degree vertices may differ. If
X is not empty, then |Φ(H1 ) ∩ Φ(H2 )| ≥ 2. If X is empty, both families contain the empty
pattern.
Proof. We use the following classical consequence of the odd-vertex pairing theorem: in a
connected bridgeless graph H, the odd vertices admit an auxiliary perfect matching M such
that
(12)                     dM (W ) ≤ dH (W ) − 2 (∅ ̸= W ⊊ V (H)).
Here dM (W ) counts the pairs of M with exactly one end in W ; the matching pairs need
not be edges of H. For the precise source, see Nash-Williams [9, Theorem 2 on p. 557, with
the definitions on pp. 555–557]. There the reduced cut degree is dH (W ) − dM (W ), and
reduced local connectivity is its minimum over sets separating a given pair of vertices. The
theorem gives a pairing for which this minimum equals the original local edge connectivity
rounded down to an even integer. In a connected bridgeless graph that integer is at least
two. Taking any vertices on opposite sides of W proves (12). See also [1, Theorem 3.4 and
the introduction’s discussion of 1-feasible pairings in 2-edge-connected graphs], which gives
the pairing inequality in this form. The original source excludes loops, so delete them before
applying the theorem; this preserves all cuts and degree parities. The one-vertex case has
empty odd set and requires no cut inequality.
  Choose such a matching separately in every connected component of H. Every transversal
P choosing one endpoint of each matching pair is feasible. Indeed, pairs entirely inside W
94                                        DAVE GOEL


contribute opposite signs, and only crossing pairs can contribute to βP (W ). For a partially
intersected component C, with its chosen matching MC , (12) gives
       |βP (W ∩ V (C))| ≤ dMC (W ∩ V (C)) ≤ dC (W ∩ V (C)) − 2 < dC (W ∩ V (C)).
A whole component has sign sum zero. Summing over components proves the required strict
inequality whenever the full cut is nonempty, since at least one component is then partially
intersected. Components without odd vertices use the empty matching.
   Apply this construction to H1 , H2 , giving perfect matchings M1 , M2 on the same set X.
Their multiset union is a disjoint union of even alternating cycles, with a common pair
counted twice. Give successive vertices of each cycle opposite signs. The plus vertices form a
transversal of both matchings and have size |X|/2, so they give a common feasible pattern.
The complementary pattern is also common and is distinct when X ̸= ∅. If X is empty,
every sign sum is zero, which directly proves the last assertion.                           □
   For bridgeless graphs with four odd-degree vertices, the definition here gives the same
patterns as Definition 27.1, which is worded using cuts of two edges. Thus the elementary
four- and six-terminal intersection arguments in Lemma 27.3 with Corollary 27.4 and in
Lemma 38.4 prove special cases of a consequence of classical pairing theory. The number of
terminals does not obstruct this common-pattern step. The next proposition concerns the
stronger requirement of an anchor.
Proposition 60.2 (A feasible cube pattern without a 5-anchor). Let Q be the cube on binary
triples, adjacent when they differ in one coordinate, and let P = {000, 100, 010, 001}. Then
P ∈ Φ(Q), but there is no integer 5-anchor of pattern P .
Proof. The cube is connected, bridgeless, simple and cubic. All eight vertices are odd. Both
P and its complement induce three-edge stars, and the cut between them has six edges.
   To check feasibility it suffices, by taking complements, to examine sets W with at most four
vertices. For |W | = 1, the cut size is three and the absolute sign sum is one. For |W | = 2, at
most one edge is internal, so dQ (W ) ≥ 4 > |βP (W )|. For |W | = 3, triangle-freeness permits
at most two internal edges, giving dQ (W ) ≥ 5 > 3 ≥ |βP (W )|. For |W | = 4, bipartiteness
permits at most four internal edges, so dQ (W ) ≥ 4. Unless W is P or its complement, its
absolute sign sum is at most two. For those two exceptions the cut size is six and the absolute
sign sum is four. The empty and whole sets have sign sum zero. All required conditions
follow.
   Suppose an integer 5-anchor exists. Let o, i count the outgoing and incoming edges of
δQ (P ) in its positive orientation. Unit divergence and cut size give o − i = 4 and o + i = 6,
hence o = 5 and i = 1. Conservation requires the single incoming magnitude to equal the
sum of five outgoing magnitudes. The incoming magnitude is at most four; the outgoing
sum is at least five. This is impossible.                                                     □
Remark 60.3 (Necessary cut capacity). More generally, let H be a finite multigraph, let
k ≥ 2 be an integer, and let F be an integer k-flow of H. Let D be its positive orientation
         Pβ(v) as outdegree minus indegree at v in D. Then for every vertex set W , with
and define
β(W ) = v∈W β(v),
                                             k−2
(13)                               |β(W )| ≤       dH (W ).
                                              k
To prove this, let o, i be its outgoing and incoming edge counts. Conservation and the
magnitude bounds 1, . . . , k−1 imply o ≤ (k−1)i and i ≤ (k−1)o. Substituting o+i = dH (W )
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                       95


and o − i = β(W ) gives (13). For the cube cut in Proposition 60.2, the necessary 5-flow
inequality would be 4 ≤ 18/5, which fails.
   The six-terminal anchor construction in Lemma 39.1 adds three directed correction paths
to a unit edge map, keeping magnitudes between one and four. With eight terminals the
analogous four paths give magnitudes between one and five only. Proposition 60.2 shows
that some feasible patterns admit no integer 5-anchor at all, regardless of how one tries to
construct it. It therefore prevents a direct extension of that anchor lemma.

 61. Eight odd-degree vertices at modulus six: statement, conventions and
                                    inputs
   Graphs are finite multigraphs, allowing loops and parallel edges. A loop contributes two
to degree and zero to divergence. Fix a reference direction on each edge. Divergence means
outgoing sum minus incoming sum. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph, including a
loop or a pair of parallel edges. Its directed traversal vector χC has entries 0, 1, −1.
   A nowhere-zero Z6 -flow is a circulation with all edge values nonzero. Its reconfiguration
graph M6 (G) joins two flows when their difference is tχC for a cycle C and t ∈ Z6 \ {0}. An
integer 6-flow is an integer circulation F with 1 ≤ |F (e)| ≤ 5; the graph I6 (G) uses integer
cycle increments with valid endpoints. These are the flow reconfiguration graphs of Esperet
et al. [3] on loopless graphs, extended here by treating loops as cycles. A flow space with
zero or one element counts as connected. In particular bridges give empty spaces, and an
edgeless graph has the single empty flow. We say two modular flows are joined when they
are in the same component of M6 (G).
Theorem 61.1. If each component of a finite multigraph G has at most eight odd-degree
vertices, then M6 (G) and I6 (G) are connected.
  This theorem concerns modulus six only. Several paths given here use increments of three.
  We use the following precise results of earlier sections and of one companion text. Their
proofs are not repeated here; all other reductions and cubic arguments appear below. An
outward value at a vertex is an edge value read away from that vertex. A balanced pair
consists of distinct nonloop incident edges whose outward values sum to zero.
   (1) The six-odd-vertex theorem (Theorem 54.1) gives modular and integer connectivity
        at six when each component has at most six odd vertices.
   (2) The universal local lemma (Lemma 56.2) sends any nowhere-zero modular 6-flow on a
        loopless graph to a balanced pair at any prescribed degree-four vertex in at most two
        cycle moves. There is no bound on the other degrees or the number of odd vertices.
   (3) Balanced splitting (Lemma 11.1, its proof and the paragraph after it) replaces distinct
        edges uv, vw in a loopless graph by uw, retaining v and allowing u = w. Copying the
        replacement value along u → v → w identifies its flows with the flows balanced on
        the chosen pair. A downstairs cycle step lifts to a sequence with the same coefficient.
        The edge count falls by one, the degree of v falls by two, and other degrees and all
        degree parities are unchanged. Integer magnitudes and the unit divergence of the
        positive orientation are preserved.
   (4) Any two bridgeless multigraphs with the same labelled odd vertices have a common
        feasible pattern (Proposition 60.1). Their even vertices may differ and the graphs
        may be disconnected. This is a classical consequence of Nash-Williams’ odd-vertex
        pairing theorem [9]; the consequence is derived in Section 60.
96                                        DAVE GOEL


    (5) Reduction modulo six gives a bijection between components of I6 (G) and M6 (G) for
         every finite multigraph [6, Theorem 2.1].
An integer lifting fibre is the set of integer 6-flows reducing to one specified modular flow.
The last input supplies paths between such fibres, including for modular increments of three;
direct adjacent lifts of every such step are not assumed. We use no outside short-cycle
contraction theorem. The triangle expansion method used in Section 67 is due to Cranston
et al. [2, Observation 4.6]; we prove the required extension and projection directly.

                                  62. Parity operations
Lemma 62.1. For a nowhere-zero modular 6-flow f , let q = f (mod 3) and let O be its
odd-valued edge subgraph. The following operations are legal.
    (1) On a cycle in O, adding any even coefficient 0, 2, 4 preserves O and translates its
        traversal residues modulo three by any desired constant. A zero coefficient means no
        step.
    (2) If O′ is an even edge subgraph containing all edges with q(e) = 0, then f is joined
        using increments of three to the unique flow with residue q modulo three and odd
        support O′ .
Proof. Reduction modulo two shows that O is even. Every edge with q(e) = 0 has value
three, so belongs to O. Even shifts keep odd values odd, hence nonzero, and 0, 2, 4 give all
residues modulo three. For the second assertion, O △ O′ is even and has no q-zero edge.
Decompose it into edge-disjoint cycles and add three on each. An affected edge has value
1, 2, 4 or 5, so stays nonzero. Each edge changes at most once. The resulting flow has the
specified residue and parity, which determine its value uniquely by Z6 ∼
                                                                       = Z2 × Z3 .        □
  In a loopless cubic graph O is a disjoint union of cycles, and the q-zero edges form a
matching: two outward values three would force the third to be zero. We also record a useful
obstruction.
Lemma 62.2. A loopless cubic graph supporting a nowhere-zero Z3 -flow is bipartite. A
connected bipartite cubic graph has exactly two such flows, constant 1 and −1 in the orientation
from one bipartition class to the other.
Proof. Three nonzero outward residues sum to zero in Z3 only if they are equal. Their
common signs at the two ends of every edge are opposite. These signs give a bipartition, and
connectedness determines all signs from one choice at one vertex. Conversely the two stated
constant maps satisfy conservation.                                                        □

                            63. A hand proof for the cube
Lemma 63.1. The cube Q3 has connected modular 6-flow graph.
Proof. Orient the edges from one bipartition class to the other. The odd support O is empty,
a four-cycle, a six-cycle, a Hamiltonian eight-cycle, or two disjoint four-cycles: the cube is
simple and bipartite on eight vertices, so its cycles have lengths four, six or eight, and two
disjoint cycles must both have length four. We treat each possibility.
Empty support. All values are two or four. Cubic conservation forces the three values at each
vertex to agree, and connectedness forces the flow to be constant two or constant four in the
fixed bipartite orientation.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          97


One four-cycle. Every four-cycle is a face: each binary coordinate is used an even number of
times, and a simple four-cycle must use exactly two coordinates twice each. The opposite
face’s vertices have three even incident values each. Equality there and connectedness of that
face force all eight edges outside O to have one value 2a, a ∈ {1, −1}. The four face values
alternate between x and 4a − x, with x odd. An even shift on the face makes x = −a; then
4a − x = 5a = −a, so all four values equal −a. Adding three on the face makes them 2a. We
have reached a constant flow in the fixed bipartite orientation.
A six-cycle with adjacent outside vertices. The five edges incident with the two outside
vertices have one even value 2a. The remaining edge outside O is a chord between opposite
vertices of O: its endpoints cannot be consecutive, and bipartiteness excludes distance two.
Each three-edge half of O between these endpoints has two internal vertices attached to the
outside vertices. If q0 , q1 , q2 are the traversal residues on that half, conservation at its two
internal vertices gives successive differences that are opposites, since their external values
are both 2a in the bipartite orientation. Thus q0 = q2 . Those three edges use at most two
residues. An even shift around O makes all three avoid zero modulo three. The chord is even
and also avoids zero. Adding three on this path and the chord is legal; the new odd support
is the complementary three-edge path and the chord, a four-cycle. Apply the preceding case.
A six-cycle with nonadjacent outside vertices. The two outside vertices lie in opposite
bipartition classes, since O uses three vertices of each class. They must therefore be opposite
vertices of the cube. Choose one, u. Its three neighbors alternate around O, so two of them,
n1 , n2 , bound a two-edge path n1 vn2 of O. Since u is outside O, its two distinct incident
edges to n1 , n2 complete a simple square un1 vn2 u. An even shift around O makes the two
path residues nonzero modulo three. The two edges at u are already even, so adding three
on this square is legal. The odd support is a six-cycle with u replacing v. The new outside
vertices are adjacent: the other original outside vertex is opposite u and adjacent to all other
vertices in u’s bipartition class. Apply the previous case.
A Hamiltonian eight-cycle. In binary coordinates, the cycle uses each coordinate a positive
even number of times. The counts sum to eight, so are 4, 2, 2. Choose a coordinate used
twice, and delete its two crossing edges from O. The remaining support inside each face
meets all four of its vertices and has precisely two degree-one vertices. It is a spanning
three-edge path: an extra cycle would disconnect O, and all other vertices have degree two.
The path’s endpoints are adjacent in the square face; their joining edge is its omitted edge.
The two omitted edges correspond across the chosen coordinate, since the endpoints of the
two crossing edges correspond. Together with those crossing edges they form a square S. An
even shift around O makes the two crossing edges avoid value three. The omitted edges are
even. Adding three on S is therefore legal and changes O to the two opposite faces. Apply
the next case.
Two four-cycles. They are opposite faces joined by four even rungs. Traverse the two faces in
corresponding cyclic directions and write their edge residues as qi , qi′ , with i modulo four.
Conservation at the two ends of a rung gives
                     qi′ − qi−1
                            ′
                                = −(qi − qi−1 ),    qi + qi′ is constant in i.
Every rung is nonzero modulo three, so consecutive residues on either face differ. If a face
uses at most two residues, shift it to avoid zero modulo three, then add three on it. This
leaves a single odd face. Otherwise each face uses all three residues. A proper three-coloring
of a four-cycle repeats exactly one color at opposite positions; index so q0 = q2 and q1 , q3
are the other colors. By independent even shifts, make q0 = q2 = 0 and q1′ = 0. Writing
98                                          DAVE GOEL


the first tuple as (0, α, 0, β), with distinct nonzero α, β ∈ Z3 , the relation above makes the
second tuple (α, 0, α, α − β). Its only zero is at position one. The full zero set of q consists
of these three edges. It lies in the six-cycle consisting of the first face except edge one, edge
one of the second face, and the two endpoint rungs. Lemma 62.1(2) makes that cycle the
odd support. Its outside vertices are adjacent, so this reduces to a preceding case.
Joining the constants. Partition the cube’s edges into coordinate perfect matchings A, B, C.
Each union of two matchings consists of two disjoint squares. Orient the A ∪ B squares
positively on A and negatively on B, and similarly for A ∪ C. Perform the following stages,
where the triples give the reference values on A, B, C:
                     +1 on A−B              −2 on A−C              +3 on A−B
           (2, 2, 2) −−−−−−−−→ (3, 1, 2) −−−−−−−−→ (1, 1, 4) −−−−−−−−→ (4, 4, 4).
Within each stage apply its two edge-disjoint square moves separately. Every edge at each
prefix has its old or its final nonzero value. These six moves join the two constants, completing
the proof.                                                                                     □

                       64. A hand proof for the Wagner graph
   Let W have vertices 0, . . . , 7, outer edges i(i + 1) with indices modulo eight, and diameters
i(i + 4) for 0 ≤ i < 4. It is triangle-free: a triangle could use at most one diameter, while
the endpoints of a diameter have no common outer neighbor; the outer eight-cycle itself
contains no triangle. It has the five-cycle (0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 0), so is not bipartite. Every five-cycle
has exactly one diameter. Indeed, diameters form a matching, so there are at most two in a
five-cycle; the number of outer steps is even, since an outer edge changes the parity of the
vertex label and a diameter does not. Thus the number of diameters is odd. Its other four
edges form a half of the outer eight-cycle. There are exactly eight such five-cycles, permuted
cyclically by the rotation R : i 7→ i + 1.
Lemma 64.1. Every nowhere-zero modular 6-flow on W is joined to one whose odd support
is a five-cycle.
Proof. Use the notation q, O of Lemma 62.1. On a cycle of length L in O, the three even
shifts have exactly L zero residues in total, counting with multiplicity across the three shifted
states: each edge is zero after exactly one shift. One shift therefore leaves at most ⌊L/3⌋
zeros. Since W is simple, triangle-free and has eight vertices, a nonempty O consists of one
cycle of length at most eight or two four-cycles. Independent shifts therefore leave at most
two q-zero edges. There cannot be zero such edges, by Lemma 62.2; this also rules out O
empty. The one or two zero edges form a matching.
   Every edge lies on a five-cycle. Every matching of two edges lies on one as well except
possibly two diameters or two opposite outer edges. To see this, a diameter and any outer
edge lie in one of that diameter’s two half-cycle five-cycles. Two outer edges that are not
opposite fit in a run of four consecutive outer edges. Two diameters cannot lie on one
five-cycle.
   The entire zero set cannot consist of two diameters. Delete those two edges and suppress
their four degree-two endpoints. Up to rotation and reflection the deleted pairs are 04, 15
or 04, 26. In the first case the remaining vertices 2, 3, 6, 7 have all six pairs adjacent after
suppression; in the second the same holds for 1, 3, 5, 7. In both cases the resulting graph
is K4 . The nonzero Z3 -flow on the deleted graph would survive suppression, contrary to
Lemma 62.2 for K4 .
                   THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                         99


   If the zero edges are opposite outer edges, label them 01, 45 using a rotation automorphism
of W ; this relabelling is not a flow move. Lemma 62.1(2) makes the square (0, 1, 5, 4, 0) the
odd support. Its two other traversal residues are nonzero; they are also distinct, for if both
were r ̸= 0, adding the constant r modulo three would send 0, r to r, 2r, both nonzero. By
an even cycle shift this would remove all zeros on the square, giving a nowhere-zero Z3 -flow
on W , impossible. Translate instead to make exactly one of these two distinct residues zero.
The previous two zero residues become nonzero, so only one zero edge remains.
   In every remaining case all zero edges lie in a five-cycle. Use Lemma 62.1(2) to make that
cycle the odd support.                                                                       □
Lemma 64.2. The modular 6-flow graph of W is connected.
Proof. Fix C = (0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 0). The seven edges outside C form a tree; the three vertices
5, 6, 7 outside the cycle form a path. For a flow with odd support C, those seven values are
even. At each outside vertex the three outward even values agree. Opposite signs at the
ends of the edges 56, 67 then determine all seven even values from one choice of sign. For a
fixed choice, the values on C are determined by one odd parameter. There are at most three
choices, and any two differ by an even multiple of χC , so are joined. Negation interchanges
the two external signs.
   Use the reference edge order
                     0 → 1, 1 → 2, . . . , 7 → 0;      0 → 4, 1 → 5, 2 → 6, 3 → 7.
One flow with odd support C is
                                 F = (1, 3, 1, 3, 4, 2, 4, 2; 1, 4, 2, 4).
For example, if xi are the outer values and di the diameter values, conservation reads
xi − xi−1 + di = 0 at 0 ≤ i < 4 and xi − xi−1 − di−4 = 0 at 4 ≤ i < 8. The displayed
entries satisfy these equations modulo six. Its three even shifts on C are all legal, as are their
negatives; thus each fixed-sign family is nonempty and joined to F or −F .
   Rotation acts on flows by carrying edge values with their directions. The image of the
diameter 3 → 7 is read negatively as 0 → 4. Consequently
                                RF = (2, 1, 3, 1, 3, 4, 2, 4; 2, 1, 4, 2).
Here are two explicit paths; the row after each move lists all edge values, so legality can be
checked directly. To join F to RF , use the respective coefficients and cycle traversals
                                        1 (0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 0)
                                        3 (1, 2, 6, 7, 3, 4, 5, 1)
                                        1 (0, 4, 5, 6, 2, 3, 7, 0).
The successive states after F are
                             (2, 4, 2, 4, 5, 3, 5, 3; 1, 4, 2, 4),
                                 (2, 1, 2, 1, 2, 3, 2, 3; 1, 1, 5, 1),
                                 (2, 1, 3, 1, 3, 4, 2, 4; 2, 1, 4, 2) = RF.
To join F to −F , use
                                         3 (0, 1, 5, 6, 2, 3, 7, 0)
                                         2 (0, 7, 6, 5, 4, 0)
                                         1 (0, 1, 5, 6, 2, 3, 7, 0).
100                                          DAVE GOEL


The successive states are
                              (4, 3, 4, 3, 4, 5, 4, 5; 1, 1, 5, 1),
                              (4, 3, 4, 3, 2, 3, 2, 3; 5, 1, 5, 1),
                              (5, 3, 5, 3, 2, 4, 2, 4; 5, 2, 4, 2) = −F.
Every listed traversal is a simple cycle in W . The displayed updates are the indicated signed
cycle additions, and every entry is nonzero. Rotation and negation preserve legal paths; they
are not themselves being presumed to be moves. Applying rotations to these paths joins all
Ri F and −Ri F . Every five-cycle is Ri C, and every flow with that odd support joins one of
these representatives by an even cycle increment. Lemma 64.1 now proves the result.         □

                            65. All cubic eight-vertex bases
Lemma 65.1. A connected loopless cubic graph on eight vertices with a parallel pair has
connected modular 6-flow graph.
Proof. Three parallel edges would form a whole two-vertex component. For a double edge uv,
write the other legs as pu, vq. Delete u, v and their four incident edges and replace them by
pq, allowing p = q and hence a loop. (In this cubic setting, p = q makes the third edge at p a
bridge, so that case has no flow.) The reduced graph is connected with six odd vertices and
unchanged degrees at retained vertices. Its modular flows are connected by Theorem 54.1.
   Orient the legs p → u, v → q and the two internal edges u → v. Conservation makes the
leg values equal to a nonzero f , and the internal values are x, f − x. Thus every original flow
projects and every reduced flow extends by choosing x ∈     / {0, f }. The extension fibre is a
clique under digon moves. For a reduced step changing f to f ′ , choose x ∈ / {0, f, f ′ } and lift
the cycle through the other internal edge and both legs, keeping x fixed. Such an x exists
modulo six. All endpoint values are nonzero. If p = q, the reduced loop lifts to a triangle.
Other steps lift unchanged. Join fibres between steps to obtain connectivity upstairs.           □
Lemma 65.2. A connected simple cubic graph on eight vertices containing a triangle has
connected modular 6-flow graph.
Proof. If it has a bridge there is nothing to prove. Otherwise contract the triangle to one
vertex. This gives a connected loopless cubic multigraph on six vertices: simplicity ensures
that every triangle vertex has exactly one external edge. The quotient is bridgeless. Indeed,
every retained edge originally lies on a cycle, whose image after contraction is a closed trail
containing that edge; it therefore still lies on a cycle. The quotient’s flows are connected by
Theorem 54.1.
   The required triangle lifting is a case of the known triangle contractibility result [2,
Corollary 5.15(ii)]; we give the direct argument. Traverse the triangle v1 v2 v3 v1 and let βi be
the external outward divergence at vi . Its sum is zero. Triangle values may be z, z − β2 , z + β1 ,
so at most three parameters z ∈ Z6 are forbidden. Every quotient flow extends, and its fibre
is a clique under triangle moves. A quotient cycle through the contracted vertex uses two
distinct external edges, whose upstairs ports are distinct because each triangle vertex has
just one external edge. Join those ports by their direct triangle edge. To lift the prescribed
step, require all three initial triangle values and that one edge’s final value to be nonzero.
These exclude at most four parameters, fewer than six. Move within the fibre to such an
extension and lift the step. Cycles avoiding the vertex lift unchanged. This joins all fibres
and proves the assertion without a contraction theorem.                                          □
                  THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                            101


Proposition 65.3. Every connected loopless cubic graph on eight vertices has connected
modular 6-flow graph.
Proof. Bridges give empty spaces. Parallel edges and triangles are covered by the preceding
lemmas, so consider a simple triangle-free graph. Choose a vertex v with neighbors a, b, c,
which are independent. Let X be the four remaining vertices. Exactly six edges join {a, b, c}
to X, so X induces three edges. A triangle-free simple graph on four vertices with three
edges is a tree, hence a star or a path.
   In the star case, its center has no neighbor among a, b, c and each leaf has two. The
bipartite graph between those leaves and a, b, c is simple and 2-regular, so is a six-cycle. The
resulting graph is the cube: its two remaining vertices are the centers v and the star center,
attached to alternating triples of that six-cycle.
   In the path case write X = x1 x2 x3 x4 . The degrees needed toward a, b, c are 2, 1, 1, 2. Each
of a, b, c chooses a nonconsecutive pair of path vertices, to avoid a triangle. The only pairs
are {x1 , x3 }, {x1 , x4 }, {x2 , x4 }. The demands at x2 , x3 force one of each of the last and first
pairs, leaving the middle pair. This determines a unique graph. It is the Wagner graph:
take v = 0, its neighbors 1, 7, 4, and the induced path (x1 , x2 , x3 , x4 ) = (3, 2, 6, 5) in W . The
respective neighbor pairs of 1, 7, 4 are {x2 , x4 }, {x1 , x3 } and {x1 , x4 }, respectively. Apply
Lemmas 63.1 and 64.2.                                                                               □

                 66. Eight-terminal anchors and balanced families
Definition 66.1. Let H be bridgeless and have exactly eight labelled odd vertices X. A
pattern is a four-element subset
                            P      P ⊂ X. Its function βP is 1 on P , −1 on X \ P , and zero
elsewhere. Write βP (U ) = v∈U βP (v) and let δH (U ) be the edges with exactly one endpoint
in U . The pattern is feasible if its sum on each component is zero and |βP (U )| < |δH (U )|
whenever the cut is nonempty. An anchor of pattern P in H is an integer 6-flow whose
positive orientation D has unit divergence div 1D = βP . Here the positive orientation directs
each edge according to the sign of its integer value, and 1D assigns one in that direction.
Feasibility is not part of the definition of an anchor.
Lemma 66.2. Every feasible pattern on eight odd vertices has an anchor.
Proof. Delete loops temporarily. Put β = βP and b(v) = (deg(v) + β(v))/2, a nonnegative
integer. For every set U ,
                     X                       |δH (U )| + β(U )
                          b(v) = |EH (U )| +                   ≥ |EH (U )|,
                                                     2
                       v∈U
and the total sum of b(v) is |E(H)|. Hall’s theorem assigns every edge to one endpoint, with
exactly b(v) edges assigned to v: make b(v) slots at each vertex, and join each edge to all
slots of its endpoints. For any edge set with incident vertex set U , its size is at most |EH (U )|,
which is at most the number of these slots. Orient each edge out of its assigned endpoint.
The resulting orientation D has unit divergence β.
   On every nonempty cut the numbers of outgoing and incoming edges are (|δH (U )|+β(U ))/2
and (|δH (U )| − β(U ))/2, both positive by feasibility. Thus each connected component with
more than one vertex is strongly connected as a directed graph: otherwise a source or sink
component of its strongly connected component quotient would give a directed cut. Pair
the four minus terminals with the four plus terminals within components, which is possible
because each component’s sign sum is zero. For each pair choose a simple directed path
from the minus terminal to the plus terminal. Add one on each of these four paths to the
102                                      DAVE GOEL


unit map of D. Each path adds divergence +1 at its minus endpoint and −1 at its plus
endpoint, cancelling β. Each of the four simple paths uses an edge at most once. Every edge
magnitude is therefore between one and 1 + 4 = 5, so this is an anchor. Components with
no terminals already have unit divergence zero. Restore loops with value one in any chosen
direction; they change neither divergence nor the required pattern.                      □
Lemma 66.3. In the same graph H, any two anchors of the same pattern have joined
reductions modulo six.
Proof. Their positive orientations have the same unit divergence. The arcs where these
orientations disagree, read in the first orientation, form a balanced directed graph: their
signed vector is half the difference of the two unit maps. Decompose them into edge-disjoint
directed cycles, allowing loops. On each cycle subtract b, where b is one more than its largest
current positive magnitude. Then 2 ≤ b ≤ 6. Every changed value becomes negative with
magnitude between one and five, preserving integer conservation. Thus each reversal is also
a legal integer 6-flow step. This reverses the cycle’s orientation. Other disagreement cycles
stay directed because they are edge-disjoint.
   Once orientations agree, decompose the difference of the two integer flows conformally
into signed unit cycles. Successive additions keep each coordinate between its same-sign
endpoints, so all magnitudes remain between one and five. Reduce this finite integer path
modulo six. A reversal with b < 6 is a legal modular move, and unit moves are legal as well.
If b = 6, the modular state is unchanged; omit this stationary step from the modular path
but keep its changed integer representative for the subsequent construction. No endpoint
reduction is zero. This proves the asserted modular joining.                                 □
   At a loopless degree-four vertex, a pairing partitions its four edges into two pairs. Its
balanced family consists of the flows balanced on both pairs; conservation makes balance on
either pair sufficient.
Proposition 66.4. A multigraph of maximum degree at most four with at most eight odd
vertices per component has connected modular 6-flow graph.
Proof. Induct on edge count in this class. Loops have independent nonzero coordinates and
can be removed. Discard isolated vertices, and use products if several components with edges
remain; each has fewer edges. Bridges give empty flow spaces. A degree-two vertex can be
suppressed: conservation equates its two values along the path, identifying flows and cycle
moves, even if a loop is created. Degrees at other vertices and their parities are unchanged.
   We may thus assume G is connected, bridgeless and loopless with degrees three or four. If
there are at most six cubic vertices, use Theorem 54.1. Otherwise there are exactly eight. If
all vertices are cubic, use Proposition 65.3. Otherwise fix one degree-four vertex v for the
entire argument. The local lemma (Lemma 56.2) sends every modular flow into one of the
three balanced families at this same vertex v.
   Each nonempty family is identified by balanced splitting with the flow space of a smaller
graph of maximum degree at most four having the same eight labelled odd vertices. It
supports a nowhere-zero flow, so is bridgeless, though it may be disconnected. Induction
connects its flows, and splitting lifts these paths to the ambient graph G. Thus the flows of
each family are joined in G.
   For any two nonempty families the two split graphs have a common feasible pattern by
Proposition 60.1, which applies to precisely these bridgeless, possibly disconnected graphs
with the same odd labels. Lemma 66.2 gives an anchor in each. Lift both anchors to G by
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                       103


splitting. Their magnitudes and unit divergences are preserved, so they are anchors of the
same pattern in the same ambient graph G. Their modular reductions lie in their respective
families and are joined by Lemma 66.3. Thus all nonempty families join, and every flow
reaches one of them.                                                                    □

                      67. Arbitrary degrees and integer flows
Proof
P       of Theorem 61.1. Remove independent loop coordinates and induct on Ψ(G) =
   v max{deg(v)   − 4, 0}. The case Ψ = 0 is Proposition 66.4. At an even vertex of degree
d ≥ 6, distribute its external ends among a new triangle in groups 2, 2, d − 4. The new
degrees are 4, 4, d − 2, lowering Ψ by two. At an odd vertex of degree d ≥ 5, use groups
1, 2, d − 3, giving degrees 3, 4, d − 1 and lowering Ψ by one. The graph remains connected
within each original component, and that component’s odd count is unchanged.
   Every prescribed modular flow extends over the triangle: for its three external outward
divergences βi summing to zero, the internal values can be z, z − β2 , z + β1 . At most three
residues are forbidden, fewer than six. Extend any two desired flows and join them on the
expanded graph by induction.
   An upstairs cycle step projects after contraction to an integer circulation on retained edges
with entries 0, 1, −1, multiplied by its modular coefficient. Orient the support of this vector
by its signs and decompose it into edge-disjoint directed cycles. Apply the same coefficient
on these cycles in turn. At each prefix every retained edge has its old or final nonzero value.
Thus the projected step is a legal path, possibly stationary. Project the whole upstairs
path and restore loop coordinates. This proves modular connectivity. This is the triangle
expansion method of [2, Observation 4.6]; the extension and projection above give the full
argument needed here.
   Finally [6, Theorem 2.1] identifies the connected components of integer and modular 6-flows
on G, proving integer connectivity. This conclusion uses that component correspondence,
not direct adjacent integer lifts for all modular steps.                                      □
104                                       DAVE GOEL


Part 6. The groups of exponent two
  This part proves Theorem 1.4. It uses statements of the sections for part (c) of Theorem 1.2,
and Lemma A.2 of the appendix.

68. The groups of exponent two and more vertices of odd degree: statement
                                 and inputs
   Graphs are finite multigraphs, with loops and parallel edges allowed. A loop contributes
two to degree. Fix reference edge directions. For a finite abelian group A, a flow is an
assignment of nonzero elements of A with outgoing sum minus incoming sum zero at each
vertex. Loops contribute zero to divergence. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph,
including loops and digons. A move adds tχC for a nonzero t ∈ A, where χC is the signed
vector of a cycle traversal and both endpoints remain nowhere-zero. Write F(G, A) for this
reconfiguration graph; a graph with zero or one flow counts as connected. Two flows are
joined if they lie in the same component. For exponent-two groups, signs of group values are
immaterial, but signed integer cycle vectors will still be used when projecting moves.
Theorem 68.1. Let A be an elementary abelian two-group of order N ≥ 4, and set B(N ) =
2N/2+1 − 4. If every connected component of G has at most B(N ) odd-degree vertices, then
F(G, A) is connected.
   The bounds for orders 4, 8, 16, 32 are respectively 4, 28, 508, 131068. Here N is the group
order, not its dimension over Z2 . The theorem is about group flows over the elementary
abelian two-groups. The known contrast of Esperet et al. [3], that K4 is flow-connected
over Z22 but not over Z4 , illustrates the role of group structure; see also [2, Introduction and
Section 5.1].
Known sharpness at order four. The bound B(4) = 4 cannot be replaced by six: K3,3
has six odd vertices and exactly two flow components over Z22 . This is the known Kempe-
coloring description [3, Theorem 4.3], with the following direct count. Such flows are proper
three-edge-colorings. In the 3 by 3 array, the first row has six orders, the second has two
derangements of it, and the third is forced, giving twelve colorings. A legal coefficient-t
move uses a cycle avoiding color t; the other two color classes are disjoint perfect matchings
whose union is one six-cycle: the union is 2-regular on six vertices, and every cycle of the
simple bipartite graph K3,3 has length four or six. Thus every move exchanges two colors on
the whole graph. Each component is one orbit of the six color permutations, so there are
12/6 = 2. We do not know whether B(N ) is best for N = 8, 16, 32.
   The additional orders relative to our earlier result are 4, 8, 16, 32. For N ≥ 64, these
groups are not cyclic, and [5, Theorem 1.2] applies with divisor 4 ≤ N/10 to every bridgeless
multigraph, with no odd-vertex bound. Graphs with a bridge have no flow. That earlier
result is not used in this proof.
   A balanced pair consists of distinct nonloop incident edges whose outward values sum to
zero; here this means equal values. We use the following precise inputs from the sections
that prove Theorem 16.1. Theorem 16.1 gives connectivity with at most two odd vertices in
total. Lemma 17.1 and its proof give balanced splitting: in a loopless graph replace distinct
edges uv, vw by uw, retaining v and allowing u = w. Flows downstairs correspond to flows
with this pair balanced upstairs, and cycle steps lift to paths with the same coefficient. Edge
count decreases by one; degree parity is preserved and maximum degree does not increase.
Lemma 17.2 says that deleting edges valued in a subgroup K ≤ A leaves a bridgeless
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                         105


quotient support, on whose cycles every K-increment is safe. The two local arguments from
Lemmas 18.1 and 18.2 and the joining argument of Proposition 18.3 are repeated below; none
restricts odd counts in its local argument. Lemma 21.1 gives the triangle extension/projection
used at the end.
   We also use the lifting statement and proof idea of Cranston et al. [2, Theorem 5.9(2)],
whose induced-case proof is written in Lemma A.2 of the appendix: if a connected induced
subgraph H of a loopless graph has the three properties below, connectivity of F(G/H, A)
implies connectivity of F(G, A). Here induced means that every edge with both ends in
V (H), parallel edges included, belongs to H; the quotient identifies V (H) to one vertex and
deletes E(H), retaining all other edges. The three properties are: existence of a nowhere-
zero edge map with any prescribed zero-sum divergence β; connectivity of maps with each
fixed β; and, for boundaries differing only at two vertices, one map for each of the two
boundaries whose difference is supported on a simple path between those vertices. These are
the flow-reconfiguration-contractibility properties of [2, Definitions 5.7–5.8].

                            69. Cycles and local balancing
Lemma 69.1 (Known exponent-two cycle criterion, sufficient direction). If A has exponent
two and 2 ≤ ℓ < N = |A|, then Cℓ has the three contractibility properties.
Proof. This is the sufficient direction of [2, Corollary 5.14]; we give its forbidden-parameter
argument directly. Digons are already included in that source statement. Reorient cyclically,
which preserves boundaries and moves after negating reversed edge values. Solutions of any
zero-sum boundary are a + ce , with fixed offsets and a ∈ A. At most ℓ < N parameters are
forbidden, so a nowhere-zero solution exists. Any two solutions differ by one constant on the
cycle and are equal or adjacent. For a nonzero boundary change +γ at u, −γ at a distinct
vertex w, let X, Y be the forbidden sets on the cyclic paths from u to w and from w to u,
respectively. Put Z = A \ (X ∪ Y ), z = |Z|; then |X| + |Y | ≤ ℓ. Adding γ on the first path
or subtracting it on the second gives the desired change. If both fail for every a ∈ Z, then
Z + γ ⊆ X and Z − γ ⊆ Y . Since γ = −γ, their common translate lies in X ∩ Y , so
                          N = |X ∪ Y | + z ≤ |X| + |Y | − z + z ≤ ℓ,
a contradiction. Coincident boundaries use the same flow twice.                                □
  A pairing at a degree-four vertex partitions its four edges into two pairs. Its balanced-pair
family consists of flows equal on each pair. A family is joined when all its flows lie in one
ambient component; the joining paths need not stay in the family.
Lemma 69.2 (The local arguments of Lemmas 18.1 and 18.2 and of the proof of Propo-
sition 18.3). Let v be a degree-four vertex of a loopless graph G and A have exponent two.
Every flow reaches a balanced pair at v in at most one move. If each nonempty balanced-pair
family at v is joined in the ambient graph, then all flows of G are joined.
Proof. The four incident edges are distinct. Write their values a, b, c, d; their sum is zero. If
two agree, they are balanced. Otherwise K = ⟨a + b, a + c⟩ has order four and the values
form the coset a + K, disjoint from K since all are nonzero. All four edges survive in the
quotient support modulo K, which is bridgeless by Lemma 17.2. Each surviving edge lies on
a surviving cycle; choose one through v, using exactly two ends. Rename the used values a, b
and the others c, d; K is unchanged. The safe nonzero increment a + c = b + d ∈ K changes
106                                       DAVE GOEL


them to c, d, matching the unchanged ends. This proves local entry. For N = 4, four distinct
nonzero values cannot occur.
   The three pairings define the balanced-pair families Bπ . Splitting one pair identifies its
family’s flow space with a smaller graph, because conservation forces balance on the other pair
as well. By hypothesis each nonempty family lies in one ambient component; intermediate
paths need not stay inside it. If there are no flows the claim is vacuous; otherwise local entry
gives a nonempty family. Form the graph on nonempty families, joining two if they intersect
or have a one-move link. If a state with all four values equal exists, all three families contain
it and are joined. Otherwise every state in a family has values a, a, b, b, with a ̸= b. Put
t = a + b and K = {0, t}. Neither a nor b lies in K, so the quotient support again contains all
four edges. By Lemma 17.2, it is bridgeless and has a cycle through v. Its safe t-move swaps
a, b on exactly two ends. Equal used values would give a monochromatic state, excluded
in this case. Different used values give a different pairing and a link to another nonempty
family. Thus the family graph has no isolated vertex and has two or three vertices, so it is
connected. Local entry finishes the proof.                                                     □

                   70. The cubic bound and the three inductions
Lemma 70.1. A nonempty simple cubic graph on at most B(N ) vertices, for even N ≥ 4,
has an induced cycle of length less than N .
Proof. Put r = N/2. If the girth is at least 2r, grow binary trees away from an edge uv to
depth r − 1 from each end. A repeated vertex on one side yields a cycle of length at most
2r − 2; a collision between sides yields one of length at most 2r − 1. An extra edge met while
expanding a vertex of depth below r − 1 likewise gives a cycle of length less than 2r. Thus
the two trees have disjoint, distinct vertices and contain
                        2(1 + 2 + · · · + 2r−1 ) = 2(2r − 1) = B(N ) + 2
vertices, a contradiction. This is the elementary cubic Moore count. A shortest cycle is
induced, since any chord would give a shorter cycle.                                  □
Proof of Theorem 68.1. Fix A, N and write S(p) for connectivity on every finite multigraph
with at most p odd vertices in total, with arbitrary degrees. We prove S(p) by induction on
even p ≤ B(N ). The cases p = 0, 2 follow from Theorem 16.1. Fix p ≥ 4 and assume S(q)
for all smaller even q.
   First, the subquartic step at this fixed p. Induct on edge count among graphs of maximum
degree at most four with at most p odd vertices in total. The edgeless graph has one flow.
Loops are independent nonzero coordinates; remove them and use induction. Isolates do not
matter, bridges give empty spaces, and distinct edge-bearing components have fewer edges
and use products. Suppress a degree-two vertex: its two edges form a path with one common
value, identifying flows and moves with the suppressed graph, also when a loop results. Edge
count decreases and parities are unchanged. Thus the remaining graph is connected, loopless
and has degrees three or four. Fewer than p odd vertices invokes the outer induction.
   If a degree-four vertex exists, fix one. Every nonempty balanced-pair family splits to a
smaller subquartic graph with the same odd labels. The edge induction connects its flows,
and balanced splitting lifts the paths. Lemma 69.2 then joins all flows of G.
   Otherwise the graph is cubic on exactly p ≤ B(N ) vertices. If it is simple, Lemma 70.1
supplies an induced cycle C of length ℓ < N . If it has parallel edges, exactly two edges join
their ends, giving an induced digon. Three parallel edges would form a dipole component,
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        107


impossible here since G is connected on p ≥ 4 vertices. Each vertex of C has exactly one
exterior end. Contracting C therefore creates a vertex of degree ℓ and leaves
                                  p − ℓ + (ℓ mod 2) ≤ p − 2
odd vertices in total. The quotient may have degree greater than four: it satisfies the outer
induction hypothesis S(q), not the current subquartic hypothesis. Lemma 69.1 and the
induced lifting input, Lemma A.2, give connectivity of G. This completes the edge induction
for fixed p.
P Second, arbitrary degrees at the same p. Remove loops and induct on Ψ(G) =
   v max{deg(v) − 4, 0}. The zero case is the subquartic conclusion just proved. Ex-
pand an even vertex of degree d ≥ 6 to a triangle, distributing its external ends in groups
2, 2, d − 4. The new degrees are 4, 4, d − 2, and Ψ drops by two. For odd d ≥ 5, groups
1, 2, d − 3 give degrees 3, 4, d − 1, lowering Ψ by one. Each component stays connected and
its odd count is preserved. Since loops were removed, the added triangle edges are the only
edges among the three new vertices. By Lemma 21.1, every A-flow extends over this triangle:
its three cyclic values have forms z, z − β2 , z + β1 , where the exterior divergences βi sum
to zero. At most three parameters are forbidden, fewer than N . An upstairs cycle vector
restricts after contraction to an integer circulation with entries 0, 1, −1. Decompose its signed
support into edge-disjoint directed cycles and apply the original coefficient successively.
Every retained edge is old or final throughout, hence nonzero. Extend any two endpoints,
join them by the smaller-Ψ induction, and project the path, with individual steps possibly
stationary (in particular for the new triangle itself). Restore free loop coordinates. This
proves S(p) and closes the outer induction. The triangle method is that of [2, Observation
4.6]; the extension/projection argument used here is explicitly supplied in Lemma 21.1.
Finally take products over the components to obtain the stated per-component bound, even
when the total odd count exceeds B(N ).                                                        □
