Part 7. More vertices of odd degree for larger moduli
   This part proves part (e) of Theorem 1.3 in five sections, then Theorem 1.5 for k ≥ 3r + 1
in six sections, then Theorem 1.5 in full in four sections, and then part (f) of Theorem 1.3
in ten sections. These statements are for the Zk -flows only. The proofs use part (c) of
Theorem 1.3, Proposition 60.1, two lemmas of the sections for part (d), and Appendix A.

 71. Eight odd-degree vertices at moduli at least thirteen: statement and
                                 conventions
   All graphs are finite multigraphs. Loops and parallel edges are allowed; a loop contributes
two to degree and zero to divergence. Fix reference edge directions. A circulation has
outgoing sum minus incoming sum zero at every vertex. A cycle is a connected 2-regular
subgraph, including a loop or a pair of parallel edges. A directed cycle traversal has a signed
vector χC with entries 0, 1, −1.
   A nowhere-zero Zk -flow is a circulation with no zero edge value. Its reconfiguration graph
Mk (G) has these flows as vertices and joins f to f + tχC whenever t ∈ Zk \ {0} and both
endpoints are nowhere-zero. These are the modular flow reconfiguration graphs of Esperet et
al. [3], extended to loops by treating them as cycles. For a finite abelian group A the same
definition gives the A-flow reconfiguration graph. A space with zero or one flow counts as
connected. In particular bridges give empty spaces, and an edgeless graph has the unique
empty flow. Two flows are joined when they lie in the same component of the corresponding
reconfiguration graph.
Theorem 71.1 (Eight odd-degree vertices per component, every k ≥ 13, Zk -flows). Let G
be a finite multigraph with at most eight odd-degree vertices in each component. Then Mk (G)
is connected for every integer k ≥ 13.
   This theorem concerns modular flows only. Theorem 61.1 treats modulus six, both
modularly and integrally. The methods below include nonrestricted moves on potentially
long cycles. They do not supply integer lifts of those moves.
Relation to earlier results. For large moduli the conclusion was already known without
any odd-vertex bound. Esperet et al. [3] prove flow connectivity for all sufficiently large
finite abelian groups on 2-edge-connected graphs; products give the conclusion component
by component. Our earlier group manuscript [5, Theorems 1.1 and 1.2] treats every group of
order at least 3024 and also groups that are not cyclic of prime-power order whose order has
a divisor d with 4 ≤ d ≤ |A|/10. Thus for large k the present theorem also follows from these
results; it removes the remaining modulus restrictions for k ≥ 13 for the specified odd-vertex
class. The short-cycle contraction framework is due to Cranston et al. [2]; the small-order
base below is an instance of their cycle-degeneracy method. In particular, their Theorem
5.6 already covers graphs on at most twenty-two vertices for group order at least thirteen,
the range needed for our main theorem; the exact count below gives order eleven for that
base. The additional ingredients here are the full-increment local moves of Section 73 and
the order bound for normalized flows in Section 74.

                    72. Imported tools and a small-order base
   At a vertex, an outward value is an incident edge value read away from that vertex. A
balanced pair consists of distinct nonloop incident edges with outward sum zero. A cubic
vertex cannot have a balanced pair in a nowhere-zero flow, since its third edge would be zero.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                       109


   Throughout the local arguments, small means a value in {1, −1}, and large means any
other nonzero residue. A restricted step has coefficient in {1, −1, 2, −2}. All these are
permitted steps in the full reconfiguration graph studied here. We use the following results
of earlier sections with their stated hypotheses.
    (1) For every k ≥ 7, graphs with at most six odd vertices per component have connected
         modular flow graphs (Theorem 45.1).
    (2) Balanced splitting in a loopless graph replaces distinct edges uv, vw by uw, retaining
         v and allowing u = w. It identifies the new flow space with flows balanced on that
         pair. Cycle moves downstairs lift to paths with the same coefficient. Edge count falls
         by one; the degree of v falls by two, and other degrees and all parities are unchanged.
         Integer magnitudes and positive unit divergences are preserved by the integer version
         of this correspondence as restated in Lemma 46.1.
    (3) For k ≥ 4, if the large edges contain a cycle, restricted steps can increase the number
         of small edges without changing a previously small edge. If a flow on a loopless graph
         has no balanced pair and a small cycle has two outward values a, a at v, then an
         off-cycle end of value other than −2a allows restricted steps on that cycle to reach a
         balanced pair at v, as restated in Lemma 46.2.
    (4) For k ≥ 4, in a loopless flow without a balanced pair, suppose a cycle has exactly
         one large edge h, and one endpoint v of h has degree four. Write a ∈ {1, −1} for its
         other, small cycle end. Restricted steps on the cycle reach balance at v unless both
         off-cycle values are −2a. In that exception the outward pattern is (a, 3a, −2a, −2a),
         with 3a on h as restated in Lemma 46.3.
    (5) Two bridgeless multigraphs with the same labelled odd vertices share a feasible
         pattern, including when the graphs are disconnected and their even vertices differ
        (Proposition 60.1). This is the classical consequence of Nash-Williams’ pairing
         theorem [9] derived in Section 60.
The local statements above do not restrict the number of odd vertices. We also use ordinary
cycle decompositions of even graphs and of integer circulations, and products over graph
components.
   We state explicitly the short-cycle input. A connected graph H is A-flow-reconfiguration-
contractible if every zero-sum prescribed vertex divergence has a nowhere-zero edge map,
each such fixed-divergence space is connected by cycle moves, and divergences differing only
at two vertices have representatives differing on a simple path between those vertices (or
equal representatives if the divergences coincide). By Lemma A.1, a cycle of length ℓ ≥ 2
has this property if ⌈2|A|/3⌉ > ℓ. By Lemma A.2, if such an H is induced in a loopless
graph G, connectivity of the flow graph of G/H implies connectivity for G. Here induced
includes every internal parallel edge, and G/H contracts H and deletes its edges. These
sufficient thresholds and the lifting direction come from Cranston et al. [2, Theorems 5.9
and 5.11; Corollaries 5.13 and 5.15], with the induced-subgraph and digon proofs supplied in
Appendix A.
Lemma 72.1 (Lifting over a cycle, including a noninduced cycle). For a finite abelian group
A, let G be loopless, let C be a cycle of length ℓ ≥ 2, and let H be obtained by identifying
V (C) and deleting all resulting loops. If ⌈2|A|/3⌉ > ℓ and H has connected A-flow graph,
then so does G. The quotient H has fewer vertices than G, and contraction does not increase
the number of odd vertices in a component.
110                                      DAVE GOEL


Proof. This is the subdivision repair in the proof of Lemma 47.1. Subdivide once each edge
outside C having both ends on C, including extra parallel edges, to obtain G∗ . Suppression
of a degree-two vertex equates its two values along the path and identifies flows and cycle
moves, so subdivision preserves the flow reconfiguration graph. Now C is induced in G∗ , and
the induced lifting input applies. In G∗ /C each new subdivision vertex lies on a digon with
the contracted vertex. Suppress it and delete its resulting independent loop. The result is
exactly H, the quotient of the original G. Restoring the loop coordinates and undoing these
suppressions gives connectivity of G∗ /C, hence of G∗ and then G.
   The vertex count of H is |V (G)| − ℓ + 1. The contracted vertex is odd precisely when an
odd number of the vertices of C were odd; all other degree parities are unchanged. This
proves the last claim.                                                                     □
Lemma 72.2 (Small-order base). Every finite multigraph on at most twenty-two vertices
has connected A-flow graph for every finite abelian group A of order at least eleven. There is
no odd-vertex restriction in this lemma.
Proof. This is an instance of the cycle-degeneracy argument of Cranston et al. [2, Corollary
5.4]. We give the needed argument using the induced lifting input just stated. For the range
|A| ≥ 13 used in Theorem 71.1, it also follows directly from their Theorem 5.6: its sufficient
bound is ⌊3 log2 (n − n2 + 2)⌋ ≤ ⌊3 log2 24⌋ = 13, where n2 counts degree-two vertices. The
count here reaches |A| ≥ 11. Induct on vertex count and take products over components.
Before each vertex-reducing step delete the independent nonzero loop coordinates; isolates do
not matter, and bridges give empty spaces. Suppress degree-two vertices, reducing the vertex
count and preserving flow spaces and moves, including when a loop results. A remaining
nontrivial connected graph has minimum degree at least three. A parallel pair supplies a
digon.
   Otherwise the graph is simple. If its girth were at least eight, grow two binary trees to
depth three away from the ends of an edge. Minimum degree three supplies two children at
each expansion, other than the parent. At the first repetition, the two routes from one root
either diverge and meet again, or the new edge returns to an ancestor; both give a cycle of
length at most six. A repeated vertex on both sides would give a cycle of length at most
seven. Hence all
                                    2(1 + 2 + 4 + 8) = 30
vertices in the two trees are distinct, contrary to the order bound. Thus a cycle of length
at most seven exists. Its length is less than ⌈2|A|/3⌉ ≥ 8. Apply vertex induction to the
smaller original quotient H from Lemma 72.1, then apply that lemma to lift connectivity.
The induction is on H, not on the possibly larger subdivision graph G∗ .                    □

                  73. Two moves using unrestricted coefficients
  The next two lemmas hold for every k ≥ 7. They provide full modular moves.
Lemma 73.1. Suppose a degree-four vertex of a loopless flow has outward values (a, a, b, c),
where a ∈ {1, −1} and b, c are large. If a cycle has exactly two large edges and uses an a-end
and the b-end at this vertex, a single legal full modular move on it reaches a balanced pair.
Proof. The other a-end and the c-end are off the cycle. Direct the cycle out along its small
a-edge and back along the b-edge. Conservation gives b + c = −2a. The two totals
                                  t1 = −2a,      t2 = a + b
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          111


balance respectively the changed small end and the changed b-end with the untouched a-end.
They are distinct: equality would give b = −3a and c = a, contrary to c being large. Neither
is zero; for t2 , zero would make b = −a small. Both are safe on every small edge, including
the incident a-edge: t1 ∈ / {1, −1}; t2 = a would give b = 0, and t2 = −a would give b = −2a
and c = 0. The incident large traversal value is −b, and neither total equals b: for t1 this
would again force c = 0, and for t2 it would force a = 0. The other large edge forbids at most
one of the two totals. At least one is therefore a legal nonzero coefficient reaching balance.
This is the two-total argument in the proof of Lemma 48.2 with unrestricted coefficients, so
its restricted-reachability exception is not an obstruction here.                           □
Lemma 73.2. Under the same local pattern (a, a, b, c), suppose the two large edges have
their other endpoints in one small-edge component not containing the degree-four vertex v.
Then a single legal full modular move reaches a balanced pair at v.
Proof. Join the two other endpoints by a simple small-edge path, using the path of length
zero if they coincide. Since this component avoids v, the path and the two large edges form a
simple cycle, or a digon. Traverse it out along b and back along c. The coefficient t = −a − b
changes these outward values to
                           b + t = −a,       c − t = c + a + b = −a.
They are nonzero and balance the untouched small ends. The coefficient is nonzero because
b ̸= −a. It also avoids a, −a: these possibilities would give respectively b = −2a, c = 0 or
b = 0. Thus t is safe on every small path edge, proving the claim.                        □

                                74. The forest reduction
   Fix k ≥ 7 and a connected loopless graph G of degrees three and four with exactly eight
cubic vertices. Suppose a flow cannot reach a balanced pair at any degree-four vertex by full
modular moves. Choose in its finite full component a flow g maximizing the number of small
edges. Every state in this component has no balanced pair: this is the supposition at degree
four, and at cubic vertices it follows from conservation and nonzero values. Let S, N be the
spanning small and large subgraphs, respectively, retaining isolated vertices.
Lemma 74.1. The graph N is a forest. Every cyclic component of S is a whole even cycle
consisting of cubic vertices. If S is a forest with s components and N has c components, then
s + c = 4 and 1 ≤ s ≤ 3.
Proof. The normalization input makes N a forest, since a large cycle would allow more small
edges in the same full component. At a small-cycle vertex the two outward small values
have the same sign a, because opposite signs would be balanced. Every off-cycle end must
be −2a by the small-cycle input. A quartic vertex would then have sum −2a ̸= 0. A cubic
vertex has the pattern (a, a, −2a) and no further small end. Thus each small cycle is a whole
component of S, with all vertices cubic. Along its traversal the values alternate 1, −1, so it
is even.
   If both spanning subgraphs are forests, write n = |V (G)|. Then
                             |E(G)| = 2n − 4 = (n − s) + (n − c),
giving s + c = 4. Each forest has at least one component, so 1 ≤ s ≤ 3.                         □
  For the rest of this section assume S is a forest and name its trees T1 , . . . , Ts . A quartic
vertex has type i if its degree in S is i; let ni count such vertices. At any vertex all small
112                                        DAVE GOEL


ends have one sign a, since otherwise two would be balanced. Type four is impossible, since
four small ends of one sign would sum to 4a ̸= 0. Let pi count cubic vertices of small degree
i. Their small degree is at most two, since 3a ̸= 0, and p0 + p1 + p2 = 8.
Lemma 74.2. There is no quartic vertex of type two.
Proof. Let such a vertex v lie in Ti , with pattern (a, a, b, c). A large edge back into Ti closes
a one-large-edge cycle with the small-tree path. The other small end at v is untouched and
has value a ̸= −2a. This excludes the exception of the one-large-edge input and would reach
balance. Thus both large neighbors lie outside Ti . If they are in the same other tree, apply
Lemma 73.2. For s = 1 there is no vertex outside Ti at all; for s = 2 there is only one other
tree. These exhaust the possibilities when s ≤ 2.
   When s = 3, the only remaining case has the two large edges from v ∈ T1 going to T2 and
T3 . Here c = 1, so N is a spanning tree. Since v has two small ends, T1 − v is nonempty.
Follow the N -path from any vertex in T1 − v to v. Its final edge comes from outside T1 ,
because neither large edge at v goes into T1 . The path therefore first leaves T1 earlier, on an
edge e′ not incident with v, into T2 or T3 . Combine e′ with the large edge e from v to that
same tree and the unique small paths in the two trees. This is a cycle with exactly two large
edges. At v it uses e and a small edge, so Lemma 73.1 reaches balance, a contradiction. □
Lemma 74.3. The number n3 of type-three quartic vertices is at most 0, 2, 4 when s = 1, 2, 3,
respectively.
Proof. A type-three vertex has pattern (a, a, a, −3a). Its sole large edge cannot go into its
own small tree, by the one-large-edge input. Call a large edge special if at least one endpoint
has type three in the current normalized flow. Such an edge has value 3 or −3 and joins
different small trees.
   There is at most one special edge between a fixed pair Ti , Tj . Indeed take a type-three
endpoint v ∈ Ti of one such edge e. Every edge between different small trees is large. Any
other edge e′ between these trees forms, with e and the two small-tree paths, a cycle with
exactly two large edges. The path at v is nonempty because e is its only large edge. Traverse
the cycle out of v along a small end and back along e. Coefficient −2a balances the changed
small end with an untouched small end. It is safe on every small edge and on e, whose
traversal value is 3a. The edge e′ must therefore have traversal value 2a, or this move would
be legal. In particular e′ cannot be another special edge, since ±3 ̸= 2a modulo k ≥ 7.
   Each type-three vertex is an endpoint of exactly one special edge, its only large edge. For
s = 1 there are no special edges, and for s = 2 at most one, giving the first two bounds.
For s = 3 suppose all three tree pairs have a special edge. These three edges and the paths
joining their endpoints inside the three disjoint small trees form a cycle. Some internal paths
may have length zero. At a type-three endpoint v, however, its path is nonempty because
it has only one large edge. All traversal values on this cycle lie in {1, −1, 3, −3}. Traverse
it out along the small end at v, whose outward value is a, and add −2a. Every resulting
value lies in {1, −1, 3, −3, 5, −5}, hence is nonzero for k ≥ 7. The changed small end is −a,
balancing an untouched a-end. This contradiction leaves at most two special edges and hence
at most four type-three endpoints.                                                           □
Proposition 74.4. In the normalized no-reachable-balance situation above, if S is a forest
then G has at most twenty-two vertices.
                                      P
Proof. Since S has s tree components, v (degS (v) − 2) = −2s. Using n2 = 0 gives
                              −n1 + n3 − 2n0 − 2p0 − p1 = −2s.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                       113


Consequently the number of quartic vertices satisfies
                   n1 + n3 + n0 = 2n3 + 2s − n0 − 2p0 − p1 ≤ 2n3 + 2s.
For s = 1, 2, 3, Lemma 74.3 bounds this by 2, 8, 14, respectively. Adding the eight cubic
vertices gives total order at most 10, 16, 22, respectively.                           □

                               75. Anchors and completion
  For clarity we recall the pattern terminology. In a bridgeless graph with eight labelled
odd vertices X, a pattern is a four-element set P ⊂ X. Its sign function βP is 1 on P , −1
on X \ P , and zero elsewhere. It is feasible if its sum on each component is zero and
                          X
                               βP (v) < |δ(U )| whenever δ(U ) ̸= ∅.
                         v∈U
Here δ(U ) is the set of edges with exactly one endpoint in U . The positive orientation of
an integer edge map directs each edge by its sign; its unit divergence is outdegree minus
indegree. An anchor is an integer circulation of magnitudes one through five whose positive
orientation has unit divergence βP ; feasibility is not required in the definition of an anchor
itself.
Lemma 75.1. Every feasible pattern on eight odd vertices has an anchor. For every k ≥ 7,
two anchors of the same pattern in one graph have joined reductions modulo k.
Proof. Existence is Lemma 66.2: Hall’s prescribed-outdegree orientation has unit divergence
βP , the strict cut inequalities make it strong in each nontrivial component, and four simple
directed correction paths pair minus and plus terminals within their respective components
and produce a circulation of magnitudes one through five. Loops are independent coordinates.
   We use the integer path explicitly constructed in the proof of Lemma 66.3, rather than
its modulus-six statement. Orient the disagreement edges of the two positive orientations
as in the first. Equality of their unit divergences makes this a balanced directed subgraph.
Decompose it into edge-disjoint directed cycles and subtract b = 1 + max F (e) on each,
reading the current values positively along that cycle. Then 2 ≤ b ≤ 6; each changed
value becomes negative with magnitude one through five. After these reversals the positive
orientations agree. Conformally decompose the remaining difference into signed unit cycles;
each successive value stays between its same-sign endpoints. This gives an integer path
with all magnitudes at most five. For k ≥ 7 every displayed coefficient is nonzero modulo k,
including b = 6, and every endpoint value is nonzero. Reducing the path gives the claimed
modular path; there are no stationary reversals in this range.                             □
Proposition 75.2. For k ≥ 13, every multigraph of maximum degree at most four with at
most eight odd vertices per component has connected modular flow graph.
Proof. Fix k ≥ 13 and induct on edge count within exactly the stated class, allowing
disconnected graphs throughout. Remove independent loop coordinates and isolates, take
products over edge-bearing components, and discard empty spaces with bridges. Suppress
degree-two vertices, which preserves flow spaces and moves and reduces edge count. We may
assume connected, loopless degrees three or four. At most six cubic vertices invokes the
six-odd-vertex input. Otherwise there are exactly eight cubic vertices.
   First suppose G contains an even cycle C all of whose vertices are cubic. Its length ℓ is
at most eight. Since ⌈2k/3⌉ ≥ 9 > ℓ, Lemma 72.1 applies. Its original quotient has exactly
114                                           DAVE GOEL


8 − ℓ ≤ 6 odd vertices: the contracted vertex is even: each edge inside V (C) removes two
from the sum of the identified degrees, including additional internal chords. The quotient
may have high degree; the six-odd-vertex theorem imposes no maximum-degree bound. That
theorem connects the quotient, so Lemma 72.1 connects G outright.
   Now suppose there is no such all-cubic even cycle. If |V (G)| ≤ 22, Lemma 72.2 settles
the graph. Otherwise, any flow with no reachable balanced pair could be normalized as
in Section 74. Lemma 74.1 would make every small-cycle component an all-cubic even
cycle, now excluded. Thus its small support is a forest, and Proposition 74.4 would give
|V (G)| ≤ 22, a contradiction. Every flow therefore reaches a balanced pair at some quartic
vertex. Such vertices exist, since |V (G)| > 22 > 8.
   At a quartic vertex a pairing partitions the four distinct edges into two pairs, and its
balanced family consists of flows balanced on both. Conservation makes balance on one pair
sufficient. Take the balanced families for all pairings at all quartic vertices; the entry vertex
need not be common to different flows. A nonempty family is identified by splitting one of its
two pairs with the flow space of a graph having fewer edges, maximum degree at most four,
and the same eight odd labels; the split vertex retains degree two, and conservation balances
its other pair. It is bridgeless because it has a nowhere-zero flow. Edge induction connects
it, and lifting joins its members in G. Any two such split graphs, even if disconnected or
obtained at different vertices, share a feasible pattern by Proposition 60.1. Choose anchors
in the two graphs using Lemma 75.1 and lift them to G. Their magnitudes and positive
unit divergences are preserved. They are therefore anchors of the same pattern in the same
ambient graph G, lying in the two respective families. Lemma 75.1 joins their modular
reductions. Thus all nonempty families join, and every flow reaches one. This closes the edge
induction.                                                                                     □
Proof of Theorem 71.1. Remove loops and induct on the degree excess
                                   X
                           Ψ(G) =     max{deg(v) − 4, 0}.
                                          v
The zero case is Proposition 75.2. At an even vertex of degree d ≥ 6, replace it by a triangle
and distribute its external ends in groups 2, 2, d − 4. The new degrees 4, 4, d − 2 lower Ψ by
two. At an odd vertex of degree d ≥ 5, use groups 1, 2, d − 3, giving degrees 3, 4, d − 1 and
lowering Ψ by one. Component odd counts are unchanged. This is the triangle expansion
method of Cranston et al. [2, Observation 4.6], with the odd-degree case and the required
projection as in Lemma 50.3 and Section 67. The new triangle has no other internal edges,
since the original graph is loopless.
   Every prescribed modular flow extends over the triangle. If its external outward divergences
are β1 , β2 , β3 of sum zero, the cyclic triangle values can be z, z − β2 , z + β1 . At most three
parameters are forbidden, fewer than k. Extend any two desired endpoints and join them
upstairs by the smaller-excess induction. After contraction an upstairs cycle vector restricts
to an integer circulation with entries 0, 1, −1 on retained edges. Orient its support by its signs
and decompose it into edge-disjoint directed cycles. Applying the original modular coefficient
on these cycles successively keeps every retained edge at its old or final nonzero value. Project
all steps in this way, allowing stationary projections, and restore the independent loops. This
proves the theorem.                                                                             □
Where the thresholds enter. The local forest analysis uses k ≥ 7. At k = 5, identities
such as 3 = −2 and 5a = 0 invalidate the separation of special edges and the safe shift on their
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        115


cycle. The small-order base requires group order at least eleven. The only use of thirteen is
to contract the possible all-cubic small cycle of length eight; length six requires at least ten,
and length four requires at least seven (a sufficient bound also for lengths two and three).
Thus within this proof, for k = 7, 8, 9 small cycles of lengths six or eight remain unhandled;
for k = 10, 11, 12 length eight remains unhandled. In addition the twenty-two-vertex base is
unavailable here for k = 7, 8, 9, 10. These are limitations of this proof. The local arithmetic
also holds at modulus six, but this proof does not apply there: the cited six-odd input starts
at seven, the four-cycle criterion requires seven, and a reversal with b = 6 would be stationary
modulo six rather than one of the nonzero steps used here.

   76. More odd-degree vertices at larger moduli: statement and earlier
                                    results
   Graphs are finite multigraphs, allowing loops and parallel edges. A loop contributes
two to degree and zero to divergence. With fixed reference edge directions, divergence is
outgoing sum minus incoming sum; a circulation has divergence zero at every vertex. A
nowhere-zero Zk -flow is a circulation taking nonzero values on every edge. A cycle is a
connected 2-regular subgraph, including a loop or a digon; a traversal has signed vector χC
with entries 0, 1, −1. The graph Mk (G) joins f to f + tχC when t ̸= 0 modulo k and both
endpoints are nowhere-zero. This is the modular flow reconfiguration graph of Esperet et
al. [3], extended to loops by treating them as cycles. Two flows are joined if they lie in the
same component. A space with zero or one flow counts as connected. Bridges give empty
spaces, and an edgeless graph has the unique empty flow. The analogous definition applies
to flows over any finite abelian group.
Theorem 76.1. Let r ≥ 4 and k ≥ 3r + 1 be integers. If every component of a finite
multigraph G has at most 2r odd-degree vertices, then Mk (G) is connected. Equivalently, for
every k ≥ 13, this conclusion holds when each component has at most 2⌊(k − 1)/3⌋ odd-degree
vertices.
   The case r = 4 is the eight-odd-vertex theorem, Theorem 71.1. For example, the present
statement permits ten odd vertices at every k ≥ 16 and twelve at every k ≥ 19. It concerns
modular flows only, with all nonzero coefficients. Its auxiliary small-order lemma applies to
all finite abelian groups of the stated orders.
   Large moduli were already covered without an odd-vertex restriction. Esperet et al.
[3] prove connectivity for all sufficiently large finite abelian groups on 2-edge-connected
graphs, with component products giving the bridgeless case. Our earlier group manuscript [5,
Theorems 1.1 and 1.2] covers every group of order at least 3024, and any group not cyclic of
prime-power order whose order has a divisor d with 4 ≤ d ≤ |A|/10. For the moduli covered
by those results, the present theorem also follows from them.
   The deductions here generalize the bounded-order argument of Section 74: the count for
vertices with two small ends and the forest of special edges now apply to arbitrarily many
small-edge trees. The base is an application of the short-cycle count and the cycle-degeneracy
method of Cranston et al. [2, Lemma 5.5 and Corollary 5.4], as used in their proof of Theorem
5.6. We give the argument with loops, parallel edges and bridges treated explicitly. Their
rounded logarithmic Theorem 5.6, stated for bridgeless graphs, also supplies the base in our
required range for r ≥ 5; we compare the rounding at r = 4 below.
116                                       DAVE GOEL


              77. Inputs and conventions for the local arguments
   An outward value is an incident edge value read away from a vertex. A balanced pair
consists of distinct nonloop edges at one vertex whose outward values sum to zero. At a
cubic vertex of a loopless graph a balanced pair is impossible in a nowhere-zero flow, since
the third edge would have value zero. We call values 1, −1 small and all other nonzero values
large. All local arguments below are used with k ≥ 7. We use the following precise inputs
from earlier sections.
    (1) Graphs with at most six odd vertices per component have connected modular flow
        graphs for all k ≥ 7 (Theorem 45.1), with no maximum-degree restriction.
    (2) Balanced splitting, as restated in Lemma 46.1, replaces distinct edges uv, vw of a
        loopless graph by uw, retaining v and allowing u = w. It identifies flows with the
        specified balanced family, and downstairs cycle steps lift to paths with the same
        coefficient. The edge count falls by one, the degree at v by two; other degrees and all
        degree parities are unchanged. The integer correspondence preserves magnitudes and
        positive unit divergences.
    (3) The local normalization inputs, as restated in Lemmas 46.2 and 46.3, have no odd-
        count restriction. A cycle of large edges permits an increase in the number of small
        edges without changing previously small edges. In a flow on a loopless graph with
        no balanced pair, a small cycle with outward values a, a at a vertex permits balance
        there if an off-cycle end is not −2a. In a loopless flow without a balanced pair, on a
        cycle with exactly one large edge incident with a quartic vertex whose other cycle
        end has small value a, balance is reachable unless both off-cycle values are −2a; the
        exceptional pattern is (a, 3a, −2a, −2a). These moves use coefficients ±1, ±2, hence
        are full modular moves.
    (4) The two full-increment moves (Lemmas 73.1 and 73.2) apply at a loopless quartic
        vertex with pattern (a, a, b, c), with a small and b, c large. A cycle with exactly
        two large edges using one a-end and the b-end balances with one of the coefficients
        −2a, a + b. If the two large neighbors lie in one small component not containing the
        vertex, a cycle formed with a small path in that component balances with coefficient
        −a − b. Digons are permitted.
    (5) Any two bridgeless multigraphs with the same labelled odd vertices have a common
        feasible pattern, including disconnected graphs and different even vertex sets (Propo-
        sition 60.1). This is the classical consequence of Nash-Williams’ pairing theorem [9]
        derived in Section 60.
    (6) For a finite abelian group A, let C be a cycle of length ℓ ≥ 2 in a loopless graph G.
        Let H identify V (C) and delete resulting loops. If ⌈2|A|/3⌉ > ℓ, connectivity of the
        A-flow graph of H implies connectivity for G (Lemma 72.1). The quotient has fewer
        vertices and no larger odd count. This uses the cycle and induced lifting results of
        Lemmas A.1 and A.2, credited there to Cranston et al. [2]. For a noninduced C,
        subdivide every extra internal edge first, then suppress the subdivision vertices: each
        lies on a digon with the contracted vertex. Delete the resulting free loops. Induction
        applies only to the quotient of the original graph, not to the enlarged subdivision
        graph.
None of these inputs gives a general integer lift for a full modular step on an arbitrarily long
cycle. We do not use such a lifting assertion.
                  THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                           117


                              78. The general forest count
   Fix k ≥ 7. Let G be connected and loopless, of degrees three and four, with exactly 2r
cubic vertices. Suppose a flow cannot reach a balanced pair at any quartic vertex by full
modular moves. In its finite full component choose g maximizing the number of small edges.
No state of this component has a balanced pair anywhere, by the supposition at quartic
vertices and conservation at cubic vertices. All small outward values at each vertex therefore
have the same sign. Let S, N be the spanning small and large subgraphs, including isolates.
   The normalization input makes N a forest. Every small cycle is a whole component of
S consisting of cubic vertices. Indeed, if its two outward values at a vertex are a, a, each
off-cycle end must be −2a by the small-cycle input. A quartic vertex would have nonzero
sum −2a. A cubic vertex has pattern (a, a, −2a) and no other small end. Traversal signs
alternate, so the small cycle is even and has length at most 2r.
   For the rest of this section assume S is a forest. Write its trees as T1 , . . . , Ts and let c be
the number of components of N . Every edge between two different small trees is large. For
n = |V (G)|, the degree sum and forest edge counts give
           |E(G)| = 2n − r = (n − s) + (n − c),          s + c = r,      1 ≤ s ≤ r − 1.
Let ni count quartic vertices of small degree i and pi cubic vertices of small degree i. Four
small ends of one sign would sum to 4a ̸= 0, and three at a cubic vertex to 3a ̸= 0. Thus the
quartic types are 0, 1, 2, 3 and the cubic types 0, 1, 2, with p0 + p1 + p2 = 2r. All types refer
to the current normalized flow g.
Lemma 78.1. The number of quartic vertices with two small ends satisfies n2 ≤ 2s .
                                                                                         

Proof. Let v ∈ Ti have type two, with pattern (a, a, b, c). A large edge returning to Ti forms
a one-large-edge cycle with the small-tree path. An untouched small end at v excludes the
exception requiring both off-cycle values −2a, so this would reach balance. If both large
neighbors lie in the same other tree, the second full-increment input reaches balance. Hence
the large edges go to two distinct other trees Tj , Tl .
   The edge e from v to Tj is the only large edge between Ti , Tj . Otherwise take another
such edge e′ = xy, x ∈ Ti . It cannot meet v, whose only other large edge goes to Tl . The
small-tree path from v to x is therefore nonempty. That path, e′ , the path in Tj , and e
form a simple cycle with exactly two large edges, using a small end and e at v. The first
full-increment input reaches balance, a contradiction. The same holds for the other large
edge at v.
   Let U consist of large edges between two different small trees that are the only edge
between their pair. There is at most one member for each unordered pair of trees, so |U | ≤ 2s .
                                                                                              
Every type-two vertex contributes exactly two endpoint incidences    with U , whereas each
edge has at most two type-two endpoints. Thus 2n2 ≤ 2|U | ≤ 2 2s . This argument does not
require N to be connected.                                                                   □
Lemma 78.2. The number of quartic vertices with three small ends satisfies n3 ≤ 2(s − 1).
Proof. A type-three vertex has pattern (a, a, a, −3a). Its only large edge cannot return to its
own small tree by the one-large-edge input. Call a large edge special if at least one endpoint
has type three. It joins different small trees and has value 3 or −3. Each type-three vertex is
an endpoint of exactly one special edge.
  At most one special edge joins any fixed pair Ti , Tj . To see this, choose a type-three
endpoint v ∈ Ti of one such edge e. Any other edge e′ between Ti and Tj forms a cycle with
118                                       DAVE GOEL


e and the two small-tree paths. Its path at v is nonempty since e is the only large edge
at v. Traverse out of v along a small a-end and back along e, whose traversal value is 3a.
Coefficient −2a balances the small end and is safe on e and all small edges. Thus e′ must
have traversal value 2a, or balance is reached. It cannot be special, since ±3 ̸= 2a modulo
k ≥ 7.
   Form the simple graph J on the s small trees, using the special edges. It is acyclic.
Otherwise choose a simple cycle of J through distinct trees. Its special edges and the paths
joining their endpoints inside those distinct trees form a simple cycle of G. Some paths may
have length zero. Choose a type-three endpoint v of a special edge on this cycle. At v its
tree path is nonempty, since that special edge is its only large edge; the cycle uses it and
a small end. All traversal values on this cycle belong to {1, −1, 3, −3}. Traverse out of v
along its small end of sign a and add −2a. All resulting values are in {1, −1, 3, −3, 5, −5},
nonzero for k ≥ 7; the changed small end becomes −a and balances an untouched a-end.
This contradiction proves that J is a forest. Hence it has at most s − 1 edges, and its endpoint
count gives n3 ≤ 2(s − 1).                                                                    □

                                79. The small-order base
   For r = 4, 5, 6, 7, the bound B(r) is respectively 25, 36, 48, 61. The eight-terminal argument
of Proposition 74.4 has the sharper bound twenty-two when r = 4; the uniform bound above
is sufficient here.
Lemma 79.1. For every integer r ≥ 4, every finite multigraph on at most B(r) vertices has
connected A-flow graph for every finite abelian group A of order at least 3r + 1.
Proof. The argument is the short-cycle method of [2, Lemma 5.5 and Corollary 5.4]; we spell
out the count and multigraph reductions. Put F (r) = B(r) + 2 = (r − 1)(r + 14)/2. Then
F (4) = 27 < 48 = 3 · 24 , and
                         F (r + 1) = F (r) + r + 7 < 2F (r) (r ≥ 4),
since F (r) − (r + 7) = (r2 + 11r − 28)/2 > 0. Induction gives B(r) < 3 · 2r − 2.
   Induct on vertex count. Take products over components; before each vertex-reducing step
delete independent loop coordinates. Isolates do not matter, bridges give empty spaces, and
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        119


degree-two suppression preserves flows and cycle moves while lowering vertex count. We may
assume minimum degree at least three. Parallel edges give a digon. Otherwise, if the girth
were at least 2r + 1, a breadth-first expansion from a vertex to depth r would contain at least
                            1 + 3(1 + 2 + · · · + 2r−1 ) = 3 · 2r − 2
distinct vertices. At the first collision, two rooted paths would give a cycle of length at most
2r. Edges between final-layer vertices may give length 2r + 1 but do not affect the vertex
count. This contradicts the order bound. Thus a cycle of length at most 2r exists, and
⌈2|A|/3⌉ ≥ 2r + 1 > 2r permits the cycle lifting input. Apply vertex induction to the smaller
quotient of the original graph, with loops deleted, then lift connectivity. This proves the
lemma.                                                                                        □
   This base uses the same classical Moore count as the proof of [2, Lemma 5.5], followed
by their Corollary 5.4. For comparison, [2, Theorem 5.6] gives the sufficient group order
⌊3 log2 (B(r) + 2)⌋. For r = 5 this is 15, since 383 = 54872 lies between 215 and 216 ; it is at
most 3r + 1 = 16. For r ≥ 6, F (6) = 50 ≤ 64 = 26 and the same doubling inequality give
F (r) ≤ 2r , so that threshold is at most 3r. At r = 6 the value of their bound is 16. At r = 4,
the rounded formula of their Theorem 5.6 gives ⌊3 log2 27⌋ = 14 > 13. Their Lemma 5.5,
however, gives a cycle of length at most ⌊2 log2 27⌋ − 1 = 8 on at most twenty-five vertices
after the standard reductions, and their Corollary 5.4 then gives group order ⌊3 · 8/2⌋ + 1 = 13.
Thus their method already supplies the base at r = 4 as well. The main theorem’s case r = 4
is already proved as Theorem 71.1, whose proof uses its sharper reduction to twenty-two
vertices.

                80. Patterns and anchors for arbitrary odd count
   Let H be bridgeless with exactly 2r labelled odd vertices X. A pattern is an r-element
subset P ⊂ X. Its sign function βP equals 1 on P , −1 on X \ P , and zero elsewhere. It
                       on every component is zero and |βP (U )| < |δH (U )| on each nonempty
is feasible if its sum P
cut, where βP (U ) = v∈U βP (v) and δH (U ) consists of edges with exactly one endpoint in
U . The positive orientation of an integer edge map directs every edge by its sign; its unit
divergence is outdegree minus indegree. An anchor of pattern P is an integer circulation of
magnitudes one through r + 1 with positive unit divergence βP . Feasibility is not part of the
anchor definition.
Lemma 80.1. Every feasible pattern on 2r odd vertices has an anchor. For r ≥ 4 and
k ≥ 3r + 1, two anchors of the same pattern in the same graph have joined reductions modulo
k.
Proof. This is the construction and integer joining argument of Lemma 66.2 and the proof of
Lemma 66.3, with r correction paths instead of four. We give the argument for this bound.
Delete loops temporarily, write β = βP , and prescribe h(v) = (deg(v) + β(v))/2. These are
nonnegative integers. For every vertex set U ,
                     X                      |δH (U )| + β(U )
                         h(v) = |EH (U )| +                   ≥ |EH (U )|.
                                                    2
                     v∈U
The inequality follows from feasibility; if the cut is empty, U is a union of components and
β(U ) = 0. The total number of slots is |E(H)|. Hall’s theorem assigns each edge to a slot at
one endpoint: for any edge set its incident vertices U provide at least |EH (U )| slots, hence
enough for that edge set. Since every slot is used, exactly h(v) edges are assigned to v.
120                                         DAVE GOEL


Direct edges out of their assigned endpoints. The orientation has unit divergence β. Every
nonempty cut has both incoming and outgoing edges by the strict cut inequality, so each
nontrivial connected component is strongly connected. Pair minus and plus terminals within
components and add the r simple directed minus-to-plus paths to the unit map. This cancels
its divergence; each edge gains at most r, giving magnitudes one through r + 1. Restore
loops with value one in any chosen direction.
   For joining, the disagreement arcs of the two positive orientations, read in the first
orientation, form a balanced directed subgraph: their signed vector is half the difference of
the two unit maps, which have the same divergence βP . Decompose it into edge-disjoint
directed cycles. On each subtract t = 1 + max φ(e), where φ is the current integer flow
read positively along that directed cycle. Then 2 ≤ t ≤ r + 2, and every changed value
becomes negative with magnitude one through r + 1. The orientations now agree. Decompose
the remaining difference conformally into signed unit cycles; intermediate coordinates stay
between their same-sign endpoints. This gives an integer path within the magnitude bound.
Since r + 1 < k and r + 2 < k, all endpoint reductions are nonzero and all move coefficients
are nonzero modulo k. Reduce the path to obtain the assertion. This uses the integer path
of the cited proof, not its statement about reductions at modulus six.                     □

                                 81. The nested inductions
Proof of Theorem 76.1. Fix a target integer R ≥ 4 and a modulus k ≥ 3R + 1. The order
of induction is as follows: first an outer induction on the odd-count parameter r, with an
arbitrary-degree conclusion; at each fixed r, an edge induction proves the subquartic case;
then a degree-excess induction proves the arbitrary-degree case before the outer induction
advances.
   For 3 ≤ r ≤ R, let P(r) assert connectivity on every finite multigraph with at most 2r odd
vertices in total, at this fixed modulus k, with arbitrary degrees. The six-odd-vertex input
proves P(3). Assume P(r − 1) at all degrees and take r ≥ 4. We have k ≥ 3R + 1 ≥ 3r + 1.
Subquartic graphs at this fixed r. Induct on edge count among graphs of maximum degree at
most four and total odd count at most 2r, allowing disconnected graphs. Delete independent
loops and isolates, take component products, discard empty spaces with bridges, and suppress
degree-two vertices. We may assume connected loopless degrees three or four. If fewer than
2r vertices are odd, parity gives at most 2r − 2, so outer P(r − 1) applies. Otherwise exactly
2r vertices are cubic.
   If there is an even cycle entirely of cubic vertices, its length ℓ is at most 2r. The cycle lifting
input applies because ⌈2k/3⌉ ≥ 2r + 1 > ℓ. Its original quotient has exactly 2r − ℓ ≤ 2r − 2
odd vertices: the contracted vertex is even, since every internal edge removes two ends from
the identified degree sum. That vertex may have high degree. The outer arbitrary-degree
hypothesis P(r − 1) therefore connects the quotient; no current subquartic hypothesis is
used. Lift to finish this case.
   Exclude such cycles. Graphs on at most B(r) vertices are settled outright by Lemma 79.1.
On a larger graph, suppose a flow has no reachable quartic balanced pair. Normalize it as
in Section 78. A small-cycle component would be an all-cubic even cycle, already excluded.
Hence S is a forest, and Proposition 78.3 gives order at most B(r), a contradiction. Every
flow therefore reaches a balanced pair at a quartic vertex.
   At a quartic vertex a pairing partitions its four distinct edges into two pairs; its balanced
family consists of flows balanced on both. Split just one pair; conservation balances the
other, and the vertex retains degree two. Every nonempty family corresponds to a smaller
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                           121


subquartic graph with the same odd labels. It is bridgeless because it supports a flow. Edge
induction and balanced splitting join its flows in the ambient graph. Consider all nonempty
families at all quartic vertices. Any two of their split graphs share a feasible pattern by the
common-pattern input, including when they are disconnected or came from different vertices.
Choose anchors by Lemma 80.1 and lift them to the original graph, preserving magnitudes
and positive unit divergences. These are anchors of the same pattern in the same ambient
graph, one in each family. Their reductions are joined by the same lemma. Thus all families
join, and every flow reaches one. This closes the subquartic edge induction at r.
Arbitrary degrees at the same r. Delete loops and induct on
                                        X
                                Ψ(G) =      max{deg(v) − 4, 0}.
                                          v
The zero case is the subquartic conclusion just proved. Expand an even vertex of degree d ≥ 6
to a triangle with external end groups 2, 2, d − 4, giving degrees 4, 4, d − 2 and decreasing
Ψ by two. For odd d ≥ 5, use groups 1, 2, d − 3, giving 3, 4, d − 1 and decreasing Ψ by one.
Odd count and component membership are preserved. The triangle has no other internal
edges, since the original graph is loopless. This is the triangle expansion method of [2,
Observation 4.6], with the odd case and projection as proved in Lemma 50.3 and in the proof
of Theorem 71.1.
   Every modular endpoint extends over the triangle: if its exterior outward divergences are
β1 , β2 , β3 of sum zero, the cyclic values z, z −β2 , z +β1 exclude at most three parameters, fewer
than k. Extend two endpoints and join upstairs by the smaller-Ψ induction. An upstairs cycle
vector restricts after contraction to an integer circulation with entries 0, 1, −1. Decompose
its signed support into edge-disjoint directed cycles and apply the original modular coefficient
successively. Every retained edge is old or final at each prefix, hence nonzero. Project the
whole path and restore independent loops. This proves P(r) and allows the outer induction
to advance.
   Finally take products over the original components to obtain the per-component bound even
when their total odd count exceeds 2R. For the equivalent form choose R = ⌊(k − 1)/3⌋ ≥ 4;
conversely k ≥ 3r + 1 implies r ≤ ⌊(k − 1)/3⌋.                                                    □

     82. A lower modulus for the same numbers of odd-degree vertices:
                            statement and credit
   Graphs are finite multigraphs, with loops and parallel edges allowed. A loop contributes
two to degree and zero to divergence. Fix reference edge directions. A nowhere-zero Zk -flow
is a nonzero edge assignment with outgoing sum minus incoming sum zero at every vertex. A
cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph, including a loop or a digon; its traversal has signed
vector χC . The reconfiguration graph Mk (G) joins flows f and f + tχC whenever t ̸= 0
modulo k and both assignments are nowhere-zero. Spaces with zero or one flow count as
connected. The analogous definition applies to a finite abelian group A.
Theorem 82.1. Let r ≥ 4 and k ≥ 3r − 2 be integers. If every component of a finite
multigraph G has at most 2r odd-degree vertices, then Mk (G) is connected. Equivalently, for
k ≥ 10, at most 2⌊(k + 2)/3⌋ odd vertices per component suffice.
  For example, the theorem permits eight odd vertices at k = 10, 11, 12, ten at k = 13, 14, 15,
and twelve at k = 16, 17, 18; each odd-count bound also holds above its first stated modulus.
Theorem 76.1 proves the same type of statement for k ≥ 3r + 1. Theorem 71.1 treats r = 4
122                                        DAVE GOEL


and k ≥ 13. The present conclusion is modular only, with all nonzero coefficients. An
auxiliary small-order lemma below concerns all finite abelian groups, and requires both its
order bound on the graph and at most 2r odd vertices in total.
    For large moduli, connectivity for bridgeless graphs without an odd-count restriction
was already known: Esperet et al. [3] treat sufficiently large groups, and our earlier group
manuscript [5] treats order at least 3024 and groups not cyclic of prime-power order whose
order has a divisor d with 4 ≤ d ≤ |A|/10. For those moduli this theorem also follows from
these results.
    The cycle contraction framework and cycle-degeneracy method are due to Cranston et
al. [2, Corollary 5.4 and Lemma 5.5]. The girth counts below are classical Moore counts.
Beyond the sections for Theorem 76.1, the deductions here are the treatment of a remaining
cubic small-cycle component, its weighted incidence bound, and the resulting threshold with
an odd-count-preserving base. The precise comparison matters: Lemma 5.5 of [2] gives a
cycle of length at most ⌊2 log2 (n + 2)⌋ − 1, which is eight when n = 26. Their Corollary
5.4 then gives order thirteen, not eleven or ten. Their proof is rooted at a vertex and also
gives eight at n = 26. The classical count from both ends of an edge, which is not in their
proof, gives length at most seven on twenty-six vertices after the standard minimum-degree
reductions, and hence order eleven. Keeping the odd-count restriction and using a degree-four
root gives length at most six in the class needed here, and hence order ten. We prove this
second count and the hereditary hypothesis in Lemma 84.1, and sketch the first count after
it.

                 83. Normalized flows with one cubic small cycle
   At a loopless vertex, read each incident value outward. A balanced pair is a pair of distinct
incident edges whose outward values sum to zero. Values 1, −1 are small ; all other nonzero
residues are large. Throughout this section r ≥ 4 and k ≥ 7.
   We use the local inputs restated in Section 77, in particular the full-increment moves of
Lemmas 73.1 and 73.2. For clarity, their uses below are these: at a quartic vertex with
pattern (a, a, b, c), where a is small and b, c large, a cycle with exactly two large edges using
an a-end and the b-end reaches balance with coefficient −2a or a + b. If both large neighbors
lie in one other small-edge component, a small path joining them gives a cycle on which
coefficient −a − b balances the vertex. The one-large-edge input applies in a loopless flow
without a balanced pair, on a cycle with exactly one large edge, at a quartic endpoint of
that edge. If its other cycle end has small outward value a, balance is reachable unless both
off-cycle values are −2a. Digons are permitted in these inputs.
   Let G be connected and loopless, with exactly 2r cubic vertices and all other vertices
quartic. Suppose a flow has no reachable balanced pair at any quartic vertex. In its finite
full component, choose a flow maximizing the number of small edges. There is no balanced
pair at any vertex, since one at a cubic vertex would force the third value to be zero. All
small ends at each vertex thus have the same sign. Let S, N be the spanning small and large
subgraphs, including isolates.
   The normalization arguments of Section 78 show that N is a forest and every cycle of S is
a whole component of S, an even cycle of cubic vertices with outward pattern (a, a, −2a).
Assume there is no all-cubic even cycle shorter than 2r in G. Then either S is a forest, or S
consists of one cycle C of length exactly 2r and s tree components T1 , . . . , Ts . In the latter
case all cubic vertices lie on C, so every vertex in a tree is quartic. Any large edge incident
with C has value 2 or −2.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                      123

                                s + c = r,     0 ≤ s ≤ r − 1.
If s = 0 then n = 2r ≤ D(r). Assume s ≥ 1. Let ni count quartic vertices of small degree i.
Four small ends of one sign cannot sum to zero, so 0 ≤ i ≤ 3.
Three small ends. A type-three vertex has pattern (a, a, a, −3a). Its unique large edge cannot
return into its own small tree, by the one-large-edge input. It cannot meet C, because
{3, −3} and {2, −2} are disjoint modulo k ≥ 7. Call a large edge special if it has a type-three
endpoint. All special edges join distinct trees and have values in {3, −3}.
   The special-edge argument of Lemma 78.2 now runs on the s trees alone. Here are the
details. At a type-three endpoint v of a special edge e between two trees, any second special
edge between those trees forms with e and their small-tree paths a simple cycle. The path at
v is nonempty, since e is its sole large edge. Traverse out of v along a small end of value a
and add −2a. Every cycle value is ±1 or ±3, so all new values lie in {±1, ±3, ±5} and are
nonzero. The changed small end becomes −a and balances an untouched a-end. Thus there
is at most one special edge per tree pair.
   Any simple cycle in the resulting simple graph on the trees lifts, using the small paths
within its distinct trees, to a simple cycle of G. Some tree paths may have length zero, but
one at a chosen type-three endpoint is nonempty. The same coefficient −2a reaches balance.
Hence this special-edge graph is a forest. Since each type-three vertex has exactly one special
incidence,
                                         n3 ≤ 2(s − 1).
Two small ends. A type-two vertex v ∈ Ti has pattern (a, a, b, c). Neither large edge can
return into Ti : the untouched small end excludes the exceptional one-large-edge pattern.
The large edges cannot both meet the same other small component either. A small path
between their other endpoints would allow the second full-increment move above. If that
component is C, choose either simple arc of C; coincident endpoints use a path of length
zero and give a digon. In fact, this case with both neighbors on C cannot occur for k ≥ 7:
both large values would be ±2, with sum 0, 4 or −4, never −2a modulo such a k. Thus the
large edges at v lead to two distinct other components.
   Each such edge e is the only large edge between Ti and its target component Q. If a
second such edge e′ existed, it could not meet v, whose other large edge has a different
target component. The small path from v to the Ti -endpoint of e′ is nonempty. Join the
two endpoints in Q by its tree path or a simple arc of C. These two paths and e, e′ form a
simple cycle with exactly two large edges, using a small end at v. The first full-increment
move above reaches balance,   a contradiction.
   There are at most 2s large edges unique between two different trees, and at most s unique

                84. The small-order base with bounded odd count
  The first values D(4), D(5), D(6), D(7) are 26, 38, 50, 64. The following strict bound will
be used:
(14)                             D(r) < 2r+1 − 3 (r ≥ 4).
Indeed, put J(r) = D(r) + 3. Then J(4) = 29 < 32, and
                J(r + 1) − J(r) = 8 + ⌊r2 /2⌋ − ⌊(r − 1)2 /2⌋ ≤ r + 8 < J(r),
where J(r) ≥ 8r − 7 > r + 8 for r ≥ 4. Thus J(r + 1) < 2J(r), which proves (14) by
induction.
Lemma 84.1. Let r ≥ 4 and let A be a finite abelian group with |A| ≥ 3r − 2. Every finite
multigraph on at most D(r) vertices with at most 2r odd vertices in total has connected
A-flow graph.
Proof. Use induction on vertex count, with the empty graph as base case. Take products
over components. Deleting independent loop coordinates is an equivalence within the same
vertex-count stage, not a decrease of the induction parameter. Isolates may be removed,
bridges give empty spaces, and degree-two suppression preserves flows and cycle moves while
decreasing vertex count. These operations do not increase the odd count. If nothing is left
after the reductions, the empty assignment is the unique flow. Otherwise assume connected,
loopless, and minimum degree at least three. Parallel edges give a digon.
   Suppose the graph is simple and has girth at least 2r − 1. A breadth-first expansion to
depth r − 1 from a root of degree d has at least
                        1 + d(1 + 2 + · · · + 2r−2 ) = 1 + d(2r−1 − 1)
distinct vertices: a repeated vertex would give a cycle of length at most 2r − 2. Edges
between vertices in the final layer may give length 2r − 1, but do not reduce this count.
Earlier non-tree edges would give a still shorter cycle. If the graph is cubic, its order is at
most 2r, since every vertex is odd. The root count gives at least 3 · 2r−1 − 2 > 2r vertices, a
contradiction. The last inequality holds at r = 4 and persists since its left-hand exponential
expression increases by more than two at each step. Otherwise choose a root of degree at
least four. The count gives at least 2r+1 − 3 > D(r) vertices, again a contradiction.
   There is therefore a cycle of length at most 2r − 2, strictly below ⌈2|A|/3⌉ ≥ 2r − 1. Use
the short-cycle contractibility criterion and induced lifting of Lemmas A.1 and A.2, with
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        125


the noninduced-cycle repair of Lemma 72.1. Specifically, subdivide extra internal edges to
make the chosen cycle induced, apply lifting, then suppress the new degree-two vertices and
delete their resulting independent loops in the quotient. The induction is applied only to the
smaller quotient of the original graph, with loops deleted, not to the enlarged graph.
   This original quotient still has at most 2r odd vertices. If the contracted vertex set
contains t odd vertices, the new vertex has degree of parity t; all outside degree parities stay
fixed, and deleting loops subtracts two from degree. The total odd count thus changes by
−t + (t mod 2) ≤ 0. The vertex induction applies and lifting proves the claim.                □
   For comparison, an unrestricted base at order 3r − 1 follows without an odd-count
hypothesis: girth at least 2r and minimum degree three force 2(1 + 2 + · · · + 2r−1 ) = 2r+1 − 2
vertices by expanding away from both ends of an edge. A collision within one side gives a
cycle of length at most 2r − 2 and across the sides at most 2r − 1. Thus a simple minimum-
degree-three graph on at most D(r) vertices has a cycle of length at most 2r − 1, suitable for
group order 3r − 1. Parallel edges give a digon; the same standard reductions and vertex
induction supply the unrestricted small-order base. At r = 4 this is 26 < 30, giving length at
most seven and order eleven. The bounded odd-count argument in Lemma 84.1 instead gives
26 < 29 from a degree-four root, or excludes the cubic case using its at most eight vertices.
It gives length at most six and order ten. Both are classical girth counts used within the
method of [2, Lemma 5.5 and Corollary 5.4].

                          85. Assembly and induction order
Proof of Theorem 82.1. Fix R ≥ 4 and k ≥ 3R − 2. As in Section 81, let P(r), for 3 ≤ r ≤ R,
assert connectivity at this fixed modulus for arbitrary-degree multigraphs with at most 2r
odd vertices in total. The six-odd-vertex theorem, Theorem 45.1, gives P(3), since k ≥ 10.
Assume P(r − 1) at all degrees.
   First prove the subquartic case at r by edge induction, allowing disconnected graphs. The
standard reductions in Section 81 leave a connected loopless graph of degrees three and four.
Fewer than 2r odd vertices invokes P(r − 1). Otherwise exactly 2r vertices are cubic. If an
all-cubic even cycle has length less than 2r, its length ℓ ≤ 2r − 2 satisfies
                                     ℓ < 2r − 1 ≤ ⌈2k/3⌉.
Its original quotient has exactly 2r − ℓ ≤ 2r − 2 odd vertices, with the contracted vertex even.
That vertex may have high degree; the outer arbitrary-degree hypothesis P(r − 1) connects
the quotient, and the cycle lifting input finishes this case. Exclude these shorter all-cubic
even cycles.
   Graphs of order at most D(r) are settled outright by Lemma 84.1, whose odd-count
hypothesis holds here. On a larger graph, a flow without a reachable quartic balanced pair
would normalize as in Section 83. Its small support is a forest or one cubic cycle of length 2r
plus trees. Proposition 83.1 would give order at most D(r), a contradiction. Hence every
flow reaches a balanced pair at some quartic vertex.
   The balanced-family and anchor arguments of Sections 80 and 81 now apply with the
following explicit threshold check. A balanced family consists of the flows in which one fixed
pair at a quartic vertex is balanced. Splitting one balanced pair gives a smaller subquartic
graph with the same odd labels; edge induction connects each nonempty family. Its split
graph is bridgeless because it supports a nowhere-zero flow. Any two such graphs share a
feasible pattern by Proposition 60.1, including disconnected graphs and families at different
126                                        DAVE GOEL


vertices. The construction in Lemma 80.1 gives anchors of that pattern with magnitudes one
through r + 1. Lift them back, preserving their positive unit divergence.
Anchor range. The proof of Lemma 80.1 gives the joining for every k > r + 2: it uses reversal
coefficients at most r + 2 and then signed unit-cycle moves, with magnitudes one through
r + 1 throughout. Thus we use its proof below the lemma’s stated modulus range. Here
                                r + 2 < 3r − 2 ≤ k       (r ≥ 4).
Thus reduction gives legal nonstationary modular moves and joins all nonempty balanced
families. This completes the fixed-r subquartic edge induction.
P Only then perform the same-r degree-excess induction from Section 81, with Ψ(G) =
  v max{deg(v) − 4, 0} after loop deletion. For even degree d ≥ 6, expand to a triangle
with external groups 2, 2, d − 4; for odd d ≥ 5, use 1, 2, d − 3. The odd count stays fixed
and Ψ decreases. Every modular endpoint extends over the triangle, since at most three
values of its free parameter are forbidden and k ≥ 10. Join upstairs by the smaller-Ψ
hypothesis. Each cycle step projects to an integer circulation with entries 0, 1, −1, whose
support decomposes into edge-disjoint directed cycles. Apply the same modular coefficient
on these cycles successively: each retained edge is old or final at every prefix, hence nonzero.
This proves P(r) at arbitrary degrees and only then permits the outer induction to advance.
  Take products over the original components to obtain the per-component assertion. For the
equivalent form, choose R = ⌊(k+2)/3⌋ ≥ 4; conversely, k ≥ 3r−2 implies r ≤ ⌊(k+2)/3⌋. □

 86. Eight odd-degree vertices at moduli seven, eight, and nine: statement,
                           scope, and dependencies
   All graphs are finite multigraphs; loops and parallel edges are allowed. A loop contributes
two to degree and zero to divergence. Fix reference edge directions. A nowhere-zero Zk -flow
assigns a nonzero residue to each edge with outgoing sum minus incoming sum zero at
each vertex. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph, including loops and digons. Its
traversal has signed edge vector χC . The graph Mk (G) joins f and f + tχC when t ̸= 0 and
both assignments are nowhere-zero. Spaces with zero or one flow count as connected. The
analogous definition applies to any finite abelian group A.
Theorem 86.1. For k ∈ {7, 8, 9}, if each component of G has at most eight odd-degree
vertices, then Mk (G) is connected.
Corollary 86.2. Using the theorems cited below, the same conclusion holds for every integer
k ≥ 6.
Proof. The case k = 6 is the modular part of Theorem 61.1. Theorem 86.1 supplies 7, 8, 9.
The case r = 4 of Theorem 82.1 supplies all k ≥ 10; Theorems 71.1 and 76.1 already supply
k ≥ 13.                                                                                □
    The statements are about the Zk -flows, with all nonzero coefficients. Modulus five is not
treated in these sections; for it the statement is false (Theorem 96.1).
    The cycle-contraction framework is due to Cranston et al. [2, Corollary 5.4 and Theorem
5.9]. The short-cycle criterion is their Corollary 5.13; we use its explicit proof and the induced
lifting proof given in Lemmas A.1 and A.2, with the original-quotient subdivision repair
of Lemma 72.1. The girth arguments here are classical Moore counts. Common feasible
patterns are the consequence of Nash-Williams’ odd-vertex-pairing theorem [9, Theorem 2]
restated in Proposition 60.1.
                THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                      127


  The precise inputs from the earlier sections are:
   (1) The local normalization and small-cycle operations of Section 13, stated for every
       k ≥ 4 without an odd-count restriction. We recall their proofs where used.
   (2) The one-large-edge exception of Lemma 46.3, restated in Section 77, and the two-
       large-edge operations of Lemmas 73.1 and 73.2. We give the full-coefficient proof
       of the one-large-edge exception below. The forest no-type-two lemma, Lemma 74.2,
       assumes only k ≥ 7, despite the stronger modulus of Theorem 71.1. The special-edge
       forest and its balancing move come from Lemma 78.2 and Lemma 74.3; below we
       extend the marked-edge argument to type-two vertices and refine the endpoint count
       using components of the large support.
   (3) All-degree connectivity with at most six odd vertices for k ≥ 7 from Theorem 45.1;
       balanced splitting as used there.
   (4) Common feasible patterns from Proposition 60.1; the four-path anchor construction
       and joining as stated for k ≥ 7 in Lemma 75.1, also given by the proof of Lemma 80.1,
       whose actual joining range is k > 6 when there are eight odd vertices. We give the
       joining argument below.
   (5) The even-degree triangle expansion of [2, Observation 4.6], with the extension, odd-
       degree version, and cycle projection in Section 50 and Section 81.
   (6) The modulus-six and higher-modulus conclusions in Theorems 61.1, 71.1, 76.1 and 82.1

                       87. Two hereditary small-order bases
Lemma 87.1. A nonempty simple graph of minimum degree at least three, with at most
fourteen vertices and at most eight odd-degree vertices, has a cycle of length at most four.
Proof. Assume girth at least five and work in a component. A vertex of degree at least five
has at least 1 + 5 + 10 = 16 vertices within distance two, so degrees are three or four. Let
p ≤ 8 count cubic vertices and q quartic vertices. If q = 0, the cubic radius-two bound
n ≥ 10 contradicts n = p ≤ 8. At a quartic vertex with t cubic neighbors the same count
gives n ≥ 17 − t ≥ 13.
   If n = 13, every quartic vertex has four cubic neighbors. Thus 4q ≤ 3p, and parity forces
p = 8, q = 5. Each quartic vertex uses six pairs among its four cubic neighbors, and no pair
                                           8
                                            
can recur without a square. But 5 · 6 > 2 , a contradiction.
   If n = 14, every quartic vertex has at least three cubic neighbors, so 3q ≤ 3p, and parity
forces p = 8, q = 6. The quartic induced graph is a matching with m ≤ 3 edges. There are
24 − 2m cross edges, so the cubic induced graph has m edges too. Write d(x) for degree in
this cubic induced graph. Distinct quartic vertices share at most one cubic neighbor, and
adjacent ones share none. Hence
                                  X 3 − d(x)
                                                  ≤ 15 − m.
                                   x
                                           2

Using 3−d    = 3 − 2d + d2 for 0 ≤ d ≤ 3 and x d(x) = 2m gives 3m ≥ 9 + x d(x)
                                            P                              P        
         2                                                                          2 . Thus
m = 3 and every d(x) ≤ 1. The quartic graph is a perfect matching on six vertices; the
cubic graph has three matching edges. For a cubic edge xy, each endpoint has two quartic
neighbors. Each neighbor set uses two of the three quartic matching classes, at most one
vertex per class. The sets share a class. Choosing the same vertex there gives a triangle
128                                        DAVE GOEL


through xy; choosing opposite endpoints of that matching edge gives a square. Both are
impossible.                                                                         □
Lemma 87.2. Under the same degree and odd-count hypotheses, at most nineteen vertices
guarantee a cycle of length at most five.
Proof. Assume girth at least six. Expanding from the endpoints u, v of an edge to depth two
on both sides gives distinct vertices: a repetition would give a cycle of length at most five.
Consequently
                           n ≥ 2 + 3(deg(u) − 1) + 3(deg(v) − 1).
A degree at least five, or an edge with two quartic endpoints, would force n ≥ 20. Thus
degrees are three or four and the quartic vertices are independent. With p ≤ 8 cubic vertices,
4q ≤ 3p gives q ≤ 6, n ≤ 14. If q > 0, a quartic–cubic edge forces n ≥ 17 by the displayed
count. If q = 0, a cubic edge forces n ≥ 14 > p. Both cases contradict the bounds.          □
Lemma 87.3. Let G have at most eight odd vertices per component. Its full A-flow graph is
connected if either
   (1) |V (G)| ≤ 14 and |A| ≥ 7, or
   (2) |V (G)| ≤ 19 and |A| ≥ 8.
Proof. This base follows the suppress–count–contract induction of [2, Lemma 5.5, Corollary
5.4 and Theorem 5.6], retaining the odd-count hypothesis as in Lemma 84.1. Lemmas 87.1
and 87.2 provide the specific bounds fourteen and nineteen: without the odd-count hypothesis,
the stated Theorem 5.6 gives group orders twelve and thirteen at those orders, respectively,
rather than the orders seven and eight obtained here. Induct on vertex count. Take component
products, delete independent loops and isolates, handle bridges by empty flow spaces, and
suppress degree-two vertices. A remaining nontrivial graph has minimum degree three.
Parallel edges give a digon; otherwise the corresponding graph lemma supplies a cycle C of
length at most four or five. In either case ⌈2|A|/3⌉ > |E(C)|.
    For completeness, the repair for a non-induced cycle is essential. Subdivide once every
non-C edge with both ends on C, obtaining G∗ in which C is induced. In G∗ /C, suppressing
these added vertices and deleting the resulting independent loops gives the quotient of the
original G by C, with loops deleted. That graph has strictly fewer vertices and no greater
odd count: the contracted vertex is odd exactly when C contained an odd number of odd
vertices. Induct on this original quotient, restore loops and suppressions, apply induced
lifting (Lemma A.2), and undo the initial subdivisions. No induction is applied to G∗ . □

                          88. Normalization and local inputs
   At a loopless vertex read all values outward. A balanced pair is a pair of distinct incident
edges whose values sum to zero. For k ≥ 7, call ±1 small and the other nonzero residues
large. Consider a connected loopless graph with eight cubic vertices and all other vertices
quartic. Suppose a full flow component K cannot reach any quartic balanced pair. At cubic
vertices balance is impossible too, since it would force the remaining value to zero. Call a
flow in such a component avoiding.
   Choose a flow maximizing the number of small edges in K. Its spanning large support N
is a forest: on a large cycle, represent traversal values in [2, k − 2] and subtract one until the
first value becomes one, increasing small edges without changing an already small edge. The
small support S retains isolates. Every small cycle is an entire even cubic component. Indeed
its two ends at a vertex have the same sign a, or balance already exists. The small-cycle
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          129


operation forces every off-cycle end to be −2a. To see this operation, if an off-cycle end
is b ̸= −2a, then t = a + b avoids 0, a, −a. Orient the cycle to enter the selected a-end
and represent t in [2, k − 2]. Starting with a step of size two and then unit steps reaches
cumulative coefficient t while all traversal values ±1 remain nonzero; the selected outward
end becomes a − t = −b. A quartic small-cycle vertex would therefore have sum 2a − 4a ̸= 0,
impossible. A cubic one has exactly its two small ends and the large end −2a; signs alternate
along the cycle. This small-cycle conclusion holds for any avoiding flow, whether or not it is
maximal.
   Let s be the number of tree components of S and c the number of components of N . Even
when S has cubic cycle components,
(15)        |E(S)| = n − s,    |E(N )| = n − c,     |E(G)| = 2n − 4,       s + c = 4.
If s = 0, all vertices are cubic and n = 8. Otherwise 1 ≤ s ≤ 3. Let ni count quartic vertices
with i small ends and pi cubic vertices with i small ends. Then i ≤ 3 and i ≤ 2, respectively,
and all small ends at a vertex have one sign. Summing small degree minus two, with cubic
cycle vertices contributing zero, gives
(16)                          q = 2n3 + n2 + 2s − n0 − 2p0 − p1 ,
where q is the number of quartic vertices and n = 8 + q.
   We also use the following precise local inputs. The one-large-edge exception is Lemma 46.3,
restated in Sections 72 and 77: on a cycle with exactly one large edge, at its quartic endpoint
with small cycle end a, balance is reachable unless the two off-cycle values are both −2a; the
exceptional tuple is (a, 3a, −2a, −2a). Here is its full-coefficient proof, valid for every k ≥ 4.
Write the outward values at the endpoint as a, x, y1 , y2 , with x on the large edge and y1 , y2
off the cycle. Traverse out along the a-end and back along the large edge. The coefficient
ti = −a − yi creates balance with the unchanged yi -end. It is legal provided ti ∈ / {0, a, −a, x}.
The first exclusion follows from no existing balanced pair; equality to −a would make yi = 0,
and equality to x would make the other off-cycle value zero by conservation. The only
remaining obstruction is ti = a, equivalent to yi = −2a. Thus at least one choice works
unless both off-cycle values are −2a, when conservation gives x = 3a.
   The two-large-edge inputs are Lemmas 73.1 and 73.2. On a cycle with exactly two large
edges using an a-end and a b-end of a quartic tuple (a, a, b, c), where b, c are large, at least
one of the coefficients −2a, a + b reaches balance. If the two large neighbors of that vertex
lie in one other small tree, a path in that tree gives the additional balancing operation of
Lemma 73.2. These statements allow digons and assume the loopless avoiding setting. In
particular, for every avoiding flow with both S and N forests,
(17)                                   n2 = 0      (k ≥ 7).
No maximality assumption is needed for this assertion once both supports are forests. Here
is the bookkeeping behind this last cited lemma. A type-two vertex cannot send a large edge
back to its own small tree by the one-large-edge exception, since its other small end is not
−2a. Nor can both large neighbors be in the same other tree. Only s = 3 with neighbors in
both other trees remains. Then N is connected by (15). Its path from another vertex of the
first small tree to the chosen vertex first leaves that tree on a different edge. Pair that edge
with the edge to the same other small tree; the two small paths close a two-large-edge cycle
at the type-two vertex, and the cited operation reaches balance.
130                                         DAVE GOEL


                         89. Modulus seven: structural bounds
   Throughout this section k = 7. A type-two quartic tuple is (a, a, 2a, 3a); a type-three
tuple is (a, a, a, −3a). For type two, negate to a = 1: the two large residues lie in {2, 3, 4, 5}
and sum to five, forcing the pair 2, 3. If s = 0, all vertices are cubic and n2 = n3 = 0. For
the auxiliary counts that follow assume s ≥ 1. Mark the large edges of magnitude three
incident with either type. Each qualifying vertex has exactly one such incidence, and these
edges cannot meet cubic small-cycle components, whose exterior values have magnitude two.
More generally, any cycle with values ±1, ±3 that contains a marked edge reaches balance
at a qualifying endpoint of that edge: its other cycle edge there is small, since that vertex
has only one magnitude-three end. Orient out along this small a-end and add −2a. The
