move is legal on every cycle edge and changes that end to −a, balancing an untouched a-end.
Thus any corresponding small path at the chosen endpoint is nonempty. Applying this rule
to lifted auxiliary loops or cycles proves that the marked edges form a forest on the small
trees. The lift uses their unique small paths, and also covers parallel auxiliary edges. This
extends the special-edge argument of Lemma 78.2 and Lemma 74.3 to marked ends of type
two. Consequently
(18)                                   n2 + n3 ≤ 2(s − 1).
  Call a marked edge special if it has a type-three endpoint. If there are e special edges
and d with two type-three endpoints, then n3 = e + d. Each double-ended edge is an entire
two-vertex component of N . Cubic vertices require another component, so d ≤ c − 1. The
auxiliary forest gives e ≤ s − 1, whence
(19)                                   n3 ≤ s + c − 2 = 2.
Equations (15), (16), (18) and (19) hold for every avoiding flow whose large support is a
forest, with s ≥ 1 for the auxiliary forest counts and the already settled s = 0 case treated
separately. Their derivations use no further maximality: maximizing the number of small
edges was used only to ensure that N is a forest.
Lemma 89.1. In any avoiding flow, not necessarily normalized, each of the three quartic
pattern classes
       T = (a, a, a, −3a),   A = (a, 2a, 2a, 2a),   D = (2a, −3a, −3a, −3a),       a = ±1,
has at most two vertices. These are three separate bounds.
Proof. Normalize on large cycles, never changing an already small edge. A starting T -vertex
has large degree one and none of its incident edges is ever changed. Large support only
loses edges during these operations. At the terminal state, s = 0 gives no quartic vertices;
otherwise (19) applies by the preceding observation, even though this terminal state need
not maximize small edges. Thus at most two starting T -vertices existed. Multiplication
by a nonzero residue modulo seven is an automorphism of the full modular flow graph
and preserves balanced pairs in both directions. Multiplication by four takes A to T , and
multiplication by two takes D to T . Apply the first bound to these scalar images. Scaling is
used only as an automorphism, not as a reconfiguration path.                               □
  The small-degree-one quartic tuples, up to permutation of large ends, are exactly
(20)        A = (a, 2a, 2a, 2a),   B = (a, 3a, −2a, −2a),    E = (a, −3a, −3a, −2a).
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          131


For a = 1, the large residues 2, 3, 4, 5 have triple sum six or thirteen. The possible triples are
                              (2, 2, 2),    (3, 5, 5),    (4, 4, 5).
A small-degree-zero quartic vertex is exactly of class D. There are no opposite ends; each
magnitude therefore has one sign. Equal signs give sum between eight and twelve, never zero
modulo seven. With opposite signs, write x ends equal to two and 4 − x equal to minus three.
Only x = 1 makes 5x − 12 zero modulo seven. A cubic small leaf has a magnitude-three end,
since two values in {2, −2} cannot sum to the negative of a small value.
Lemma 89.2. The small path closing a large edge inside one small tree has no internal
quartic vertex. A quartic endpoint must have pattern B. If that path has length one and a
quartic endpoint, balance is reachable.
Proof. At an internal type-two vertex (a, a, 2a, 3a), either coefficient 3a or −3a changes
its two small ends to 4a, −2a, balancing the off-cycle ends. Both are safe on small edges;
the single large edge forbids at most one. At type three the same argument uses 2a, −2a,
producing 3a, −a. The endpoint assertion is precisely the one-large-edge exception recalled
above. For a length-one path at a B-endpoint, orient out on its small a-edge and add a. Its
two cycle ends become 2a, 2a, balancing the other ends; the digon move is legal.         □

                      90. Modulus seven: cyclic small support
   Choose g to maximize small edges over K ∪ 4K ∪ 2K. Here λK = {λf : f ∈ K} is
the scalar image of the component. This is the scalar-orbit maximization method used at
modulus five in Section 41, applied here to the orbit under four. This finite union is invariant
under multiplication by four; every component in it is avoiding. The chosen flow is also
maximal in its own component, so all preceding normalization results apply. If its small
support is a forest, proceed to the next section. Here assume that this chosen flow has a
small cycle.
   An all-cubic cycle of length two or four already settles connectivity of G: contract it by
Lemmas A.1 and A.2 and the original-quotient repair, since ⌈14/3⌉ = 5 > 4. The quotient
has at most six odd vertices, so Theorem 45.1 applies. Exclude these solved graph cases. If
S has a cycle, it is the only one and has length ℓ = 6 or 8.
   We show next that every large edge may be assumed to join distinct small components,
the other cases already proving connectivity. For an internal tree edge, Lemma 89.2 restricts
its closing path to cubic internal vertices and B quartic endpoints. When ℓ = 8, no outside
cubic vertex exists, leaving only a forbidden length-one path between quartics. When ℓ = 6,
only two cubic vertices remain outside the cycle. If both endpoints are quartic, their small
signs are opposite because their large values are 3a and opposite across the edge. Small signs
alternate along the small path, so it has odd length, not one, hence exactly three. With
one cubic endpoint, length one is excluded at the quartic end by Lemma 89.2, and at most
one internal cubic vertex remains, so its length is exactly two. With two cubic endpoints its
length is one, giving an all-cubic digon already excluded above. In all cases the resulting
cycle has length at most four and exactly two odd vertices. Its original quotient has at most
six odd vertices, so contraction again proves connectivity of G. Finally, a large chord of the
cubic small cycle joins opposite small signs. Its shorter arc has odd length one or three,
giving an all-cubic digon or square, already settled. These alternatives establish connectivity
of the whole graph, not necessarily a direct balance in the chosen state.
132                                       DAVE GOEL


   Consider the remaining case for this same chosen flow g. Let m2 , m3 count edges of
magnitudes two and three. In 4g the m2 edges are small, while the cubic cycle has alternating
traversal values ±3. Adding two along it is legal and makes exactly ℓ/2 further edges small.
Hence
(21)              n − s ≥ m2 + ℓ/2,       m3 ≥ s − c + ℓ/2 = 2s − 4 + ℓ/2.
Every magnitude-three edge joins two small trees.
   Let J be the auxiliary forest of marked magnitude-three edges constructed for (18). An
unmarked magnitude-three edge joining vertices already connected in J would close a lifted
{±1, ±3}-cycle and balance at a qualifying endpoint by −2a. If J is a spanning tree, therefore
m3 = s − 1, contradicting (21). If J is empty, (16) gives q ≤ 2s ≤ 6, hence n ≤ 14, already
covered by Lemma 87.3. The sole remaining case has s = 3 and one marked edge. An
additional magnitude-three edge cannot join its end trees, nor can such edges join the third
tree to both of them: that would give a forbidden auxiliary triangle. All additional edges
therefore join the third tree T0 to one marked tree U .
   The tree T0 has no quartic type-two or type-three vertex, so it is a path or an isolate.
An internal cubic vertex has only a magnitude-two large end. A small leaf has at most two
magnitude-three ends: three equal-sign such ends cannot sum to the negative of its small
end. An isolate has at most three such ends. Thus at most four magnitude-three incidences
meet T0 , and m3 ≤ 5. Equation (21) excludes ℓ = 8 and forces ℓ = 6, m3 = 5.
   There are exactly four unmarked edges between T0 and U . The tree T0 is a nontrivial
path with endpoints x, y, each incident with two of them. The tree U cannot be isolated,
because the marked edge and these four edges would give degree at least five. It contains
at most one qualifying quartic vertex, already using its unique magnitude-three end on the
marked edge. All other internal vertices are cubic and cannot meet those four edges. Thus
they meet only leaves of U . If U has no type-three vertex, it has at most two leaves, giving
four large edges on at most four vertices in the forest N , impossible. Otherwise U has one
branch z and three leaves. The four edges form a forest on the five vertices x, y, u1 , u2 , u3
and therefore a tree. Their degrees at x, y are two; after renaming, they form the path
                                    u1 − x − u2 − y − u3 .
Close it by the small path in U from u1 to u3 . This is a simple cycle through z, avoiding the
third leaf internally. Its values are ±1, ±3; either coefficient ±2a is safe. At z its two small
cycle ends become 3a, −a, balancing the untouched ends −3a, a. This excludes the last case.
We have proved that, except for solved graph cases, the scalar-union maximum on a graph
of order greater than fourteen has S a forest.

                      91. Modulus seven: the remaining trees
  We retain the scalar-union maximum, assume n > 14, and now have both S, N forests.
By (17), n2 = 0, and (19) gives n3 ≤ 2.
  Any magnitude-three edge internal to a small tree closes a path whose internal vertices
are cubic by Lemma 89.2. A quartic endpoint is B, hence a small leaf. A cubic endpoint
cannot have small degree two, whose large value would be ±2; it too is a leaf. This path
therefore covers the entire small tree, which is a path with no branch vertex. It has at most
one internal magnitude-three edge, since a second would be parallel in the forest N .
  Special edges at type-three vertices form an auxiliary forest. An additional intertree
magnitude-three edge closing a path of special edges would balance at a type-three endpoint
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                         133


by the safe −2a move. In particular, a spanning special tree leaves no additional intertree
magnitude-three edge. With just one special edge joining Y, P and a third tree Q, no
additional such edge joins Y, P , and edges cannot occur on both pairs Y, Q and P, Q.
   Suppose n3 = 2. For s = 1 the special forest excludes this. For s = 2, the two special
incidences are the two ends of one edge. Each small tree has one branch and three leaves. It
has neither an internal magnitude-three edge nor another intertree one, so all its leaves are
quartic A-vertices, contradicting Lemma 89.1. For s = 3, N is connected; a double-type-three
edge would be an isolated N -component, impossible with cubic vertices present. The two
special edges are therefore distinct and span the three trees. If their type-three vertices lie in
different trees, one such tree is an auxiliary leaf and its three leaves are all A. If they lie in
the same tree, that tree has four leaves, all A. Both cases contradict the same bound. Thus
n3 ≤ 1.
   Equation (16) settles n3 = 0 and n3 = 1, s ≤ 2 by n ≤ 14. Hence n3 = 1, s = 3, c = 1. Put
L = n0 + 2p0 + p1 . Then q = 8 − L > 6, so L ≤ 1 and p0 = 0. Let Y contain the unique
branch z, with special edge e to P ; let Q be the third tree. The three leaves of Y cannot all
be A. By (20) and the cubic-leaf observation, a leaf not of class A has an end of magnitude
three. There is no internal magnitude-three edge in Y , so an additional one goes from Y to
Q. The auxiliary-cycle observation excludes every such edge P –Q and any additional one
Y –P . Such an edge can meet Y only at a leaf: z has its sole large end on e, and every other
internal vertex is cubic with tuple (a, a, −2a). All Y –Q magnitude-three edges meet the
same leaf of Y . Otherwise two of them, the joining path in Q (possibly of length zero), and
the path through z in Y form a simple {±1, ±3}-cycle balancing at z by ±2a. The other
two leaves of Y are thus A-vertices.
   If P is isolated, it is quartic since p0 = 0, hence of class D. It needs three magnitude-three
ends, but after e no destination is allowed for the other two. If Q is isolated it similarly needs
three such ends, all to one leaf of Y , which has capacity at most two. Hence P, Q are nontrivial
paths, n0 = 0, p1 ≤ 1. The path P has only one intertree magnitude-three incidence, e. Its
endpoint u is a leaf of P : an internal vertex is cubic and has only a magnitude-two large end,
since n2 = 0 and the only branch is in Y . Without an internal magnitude-three edge, the
other leaf of P would be a third A. It therefore has an internal edge j joining its two leaves.
If u were quartic, its pattern B would allow only one magnitude-three end, not both e, j.
Thus u is the sole cubic small leaf, with tuple (b, 3b, 3b). The other endpoint v is quartic
B = (a, 3a, −2a, −2a).
   In h = 4g its tuple is (−3a, −2a, −a, −a). Traverse P + j out from v along the small path
and back along j. Path traversal values in h are ±3a and the return value is 2a. Add 2a.
The resulting traversal values are 5a, −a on the path and 4a on the return edge, all nonzero.
At v the outward tuple becomes (−a, 3a, −a, −a), a T -pattern. The two A-vertices in Y
became T under scaling by four and are untouched by this cycle in P . We have an avoiding
flow with three T -vertices, contradicting Lemma 89.1. This completes the modulus-seven
local argument, including its solved small-order and contraction alternatives.

                    92. Modulus eight: parity and marked edges
  The following operation extends the safe even shifts on odd-edge cycles from Section 56
and Lemma 62.1 to arbitrary even cyclic moduli.
134                                       DAVE GOEL


Lemma 92.1. In a loopless nowhere-zero flow over a cyclic group of even order, a quartic
vertex with four odd incident residues can reach a balanced pair by a full cycle move, unless
one already exists there.
Proof. Reduction modulo two makes the odd-edge support an even graph. Every edge of
this support belongs to a cycle, including parallel-edge digons. Choose such a cycle through
an edge at the vertex and orient out along its value x. Choose one off-cycle end of value z.
The coefficient t = −x − z is even and nonzero if that pair is not already balanced. All cycle
residues remain odd, hence nonzero. The chosen end becomes x + t = −z, giving balance. □
   At modulus eight, normalize an avoiding component as above. If s = 0, then n = 8
and Lemma 87.3 applies; hence assume s ≥ 1 before the auxiliary forest counts below. A
type-three tuple (a, a, a, −3a) has four odd ends and is excluded by Lemma 92.1. Thus
n3 = 0. At a type-two vertex (a, a, b, c), b, c have the same parity; both odd is again excluded.
Among 2, 4, 6, the only unordered pair summing to −2a is {2a, 4a}. Thus the tuple is exactly
(a, a, 2a, 4a), with one value-four edge.
   Mark these value-four edges. They cannot meet a cubic small-cycle component, whose
exterior values are ±2. The auxiliary graph on small trees is a forest: a loop or cycle lifts to
a simple cycle of values ±1, 4. At a qualifying type-two endpoint the small path is nonempty,
since it has only one value-four end. Orient out along its small a-end and add −2a. All
changed values are nonzero, including 4 − 2a, and the changed small end balances the other
small end. This contradiction excludes every auxiliary loop and cycle. Hence n2 ≤ 2(s − 1),
and (16) gives
                   q = n2 + 2s − n0 − 2p0 − p1 ≤ 4s − 2 ≤ 10,         n ≤ 18.
Both cyclic and forest small support are included. Lemma 87.3 covers every such graph.

           93. Modulus nine: a weighted count for two edge classes
  Normalize an avoiding component at modulus nine. Type three has tuple (a, a, a, −3a). At
type two, negating to a = 1, the large residues are 2, . . . , 7 and sum to seven: their ordinary
sum lies from four to fourteen. Thus the exact possibilities are
                               (a, a, 2a, −4a),     (a, a, 3a, 4a).
Each type-two vertex has exactly one magnitude-four end, though it may also have a
magnitude-three end. If s = 0, then n = 8 and Lemma 87.3 applies; hence assume s ≥ 1
before the counts that follow.
   Mark class-three edges of magnitude three incident with type three, and class-four edges
of magnitude four incident with type two. These edge classes are disjoint and cannot meet
small-cycle components, whose exterior values have magnitude two. Their union forms an
auxiliary forest on the small trees. To prove this, consider a lifted auxiliary loop or cycle.
If it contains a class-three edge, select a type-three endpoint. Its small path is nonempty
because it has just one large end. If the cycle has only class-four edges, select a qualifying
type-two end; its path is nonempty because it has only one magnitude-four end. A possible
class-three edge there is not on this all-class-four cycle. In either case orient out along a
small a-end and add −2a. The traversal residues lie in {±1, ±3, ±4}, which excludes 2a
modulo nine. The move is legal and the new small end −a balances an untouched a-end.
This contradiction proves the forest assertion, including digons and zero-length joining paths
in other trees.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                      135


  Write e3 , e4 for the class sizes and d for class-three edges with two type-three ends. Then
                      e3 + e4 ≤ s − 1,      n3 = e3 + d,      n2 ≤ 2e4 .
Each double-type-three edge is an entire two-vertex component of N , and cubic vertices
require another component. Thus d ≤ c − 1. The key weighted bound is
                   2n3 + n2 ≤ 2(e3 + e4 ) + 2d ≤ 2(s − 1) + 2(c − 1) = 4.
Type-two endpoints on class-three edges are not counted as type-three ends; they are
counted at their own class-four incidence. Substituting in (16) gives q ≤ 4 + 2s ≤ 10, hence
n ≤ 18. Lemma 87.3 applies. This argument uses only that lemma’s independent graph and
contraction proof, not the global modulus-eight result or its parity operation.

                  94. Splitting, anchors, and the two inductions
Proof of Theorem 86.1. Fix one of k = 7, 8, 9. First use edge induction for graphs of max-
imum degree four with at most eight odd vertices in total, allowing disconnected graphs.
Delete independent loops and isolates, take component products, discard bridge cases with
empty spaces, and suppress degree-two vertices. At most six cubic vertices is the all-degree
result of Theorem 45.1. With exactly eight cubic vertices, Lemma 87.3 settles order at most
fourteen when k = 7, and at most nineteen when k = 8, 9. Sections 90 and 91 use n > 14 at
seven; Sections 92 and 93 bound every normalized avoiding graph by n ≤ 18 at eight and nine.
In graphs above the corresponding small-order threshold, the preceding modulus-specific
arguments show that every full component reaches a quartic balanced pair, or they already
establish connectivity of the whole graph by a short-cycle contraction.
   For the former alternative, split a chosen balanced pair at its quartic vertex, replacing
its two edges by one. This reduces edge count and preserves odd labels. A split of a digon
may create a loop; it is an independent coordinate handled by the loop reductions. The
smaller-edge hypothesis and balanced splitting connect each nonempty balanced family.
Every nonempty split graph is bridgeless because it supports a flow. The common-pattern
result, Proposition 60.1, supplies a feasible pattern on the same eight labelled odd vertices
for any two split graphs, including disconnected ones and ones with different even vertices. A
pattern assigns positive unit divergence to four odd vertices, negative unit divergence to the
                                      P β for this sign function. Feasibility means that its
other four, and zero to all others. Write
sum on each component is zero and | v∈U β(v)| < |δ(U )| for every nonempty cut δ(U ). The
positive orientation of an integer edge map directs each edge by its sign; its unit divergence
is outdegree minus indegree. An anchor is an integer circulation whose positive orientation
has these divergences and whose magnitudes are one through five.
   We recall the relevant range of the anchor construction in the proof of Lemma 80.1, also
stated for k ≥ 7 in Lemma 75.1. Remove loops temporarily, as in those proofs. Feasibility
permits a strongly connected orientation in each component with that unit-divergence pattern.
Add four simple directed paths from negative to positive terminals to its unit map, producing
an anchor of magnitudes at most five. Restore the removed loops with value one in a chosen
direction. These anchors lift through balanced splitting, preserving positive unit divergence.
For two lifted anchors of the same pattern, their disagreeing orientation arcs form a balanced
directed subgraph in the first orientation. Decompose it into edge-disjoint directed cycles.
On each subtract one plus its largest current positive value. This coefficient lies between two
and six, changes every value on that cycle to a negative value of magnitude at most five, and
makes the orientations agree. Finally decompose the remaining difference conformally into
136                                       DAVE GOEL


signed unit cycles. All intermediate values lie between same-sign endpoints. Since 5 < k for
the values and 6 < k for the coefficients, reducing this integer path gives legal full modular
moves. We invoke the proof’s actual range, not the stronger printed hypothesis of that
lemma. All nonempty balanced families therefore join, even at different quartic vertices.
This completes the subquartic induction.        P
   Only now induct on degree excess Ψ(G) = v max{deg(v) − 4, 0} after loop deletion, at
the same odd-count bound. Expand an even vertex d ≥ 6 to a triangle with external groups
2, 2, d − 4, and an odd vertex d ≥ 5 with groups 1, 2, d − 3. The even expansion is that of [2,
Observation 4.6]; the extension, odd-degree case and cycle projection are as in Section 50
and Section 81. The number of odd vertices is unchanged and Ψ decreases. Every modular
endpoint extends because at most three choices of the triangle parameter are forbidden and
k > 3. Join upstairs by induction. A cycle move projects to an integer circulation with
entries 0, 1, −1 on retained edges. Decompose its support into edge-disjoint signed cycles and
apply the same coefficient successively. Each retained coordinate is old or final throughout,
hence nonzero. A projection with empty support is simply omitted. Restore independent
loops. Products over original components give the asserted per-component statement. □

                                      95. What is used
   The deductions here are the type-two marked-edge extensions, the refinement using
components of the large support, their combination with scalar maps in the modulus-seven
elimination, the modulus-eight forest count after the parity operation, and the mixed count at
nine. The fourteen- and nineteen-vertex counts are specific to these sections; they use classical
Moore counting in the odd-count-preserving base pattern of Lemma 84.1 and the contraction
method of [2]. The special-edge forest and balancing move of Lemmas 74.3 and 78.2, the
scalar-orbit method of Section 41, and the parity method of Section 56 and Lemma 62.1 are
reused here. The other local inputs, six-odd theorem, splitting, common patterns, anchors,
triangle expansion and projection, and contraction framework are dependencies. In particular
the scalar maps at seven are used as automorphisms only, and none of the modular arguments
of these sections supplies a global integer-flow transfer.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                      137


Part 8. The modulus five: a graph with eight vertices of odd degree
   This part proves Theorem 1.6 in five sections: it gives a graph with twelve vertices, eight
of them of odd degree, that is neither Z5 -flow-connected nor 5-flow-connected. The sections
give their own words. The statements on this graph use no other section of the paper.
The two corollaries use part (c) of Theorem 1.3 for k = 5 and Corollary 86.2. Three more
sections, from Section 101 on, prove Theorem 101.1: up to isomorphism, this graph and
the graph of Remark 100.1 are the only connected graphs with no loop, with exactly eight
vertices of degree 3 and all other vertices of degree 4, in which a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow is an
isolated vertex of the graph of the flows. The last four sections, from Section 104 on, prove
Theorem 1.7: a connected graph with at least one edge and at most eight vertices of odd
degree has such a flow if and only if it is a subdivision of one of these two graphs.

96. A graph with eight odd-degree vertices that is not Z5 -flow-connected:
                            statement and credit
   Graphs are finite; loops and parallel edges are allowed in general, but the graph of these
sections has none. A loop adds two to the degree of its vertex. Fix a direction of every edge.
For an integer k ≥ 2, a Zk -flow of a graph G is a map f : E(G) → Zk such that at every
vertex the sum of the values on the edges directed out of it equals the sum of the values on
the edges directed into it. It is nowhere-zero if no edge has the value 0. A nowhere-zero
k-flow is a map g : E(G) → Z with the same condition at every vertex, the sums being taken
in Z, and with 1 ≤ |g(e)| ≤ k − 1 for every edge e.
   A cycle is a connected subgraph in which every vertex has degree two. Following [3],
the graph F(G, Zk ) has the nowhere-zero Zk -flows of G as its vertices, and two of them
are adjacent if the set of the edges on which they differ is the edge set of a cycle. The
graph F(G, k) is defined in the same way on the nowhere-zero k-flows. A graph with no
vertex counts as connected. The graph G is Zk -flow-connected if F(G, Zk ) is connected, and
k-flow-connected if F(G, k) is connected. An isolated vertex of a graph is a vertex with no
neighbour. If two flows differ exactly on the edges of a cycle C, then one of them comes from
the other by adding one value x ̸= 0 on the edges of C that are passed along their directions
and subtracting it on the others, when C is passed once in one direction (Lemma 98.2); and
two flows of which one comes from the other in this way differ exactly on the edges of C. So
F(G, Zk ) is the graph Mk (G) of Section 86, for k = 5 it is the graph M5 (G) of Section 36,
and F(G, 5) is the graph I5 (G) of that section.
Theorem 96.1. Let G0 be the graph of Section 97. It has twelve vertices and twenty edges,
no loop and no parallel edge, and it is connected and bipartite. Eight of its vertices have
degree three and four have degree four; so it has exactly eight vertices of odd degree. The
map f0 of Section 97 is a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow of G0 and an isolated vertex of F(G0 , Z5 ).
Neither F(G0 , Z5 ) nor F(G0 , 5) is connected.
Corollary 96.2. Let k ≥ 4 be an integer. The following two statements are equivalent.
   (i) Every graph in which every connected component has at most eight vertices of odd
       degree is Zk -flow-connected.
  (ii) k ≥ 6.
Corollary 96.3. Every graph in which every connected component has at most six vertices
of odd degree is Z5 -flow-connected and 5-flow-connected, and in neither of the two statements
can “six” be replaced by “eight”.
138                                          DAVE GOEL


   The number of the vertices of odd degree of a graph, and so of each connected component,
is even. So Corollary 96.3 says that, for the modulus five, six is the largest even number t
such that every graph in which every connected component has at most t vertices of odd
degree is Z5 -flow-connected; and the same for 5-flow-connected.
Credit. A graph that has a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow and is not Z5 -flow-connected was given
by Esperet, Hendrey, Lagoutte, Marseloo, Norin and Steiner [3, Theorem 3.4]; we know of
no earlier one. Their Lemma 3.5 describes all nowhere-zero Z5 -flows with the values 1 and 2
that are isolated vertices of the reconfiguration graph, by two maps η1 , η2 : V (G) → Z. Our
proof that f0 is isolated (Lemma 98.1 and Section 98) is the “if” direction of that lemma
for the maps with η1 = 0 on P ∪ Q, η1 = 1 on R ∪ S, η2 = 0 on P ∪ R and η2 = 1 on
Q ∪ S, in the notation of Section 97; we write the short argument out, so that these sections
can be read alone. The step from the Z5 -flows to the integer 5-flows is the argument of [3,
Observation 2.5]; we give it with an explicit integer flow (Lemma 98.3), and so we do not
use the lifting theorem of Tutte. That a graph with an isolated flow is not flow-connected,
because a multiple of the flow is a second flow, is noted there also. The example of [3] (their
Figure 3) has twenty vertices, all of degree three; so it has twenty vertices of odd degree.
They write that it was found with the help of Y. Wigderson and P. Morawski and of the tool
AlphaEvolve. Their paper also describes a picture for such examples: the vertex set is a set
of points of a grid, and η1 and η2 are the two coordinates. The graph G0 is of the same kind,
with two columns and two rows, but with three vertices at each of the four points; their
Lemma 3.5 allows this.
   The graph G0 is a graph with only eight vertices of odd degree that is not Z5 -flow-connected.
By Theorem 36.1 every graph that is not Z5 -flow-connected has a connected component with
at least eight vertices of odd degree, and G0 shows that eight is possible. The positive parts
of the two exact statements, Corollaries 96.2 and 96.3, are earlier results: Theorem 36.1 for
six vertices of odd degree at the modulus five, and Corollary 86.2 for eight vertices of odd
degree at every modulus k ≥ 6. That corollary collects four results: the modulus 6 is from
Theorem 61.1, the moduli 7, 8 and 9 are Theorem 86.1, the moduli from 10 on are from
Theorem 82.1, and the moduli from 13 on were proved before that in Theorems 71.1 and 76.1.
That K4 is not Z4 -flow-connected is [3, Section 2.4].
   Theorem 96.1 is on the graph G0 only. Remark 100.1 gives a second graph; there the
changes of the proof are named and not written out. Remark 100.2 reports a count by
computer. No proof in these sections uses the two remarks.

                               97. The graph and the flow
  The vertex set of G0 is P ∪ Q ∪ R ∪ S with
      P = {p0 , p1 , p2 },   Q = {q0 , q1 , q2 },       R = {r0 , r1 , r2 },   S = {s0 , s1 , s2 }.
The graph has twenty edges, in two sets E1 and E2 of ten edges each. We write u → v for
an edge with the ends u and v that is directed from u to v.
                    E1 : p0 → q0 ,   p0 → q1 ,      p0 → q2 ,   p1 → q1 , p2 → q2 ,
                         r0 → s0 ,   r1 → s0 ,      r2 → s0 ,   r1 → s1 , r2 → s2 ;
                    E2 : p0 → r0 ,   p1 → r0 ,      p2 → r0 ,   p1 → r1 , p2 → r2 ,
                         q0 → s0 ,   q0 → s1 ,      q0 → s2 ,   q1 → s1 , q2 → s2 .
                    THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                                139


The map f0 : E(G0 ) → Z5 has the value 1 on every edge of E1 and the value 2 on every edge
of E2 .
Lemma 97.1.       (a) The twenty edges join twenty different pairs of different vertices; so
      G0 has no loop and no parallel edge.
  (b) Every edge has one end in P ∪ S and the other end in Q ∪ R; so G0 is bipartite.
  (c) The vertices p0 , q0 , r0 , s0 have degree four, and the other eight vertices have degree
      three.
Proof. (a) and (b) are read from the list: the first line of E1 joins P to Q, the second line
joins R to S, the first line of E2 joins P to R, and the second line joins Q to S; inside each
line the five pairs are different. For (c) we list the neighbours of each vertex, for i = 1, 2:
      p0 : q0 , q1 , q2 , r0 ;   pi : qi , r0 , ri ;   q0 : p0 , s0 , s1 , s2 ;   qi : p0 , pi , si ;
      r0 : p0 , p1 , p2 , s0 ;   ri : pi , s0 , si ;   s0 : r0 , r1 , r2 , q0 ;   si : ri , q0 , qi .
The sum of the degrees is 4 · 4 + 8 · 3 = 40, twice the number of the edges.                            □
Lemma 97.2. The map f0 is a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow of G0 .
Proof. No value is 0. At each vertex we compare the sum of the values on the edges directed
out of it with the sum on the edges directed into it, for i = 1, 2:
           p0 : out 1 + 1 + 1 + 2 = 5, in 0;                pi : out 1 + 2 + 2 = 5, in 0;
           q0 : out 2 + 2 + 2 = 6, in 1;                    qi : out 2, in 1 + 1 = 2;
           r0 : out 1, in 2 + 2 + 2 = 6;                    ri : out 1 + 1 = 2, in 2;
           s0 : out 0, in 1 + 1 + 1 + 2 = 5;                si : out 0, in 1 + 2 + 2 = 5.
In every case the two sums are equal modulo 5.                                                          □
Lemma 97.3.       (a) The edges of E1 form two trees: one with the vertex set P ∪ Q and
      one with the vertex set R ∪ S.
  (b) The edges of E2 form two trees: one with the vertex set P ∪ R and one with the vertex
      set Q ∪ S.
  (c) E2 is the set of all edges with one end in P ∪ Q and the other end in R ∪ S, and each
      of them is directed from P ∪ Q to R ∪ S.
  (d) E1 is the set of all edges with one end in P ∪ R and the other end in Q ∪ S, and each
      of them is directed from P ∪ R to Q ∪ S.
  (e) G0 is connected.
Proof. (a) The five edges of the first line of E1 have their ends in P ∪ Q. The vertex p0 is
joined to q0 , q1 and q2 , the vertex p1 to q1 , and the vertex p2 to q2 ; so these five edges form
a connected graph on the six vertices of P ∪ Q, and a connected graph with six vertices and
five edges is a tree. In the same way the five edges of the second line have their ends in
R ∪ S: the vertex s0 is joined to r0 , r1 and r2 , the vertex s1 to r1 , and the vertex s2 to r2 .
   (b) The five edges of the first line of E2 have their ends in P ∪ R: the vertex r0 is joined to
p0 , p1 and p2 , the vertex r1 to p1 , and the vertex r2 to p2 . The five edges of the second line
have their ends in Q ∪ S: the vertex q0 is joined to s0 , s1 and s2 , the vertex s1 to q1 , and the
vertex s2 to q2 . As in (a), each of the two sets of five edges forms a tree on its six vertices.
   (c) Every edge of E2 is directed from P to R or from Q to S, so from P ∪ Q to R ∪ S.
Every edge of E1 has both ends in P ∪ Q or both ends in R ∪ S. Since every edge is in E1 or
in E2 , this proves (c).
140                                         DAVE GOEL


  (d) Every edge of E1 is directed from P to Q or from R to S, so from P ∪ R to Q ∪ S.
Every edge of E2 has both ends in P ∪ R or both ends in Q ∪ S.
  (e) By (a) the vertices of P ∪ Q are joined to each other, and so are the vertices of R ∪ S;
the edge p0 → r0 joins the two sets.                                                        □

                                98. The flow f0 is isolated
  Let C be a cycle of a graph, and choose one of the two directions in which C can be
passed. An edge of C is passed along its direction or against its direction. If C has one edge
only, a loop, we say that it is passed along its direction.
Lemma 98.1. Let C be a cycle of G0 with a chosen direction of passing. Then C has an
edge of E1 that is passed along its direction, an edge of E1 that is passed against its direction,
an edge of E2 that is passed along its direction, and an edge of E2 that is passed against its
direction.
Proof. By Lemma 97.3(a) the edges of E1 form a graph with no cycle. So C has an edge of
E2 , and by Lemma 97.3(c) this edge has one end in P ∪ Q and the other end in R ∪ S. We
pass once around C and return to the first vertex. So we go from P ∪ Q to R ∪ S as many
times as we go from R ∪ S to P ∪ Q, and, as C has an edge between the two sets, at least
once in each of the two ways. By Lemma 97.3(c) an edge on which we go from P ∪ Q to
R ∪ S is an edge of E2 that is passed along its direction, and an edge on which we go from
R ∪ S to P ∪ Q is an edge of E2 that is passed against its direction.
   The statement for E1 is proved in the same way, with (b) and (d) of Lemma 97.3 in the
place of (a) and (c): the edges of E2 form a graph with no cycle, so C has an edge of E1 ; this
edge joins P ∪ R to Q ∪ S; and every edge between these two sets is an edge of E1 directed
from P ∪ R to Q ∪ S.                                                                         □
Lemma 98.2. Let f and f ′ be two Zk -flows of a graph G, or two integer flows (maps from
E(G) to Z with the condition of a flow at every vertex, the sums being taken in Z), and
suppose that the set of the edges on which they differ is the edge set of a cycle C. Choose a
direction of passing of C. Then there is a value x ̸= 0 such that f ′ (e) = f (e) + x for every
edge e of C that is passed along its direction and f ′ (e) = f (e) − x for every edge e of C that
is passed against its direction.
Proof. Let h = f ′ − f . It satisfies the condition of a flow at every vertex, and it is 0 exactly
on the edges that are not in C. For an edge e of C let h∗ (e) = h(e) if e is passed along its
direction and h∗ (e) = −h(e) if not. Let v be a vertex of C, and let e and e′ be the edge of
C on which we come to v and the edge of C on which we leave v. No other edge at v has
a value of h other than 0. If C has one edge only, a loop at v, then x is the value of h on
it, and there is nothing more to prove. If not, the sum of h on the edges directed out of v
minus the sum on the edges directed into v is h∗ (e′ ) − h∗ (e), and it is 0. So h∗ has the same
value x on any two edges of C that follow each other, and so on all edges of C; and x ̸= 0
since h is not 0 on C.                                                                          □
Proof that f0 is an isolated vertex of F(G0 , Z5 ). Suppose that f ′ is a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow
of G0 that is adjacent to f0 . Let C be the cycle on whose edges they differ, choose a direction
of passing of C, and let x ∈ {1, 2, 3, 4} be the value of Lemma 98.2. By Lemma 98.1 the
cycle C has edges a1 , b1 ∈ E1 and a2 , b2 ∈ E2 such that a1 and a2 are passed along their
directions and b1 and b2 against. Then
         f ′ (a1 ) = 1 + x,   f ′ (b1 ) = 1 − x,   f ′ (a2 ) = 2 + x,    f ′ (b2 ) = 2 − x.
                    THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        141


If x = 1 then f ′ (b1 ) = 0. If x = 2 then f ′ (b2 ) = 0. If x = 3 then f ′ (a2 ) = 5 = 0. If x = 4
then f ′ (a1 ) = 5 = 0. In each case f ′ has the value 0 on an edge, against the assumption. So
f0 has no neighbour in F(G0 , Z5 ).                                                              □
Lemma 98.3. Let g0 : E(G0 ) → Z have the value f0 (e) − 5 on the six edges
        p0 → r0 ,       p 1 → q1 ,   p2 → q2 ,      q0 → s 0 ,    q1 → s1 ,      q2 → s 2 ,
and the value f0 (e) on the other fourteen edges; here the values 1 and 2 of f0 are read as
integers. Then g0 and −g0 are nowhere-zero 5-flows of G0 . Modulo 5, the flow g0 is f0 and
the flow −g0 is −f0 .
Proof. The values of g0 are 1, 2, 1 − 5 = −4 and 2 − 5 = −3; so 1 ≤ |g0 (e)| ≤ 4 on every
edge. The values of g0 are: −4 on p1 → q1 and on p2 → q2 , and 1 on the other eight edges of
E1 ; −3 on p0 → r0 , q0 → s0 , q1 → s1 and q2 → s2 , and 2 on the other six edges of E2 . The
sums in Z, for i = 1, 2:
          p0 : out 1 + 1 + 1 − 3 = 0, in 0;            pi : out − 4 + 2 + 2 = 0, in 0;
          q0 : out − 3 + 2 + 2 = 1, in 1;              qi : out − 3, in 1 − 4 = −3;
          r0 : out 1, in − 3 + 2 + 2 = 1;              ri : out 1 + 1 = 2, in 2;
          s0 : out 0, in 1 + 1 + 1 − 3 = 0;            si : out 0, in 1 + 2 − 3 = 0.
So g0 is a nowhere-zero 5-flow, and then −g0 is one also. The last statement holds because
g0 (e) − f0 (e) is 0 or −5.                                                             □
Proof of Theorem 96.1. The statements on G0 are Lemma 97.1 and Lemma 97.3(e); a graph
with eight vertices of degree three and four of degree four has exactly eight vertices of odd
degree. By Lemma 97.2 the map f0 is a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow, and we have proved that it
is an isolated vertex of F(G0 , Z5 ). The map −f0 is a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow also, with the
values −1 and −2, and it is not f0 , because −1 ̸= 1 in Z5 . So F(G0 , Z5 ) has at least two
vertices, one of them isolated: it is not connected.
    For the integer flows we use the argument of [3, Observation 2.5]. Let g0 be the flow of
Lemma 98.3, and suppose that F(G0 , 5) has a path g0 = h1 , h2 , . . . , hm = −g0 . For each j
let h̄j be the map hj modulo 5. It is a Z5 -flow, and it is nowhere-zero, since 1 ≤ |hj (e)| ≤ 4.
We show by induction on j that h̄j = f0 . For j = 1 this is Lemma 98.3. Let j < m and
h̄j = f0 . The flows hj and hj+1 differ exactly on the edges of a cycle C, and by Lemma 98.2
there is an integer x ̸= 0 such that hj+1 = hj ± x on the edges of C, with the sign + on
the edges passed along their directions and the sign − on the others. If x is a multiple of 5,
then h̄j+1 = h̄j = f0 . If not, then h̄j+1 and h̄j differ exactly on the edges of C, and so h̄j+1
is a neighbour of f0 in F(G0 , Z5 ); but f0 has no neighbour. So h̄j+1 = f0 . For j = m this
gives −f0 = f0 , by Lemma 98.3; this is false. So g0 and −g0 are in different components of
F(G0 , 5).                                                                                     □

                               99. The two exact statements
Proof of Corollary 96.2. If k ≥ 6, then (i) holds by Corollary 86.2. Let k = 5. The graph
G0 is connected and has exactly eight vertices of odd degree, and it is not Z5 -flow-connected
(Theorem 96.1); so (i) does not hold. Let k = 4. The complete graph K4 is connected and
has four vertices of odd degree, and F(K4 , Z4 ) is not connected [3, Section 2.4]; so (i) does
not hold.                                                                                    □
142                                           DAVE GOEL


Proof of Corollary 96.3. The first statement is Theorem 36.1. The graph G0 is connected and
has exactly eight vertices of odd degree, and by Theorem 96.1 it is neither Z5 -flow-connected
nor 5-flow-connected.                                                                       □

                                           100. Remarks
   The remarks of this section are not used in any proof above.
Remark 100.1 (A second graph). Let G′0 be the graph that comes from G0 when the
two edges q1 → s1 and q2 → s2 are replaced by q1 → s2 and q2 → s1 , with the value 2 on
both. Every statement of Lemmas 97.1, 97.2 and 97.3 holds for G′0 with the same proofs,
where now s1 is joined to q2 and s2 to q1 ; so the proof of Section 98 gives that G′0 is not
Z5 -flow-connected either. The two graphs are not isomorphic. In both graphs the vertices of
degree three are pi , qi , ri , si for i = 1, 2. In G0 the edges that join two of them are pi qi , qi si ,
si ri and ri pi for i = 1, 2: two cycles with four edges each. In G′0 they are p1 q1 , q1 s2 , s2 r2 ,
r2 p2 , p2 q2 , q2 s1 , s1 r1 and r1 p1 : one cycle with eight edges.
Remark 100.2 (A count by computer). The following was found by two programs written for
these sections, and it is evidence only. The graph G0 has 225 cycles and 15948 nowhere-zero
Z5 -flows, and F(G0 , Z5 ) has nine components: eight isolated vertices, among them the four
flows f0 , 2f0 , 3f0 and 4f0 , and one component with the other 15940 flows. The graph G′0
of Remark 100.1 has 262 cycles and 14460 nowhere-zero Z5 -flows, again with eight isolated
vertices and one component with the other 14452 flows. Among the connected graphs with no
loop, no parallel edge and no triangle, with eight vertices of degree three and all other vertices
of degree four, and with at most twelve vertices, there are 2 + 3 + 22 + 134 + 1279 = 1440
graphs up to isomorphism (on 8, 9, 10, 11 and 12 vertices); all of them are Z5 -flow-connected,
with two exceptions on twelve vertices, the graphs G0 and G′0 . The example was found by
this count; the proof above does not use it.

      101. Classification with eight cubic vertices: statement and credit
Credit. Esperet, Hendrey, Lagoutte, Marseloo, Norin and Steiner [3, Lemma 3.5] characterize
isolated nowhere-zero Z5 -flows, after orienting their values to lie in {1, 2}, by two integer-
valued vertex maps with forest level sets and strict increase on the other edge class. Both
directions of that criterion are theirs. Their proof of the necessary direction gives the two
forests and their acyclic directed quotients. We repeat that argument in the form needed
here; our converse is its sufficient direction. The additional conclusion here is the exact
classification in the specified degree class, obtained from component counts and four tree
equations. The two resulting graphs are already given in Theorem 96.1 and Remark 100.1.
   All graphs are finite multigraphs. A loop contributes two to degree. The graphs in
the theorem have no loop; parallel edges are initially allowed. Fix reference directions. A
nowhere-zero Z5 -flow assigns a nonzero residue to every edge, with outgoing sum equal to
incoming sum at each vertex. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph, including a digon,
or a loop when loops are allowed. The graph F(G, Z5 ) has these flows as vertices; two flows
are adjacent when the support of their difference is exactly a cycle. A vertex is isolated if it
has no neighbor. Equivalently, a step adds a nonzero constant along a traversal of one cycle,
with signs from the reference directions, and keeps all values nonzero. Indeed, conservation
makes a difference supported on a cycle constant in its traversal direction; for a loop its
nonzero difference is that constant.
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        143


    We specify the two graphs explicitly. Let P, Q, R, S each have three vertices, denoted
p0 , p1 , p2 , and likewise for the other letters. For a permutation σ of {1, 2} let Gσ have the
following directed edges, with the indicated values:
           E1 = {p0 → qj , rj → s0 : 0 ≤ j ≤ 2} ∪ {pi → qi , ri → si : 1 ≤ i ≤ 2},
           E2 = {pj → r0 , q0 → sj : 0 ≤ j ≤ 2} ∪ {pi → ri , qi → sσ(i) : 1 ≤ i ≤ 2}.
Edges in Ei have value i. The underlying graphs for the identity and the transposition are
respectively G0 and G′0 of Section 97 and Remark 100.1; write them as Gid and Gsw here.
Theorem 101.1. Let G be a connected loopless multigraph with exactly eight vertices of
degree three and every other vertex of degree four. Then F(G, Z5 ) has an isolated vertex if
and only if G is isomorphic to Gid or Gsw . These two graphs are not isomorphic.
Corollary 101.2. Under the hypotheses of Theorem 101.1, if an isolated nowhere-zero
Z5 -flow exists, then G has exactly twelve vertices, four of degree four, and twenty edges, and
G is simple.

                      102. Two forests and two directed cuts
  Fix an isolated flow f . Reverse reference directions on edges of value 3 or 4, negating
those values, so every value is 1 or 2. This operation is a bijection preserving conservation
and adjacency, and hence isolation. Write F1 = (V (G), E1 ) and F2 = (V (G), E2 ) for the
spanning subgraphs of the two values. An edge has color i if it belongs to Ei . Component
counts include components consisting of a single graph vertex.
Lemma 102.1. Both F1 , F2 are forests. Contracting the trees of either forest gives an acyclic
directed multigraph on the edges of the other color, with no loop.
Proof. On a cycle with all reference values i ∈ {1, 2}, the signed traversal values are i or
−i. Adding 2i changes them to 3i or i, both nonzero modulo five. Such a cycle contradicts
isolation, so both spanning subgraphs are forests.
   Contract the trees of F1 . A quotient loop, given by an E2 edge, lifts to that edge and
the unique F1 -path between its ends. The original ends are distinct because G is loopless.
Traverse the edge forward and add 2 along this cycle: its value becomes 4, while the signed
values 1, −1 of its other edges become 3, 1. This is a legal step, a contradiction. If a directed
quotient cycle exists, take one with distinct vertices apart from its coincident first and last
vertex. At each contracted tree join the arriving and departing ends by its unique simple
path. The path can have length zero. Paths in different trees are disjoint, and each tree is
visited only once; consequently their union with the quotient edges is a cycle in G, including
the possible digon. Every E2 edge on it is traversed forward. The same increment 2 is legal
on all its edges, again a contradiction.
   For contraction of F2 , repeat the argument with a directed cycle of E1 edges and increment
1. The signed values on those edges become 2; the signed values 2, −2 on the joining paths
become 3, −1. All are nonzero. This also excludes quotient loops and directed cycles.          □
Lemma 102.2. Each of F1 , F2 has exactly two components. Every E2 edge goes in one
common direction between the two F1 components, and every E1 edge goes in one common
direction between the two F2 components. The graph is simple, every vertex meets both colors,
and all four intersections of the two component partitions are nonempty.
144                                        DAVE GOEL


Proof. Put n = |V (G)|, and let s, c count the components of F1 , F2 . Their edge counts are
n − s, n − c. The degree sum is 4n − 8, whence
                     (n − s) + (n − c) = |E(G)| = 2n − 4,         s + c = 4.
If s = 1, every E2 edge would be a loop after contraction of F1 , contrary to Lemma 102.1.
Thus E2 would be empty and G = F1 would be a tree, impossible with minimum degree
three. Similarly c ̸= 1. Both are positive, so s = c = 2. No E2 edge is internal to an F1
component. If edges ran in both directions between the two components, the quotient would
have a directed digon. Thus they all run one way; the assertion for E1 is symmetric. For
each i ∈ {1, 2}, the class Ei consists of all edges between the two components of the other
forest F3−i , and all these edges point from one component to the other.
   Parallel edges of one color would be a cycle in that forest. Parallel edges of different colors
are impossible too: an E1 edge has its ends in one F1 component, while an E2 edge has its
ends in different F1 components. Hence G is simple. If all edges at a vertex had the same
color i, they would all point the same way, because they cross the directed cut given by
the two components of the other forest F3−i . Its divergence would be ±3i or ±4i, nonzero
modulo five. Thus each vertex has both colors.
   Label the F1 components X0 , X1 so E2 runs from X0 to X1 , and label the F2 components
Y0 , Y1 so E1 runs from Y0 to Y1 . Put
                P = X0 ∩ Y0 ,     Q = X0 ∩ Y1 ,   R = X1 ∩ Y0 ,    S = X1 ∩ Y1 .
An occupied cell has a neighbor in each adjacent cell, since each vertex meets both colors.
Starting from any vertex therefore forces all four cells to be occupied.                □

                       103. The four cells and the two graphs
  In the cell notation of Lemma 102.2, value-one edges go P → Q and R → S, and value-two
edges go P → R and Q → S. Each of the four bipartite subgraphs on these cell pairs is an
entire component of one color forest, hence a tree.
  Let d1 , d2 count the two colors at a vertex. Both are positive. Here is the complete list of
possibilities before conservation is imposed; the last two columns are ordinary integers:
                             degree (d1 , d2 ) d1 + 2d2 d1 − 2d2
                               3     (1, 2)           5      −3
                               3     (2, 1)           4        0
                               4     (1, 3)           7      −5
                               4     (2, 2)           6      −2
                               4     (3, 1)           5        1
In P, S both colors point the same way, so the sum column must vanish modulo five: cubic
vertices have pair (1, 2) and quartic vertices pair (3, 1). In Q, R the colors point opposite
ways, so the difference column must vanish: the pairs are (2, 1) and (1, 3) respectively.
   Write the numbers of cubic and quartic vertices in the four cells as
                    P : (a, b),     Q : (c, d),    R : (e, f ),   S : (g, h).
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                           145


For each tree, the sum of the degrees in that tree on either side is its number of edges, and
this is its number of vertices minus one. Thus
     PQ :    a + 3b = 2c + d = a + b + c + d − 1,               c = a + b − 1, d = b + 2 − a;
     QS :    c + 3d = 2g + h = c + d + g + h − 1,               g = c + d − 1, h = d + 2 − c;
     RS :    2e + f = g + 3h = e + f + g + h − 1,               e = g + h − 1, f = h + 2 − g;
     PR :    2a + b = e + 3f = a + b + e + f − 1,               a = e + f − 1, b = f + 2 − e.
Substitute the first pair into the second, then into the third:
                 g = 2b,   h = 5 − 2a,    e = 2b + 4 − 2a,          f = 7 − 2a − 2b.
The last pair gives a = 10 − 4a and b = 5 − 4b. These are integer equations, so a = 2, b = 1.
Substitution yields
                          a = c = e = g = 2,       b = d = f = h = 1.
Every cell has two cubic vertices and one quartic vertex.
   Label the quartic vertex of each cell with subscript zero. In the P Q tree, p0 has three
neighbors; simplicity makes them all three vertices of Q. The vertex q0 now has its one
value-one edge, and each of q1 , q2 needs one more. The two cubic vertices of P have one such
edge each, so the remaining edges form a perfect matching between the two cubic pairs. In
the same way the three-edge centers of P R, RS, QS are r0 , s0 , q0 , respectively. This forces
the twelve center edges in the definition of Gσ ; the other eight edges are four matchings
between cubic pairs.
   Choose labels for the two cubic vertices in P . Transport them along the P Q and P R
matchings, then along the RS matching. These three cell pairs form a spanning tree of the
cell square, so this labels all pairs consistently and makes those three matchings identities.
The remaining QS matching is either the identity or the transposition. The graph is therefore
Gid or Gsw . They are not isomorphic: the induced graph on the degree-three vertices consists
of two disjoint four-cycles for the identity, but for the transposition it is the single cycle
                                     p1 q1 s2 r2 p2 q2 s1 r1 p1 .
Isomorphisms preserve degrees and the induced graph on these vertices.
Converse for both graphs. In either definition of Gσ , the pairs (d1 , d2 ) at cubic and
quartic vertices are exactly those in the table selected above. With the displayed directions,
this proves conservation modulo five; all values are nonzero. In each cell-pair subgraph
the center meets all three opposite vertices and the other two edges form a matching. It
is connected on six vertices with five edges, hence a tree, for both choices of σ. The two
value-one trees on P Q, RS are joined by value-two edges, so the full graph is connected.
All value-two edges go from P ∪ Q to R ∪ S, and all value-one edges from P ∪ R to Q ∪ S.
Every cycle uses both colors, since each color subgraph is a forest. It crosses each of these
cuts equally often in either direction, at least once each. Its signed values therefore include
1, −1, 2, −2. For any nonzero increment x ∈ Z5 , an edge has signed value −x and becomes
zero. No step is legal, proving isolation for both graphs. This completes Theorem 101.1;
their explicit vertex and edge lists give Corollary 101.2.

104. Isolated flows with at most eight odd-degree vertices: statements and
                                     credit
Credit. Esperet, Hendrey, Lagoutte, Marseloo, Norin and Steiner [3, Lemma 3.5] characterize
the isolated nowhere-zero Z5 -flows with the values 1 and 2. The first part of their proof shows
146                                          DAVE GOEL


that the edges of each value form a forest, and that the contraction of the trees of one forest
leaves the edges of the other value as a directed graph with no directed cycle. Lemma 105.1
below is this part of their proof; Lemma 102.1 states it for the graphs of Theorem 101.1,
and we repeat the proof because we need it for all graphs with no loop. The two graphs G0
and G′0 are those of Theorem 96.1 and Remark 100.1. The classification for the graphs with
eight vertices of degree three and all other vertices of degree four is Theorem 101.1; in that
class its proof counts the components of the two forests. The additional deductions of these
sections are the same count for all degrees (Proposition 105.2) and the reduction to that class
of the connected graphs with at least one edge and at most eight vertices of odd degree that
have an isolated nowhere-zero Z5 -flow (Lemma 106.1 together with Proposition 105.2(d)).
Conventions. All graphs are finite; loops and parallel edges are allowed. A loop adds two
to the degree of its vertex, and d(v) is the degree of the vertex v. For a graph G, nd is the
number of its vertices of degree d. Fix a reference direction of every edge. A nowhere-zero
Z5 -flow gives every edge a value other than 0 in Z5 such that at every vertex the sum of
the values that leave is equal to the sum of the values that enter. A cycle is a connected
subgraph in which every vertex has degree two; a loop is a cycle, and two parallel edges are a
cycle. The graph F(G, Z5 ) has the nowhere-zero Z5 -flows of G as its vertices; two flows are
adjacent if the set of the edges on which they differ is the edge set of a cycle. Then one of
them comes from the other by a step: a constant other than 0 is added along one direction
of the cycle (it is subtracted on the edges whose reference direction is against this direction).
A flow is isolated if it has no neighbor in F(G, Z5 ). A graph with no edge has exactly one
nowhere-zero Z5 -flow, the empty one, and this flow is isolated; for this reason the statements
below name the graphs with at least one edge. The graph G is Z5 -flow-connected if the
graph F(G, Z5 ) is connected; a graph F(G, Z5 ) with no vertex or with exactly one vertex
counts as connected, as in Section 36. This is a property of the whole graph of flows; an
isolated flow is one vertex of it with no neighbor. A graph of flows that is not connected is
not assumed to have an isolated vertex. A subdivision of a graph H with no loop is a graph
that comes from H when each edge is replaced by a path with one or more edges between
the same two ends; the inner vertices of these paths are new vertices, and the paths of two
different edges have no inner vertex in common. So H is a subdivision of itself. We use
subdivisions only of graphs with no loop.
   The graphs G0 and G′0 have the twelve vertices pi , qi , ri , si with i = 0, 1, 2, eight of degree
three and four of degree four, and twenty edges; they have no loop and no parallel edges,
and they are not isomorphic (Theorem 96.1 and Remark 100.1).
Theorem 104.1. Let G be a connected graph with at least one edge and with at most eight
vertices of odd degree. Then F(G, Z5 ) has an isolated vertex if and only if G is a subdivision
of G0 or of G′0 .
Corollary 104.2.       (a) Let G be a graph with at least one edge in which every vertex has
      degree at least three. If G has an isolated nowhere-zero Z5 -flow, then
                                             X
                                 n3 ≥ 8 +       (d − 4) nd .
                                                  d≥5
          In particular G has at least eight vertices of degree three, and so at least eight vertices
          of odd degree.
      (b) A connected graph with at least one edge and at most eight vertices of odd degree
          that has an isolated nowhere-zero Z5 -flow has at least twelve vertices; if it has exactly
          twelve, it is isomorphic to G0 or to G′0 .
                  THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                           147


    (c) Let G be a graph in which every connected component has at most eight vertices
        of odd degree. Then F(G, Z5 ) has an isolated vertex if and only if every connected
        component of G is one vertex with no loop, or a subdivision of G0 or of G′0 .
  These statements are about the isolated nowhere-zero Z5 -flows.

                              105. Two forests and a count
   Let G be a graph with no loop, and let f be an isolated nowhere-zero Z5 -flow of G.
Reverse the reference direction of every edge with the value 3 or 4 and replace its value by
the negative value. This keeps the flows, the steps, and so the isolated flows. So we assume
that every value of f is 1 or 2. Let Ei be the set of the edges with the value i, and let
Fi = (V (G), Ei ) for i = 1, 2; a vertex that meets no edge of Ei is a component of Fi .
Lemma 105.1 ([3], from the proof of Lemma 3.5). The graphs F1 and F2 are forests. For
i = 1, 2 and j = 3 − i, contract every tree of Fi to one vertex and keep the edges of Ej with
their directions. The directed graph that comes up has no loop and no directed cycle.
Proof. Let C be a cycle of G all of whose edges are in Ei . Add 2i along one direction of
C. An edge along this direction gets the value 3i, and an edge against it gets the value
i − 2i = −i. Both are not 0 in Z5 . So this is a step from f to another nowhere-zero flow,
against the assumption. So F1 and F2 are forests.
   Let i = 1 and j = 2. Suppose that an edge e of E2 has both ends in one tree T of F1 . Its
ends are different, because G has no loop. Let C be the cycle made of e and of the path of
T between the ends of e, and add 2 along the direction of C in which e is passed along its
reference direction. The edge e gets the value 4. An edge of the path has the value 1, and it
gets the value 3 or the value 1 − 2 = −1. So this is a step, against the assumption. So the
contracted directed graph has no loop. Suppose now that it has a directed cycle, and take
one that passes no vertex twice. It has ℓ ≥ 2 arcs e1 , . . . , eℓ in E2 and passes ℓ different trees
T1 , . . . , Tℓ of F1 , where em goes from a vertex of Tm to a vertex of Tm+1 , with Tℓ+1 = T1 . In
Tm let Pm be the path from the end of em−1 to the start of em , where e0 = eℓ ; it can have no
edge. The paths P1 , . . . , Pℓ lie in different trees, so no two of them have a vertex in common.
So the arcs e1 , . . . , eℓ and the paths P1 , . . . , Pℓ form a cycle C of G (two parallel edges, if
ℓ = 2 and both paths have no edge), and along one direction of C every em is passed along
its reference direction. Add 2 along this direction. Every em gets the value 4, and every edge
of a path gets the value 3 or −1, as before. This is a step, against the assumption.
   For i = 2 and j = 1 the argument is the same with the constant 1 in the place of 2: an
edge of E1 that is passed along its reference direction gets the value 2, and an edge of a path,
which has the value 2, gets the value 3 or the value 2 − 1 = 1.                                    □
Proposition 105.2. Let G be a graph with no loop and with at least one edge, and let f be
an isolated nowhere-zero Z5 -flow of G in which every value is 1 or 2. Let c1 and c2 be the
numbers of the components of F1 and of F2 . Then:
        c1 ≥ 2 and c2 ≥ 2;
    (a) P
    (b)    v∈V (G) 4 − d(v) = 2(c1 + c2 ) ≥ 8;                                  P
    (c) if every vertex of G has degree at least three, then n3 = 2(c1 + c2 ) + d≥5 (d − 4) nd ;
    (d) if every vertex of G has degree at least three and at most eight vertices of G have odd
        degree, then G has exactly eight vertices of degree three, every other vertex of G has
        degree four, and c1 = c2 = 2.
148                                        DAVE GOEL


Proof. (a) Let c1 = 1. Then F1 is a tree that contains all vertices of G. Every edge of E2 has
both ends in this tree, so it is a loop of the contracted directed graph, which has no loop by
Lemma 105.1. So E2 is empty, and G = F1 is a tree with at least one edge. Such a tree has
a vertex of degree one. At a vertex of degree one, a flow has the value 0 on the one edge.
This is not so for f . So c1 ≥ 2, and in the same way c2 ≥ 2.
  (b) Let n be the number of the vertices of G. A forest with n vertices and c components
has n − c edges. So G has (n − c1 ) + (n − c2 ) edges, and the sum of the degrees of G is
4n − 2(c1 + c2 ). With (a) this is (b).                           P
  (c) If all degrees are at least three, the sum in (b) is n3 − d≥5 (d − 4) nd , because the
vertices of degree four add nothing.
  (d) The number of the vertices of odd degree is n3 + n5 + n7 + · · · , and it is at most
8. By (c) and (a),P n3 ≥ 8. So n3 = 8, and nd = 0 for every odd d ≥ 5. Now (c) says
8 = 2(c1 + c2 ) + d≥6 (d − 4) nd with 2(c1 + c2 ) ≥ 8. So c1 + c2 = 4 and nd = 0 for every
d ≥ 6. With (a), c1 = c2 = 2.                                                               □

                       106. Loops and vertices of small degree
Lemma 106.1. Let G be a graph.
  (a) If G has a loop, then no nowhere-zero Z5 -flow of G is isolated.
  (b) If G has a vertex of degree one, then G has no nowhere-zero Z5 -flow.
  (c) Let v be a vertex of degree two of G with no loop at v, and let e′ and e′′ be the two
      edges at v; e′ joins v to a vertex u and e′′ joins v to a vertex w, where u ̸= v, w ̸= v,
      and u = w is possible. Let G/v be the graph that comes from G when v, e′ and e′′
      are deleted and one new edge e between u and w is added; e is a loop if u = w. Then
      F(G, Z5 ) and F(G/v, Z5 ) are isomorphic. Every vertex of G/v has the same degree
      in G/v as in G.
Proof. (a) Let e be a loop and f a nowhere-zero flow. A loop is a cycle. At its vertex it adds
its value to the sum of the values that leave and to the sum of the values that enter, so it
adds nothing to the difference of the two sums, whatever its value is. Add to f (e) a constant
other than 0 and −f (e). This is a step from f to another nowhere-zero flow.
   (b) At a vertex of degree one, the one edge is not a loop, and the equation of the flow at
this vertex says that its value is 0.
   (c) The reversal of a reference direction, with the negative value on that edge, changes
nothing. So let e′ be directed from u to v, e′′ from v to w, and e from u to w. The equation
at v says f (e′ ) = f (e′′ ) for every flow f of G. For a flow f of G let f¯ be the map on the
edges of G/v with f¯(e) = f (e′ ) and with the values of f on the other edges. At u and at w
the edge e adds to the sums what e′ and e′′ add; if u = w, the edges e′ and e′′ together add
the same value to the sum of the values that leave u and to the sum of the values that enter
u, and so does the loop e. So f¯ is a flow of G/v, and it is nowhere-zero if and only if f is. In
the other direction, a flow of G/v gives a flow of G when both e′ and e′′ get the value of e.
So f 7→ f¯ is a bijection between the flows of G and of G/v, and between the nowhere-zero
flows of G and of G/v. A cycle of G that contains v contains both e′ and e′′ ; if u = w, it
is the cycle of these two edges. Replace e′ and e′′ by e: this gives a bijection between the
cycles of G through v and the cycles of G/v through e (for u = w: the loop e). The cycles
of G that do not contain v are the cycles of G/v that do not contain e. Two flows f, g of
G differ on e′ if and only if they differ on e′′ , and if and only if f¯ and ḡ differ on e. So the
set of the edges on which f and g differ is the edge set of a cycle of G if and only if the set
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                        149


of the edges on which f¯ and ḡ differ is the edge set of the corresponding cycle of G/v. So
f 7→ f¯ is an isomorphism of the two graphs of flows. The statement on the degrees is clear:
e meets u and w as e′ and e′′ did.                                                         □

              107. The proofs of the theorem and of the corollary
Proof of Theorem 104.1. Let G be a subdivision of H, where H is G0 or G′0 . Every vertex
of G that is not a vertex of H has degree two and no loop. Apply Lemma 106.1(c) to these
vertices one after the other; the last graph is H. So F(G, Z5 ) and F(H, Z5 ) are isomorphic,
and F(H, Z5 ) has an isolated vertex (Theorem 101.1).
   Now let G be a connected graph with at least one edge and at most eight vertices of odd
degree, and let F(G, Z5 ) have an isolated vertex. By Lemma 106.1(a) and (b), G has no
loop and no vertex of degree one. As long as the graph has a vertex of degree two, apply
Lemma 106.1(c) to it. Each time the number of the vertices goes down by one, so this ends.
Each graph that comes up is connected, because the new edge joins the two neighbors of
the vertex that goes away. It has at least one edge, because a graph with a vertex of degree
two and no loop at this vertex has at least two edges, and the step takes away one edge.
Its graph of flows is isomorphic to F(G, Z5 ) and so has an isolated vertex, and so it has no
loop, by Lemma 106.1(a); and its vertices have the degrees that they have in G. Let G∗
be the last graph. It is connected, it has at least one edge and no loop, it has no vertex of
degree 0, 1 or 2, and it has the same vertices of odd degree as G, so at most eight. Take
an isolated nowhere-zero Z5 -flow of G∗ and change the reference directions as at the start
of Section 105, so that every value is 1 or 2; this changes neither the graph nor a degree,
and the flow stays isolated. By Proposition 105.2(d), G∗ has exactly eight vertices of degree
three, and every other vertex of G∗ has degree four. By Theorem 101.1, G∗ is isomorphic to
G0 or to G′0 . The graph G comes from G∗ when the steps of Lemma 106.1(c) are taken back:
each of them puts one new vertex on an edge. So G is a subdivision of G∗ .                 □
Proof of Corollary 104.2. (a) By Lemma 106.1(a), G has no loop. After the change of the
directions at the start of Section 105, which changes no degree, every value of the flow is 1
or 2. So Proposition 105.2(c) and (a) give the inequality. The number of the vertices of odd
degree is at least n3 .
   (b) By Theorem 104.1 the graph is a subdivision of G0 or of G′0 . These two graphs have
twelve vertices, and a subdivision other than the graph itself has more.
   (c) If G has no vertex, it has no component, so the condition on the components holds;
and F(G, Z5 ) is one vertex, the empty flow, which is isolated. Now let G have a vertex,
and let K1 , . . . , Km be the connected components of G. A nowhere-zero flow of G is one
nowhere-zero flow on each Ka , and a cycle of G lies in one Ka . So a nowhere-zero flow of G
is isolated if and only if its part on each Ka is an isolated vertex of F(Ka , Z5 ). So F(G, Z5 )
has an isolated vertex if and only if every F(Ka , Z5 ) has one. If Ka has no edge, it is one
vertex with no loop, and F(Ka , Z5 ) is one vertex. If Ka has an edge, Theorem 104.1 says
that F(Ka , Z5 ) has an isolated vertex if and only if Ka is a subdivision of G0 or of G′0 ; one
vertex with a loop is a component with an edge, and it is not such a subdivision.             □
Remark 107.1. Two controls of the count. For G0 and G′0 we have n3 = 8 and c1 = c2 = 2.
The graph of [3, Theorem 3.4] has twenty vertices, all of degree three, and the isolated flow
displayed in [3, Figure 3] has fifteen edges of the value 1 and fifteen edges of the value 2 (a
count of the edges of that figure); so c1 = c2 = 5, and Proposition 105.2(c) says 20 = 2 · 10.
No proof of these sections uses these two controls.
150                                      DAVE GOEL


Remark 107.2. By Corollary 104.2(c), a graph with at least one edge in which every
connected component has at most six vertices of odd degree has no isolated nowhere-zero
Z5 -flow: a component with an edge is not a subdivision of G0 or of G′0 , which have eight
vertices of odd degree. The words “at least one edge” are needed: a graph with no edge has
the empty flow, which is isolated. For graphs with vertices of degree two this statement uses
Lemma 106.1 and a component with an edge; Proposition 105.2 alone gives it only for the
graphs with all degrees at least three. The statement also follows from Theorem 36.1, which
gives more: such a graph is Z5 -flow-connected. This gives the statement again: if such a
graph has a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow f , then 2f is a nowhere-zero Z5 -flow other than f , because
the graph has an edge; so the connected graph of flows has more than one vertex, and no
vertex of it is isolated. If the graph has no nowhere-zero Z5 -flow, its graph of flows has no
vertex, and so no isolated vertex.
