Part 9. Integer flows at more moduli
   This part proves Theorem 1.8, on the integer flows at seven moduli. Its first four sections
prove the statement for the modulus 7 (Theorem 108.1): every graph in which every connected
component has at most eight vertices of odd degree is 7-flow-connected. They give their
own words; the graphs of flows are written M7 (G) and I7 (G) there. They use the theorem
of the sections for part (f) of Theorem 1.3 at the modulus 7 and give the first counts of
those sections again, for one component of the integer flows; they also use part (c) for k = 7,
Proposition 60.1, the proof of Lemma 75.1, and three statements of [6]. The last section,
Section 112, gives the statements for the moduli 8, 9, 12, 16, 18 and 36 from a theorem of [6].

  108. Integer seven-flows with eight odd vertices: statement and inputs
   Graphs are finite multigraphs; loops and parallel edges are allowed. A loop contributes
two to degree and zero to divergence. Fix reference directions. Divergence is outgoing sum
minus incoming sum. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph, including a loop or a digon.
For a cycle traversal, χC is 1 on forward edges, −1 on backward edges and zero elsewhere;
on a loop choose its reference direction.
   An integer 7-flow is an integer circulation F with 1 ≤ |F (e)| ≤ 6 on every edge. A modular
flow here means a nowhere-zero Z7 -flow. Write I7 (G) and M7 (G) for their reconfiguration
graphs: a step adds a nonzero integer, respectively nonzero residue, times χC , keeping both
endpoints within the specified flow space. An empty or one-vertex reconfiguration graph
counts as connected. An edgeless graph has the unique empty flow. Reduction modulo seven
sends an integer step to a modular step or to equality. An integer lift of a modular flow is an
integer 7-flow with that reduction. A restricted step has modular coefficient in {1, −1, 2, −2};
the conclusion below allows all legal integer steps.
Theorem 108.1. Let G be a finite multigraph in which every connected component has at
most eight odd-degree vertices. Then I7 (G) is connected.
  This theorem is about the integer 7-flows.
Dependencies and credit. The following already established inputs are stated explicitly.
 (D1) The modular-seven case of Theorem 86.1 states that M7 (G) is connected under the
       odd-vertex hypothesis of Theorem 108.1.
 (D2) Tutte’s lifting theorem, restated in [3, Theorem 2.3], gives an integer lift of every
       modular flow. The same-component conclusion for two lifts of a fixed flow is [3,
       Lemma 5.5]. Its proof joins those lifts within their common fibre by cycle steps with
       coefficients that are multiples of seven; hence the fibre induces a connected subgraph
       of I7 (G). These lifting facts, including free loop coordinates under our multigraph
       convention, are proved again in [6, Section 3]. The restricted-step corollary in [6,
       Section 4] gives adjacent integer endpoint lifts for every legal restricted modular step.
       Theorem 2.3 of [6] gives adjacent integer endpoint lifts for every legal modular-seven
       step on a cycle with at most twenty-one edges. It does not say that a prescribed
       integer lift accepts that step.
 (D3) The integer conclusion of Theorem 45.2 gives connectivity when each component has
       at most six odd vertices.
 (D4) Any two bridgeless multigraphs with the same labelled odd-vertex set have a common
       feasible sign pattern, including when they are disconnected and their even vertices
       differ (Proposition 60.1). That common-pattern consequence is derived there from
       Nash-Williams’ classical odd-vertex pairing theorem [9, Theorem 2].
152                                       DAVE GOEL


  (D5) With eight odd vertices, every feasible pattern has an integer anchor of magnitudes
        one through five. Two anchors of the same pattern in one graph are joined by an
        integer path within those magnitudes. The statement of Lemma 75.1 is modular; its
        proof constructs this integer path before reduction. We recall that construction in
        Section 111.
Here a graph is bridgeless if it has no bridge. A bridge carries zero in any circulation, so a
graph supporting a nowhere-zero flow is bridgeless. Our local normalization and marked-edge
counts are those in Sections 88 and 89, before Lemma 89.1. We give their proofs again to
check that they work in the projection of one integer component, using only restricted steps.
We do not use the later scalar argument of that text. The even-degree triangle expansion
is the method of Cranston et al. [2, Observation 4.6]. The odd-degree version, and the
assembly by splitting, modular triangle extension, fibre paths and integer projection, are
those of Sections 51 to 53. All required assembly details are included below. The deduction
of these sections is the integer conclusion obtained by combining the weaker twenty-vertex
structural bound with (D1) and (D2). In particular this proof depends on the previously
proved modular theorem, and does not replace its proof.

         109. Integer components, balance, and the small-order case
   At a nonloop edge end, read its flow value outward from its vertex. Two distinct nonloop
incident edges are balanced if their outward values sum to zero, in the integers or modulo
seven as specified. A quartic pair means such a balanced pair at a vertex of degree four.
Splitting a balanced pair uv, vw replaces it with uw, retaining v; u = w is permitted and
creates a loop. Orient the path u → v → w and copy the replacement edge’s value along it.
This identifies the split flows with flows balanced on that pair, both integrally and modularly.
Edge count decreases by one, every vertex keeps its degree parity, and maximum degree does
not increase. A cycle in the split graph lifts to an edge-simple closed trail. Decompose its
signed support into edge-disjoint directed cycles and apply the same coefficient on each. Every
edge has its old or final value throughout; thus all intermediate values remain admissible.
The same reasoning applies if the replacement edge is a loop.
Lemma 109.1. Let K be a component of I7 (G) and let R be its set of modular reductions.
Then R is closed under legal restricted modular steps. If K contains no integer-balanced pair
at a quartic vertex, no state in R has a modular-balanced pair there.
Proof. For a restricted step f → g with f ∈ R, (D2) supplies adjacent integer lifts A, B of
f, g. Some lift of f is in K; its fibre connects it to A, so B ∈ K and g ∈ R. If f ∈ R is
modularly balanced at a quartic pair, split the pair. The split modular flow has an integer
lift by (D2). Copying its replacement value back gives an integer-balanced lift of f . The
fibre over f joins this lift to K, a contradiction.                                      □
  The set R need not be a full modular component and will not be treated as one. Nor will
multiplication by a scalar be used as an operation on K or R.
Lemma 109.2. If G has at most twenty vertices and at most eight odd-degree vertices per
component, then I7 (G) is connected.
Proof. By (D1), its modular graph is connected. Every cycle has at most twenty edges, also
for loops and digons. This is within the twenty-one-edge bound in (D2), so every modular
step has adjacent integer endpoint lifts. Insert fibre paths between consecutive choices and
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          153


at the two prescribed endpoints. This connects any two integer flows; empty and singleton
spaces satisfy the conclusion as well.                                                  □

                 110. The local bound using restricted moves only
Proposition 110.1. Suppose G is connected and loopless, has exactly eight cubic vertices,
and all its other vertices are quartic. If a component of I7 (G) contains no integer-balanced
quartic pair, then |V (G)| ≤ 20.
Proof. Let R be its modular image. It is a finite nonempty set, closed under restricted steps,
with no modular-balanced quartic pair by Lemma 109.1. A modular-balanced pair at a
cubic vertex is impossible too: conservation would make its remaining edge zero. Choose
g ∈ R maximizing the number of edges of magnitude one. Residues ±1 are small ; the others,
±2, ±3, are large. Let S, N be their spanning supports.
   Normalization. The graph N is a forest. Otherwise orient a cycle of N for traversal and
take its residues in {2, 3, 4, 5}. Subtract one repeatedly until a first value becomes one. Every
intermediate value is nonzero; no previously small edge is changed, and the small count
increases. These are legal restricted steps in R, contrary to the choice of g.
   Small cycles. At a vertex of a small cycle, its two outward cycle values agree, say
a ∈ {1, −1}, because opposite values would be balanced. Choose one of these two cycle
ends. If an off-cycle end has value b ̸= −2a, put t = a + b. Then t ∈       / {0, a, −a}: the three
excluded equalities would give, respectively, an existing balanced pair, b = 0, or b = −2a.
Consequently t = ja for some j ∈ {2, 3, 4, 5}. Traverse the cycle into the chosen a-end and
use cumulative offsets
                                        0, 2a, 3a, . . . , ja.
The first increment is 2a and each subsequent increment is a. Every signed starting cycle
value is a or −a; after offset ℓa, 2 ≤ ℓ ≤ 5, it is (ℓ + 1)a or (ℓ − 1)a, nonzero modulo seven.
The selected outward end becomes a − t = −b, balanced with the unchanged off-cycle end.
Thus all these steps stay in R and give a contradiction. This uses a sign a in the coefficients,
not scalar multiplication of an integer component. All off-cycle ends must therefore be −2a.
A quartic cycle vertex would have sum 2a − 4a = −2a ̸= 0, impossible. Every small cycle
consists of cubic vertices, each with exactly two small ends, and is an entire component of S.
The signs alternate along its traversal, so its length is even.
   The two component counts. Let n = |V (G)|, let q = n − 8 be the number of quartics, let s
count the tree components of S, and let c count all components of N . Components with one
vertex are included in both counts. Then
(22)        |E(S)| = n − s,    |E(N )| = n − c,     |E(G)| = 2n − 4,       s + c = 4.
All small ends at a vertex have one outward sign. Let ni count quartics with i small ends
and pi count cubics with i small ends. Quartics have at most three small ends and cubics at
most two, since four or three equal values ±1 cannot sum to zero modulo seven. Summing
small degree minus two gives
                              −2s = −2n0 − n1 + n3 − 2p0 − p1 .
Using q = n0 + n1 + n2 + n3 , this becomes
(23)                          q = 2n3 + n2 + 2s − n0 − 2p0 − p1 .
If s = 0, every vertex lies in a cubic cycle component, so n = 8. Henceforth s ≥ 1, and (22)
gives s ≤ 3.
154                                        DAVE GOEL


   The marked edges. Call a quartic vertex with i small ends type i. A type-two quartic
has outward tuple (a, a, 2a, 3a): after dividing the identity by the sign a, two large residues
from {2, 3, 4, 5} must sum to 5 modulo seven, whose only such unordered pair is {2, 3}. A
type-three quartic has tuple (a, a, a, −3a). Thus each vertex of either type has exactly one
magnitude-three incidence. Mark all magnitude-three edges with an endpoint of one of these
types. A qualifying endpoint is a type-two or type-three endpoint of a marked edge. No such
edge meets a small cubic cycle, whose exterior values have magnitude two.
   Form the auxiliary multigraph on the s small trees using the marked edges. It has no
loop or cycle. Indeed an auxiliary loop lifts by its edge and the unique tree path between its
distinct original ends. An auxiliary cycle, including a digon, lifts using the unique path in
each visited small tree between its arriving and departing ends. Different trees are disjoint,
and are each visited only once, so the lift is an actual cycle; paths of length zero at other
vertices cause no problem. At a qualifying endpoint its path is nonempty, since the vertex
has only one magnitude-three end. Orient the lifted cycle out along that vertex’s small
a-end and add −2a. Every cycle value has magnitude one or three, so it stays nonzero after
this increment. The chosen end becomes −a and balances an untouched a-end. This is a
restricted step in R, a contradiction. There are at most s − 1 marked edges and each has at
most two qualifying endpoints. Therefore
(24)                                  n2 + n3 ≤ 2(s − 1).
  Call a marked edge special if it has a type-three endpoint. Let e be the number of special
edges and d the number having two type-three endpoints. Then n3 = e + d and e ≤ s − 1.
Every double-ended edge is an entire two-vertex component of N , because each type-three
vertex has large degree one. These components are distinct and contain no cubic vertex.
The eight cubic vertices require at least one further component of the spanning graph N , so
d ≤ c − 1. Hence
(25)                                  n3 ≤ s + c − 2 = 2.
Finally, (23)–(25) imply
          q ≤ 2n3 + n2 + 2s = n3 + (n2 + n3 ) + 2s ≤ 2 + 2(s − 1) + 2s = 4s ≤ 12.
Thus n = 8 + q ≤ 20. Neither the later scalar rescaling in Sections 86 to 95 nor its short-cycle
contraction alternatives have entered this proof.                                             □

                   111. Balanced families and arbitrary degrees
   We first spell out the anchor input and the integer path used in it. Let H be a graph
whose odd-vertex set is exactly a set X of eight labelled vertices. A pattern chooses four of
them; its sign function β is 1 on these four, −1 on the other four, and zero elsewhere. It is
feasible for H if it sums to zero on every connected component and
                         |β(U )| < |δH (U )|   whenever δH (U ) ̸= ∅.
              P
Here β(U ) = v∈U β(v) and δH (U ) is the set of edges with exactly one endpoint in U . An
anchor is an integer circulation with magnitudes in {1, 2, 3, 4, 5} whose positive orientation
has unit divergence β. Positive orientation means orienting each edge by the sign of its
integer value; unit divergence is outdegree minus indegree.
   For existence, the construction cited in (D5) gives a strongly connected orientation of each
nontrivial bridgeless component with unit divergence β. Pair the four negative terminals
with the four positive terminals within their respective components, using each terminal once;
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                       155


the zero sum of β on each component permits this. Strong connectivity supplies one simple
directed path from the negative to the positive terminal of each pair. Adding the four path
vectors corrects the unit map’s divergence to zero. Each edge is used at most four times, so
the resulting magnitudes are one through five. Loops are assigned one in a chosen direction.
   For joining two anchors A, B of the same pattern, orient their disagreement edges as in
the positive orientation of A. Equality of the two unit divergences makes this subgraph
balanced: the difference of the unit maps is twice its signed incidence map. Decompose it
into edge-disjoint directed cycles, including loops. On each cycle subtract one plus its largest
current positive value. Every changed value becomes negative with magnitude one through
five. The cycles are edge-disjoint, so after these operations the positive orientations agree.
The remaining difference is an integer circulation; decompose it conformally into signed unit
cycles. All intermediate values lie between the same-sign endpoint values, and so remain
nonzero with magnitude at most five. This is an integer path, hence valid in I7 (G). It is the
construction in the proof of Lemma 75.1.
Proposition 111.1. Theorem 108.1 holds for graphs of maximum degree at most four.
Proof. Induct on the number of edges in that class. The edgeless graph has one flow. Loops
are free nonzero coordinates, so remove them, use induction and restore them by loop steps.
Isolated vertices do not matter. Bridges give empty spaces, and separate edge-bearing
components use products and induction. A remaining degree-one vertex would give a bridge.
Suppressing a degree-two vertex identifies its two-edge path with one edge, preserving flows,
integer bounds and cycle steps, also when a loop results. Thus the remaining graph is
connected, loopless, of degrees three and four. If there are at most six cubic vertices, apply
(D3). Otherwise there are exactly eight, since odd counts are even.
   If it has at most twenty vertices, apply Lemma 109.2. Otherwise Proposition 110.1 implies
that every integer component contains an integer-balanced quartic pair. For a fixed pair,
the balanced family is identified with flows of the split graph, which has fewer edges and
the same odd labels. It remains subquartic, and each of its components has at most eight
odd vertices. Edge induction joins its flows; the splitting lift described above joins the whole
family in the ambient graph.
   Two nonempty families give two split graphs supporting flows, hence bridgeless graphs.
Their odd labels coincide, though their components may differ. By (D4) they have a common
feasible pattern. Choose an anchor in each split graph by (D5) and lift it through the split
pair. This preserves magnitudes and positive unit divergence: the reinserted path contributes
one incoming and one outgoing end at the split vertex, with all other ends unchanged.
The two lifted anchors in G therefore have the same pattern. The integer anchor path
just described joins them with magnitudes at most five. All nonempty balanced families
consequently join, and every integer component meets one. This proves connectivity.           □
Proof of Theorem 108.1. Remove loops first: their values are free nonzero coordinates,
independent
P             of conservation, and can be changed by loop steps. Induct on Ψ(G) =
   v max{deg(v)  − 4, 0} within the stated odd-vertex class. The case Ψ = 0 is Proposi-
tion 111.1. For an even vertex of degree d ≥ 6, expand it to a triangle with exterior ends
distributed in groups 2, 2, d − 4. The new degrees are 4, 4, d − 2, lowering Ψ by two. For odd
d ≥ 5, groups 1, 2, d − 3 give degrees 3, 4, d − 1, lowering Ψ by one. The connected component
remains connected and its odd count is unchanged. There are no other edges between the
three new vertices because loops were removed.
156                                       DAVE GOEL


   Any prescribed modular flow downstairs extends over this triangle. If β1 , β2 , β3 are
its exterior divergences, their sum is zero. In a cyclic orientation of the triangle, assign
z, z − β2 , z + β1 . These solve conservation, and at most three values of z ∈ Z7 are forbidden
by nonzero requirements. An allowed z exists.
   For a desired downstairs integer endpoint F , extend its modular reduction this way, then
choose an integer lift upstairs by (D2). Its projection F ′ is a downstairs integer 7-flow in
the same modular fibre as F . A fibre path joins F to F ′ . Perform this construction for
both desired endpoints. By induction the upstairs lifts are joined. The signed cycle vector
of an upstairs step restricts after contraction to an integer circulation with entries 0, 1, −1.
Decompose its signed support into edge-disjoint directed cycles downstairs and apply the
original integer coefficient on them successively. Each retained edge has either its old or its
final value throughout, so it stays nonzero and of magnitude at most six. A zero projected
vector gives a stationary step. This projects the path, and the two downstairs fibre paths
connect it to the original endpoints. Restore free loop coordinates. This proves the theorem
without assuming that a prescribed integer flow extends while keeping its outside values
fixed, or invoking an integer contraction theorem.                                            □

                  112. The integer flows at six composite moduli
   This section gives the statements of Theorem 1.8 for the moduli 8, 9, 12, 16, 18 and
36. Its one tool is not proved in this paper: for each of these six k and for every graph G,
reduction modulo k induces a bijection between the connected components of F(G, k) and
the connected components of F(G, Zk ) [6, Theorem 18.1]; in particular F(G, k) is connected
if and only if F(G, Zk ) is connected. Here F(G, Zk ) and F(G, k) are the graphs of Section 1;
a loop is a cycle, and a graph of flows with no vertex or with one vertex counts as connected.
In several other sections of this paper they are written Mk (G) and Ik (G). The corollary and
its proof are those of [6], where the corollary stands after that theorem; only the references
are changed, to references inside this paper.
Corollary 112.1. Let t and k be integers, and let G be a graph in which every connected
component has at most t vertices of odd degree. Then G is k-flow-connected in each of these
cases:
    (a) t = 8 and k is 8, 9 or 12;
    (b) t = 12 and k is 16 or 18;
    (c) t = 24 and k = 36.
Proof of Corollary 112.1. (a) By Corollary 86.2 the graph F(G, Zk ) is connected for every
k ≥ 6. (b) and (c) By Theorem 82.1, for integers r ≥ 4 and k ≥ 3r − 2, a graph in which
every connected component has at most 2r vertices of odd degree is Zk -flow-connected; use
r = 6, where 3r − 2 = 16, for k = 16 and k = 18, and r = 12, where 3r − 2 = 34, for k = 36.
In each case [6, Theorem 18.1] gives that F(G, k) is connected.                          □
   The corollary is for the six moduli 8, 9, 12, 16, 18 and 36. In part (a) the moduli 16, 18
and 36 are not named, because parts (b) and (c) contain them: a graph of part (a) is a graph
of parts (b) and (c).
                 THE CRANSTON–LI–SU–WANG–XU (2026) CONJECTURE IS TRUE                          157


   Appendix A. Short cycles and the contraction of an induced subgraph
Credit. The statements and proof ideas below are due to Cranston, Li, Su, Wang and Xu [2,
Theorems 5.9 and 5.11; Corollaries 5.13 and 5.15]. We include proofs for induced subgraphs
and every finite abelian group, covering the digon in the same argument.
   All graphs in this appendix are finite loopless multigraphs. Fix a finite abelian group
A, written additively, and reference edge orientations. Divergence means outgoing sum
minus incoming sum. A β-flow is an assignment of nonzero elements of A to the edges with
divergence β; a flow has divergence zero. A cycle move adds a nonzero constant along a
traversal of a cycle (with the corresponding signs in the reference orientation), keeping every
edge value nonzero. A cycle is a connected 2-regular subgraph; digons are cycles. A graph is
A-flow-connected if any two flows are joined by cycle moves, with this condition vacuous
when no flow exists.
   Reversing an edge and negating its value is a bijection preserving boundaries, nonzero
values, cycle moves and difference supports. Thus these notions and the three properties
below are independent of orientation. Following [2, Definitions 5.7 and 5.8], a connected
graph H is A-flow-reconfiguration-contractible if:
                                     P
     (i) every β : V (H) → A with v β(v) = 0 has a β-flow;
    (ii) for every zero-sum β, any two β-flows are joined by cycle moves within H;
   (iii) if zero-sum boundaries β1 , β2 differ only at distinct vertices u, w, there are respective
         boundary flows whose difference is supported on a simple u–w path (or is zero if the
         boundaries coincide).
Lemma A.1. Let n ≥ 2 and N = |A|. If ⌈2N/3⌉ > n, then the cycle Cn is A-flow-
reconfiguration-contractible.
Proof. Orient Cn cyclically. For a zero-sum boundary, all solutions have edge values a + ce ,
where a ∈ A and the offsets ce are fixed: solve consecutively around the cycle, and the zero
sum closes the recurrence. At most n parameters are forbidden by zero edge values. Since
N ≥ ⌈2N/3⌉ > n, a valid parameter exists, proving (i). Two solutions differ by a constant
on the entire cycle, so they are equal or adjacent, proving (ii).
   For (iii), suppose β2 − β1 equals γ ̸= 0 at u and −γ at w. Let P1 run cyclically from u
to w and P2 from w to u. For the β1 parameterization, let X, Y be the sets of forbidden
parameters on P1 , P2 , respectively, and put s = |X| + |Y | ≤ n and Z = A \ (X ∪ Y ), z = |Z|.
Starting at any a ∈ Z, adding γ on P1 gives a β2 -flow exactly when a + γ ∈   / X; subtracting γ
on P2 gives one exactly when a − γ ∈   / Y . If both conversions fail for every a ∈ Z (including
Z = ∅), then Z + γ ⊆ X and Z − γ ⊆ Y . Translation is bijective, so s ≥ 2z, while
                               N = |X ∪ Y | + z ≤ s + z ≤ 3s/2.
Thus s ≥ ⌈2N/3⌉ > n, a contradiction. One of the conversions therefore supplies (iii).
Coincident boundaries use the same flow twice. For n = 2, the two paths are simply the two
distinct edges.                                                                          □
  An induced subgraph H contains every edge of G with both ends in V (H), including
parallel edges. An induced digon therefore has exactly two edges between its vertices. Define
G/H by identifying V (H) to one vertex h and deleting E(H), retaining all other edges. Since
H is induced and G is loopless, G/H is loopless too; this is where the loopless hypothesis is
used in the proof below.
158                                             DAVE GOEL


Lemma A.2. Let G be loopless and H an induced connected subgraph satisfying (i)–(iii). If
G/H is A-flow-connected, then so is G.
Proof. Restricting a flow of G to the retained edges gives a flow of G/H: at h the divergence
is the sum over V (H), with the contributions of edges of H cancelling. Conversely, a quotient
flow prescribes on H the boundary equal to the negative of the exterior divergence. Its sum
is zero by conservation at h, so (i) extends it to a flow of G. Thus restriction is surjective.
Two extensions of the same quotient flow agree outside H and induce the same boundary
inside it. Property (ii) joins them by moves in H; in other words, every fibre is connected.
   Consider a cycle move between quotient flows f1 , f2 . If its cycle avoids h, lift f1 arbitrarily
and apply the same move outside H. If the cycle contains h, let x, y ∈ V (H) be the original
ends in G of its two edges at h. If x = y, its edges already form a cycle in G, and the same
move lifts. Otherwise they form an exterior x–y path P with at least one internal vertex,
all internal vertices outside H. The two prescribed boundaries on H differ only at x, y,
by opposite nonzero elements. By (iii), choose internal boundary flows whose difference is
supported on an x–y path Q. Conservation at internal vertices of Q forces a constant signed
difference along Q. It is nonzero, since the boundaries differ at x, so its support is all of Q;
the endpoint divergences give precisely the coefficient needed to close the exterior move on
P . Their interiors are disjoint, so P ∪ Q is a cycle in G. Combining each internal flow with
its exterior quotient values gives adjacent lifts.
   Finally, join any two original flows by lifting a quotient path and inserting fibre paths
between consecutive chosen lifts, including at both ends. If no original flows exist, the
conclusion is vacuous.                                                                            □
  Of the two directions of [2, Theorem 5.9(2)], only the lifting direction is stated in Lemma A.2
and used here.
Corollary A.3. Let A be any finite abelian group with |A| ≥ 7. If a loopless graph G
contains an induced cycle C of length 2, 3 or 4, and G/C is A-flow-connected, then G is
A-flow-connected.
Proof. For N ≥ 7, ⌈2N/3⌉ ≥ 5 > |E(C)|. Apply both lemmas.                                                   □
